# sigma-predict — All Findings (raw, with evidence)

Companion to [`AUDIT.md`](./AUDIT.md). 275 findings, every pass, confirmed and refuted.


## CRITICAL

### `BIZ:-139` — Platt scaling phase (Phase 2) is permanently dead — params are never fit or saved anywhere in production code
- **confirmed ✓** · source: biz-logic · effort: medium · file: `src/pipeline/calibration.py:62, src/pipeline/orchestrator.py:336`
- **evidence:** calibrate() Phase 2 gate is `if n_resolved >= config.calibration.min_resolved_for_platt and platt_params:`. The orchestrator supplies platt_params via `platt_params = load_platt_params(self.config)` which reads `platt_global.json`. But `fit_platt()` and `save_platt_params()` are defined and ONLY ever called from tests (grep confirms no caller in src/ or scripts/). resolve.py computes brier/log scores on resolution but never refits or persists Platt params. Therefore platt_global.json is never written, load_platt_params() always returns None, and the Phase 2 branch can never execute regardless of n_resolved. The CalibrationConfig docstring/comment `platt_refit_interval: refit every N resolved predictions` describes a refit loop that does not exist. The module docstring claims 'Phase 2 (N>=50): Platt scaling on historical data' as an active capability.
- **recommendation:** Either wire a refit step into resolve.py (after updating resolutions, if count_resolved crosses platt_refit_interval, call fit_platt on get_calibration_pairs() and save_platt_params), or remove the Platt phase, load_platt_params call, platt_params_used model field, and platt_refit_interval/min_resolved_for_platt config to stop advertising a control that has no effect. This is the same dead-Platt-phase class flagged previously.
- **verifier:** CONFIRMED. Every load-bearing claim verifies against the actual code.

WHAT THE CODE CLAIMS (intent):
- Module docstring (calibration.py:1-6) advertises "Phase 2 (N>=50): Platt scaling on historical data" as an active capability.
- calibrate() docstring (lines 47-49) lists "Phase 2 (N>=50): Platt scaling" as a live phase.
- CalibrationConfig (config.py:40-41) exposes platt_refit_interval=50 with comment "refit every N resolved predictions" and min_resolved_for_platt=50, advertising a refit loop.
- PredictionRecord/CalibrationData carries platt_params_used (models.py:127) and human_review.py:135-136 prints "Platt scaled: a=..., b=..." — the system presents Platt scaling as a user-visible feature.

WHAT THE CODE ACTUALLY DOES:
- calibration.py:62 Phase 2 gate is `if n_resolved >= config.calibration.min_resolved_for_platt and platt_params:` — requires a non-None platt_params object.
- orchestrator.py:336 supplies it via `load_platt_params(self.config)`, which (calibration.py:280-287) reads platt_{label}.json from calibration_dir and returns None if the file does not exist.
- grep across the entire repo: fit_platt() is called ONLY from tests/test_calibration.py (lines 72, 78) — zero callers in src/ or scripts/. save_platt_params() has ZERO callers anywhere (only its def). No json.dump for platt outside the unused save_platt_params body.
- scripts/resolve.py (the resolution path) computes brier_score/log_score and stores Resolution data (lines 104-114) but never calls fit_platt or save_platt_params and never references platt_refit_interval. There is no refit hook anywhere.
- No platt_*.json file exists on disk (find returned none) and none is git-tracked.

CONCLUSION ON THE GAP: Because save_platt_params is never invoked, platt_global.json is never written; load_platt_params therefore always returns None in production; the Phase 2 branch's `and platt_params` condition is always False regardless of n_resolved. Phase 2 is permanently unreachable. The control surface (platt_refit_interval, the "refit every N" comment, the active-capability docstrings, the platt_params_used field/human-review printout) advertises behavior that cannot execute. This is a genuine, code-grounded intent-vs-implementation divergence.

REFUTATION ATTEMPTS THAT FAILED: (1) A pre-shipped/committed params file — none tracked, none on disk. (2) An alternate writer of the json — grep shows none. (3) A second resolution module wiring the refit — scripts/resolve.py is the only resolution path and contains no refit. (4) get_calibration_pairs feeding a fit — it only feeds isotonic_data into calibrate(), not Platt fitting.

NOTE: One nuance worth flagging on materiality, not existence. Phase 1 isotonic regression IS wired and live (orchestrator passes get_calibration_pairs as isotonic_data; fit_isotonic_logit is called inline at calibration.py:74). So the system is not un-calibrated above N>=20 — it just never escalates past isotonic to Platt. The functional impact of the dead Phase 2 is therefore "isotonic is used where Platt was intended for N>=50," which is a degraded-but-not-broken calibration, plus a misleading config/docstring surface. That is a real correctness/honesty gap but the practical harm is bounded by the working isotonic fallback.

### `BIZ:-151` — Human Review gate is inert: the UI submits to the live platform BEFORE the human reviews
- **confirmed ✓** · source: biz-logic · effort: medium · file: `/Users/bjgilbert/Projects/Zoltar/sigma-predict/pages/2_Predict.py:72-81 and :300-377`
- **evidence:** The Predict page runs the pipeline via orchestrator.predict(..., auto_submit=True) (line 72-78). In orchestrator.py:492-499, auto_submit=True calls submit_after_review(record, ..., human_review=HumanReview(reviewed=False)), which at orchestrator.py:430 calls platform_client.submit_prediction(...) and saves the record to the registry. ONLY AFTER that thread completes does the UI render the 'Human Review' section (pages/2_Predict.py:300) with Accept/Adjust/Reject radio buttons. So the prediction is already submitted to Metaculus/Polymarket/Kalshi and persisted before the human sees the review form. The orchestrator's own docstring (orchestrator.py:471-473) says: 'Use predict_until_review() + submit_after_review() directly when you need to show results before submission (e.g. Streamlit UI).' The UI does the exact opposite of the documented contract. The named safeguard (human review before submission) runs but has no effect on what was submitted.
- **recommendation:** Replace the threaded orchestrator.predict(auto_submit=True) call with orchestrator.predict_until_review(question, platform_client, num_runs, personas), which returns the record WITHOUT submitting. Then wire the 'Confirm Review' button (currently doing only cache._store.update at line 376) to call orchestrator.submit_after_review(record, question, platform_client, human_review). The required APIs already exist and are documented for exactly this UI use case.
- **verifier:** VERIFIED. The intent-vs-implementation gap is real, material, and code-grounded. Traced the full chain:

1. UI (pages/2_Predict.py:72-78) runs the pipeline in a background thread via orchestrator.predict(..., auto_submit=True).
2. orchestrator.predict (src/pipeline/orchestrator.py:461-505): with auto_submit=True it runs predict_until_review() (which explicitly does NOT submit/save, docstring line 81), then at lines 492-499 immediately calls submit_after_review(record, question, platform_client, human_review=HumanReview(reviewed=False)).
3. submit_after_review (lines 384-459): the only submission gate is line 416, which rejects only when review.human_reasoning starts with 'REJECTED'. A default HumanReview(reviewed=False) has human_reasoning=None (confirmed in src/models.py:139-144), so it does NOT trip the gate. The reviewed flag itself is never consulted. Execution proceeds to line 430 platform_client.submit_prediction(...) — a real live HTTP POST (confirmed in metaculus.py:131-154, hits /questions/{id}/predict/) — and line 451 store.save(record).
4. Only after the thread completes (UI lines 80-81, sentinel at 86, state->'awaiting_review' at line 278) does the UI render the 'Human Review' Accept/Adjust/Reject section (line 300).
5. The 'Confirm Review' button (lines 345-377) does NOT call submit_after_review — it only does cache._store.update(record)+invalidate (lines 375-377). An explicit TODO at line 373 confirms the intended API is unwired. TODOs at lines 62, 71 confirm the same on the run side.

So the named safeguard — human review before submission — runs only after the prediction has already been submitted to the live platform and persisted. A reviewer's 'Reject' cannot retract or alter the live submission; it only annotates the local record. The orchestrator's own docstring (lines 471-473) prescribes predict_until_review()+submit_after_review() for exactly the Streamlit UI case, and the UI does the opposite. The recommendation is sound and the required APIs already exist.

Refutation attempts failed: (a) line numbers in the finding are off by ~2 in one citation (docstring is 469-473 not 471-473), but every claimed behavior is present at the cited region — immaterial. (b) The only mitigating path is when no API key is set (submit_prediction returns False) or NotImplementedError is caught and logged as '_logged_only'; this is accidental, not the gate working — in normal operation with keys present it submits live.

### `BIZ:-152` — TODO comments claim Orchestrator review API is unfinished, but it has shipped — driving the wrong code path
- **confirmed ✓** · source: biz-logic · effort: small · file: `/Users/bjgilbert/Projects/Zoltar/sigma-predict/pages/2_Predict.py:61-71, :373`
- **evidence:** Three comments assert the review-split API does not yet exist: line 61 'TODO: replace with actual Orchestrator.predict_until_review() when TA finalises API', line 64 'For now wraps orchestrator.predict() with auto_submit=True', line 71 'TODO: switch to orchestrator.predict_until_review() once available', line 373 'TODO: call orchestrator.submit_after_review(record, human_review) when TA finalises API'. But orchestrator.py:70 defines predict_until_review(question, platform_client, num_runs, personas) and orchestrator.py:384 defines submit_after_review(record, question, platform_client, human_review) with full docstrings. The 'not yet available' premise is false; the stale TODO is the direct cause of the inert-review bug above.
- **recommendation:** Delete the stale TODOs and adopt the two-phase API now that it exists. This finding and the one above share a single fix.
- **verifier:** Confirmed by direct code reading. The three TODOs exist verbatim in pages/2_Predict.py (lines 62, 71, 373) and assert the two-phase review API does not yet exist ("when TA finalises API", "once available"). That premise is false: orchestrator.py defines predict_until_review() at line 70 and submit_after_review() at line 384, both fully implemented with complete docstrings. Decisively, predict()'s own docstring (orchestrator.py:471-473) instructs: "Use predict_until_review() + submit_after_review() directly when you need to show results before submission (e.g. Streamlit UI)" — the intended consumer of the new API is literally the Streamlit UI that the stale TODO is steering away from it.

The wrong-path consequence is material and matches the referenced inert-review bug: the UI calls predict(..., auto_submit=True) (line 72), which internally constructs HumanReview(reviewed=False) and submits to the platform automatically (orchestrator.py:492-494) BEFORE any human review. Then after the human reviews in the UI, line 376 does cache._store.update(record) directly and never calls submit_after_review, so the human's accept/adjust/reject decision is persisted to the local registry but never affects platform submission — the auto-submit already fired. Human review is collected but inert for submission, and un-reviewed predictions are auto-submitted to live platforms.

Two minor citation inaccuracies that do not weaken the finding: the actual predict_until_review signature also includes a progress_queue param (finding omitted it), and the line-373 TODO's submit_after_review(record, human_review) omits the question and platform_client params the real method requires. Neither affects the intent-vs-implementation divergence, which is unambiguous.

### `DEPS-89` — sigma-verify declared as a PyPI dependency but does not exist on PyPI — dependency-confusion risk and broken clean-install
- **confirmed ✓** · source: framed · effort: small · file: `/Users/bjgilbert/Projects/Zoltar/sigma-predict/pyproject.toml:10`
- **evidence:** dependencies includes "sigma-verify>=0.1.0". `curl https://pypi.org/pypi/sigma-verify/json` returns HTTP 404 — the package is NOT on PyPI. The only working install is the editable local checkout: `pip freeze` shows `-e git+https://github.com/coloradored13/sigma-verify.git@273d83d...#egg=sigma_verify`, Location is `Editable project location: /Users/bjgilbert/Projects/sigma-verify`. pyproject contains NO git URL, no `[tool.uv.sources]`, no `--index-url`/`--find-links` pointing at the real source (grep confirmed only the bare line 10). A `pip install .` on a clean machine resolves `sigma-verify>=0.1.0` against public PyPI and will either fail or — worse — silently install whatever a third party later publishes under that name (classic dependency-confusion / namesquatting). The whole verification/challenge path (llm_router.py imports sigma_verify.PROVIDERS) depends on this.
- **recommendation:** Pin the internal dep to its real source explicitly. With setuptools use a PEP 508 direct reference: `"sigma-verify @ git+https://github.com/coloradored13/sigma-verify.git@<tag-or-sha>"`, or migrate to uv/PDM and declare `[tool.uv.sources] sigma-verify = { git = "...", rev = "..." }`. Pin to an immutable tag or commit SHA, not a floating branch. As defense-in-depth, reserve/squat the `sigma-verify` name on PyPI or configure index priority so public PyPI cannot shadow it.
- **verifier:** Tried to refute and could not — every checkable claim holds.

CONFIRMED from code:
- pyproject.toml:10 declares "sigma-verify>=0.1.0" as a bare PEP 508 name requirement (Read confirmed).
- `curl https://pypi.org/pypi/sigma-verify/json` returns HTTP 404 — package does not exist on public PyPI (verified live).
- The only working install is the editable local checkout: `python3 -c "import sigma_verify"` resolves to /Users/bjgilbert/Projects/sigma-verify/src/sigma_verify/__init__.py. `pip freeze | grep sigma-verify` returns nothing (editable git-egg not even visible in this env's freeze), reinforcing that nothing public satisfies the requirement.
- No git URL, no [tool.uv.sources], no --index-url/--find-links anywhere — grep of pyproject.toml returned nothing for git+/tool.uv/find-links/index-url/sources. Line 10 is the only reference.
- No lockfile or constraints file (no uv.lock, requirements.txt, poetry.lock, pdm.lock, setup.py/cfg) that could pin the real source.
- The verification path genuinely depends on it: llm_router.py imports `from sigma_verify import PROVIDERS` (lines 121, 175), and `classify_error, VerificationError` (266, 357); config.py:99 references the PROVIDERS registry. This is the project's core cross-model verification/challenge feature.

DEPENDENCY-CONFUSION VECTOR IS REAL: a clean `pip install .` resolves sigma-verify>=0.1.0 against public PyPI. Today it fails (404 → unsatisfiable). If any third party later publishes `sigma-verify` on PyPI, a clean install silently pulls that arbitrary package, which llm_router.py then imports and instantiates (cls() at line 134, calling provider code) — arbitrary code execution on import. Classic namesquatting/dependency-confusion supply-chain risk.

Two-sided honesty: the runtime import at llm_router.py:120-124 is wrapped in try/except ImportError and degrades gracefully (logs "sigma-verify not installed", zero external providers). So a *missing* dep does not crash at import time — but install itself fails at resolution before that path matters, and the security vector (attacker-published package) bypasses the graceful path entirely because the import would then succeed with malicious code. The graceful handling does not mitigate either the broken-install or the supply-chain concern.

Recommendation is sound: PEP 508 direct reference `sigma-verify @ git+https://.../sigma-verify.git@<sha>` pinned to an immutable commit/tag (not a floating branch), or migrate to uv/PDM with [tool.uv.sources]; defense-in-depth reserve the name on PyPI / set index priority.

### `READ-256` — No spend ceiling anywhere — pipeline can run away on cost with only per-call max_tokens as a brake
- **confirmed ✓** · source: readiness:ops-cost · effort: medium · file: `/Users/bjgilbert/Projects/Zoltar/sigma-predict/src/pipeline/orchestrator.py:99,230,365`
- **evidence:** grep for budget|spend_cap|cost_cap|max_cost|max_spend|cost_limit|ceiling across src/, scripts/, components/, pages/, app.py returns ZERO matches. The orchestrator instantiates `total_cost = CostTracking()` (line 99) and only ACCUMULATES via `_add_costs` (line 230) after each run completes — it never checks the running total against any threshold. A single predict() fans out to many paid calls: decompose (decomposer.py:61), isolated base rate (base_rate.py:54), search-augmented base rate (base_rate.py:199), per-run forecast + pre-mortem (forecaster.py:161,204 — 2 calls x num_runs, num_runs up to 10), deliberation (deliberation.py:127), plus verification cross_verify (verification.py:75,127,164) and challenge (verification.py:236) which each fan out to up to 10 external providers (config.py:102-105 default verification_providers has 10 entries). With num_runs=10 and verification on, one question can trigger 50+ paid LLM calls with no upper bound on dollars. batch_predict.py loops this over N questions (batch_predict.py:82-92) with the same absence of any cap. Cost is reported AFTER the fact (orchestrator.py:375) — purely observational, never enforced.
- **recommendation:** Add a CostConfig with per_run_usd_cap and per_batch_usd_cap (and optionally a global daily cap persisted to disk). In the orchestrator's run loop (orchestrator.py:230) and after each verification/challenge stage, compare total_cost.total_cost_usd against the cap and raise a PipelineError / abort the remaining runs when exceeded. In batch_predict.py:93 abort the batch when the cumulative total crosses the batch cap. Surface the cap in the CLI (--max-cost) and the Streamlit Predict page.
- **verifier:** CONFIRMED. Every load-bearing claim is code-accurate. (1) Independent grep for budget|spend_cap|cost_cap|max_cost|max_spend|cost_limit|ceiling|spend_limit|cost_budget across src/, scripts/, components/, pages/, app.py returns ZERO matches. (2) orchestrator.py:99 instantiates total_cost=CostTracking(); line 230 _add_costs only ACCUMULATES; lines 365/375 record and log cost purely observationally — there is no comparison against any threshold anywhere in predict_until_review. (3) config.py confirms the only brakes are per-call ModelConfig.max_tokens=4096 (line 18) and strict_validation (a quality gate, not a spend gate); no cost field exists. The 10-entry verification_providers default (config.py:102-105) is confirmed. (4) llm_router.py:322 cross_verify loops over all _external_clients() (up to 10 providers) with no cost gating; verification.py base_rate/actors/forecast_run and challenge each invoke this fan-out. (5) batch_predict.py:79-102 accumulates total_cost across N questions and prints at the end with no cap. (6) CLI predict.py:144 and Streamlit pages/2_Predict.py only display cost, never cap it. So the control genuinely does not exist anywhere in the repo and the issue is not already handled. One minor inaccuracy in the finding (does not change the verdict): challenge_pre_mortem runs once on the aggregate (orchestrator.py:297-303), not per-run, so the precise call-count arithmetic is slightly loose — but the core claim of unbounded fan-out with no dollar ceiling is unaffected.

### `READ-246` — No prompt-template version or hash is recorded — prompts are loaded from mutable disk files and not captured
- **confirmed ✓** · source: readiness:provenance · effort: medium · file: `src/pipeline/forecaster.py:118-129; src/pipeline/utils.py (_load_prompt)`
- **evidence:** The forecaster system prompt is assembled at runtime from on-disk files: forecaster_base.md, pre_mortem.md, personas/<persona>.md (forecaster.py:118-126), and the same pattern is used for base_rate.md and decomposer.md. None of these template contents, versions, or hashes are stored on the PredictionRecord or RunResult (models.py:92-111 has model/provider/persona but no prompt identity). Editing any prompt file silently changes how every future prediction is generated, and a stored record gives no way to know which prompt text produced it. The exact `system` and `user_content` strings constructed at forecaster.py:129-155 are never persisted either (only the hashed structural summary in the optional audit log).
- **recommendation:** Record a hash (and ideally the resolved text or a version tag) of every prompt template used, per stage, on the record. Version the prompts/ directory (git SHA or per-file content hash) and store those hashes in the record so a forecast can be tied to the exact instructions that produced it.
- **verifier:** Confirmed by reading the code. Every factual claim holds:

1. Prompts are loaded from mutable on-disk files at runtime. forecaster.py:118-126 calls _load_prompt for forecaster_base.md, pre_mortem.md, and personas/<persona>.md; utils.py:30-33 just opens config.prompts_dir/<filename> and returns the raw text. The same _load_prompt pattern is used for base_rate.md and decomposer.md.

2. No prompt identity is stored. RunResult (src/models.py:92-111) records model/provider/search_persona but no prompt hash, version, or text. PredictionRecord (src/models.py:205-230) likewise has no prompt-provenance field.

3. The assembled system string (forecaster.py:129) and user_content (forecaster.py:132-155) are passed to router.call() and never persisted on the record.

4. The audit log (src/pipeline/llm_audit.py) is the only prompt-adjacent capture and explicitly does NOT close the gap: it never writes raw prompt text (line 7, _structural_summary lines 30-48), its params_hash is a SHA of a STRUCTURAL summary (types/lengths only, line 89/95) — not a content hash, so different prompts of equal length collide — it is optional (audit_path None disables it), and entries are keyed by timestamp/method with no prediction_id/run_id join, so even when enabled it cannot tie a stored prediction to the prompt text. The finding's characterization ("only the hashed structural summary in the optional audit log") is precise.

5. Partial mitigation the finding under-weights: prompts/ IS git-tracked (git ls-files confirms all templates tracked). Historical prompt content is therefore recoverable IF the producing commit is known — but nothing in the record captures a git SHA or content hash, and the working tree can be edited without committing, so this does not close the provenance gap for a stored record.

Minor inaccuracy in the finding: it cites the models file as src/pipeline/models.py; the actual path is src/models.py. Line numbers (92-111, 205-230) and content match exactly, so this is a cosmetic path typo that does not undermine the finding.

Net: the absent control genuinely does not exist anywhere in the repo. The issue is real and material for a forecasting system whose value rests on tying outcomes to the exact reasoning instructions.

### `READ-225` — Unguarded primary LLM call has zero retry/backoff/429 handling — a single transient API error crashes the entire forecast run
- **REFUTED ✗** · source: readiness:resilience · effort: small · file: `/Users/bjgilbert/Projects/Zoltar/sigma-predict/src/pipeline/llm_router.py:46-70`
- **evidence:** AnthropicClient.call() does `client = anthropic.Anthropic(...); response = client.messages.create(...)` with NO try/except, NO retry loop, and NO backoff. router.call() (lines 188-227) dispatches to it with no error handling either. The anthropic SDK raises RateLimitError (429), APITimeoutError, APIConnectionError, and InternalServerError (5xx) — every one of these propagates as a raw exception. There is no rate-limit/Retry-After handling, no exponential backoff, and no circuit breaker anywhere in the file. The module imports `time` but uses it only for latency measurement (t0 = time.monotonic()), never for sleeping/backoff.
- **recommendation:** Wrap the SDK call in a retry decorator with exponential backoff + jitter that specifically handles anthropic.RateLimitError (respecting Retry-After), APITimeoutError, APIConnectionError, and 5xx InternalServerError. Cap retries (e.g. 3-5) and surface a typed terminal failure rather than a raw exception. Note the anthropic SDK has a built-in `max_retries` constructor arg that is currently left at default and not configured — at minimum set it explicitly.
- **verifier:** REFUTED as stated. source: code-read /Users/bjgilbert/Projects/Zoltar/sigma-predict/src/pipeline/llm_router.py:46-70, scripts/batch_predict.py:82-98, anthropic SDK 0.84.0.

The finding's two load-bearing technical claims are factually wrong:

(1) "NO retry loop, NO backoff, no Retry-After handling; max_retries left at default" — FALSE. The installed anthropic SDK is 0.84.0 with constructor default max_retries: int = 2 (DEFAULT_MAX_RETRIES = 2 in anthropic/_constants.py). Line 58 builds anthropic.Anthropic(api_key=...) WITHOUT overriding max_retries, so 2 automatic retries are active. The SDK's _should_retry retries precisely the error classes the finding enumerates — 429 (respecting Retry-After via _calculate_retry_timeout), 408, 409, and >=500 — plus APIConnectionError/APITimeoutError at the transport layer, with exponential backoff + jitter. The recommendation's own minimum bar ("at least set max_retries explicitly") is already met at the default of 2. The finding's premise of zero retry handling is incorrect.

(2) "A single transient API error crashes the entire forecast run" — FALSE for the production batch path. scripts/batch_predict.py:87-98 wraps each orchestrator.predict() in try/except Exception: continue, so a question that fails after the SDK's retries is logged and skipped; the batch proceeds. The claimed blast radius does not hold.

What is genuinely true (but not what the finding claims): there is no APPLICATION-level retry/backoff/circuit-breaker in llm_router.py, and per-question callers (forecaster.py:161/204, decomposer.py:61, base_rate.py, deliberation.py) catch only json.JSONDecodeError, not API exceptions. So within a single question an error that exhausts the SDK's 2 retries aborts that question's pipeline (and surfaces to the user in single-question UI paths like pages/2_Predict.py). That is a modest hardening opportunity — increase retries, add jitter/circuit-breaker, return a typed terminal failure — not a critical "unguarded call with zero retry that crashes everything" defect.

Because the finding misdiagnoses the mechanism (asserts zero retry when 2 retries with backoff + Retry-After are active by default) and overstates the blast radius (batch driver isolates failures), it should be marked not real as written. A correctly-scoped LOW/MEDIUM resilience-hardening note could be filed instead.

### `READ-226` — No call site in the forecasting pipeline guards router.call() — one transient failure in decompose/base_rate/forecaster aborts the whole run with no degradation
- **confirmed ✓** · source: readiness:resilience · effort: medium · file: `/Users/bjgilbert/Projects/Zoltar/sigma-predict/src/pipeline/forecaster.py:161-165`
- **evidence:** forecaster.run_forecast calls `router.call(...)` for the main forecast (line 161) and again for the pre-mortem (line 204) with NO try/except — only the subsequent json.loads is guarded (lines 173-179). decomposer.py:61 and base_rate.py:54 call router.call the same way with no guard. The orchestrator's predict_until_review (orchestrator.py:138-228) wraps NONE of stages 1-3 in try/except. So a 429 or timeout during decomposition (the very first LLM call, orchestrator.py:141) propagates straight up and kills the entire prediction before any partial result is produced. In a multi-run setup (orchestrator.py:207 loop), a transient failure on run 3 of 5 discards the 2 successful runs and the locked base rate — there is no per-run try/except to let surviving runs aggregate.
- **recommendation:** Wrap each per-run forecast call (orchestrator.py:219) in try/except so a single failed run is recorded as parse_failed/excluded (the aggregation already tolerates excluded runs, orchestrator.py:251-273) rather than aborting the batch. Guard the decompose/base_rate stages so a transient failure degrades to a default Decomposition / 0.5 base rate with a pipeline_warning instead of crashing.
- **verifier:** Code-confirmed at all cited locations. router.call() (llm_router.py:188-227) has zero retry/backoff/try-except of its own. forecaster.py:161 and :204, decomposer.py:61, and base_rate.py:54 call router.call() with no guard — the only try/except in those functions wraps json.loads (parse failure), not the network call. base_rate.py's `except Exception` blocks (128, 207) are inside the OPTIONAL search_augmented_base_rate, not the primary estimate_base_rate call, so the finding correctly identifies the primary path as unguarded. orchestrator.predict_until_review (70-382) wraps none of stages 1-3; the per-run loop (207-230) has no per-run try/except, so a failure on run 3 of 5 propagates up and discards the 2 successful runs + locked base rate. Aggregation already tolerates excluded runs (251-273), so the recommended fix is sound and aligns with existing machinery.

However the finding overstates the blast radius in two ways. (1) The Anthropic SDK does 2 automatic retries with exponential backoff on transient errors (DEFAULT_MAX_RETRIES=2 in anthropic/_constants.py), so a SINGLE transient 429/5xx/timeout on the primary path is already retried twice at the SDK layer — it does not abort the run as the title literally claims. The gap bites on persistent rate-limits/timeouts beyond ~3 attempts, and on sigma-verify provider clients whose retry behavior is external/unknown. (2) The batch claim is partly refuted: batch_predict.py:87-98 wraps each orchestrator.predict() in try/except+continue, so a failure does NOT abort a multi-QUESTION batch. The finding's surviving sub-claim is the multi-RUN loop within one prediction, which is genuinely unguarded.

Net: a real graceful-degradation defect — a persistent transient failure during decompose/base_rate/forecast aborts an entire single prediction and throws away partial work — but there is no data corruption, human review gates submission, the SDK absorbs single blips, and the multi-question path is already resilient.

### `SWEE-35` — Human Review panel is cosmetic — prediction is already submitted and saved before review is shown
- **confirmed ✓** · source: sweep · effort: medium · file: `/Users/bjgilbert/Projects/Zoltar/sigma-predict/pages/2_Predict.py:72-78,345-377`
- **evidence:** The background thread calls orchestrator.predict(question=..., auto_submit=True). In src/pipeline/orchestrator.py:485-499, predict(auto_submit=True) runs predict_until_review() and then immediately calls submit_after_review(record, ..., human_review=HumanReview(reviewed=False)) — and submit_after_review (orchestrator.py:~485 docstring 'Apply human review, submit to platform, and save to registry') performs the platform submission AND self.store.save(record). Only AFTER that does the UI enter pipeline_state=='awaiting_review' and render the 'Human Review' radio (Accept/Adjust/Reject). When the human then clicks Confirm Review, the code does cache._store.update(record) on the already-submitted record. So the 'Reject' decision cannot un-submit, the human_adjustment is recorded but the platform already received the unadjusted probability, and the record was saved with human_review.reviewed=False before the human touched it. The TODO comments at 2_Predict.py:62-63 and 373 claim predict_until_review()/submit_after_review() are 'not yet available', but both already exist (orchestrator.py:70, and submit_after_review).
- **recommendation:** Switch the thread to orchestrator.predict_until_review(question, client, num_runs, personas, progress_queue=q) which explicitly does NOT submit or save, then on Confirm Review call orchestrator.submit_after_review(record, question, client, human_review). This is the documented intended flow ('Use predict_until_review() + submit_after_review() directly ... e.g. Streamlit UI', orchestrator.py:472-474). The supporting APIs already exist; the TODOs are stale.
- **verifier:** Confirmed against the actual code at both cited locations.

2_Predict.py:72-78 — the background thread calls orchestrator.predict(auto_submit=True).

orchestrator.py:485-499 — predict() with auto_submit=True calls predict_until_review() and then immediately submit_after_review(record, ..., human_review=HumanReview(reviewed=False)).

orchestrator.py:407-451 — submit_after_review applies the (reviewed=False, human_adjustment=None) review, computes final_prob = cal_prob (no adjustment), calls platform_client.submit_prediction(...), records record.submitted, and calls self.store.save(record). So both the platform submission and registry persistence occur BEFORE any human input.

2_Predict.py:273-300 — the UI only transitions to pipeline_state=="awaiting_review" after the thread posts its completion sentinel, i.e. after submit+save already ran. The 'Human Review' radio (Accept/Adjust/Reject) at lines 314-369 is therefore shown post-submission.

2_Predict.py:371-377 — Confirm Review sets record.human_review and does cache._store.update(record); cache.invalidate(). It cannot un-submit (the platform already received cal_prob), the Adjust delta never feeds the submission probability, and Reject only writes human_reasoning='REJECTED' onto an already-submitted/saved record. The record was first persisted with reviewed=False.

Stale TODOs confirmed: 2_Predict.py:62-63, 71, 373 claim predict_until_review()/submit_after_review() are 'not yet available' / 'when TA finalises API', but predict_until_review exists at orchestrator.py:70 (docstring: 'Does NOT save to the registry', line 81) and submit_after_review at orchestrator.py:384. The orchestrator docstring at 472-474 explicitly names the predict_until_review() + submit_after_review() split as the intended Streamlit-UI flow, exactly matching the recommendation.

The data-integrity and review-gate defects are unconditional: the human-in-the-loop gate is entirely cosmetic with respect to what is submitted and persisted. One nuance: actual external platform submission may be a no-op because submit_after_review catches NotImplementedError (orchestrator.py:442-448) and degrades to logged-only; whether a live bet is placed depends on each client's submit_prediction. But the registry-persistence and adjustment/reject defects hold regardless. The recommended fix is low-risk since the correct APIs already exist and are documented.


## HIGH

### `BIZ:-114` — Temporal firewall in search_augmented_base_rate is inert — SearchResult has no date field, so the recency filter matches nothing
- **confirmed ✓** · source: biz-logic · effort: medium · file: `/Users/bjgilbert/Projects/Zoltar/sigma-predict/src/pipeline/base_rate.py:136-165`
- **evidence:** The filter reads `pub_date_str = getattr(r, "published_date", None) or getattr(r, "date", None)` and only excludes a result when `pub_date_str` is truthy. But the SearchResult dataclass (src/pipeline/search.py:60-66) defines ONLY `title, url, content, score` — no `published_date` and no `date` attribute, and SearchModule.search() (search.py:114-122) never populates one. So `getattr` always returns None for every result, the `if pub_date_str:` block is never entered, and every result is appended to filtered_results unconditionally. The 180-day cutoff computed at line 137 is never compared against anything. The docstring at lines 101-103 even still asserts 'temporal firewall is not currently active — SearchResult has no published_date field. All search results pass through regardless of recency,' directly contradicting the firewall code added below it AND the user-facing context string at line 193 ('temporal firewall applied') fed into the LLM prompt.
- **recommendation:** Either (a) add published_date to SearchResult and populate it from the Tavily response (Tavily returns a `published_date` field for news results) so the firewall actually filters, or (b) remove the dead filter block and the false 'temporal firewall applied' claim from the prompt. Do not ship a contamination control that the data model cannot feed.
- **verifier:** Verified against the actual code. SearchResult (src/pipeline/search.py:60-66) defines only title/url/content/score — no published_date or date attribute. SearchModule.search() (search.py:113-122) constructs SearchResult with exactly those four fields and never sets a date, even though include_raw_content is False and Tavily date fields are ignored. A repo-wide grep confirms search.py:116 is the ONLY SearchResult construction site and no code anywhere sets published_date/date. Therefore in base_rate.py:140 `getattr(r, "published_date", None) or getattr(r, "date", None)` always evaluates to None for every result. The `if pub_date_str:` guard (line 141) is never True, so the only exclusion path (`continue` at 151) is unreachable and every result reaches `filtered_results.append(r)` at line 158 unconditionally. The 180-day cutoff at line 137 is computed but never compared against live data — dead code. The intent-vs-implementation gap is real on two axes: (1) the firewall block is inert relative to its evident intent ('Temporal firewall: exclude results published within the last 6 months'); (2) the user_content prompt at line 193 asserts to the extraction LLM that results are 'historical only — temporal firewall applied', which is false. This matters because the function's entire stated purpose is contamination control for base-rate forecasting; shipping a false 'firewall applied' claim is exactly the silent contamination-control failure the system is supposed to prevent. Nuance: the docstring at 100-101 is actually honest (it admits the firewall is inactive), so the finding's phrasing 'docstring ... contradicting the firewall code' should be read as an internal contradiction — honest comment vs. dead code vs. false prompt claim — not a lying docstring. This does not reduce severity; it confirms the code was half-wired (firewall + prompt claim added, data field + docstring update omitted). Could not refute.

### `BIZ:-115` — Phase 2 Platt scaling is structurally unreachable in production — nothing ever writes platt params, and refit is never invoked
- **confirmed ✓** · source: biz-logic · effort: medium · file: `/Users/bjgilbert/Projects/Zoltar/sigma-predict/src/pipeline/calibration.py:62-69`
- **evidence:** calibrate() enters Phase 2 only when `n_resolved >= min_resolved_for_platt AND platt_params` is truthy (line 62). The orchestrator loads params via `load_platt_params(self.config)` (orchestrator.py:336), which returns None unless data/calibration/platt_global.json exists (calibration.py:283). But `fit_platt` and `save_platt_params` are called ONLY from tests/test_calibration.py — grep across the entire repo shows zero production callers. The pipeline never fits or saves Platt params, so the file never exists, `load_platt_params` always returns None, and Phase 2 can never execute no matter how many predictions resolve. The config option `platt_refit_interval = 50` (config.py:40) that is supposed to drive periodic refitting is referenced nowhere in the codebase. The data/calibration directory is empty.
- **recommendation:** Wire a refit step (e.g. in submit_after_review or a resolution handler) that calls fit_platt on resolved pairs every platt_refit_interval and save_platt_params, OR document that Platt is aspirational and the system tops out at isotonic (Phase 1). As written, the docstring's '3-phase gate' is really a 2-phase gate.
- **verifier:** CONFIRMED. Every load-bearing claim in the finding is verified against the code at the cited locations.

1. Phase 2 gate (calibration.py:62): `if n_resolved >= config.calibration.min_resolved_for_platt and platt_params:` — Phase 2 requires `platt_params` to be truthy. VERIFIED.

2. Orchestrator load path (orchestrator.py:336): `platt_params = load_platt_params(self.config)` is the ONLY source of platt_params fed into calibrate() at line 342. VERIFIED.

3. load_platt_params returns None when file absent (calibration.py:283-284): `if not os.path.exists(path): return None`. The path is `{calibration_dir}/platt_global.json`. VERIFIED.

4. Zero production writers. Grep across ALL .py and .ipynb files in the repo shows `fit_platt` and `save_platt_params` are called ONLY from tests/test_calibration.py (lines 11, 72, 78). No caller in src/, no script, no CLI, no streamlit, no notebook. The orchestrator imports only `calibrate` and `load_platt_params` (orchestrator.py:38) — never the write/fit functions. VERIFIED.

5. data/calibration/ is empty (only `.` and `..`). So platt_global.json never exists → load_platt_params always returns None → the Phase 2 branch at line 62 is structurally unreachable in production regardless of n_resolved. VERIFIED.

6. platt_refit_interval (config.py:40) is referenced NOWHERE except its own definition. Grep for `platt_refit_interval` returns a single hit: the config declaration. No refit loop, no resolution handler invokes it. submit_after_review() (orchestrator.py:384-460) handles review/submission/registry-save and contains zero calibration-fitting logic. VERIFIED.

The intent-vs-implementation gap is genuine: the module docstring (lines 1-6) and calibrate() docstring (lines 47-51) advertise a "3-phase gate" with Phase 2 Platt scaling at N>=50, and the config exposes platt_refit_interval=50 as a tunable promising "refit every N resolved predictions." In reality the system tops out at Phase 1 isotonic. The Platt machinery (fit_platt, save_platt_params, platt_transform, PlattParams) is dead code in production — fully implemented and unit-tested but never wired into any runtime path. This is material: it means the system silently never reaches its most sophisticated calibration tier, and a maintainer reading the docstring/config would reasonably believe Platt is active once 50 predictions resolve. No mitigating mechanism (bootstrap, migration, manual CLI) was found.

Severity: high is justified and should be kept. This is not a cosmetic doc mismatch — it is a silently degraded core capability (calibration is the scientific heart of a prediction pipeline) with a misleading config knob that implies functionality that does not exist. The fix is also clear and low-risk (wire a refit step keyed on platt_refit_interval, or downgrade the docs/config to reflect 2-phase reality).

### `BIZ:-125` — Cross-platform signal collection branch is dead: the {platform}_community key path never executes
- **confirmed ✓** · source: biz-logic · effort: trivial · file: `src/pipeline/orchestrator.py:547-551`
- **evidence:** signals = PlatformSignals() creates an object whose prices_at_prediction_time defaults to an empty dict (models.py:133 `Field(default_factory=dict)`). The collector then does: `platform_key = f"{question.platform.value}_community"; if platform_key in signals.prices_at_prediction_time: signals.prices_at_prediction_time[platform_key] = community; else: signals.prices_at_prediction_time[question.platform.value] = community`. Because the dict is always empty at this point, the `in` test is ALWAYS False, so the `_community`-suffixed key branch is unreachable and the else-branch (plain platform key) always runs. The suffixed key was presumably intended for a downstream comparison.
- **recommendation:** Remove the always-false `if platform_key in ...` guard and decide deliberately which key to write. The branch as written is inert; either populate the dict with expected keys beforehand or drop the dead branch.
- **verifier:** Confirmed at the cited location. orchestrator.py:542 creates signals = PlatformSignals(), and models.py:133 defaults prices_at_prediction_time to an empty dict (Field(default_factory=dict)). At line 548 the guard `if platform_key in signals.prices_at_prediction_time` tests membership against a dict that is always empty at this point, so it is unconditionally False. The _community-suffixed branch (line 549) is dead; the else branch (line 551) always runs, writing the plain platform key (e.g. "metaculus").

The intent-vs-implementation gap is not only real but more material than the finding claims. I traced the downstream consumer: human_review.py:340 does `community = ps.get("metaculus_community")` — it reads the exact _community-suffixed key the dead branch was meant to produce. Because the orchestrator never writes that key, this .get() always returns None, so the community-divergence review flag at human_review.py:341-345 ("Diverges from community by ...") can NEVER fire for Metaculus questions. A calibration safety check is silently disabled. This is a genuine functional defect, not merely cosmetic dead code.

Caveat narrowing scope: the human_review consumer only checks the metaculus_community key, so impact is concentrated on the Metaculus platform; other platforms have no consumer of the suffixed key. But the divergence-flag suppression on Metaculus is concrete and real.

### `BIZ:-126` — PlatformSignals anomaly/divergence fields are claimed features but never computed anywhere
- **confirmed ✓** · source: biz-logic · effort: medium · file: `src/pipeline/orchestrator.py:536-555`
- **evidence:** PlatformSignals declares cross_platform_divergence, anomaly_detected, anomaly_details (models.py:134-136), and human_review.py:153/352 reads `ps.anomaly_detected` to raise review flags. But _collect_platform_signals only ever sets prices_at_prediction_time for a SINGLE platform (the question's own platform) and never sets cross_platform_divergence or anomaly_detected. No code path computes divergence across platforms or sets the anomaly flag. The anomaly-driven human-review flag at human_review.py:352 can therefore never fire.
- **recommendation:** Either implement cross-platform price collection + divergence/anomaly computation, or remove the anomaly fields and the dead human-review flag so the claim matches behavior.
- **verifier:** Confirmed via code. PlatformSignals (src/models.py:132-136) declares cross_platform_divergence (float|None=None), anomaly_detected (bool=False), and anomaly_details (str|None=None). A repo-wide grep for any ASSIGNMENT to these three fields in src/ returns ZERO matches — they are only ever declared with their defaults and read.

The sole producer of PlatformSignals is _collect_platform_signals (orchestrator.py:536-555, constructed at line 542, attached at line 362). It only ever populates prices_at_prediction_time, and only for a single platform: it calls platform_client.get_community_prediction(question.question_id) once, against the question's own platform client. get_community_prediction (base.py:87 and the three platform impls) returns a single float|None — there is no cross-platform fan-out and no divergence math anywhere.

Consumers confirm the dead-flag claim: human_review.py:153 reads ps.anomaly_detected to print 'ANOMALY DETECTED', and human_review.py:352 raises the 'Platform behavioral anomaly detected' human-review flag on the same field. Since nothing ever sets anomaly_detected to True, both branches are unreachable in practice — the flag at line 352 can never fire. cross_platform_divergence is declared but read by nothing in src/ at all (only defaulted in two test fixtures).

Material: this is a high-severity intent/implementation gap because human review is a quality gate. The system advertises anomaly-driven review escalation that structurally cannot trigger, giving false assurance that cross-platform anomalies would be caught. The recommendation (implement the computation, or remove the dead fields + flag) is correct.

Citation note: the finding cites models.py:134-136, which resolves to src/models.py (not src/pipeline/models.py). Line numbers and content match exactly, so this is a path-prefix imprecision, not a substantive error — does not affect validity.

I tried to refute by looking for (a) any assignment to the fields, (b) a multi-platform community-price source that could feed divergence, (c) any other PlatformSignals constructor. All three refutation paths failed; the code matches the finding.

### `BIZ:-127` — Kalshi get_questions never returns a community prediction — listing path hardcodes None
- **confirmed ✓** · source: biz-logic · effort: medium · file: `src/platforms/kalshi.py:103-130, 61-75`
- **evidence:** get_questions hits `/events` and maps each via _map_event, which hardcodes `community_prediction=None` (kalshi.py:72) and `resolution_criteria=""`, `close_date=""`, `resolve_date=""`. The price-parsing logic in _map_market (kalshi.py:23-32) is never used by the listing path. So every question surfaced via Kalshi browsing carries no price, no close date, and no resolution criteria — the orchestrator's _compute_horizon (orchestrator.py:525-534) will always return None for Kalshi-listed questions because resolve_date is empty.
- **recommendation:** After listing events, fetch the markets within each event (Kalshi /events?with_nested_markets or /markets?event_ticker=) and map via _map_market, or document that Kalshi listing yields container-only stubs requiring a follow-up get_question call.
- **verifier:** CONFIRMED. The intent-vs-implementation gap is real and material. Source: direct code read at all cited locations.

WHAT THE CODE CLAIMS: get_questions docstring (kalshi.py:111-115) says it "Fetch markets from Kalshi" and the class docstring (kalshi.py:79-82) says it "Supports listing events and markets." The dataframe in the Streamlit search UI (pages/2_Predict.py:132-133) presents columns "Close" and "Community P" implying browsed questions carry close dates and community predictions.

WHAT IT ACTUALLY DOES: get_questions hits /events and maps each result via _map_event (kalshi.py:124-130). _map_event hardcodes community_prediction=None (line 72), resolution_criteria="" (67), close_date="" (70), resolve_date="" (71), created_at="" (69). These hardcoded values are byte-identical to the PlatformQuestion field defaults (models.py:240-245), so they propagate verbatim. The functional price-parsing logic in _map_market (lines 23-32) is referenced ONLY by get_question (line 148) and get_community_prediction — never by the listing path. No event-to-market expansion exists (no with_nested_markets, no /markets?event_ticker= call; grep confirms _map_market is unreachable from get_questions).

MATERIALITY CONFIRMED VIA CALLERS:
1. scripts/batch_predict.py:62-89 feeds get_questions output DIRECTLY into orchestrator.predict() with zero re-fetch. Every Kalshi question is an empty-field event stub.
2. pages/2_Predict.py Search mode (lines 119,150-151,72): the selected get_questions stub is placed in KEY_PIPELINE_QUESTION and passed straight to orchestrator.predict() — no get_question re-fetch.
3. orchestrator._compute_horizon (orchestrator.py:525-534) returns None when resolve_date is empty — confirmed exactly as cited. So every Kalshi-browsed question gets horizon_days=None.

The runtime fallback does NOT rescue this: _collect_platform_signals (orchestrator.py:545) calls get_community_prediction(question.question_id), but for a browsed event question_id is the event_ticker (_map_event line 62), and get_community_prediction queries /markets/{event_ticker} (kalshi.py:167) — wrong endpoint shape (markets are keyed by market ticker, not event ticker), so it fails and returns None. No price recovery for browsed Kalshi events.

REFUTATION ATTEMPTED AND FAILED: The only paths that work correctly are get_question by-ID and by-URL (pages/2_Predict.py:162,182), which use _map_market. But the discovery/browse/batch flow — the primary way questions are surfaced — yields container-only stubs with no price, no close date, no resolution criteria, no horizon. The Recommendation (expand events into nested markets or document the stub limitation) is sound.

Note on scope precision: the orchestrator still runs (title/description are populated from _map_event lines 64-65), so it does not crash — it silently produces a degraded prediction lacking horizon, resolution criteria text, and any platform-price anchor for Kalshi. That is exactly the intent-vs-implementation divergence described.

### `BIZ:-140` — deliberation_stdev_threshold config parameter is read nowhere — deliberation uses a hardcoded, inconsistent threshold
- **confirmed ✓** · source: biz-logic · effort: small · file: `src/config.py:34, src/pipeline/deliberation.py:22`
- **evidence:** AggregationConfig defines `deliberation_stdev_threshold: float = 0.15`. grep shows this field appears ONLY in config.py — no module reads it. should_deliberate() instead uses module-level `_INTER_MODEL_AGREEMENT_THRESHOLD = 0.6` and triggers when `aggregation.inter_model_agreement < 0.6`. Since `inter_model_agreement = max(0.0, 1.0 - 2.0*stdev)` (aggregator.py:112), the hardcoded gate fires at stdev > 0.20, NOT the configured 0.15. So the operator-facing knob is inert and the effective threshold is both hidden and looser than the documented default. The deliberation module docstring even says 'inter_model_agreement < threshold' implying configurability.
- **recommendation:** Either make should_deliberate() read config.aggregation.deliberation_stdev_threshold (convert to the agreement scale or compare stdev directly), or delete the config field. Reconcile the 0.15 vs effective-0.20 discrepancy.
- **verifier:** Verified all claims against the code. (1) config.py:34 defines `deliberation_stdev_threshold: float = 0.15`. (2) grep across src/ and tests/ confirms this exact field name appears ONLY at config.py:34 — nothing reads `config.aggregation.deliberation_stdev_threshold`, so the operator knob is genuinely inert. (3) deliberation.py:22 defines `_INTER_MODEL_AGREEMENT_THRESHOLD = 0.6` and should_deliberate() at line 40 gates on `aggregation.inter_model_agreement < 0.6`; the `config` argument is passed (and re-passed from orchestrator.py:319) but never consulted for this threshold. (4) aggregator.py:112 computes `inter_model_agreement = max(0.0, 1.0 - 2.0*stdev)`, so the `<0.6` agreement gate is algebraically equivalent to `stdev > 0.20`, NOT the configured 0.15. The intent-implementation gap is real on two axes: the config is dead, and even its intended value (0.15 stdev → agreement 0.70) does not match the hardcoded 0.60. (5) The docstring (lines 4, 35) says 'inter_model_agreement < threshold', implying configurability that does not exist. The finding's evidence and math are accurate; the recommendation is sound.

### `BIZ:-141` — Community-divergence flag can never fire: signals stored under key 'metaculus' but flag reads 'metaculus_community'
- **confirmed ✓** · source: biz-logic · effort: trivial · file: `src/pipeline/orchestrator.py:546-551, src/review/human_review.py:340`
- **evidence:** _collect_platform_signals builds `signals = PlatformSignals()` with an empty prices_at_prediction_time dict, then: `platform_key = f"{question.platform.value}_community"` (= 'metaculus_community'); `if platform_key in signals.prices_at_prediction_time: ... else: signals.prices_at_prediction_time[question.platform.value] = community`. Because the dict starts empty, the `in` test is always False, so the value is ALWAYS stored under the bare platform key 'metaculus'. But _generate_flags reads `community = ps.get("metaculus_community")`, which is always None. The 'Diverges from community by X' safeguard therefore never triggers for any prediction.
- **recommendation:** Store community price under a single agreed key and read the same key. Simplest fix: store under f'{platform}_community' unconditionally and have the flag read that, or have the flag read the bare platform key. Add a test asserting the divergence flag fires on a known-divergent record.
- **verifier:** Confirmed against the cited code. The divergence flag is structurally unreachable.

Trace:
1. models.py:133 — PlatformSignals.prices_at_prediction_time = Field(default_factory=dict), i.e. starts EMPTY.
2. orchestrator.py:542 — _collect_platform_signals creates `signals = PlatformSignals()` fresh, so the dict is empty.
3. orchestrator.py:547 — platform_key = f"{question.platform.value}_community". With Platform.METACULUS = "metaculus" (models.py:14), this is "metaculus_community".
4. orchestrator.py:548 — `if platform_key in signals.prices_at_prediction_time:` — the dict is empty, so this is ALWAYS False.
5. orchestrator.py:551 — the else branch always runs, storing the community price under the BARE key question.platform.value = "metaculus".
6. human_review.py:340 — _generate_flags reads `community = ps.get("metaculus_community")`. The orchestrator never writes that key, so this is ALWAYS None.
7. human_review.py:341 — guard `if community is not None and abs(cal_p - community) > 0.15:` — community is always None, so the "Diverges from community by X" flag NEVER fires.

Intent-vs-implementation gap is real: the code constructs a "_community" suffixed key and a reader keyed to it, signaling clear intent to store/read community price under that key. The implementation breaks because the dict is initialized empty, making the suffix-write branch dead code; the bare-key write path runs every time. The reader and writer therefore use different keys and never rendezvous.

Material: this is a documented analytical safeguard (flag when our calibrated probability diverges sharply from community/market consensus). It is completely inert for every prediction. The community price is still displayed (human_review.py:149 iterates all keys), so it is not silently lost from display — but the safety flag is non-functional. No test exists for it.

Additional point strengthening the finding: the reader key "metaculus_community" is hardcoded, not platform-derived, so the flag would be dead for Polymarket/Kalshi too even if the orchestrator key issue were the only problem. The finding's claim ("never triggers for any prediction") is correct and slightly understated.

Could not refute. Behavior diverges from intent; severity high is justified.

### `BIZ:-142` — Isotonic calibration trains on submitted (already-calibrated, human-adjusted) probabilities but is applied to the raw pre-calibration aggregate — domain mismatch
- **confirmed ✓** · source: biz-logic · effort: medium · file: `src/registry/query.py:104-121, src/pipeline/orchestrator.py:339-345, src/pipeline/calibration.py:70-80`
- **evidence:** Orchestrator calls `calibrate(probability=aggregation.raw_aggregate, ..., isotonic_data=pairs)` where pairs come from get_calibration_pairs(). get_calibration_pairs() sets `predicted = record.submitted.probability` when submitted exists. But submitted.probability is `final_prob` = calibrated_probability + human_adjustment (orchestrator.py:421-434) — i.e. the post-calibration, post-human value. fit_isotonic_logit then learns a map from calibrated-space predictions to outcomes, but isotonic_transform is applied to raw_aggregate (pre-calibration). The training inputs and the inference input live in different probability spaces, so the isotonic correction is systematically misapplied. The same mismatch affects the Platt path if it were ever live.
- **recommendation:** Calibration must be fit on the SAME quantity it is applied to. Store the raw aggregate (pre-calibration) on the record and use that as the 'predicted' value in calibration pairs, or apply calibration to the submitted-space value. Decide one canonical calibration input and use it consistently for both fit and transform.
- **verifier:** CONFIRMED at all three cited locations. (1) query.py:116-117 sets predicted = record.submitted.probability. (2) orchestrator.py:407-435 shows submitted.probability = final_prob = calibration.calibrated_probability + human_adjustment — i.e. post-calibration, post-human space. (3) orchestrator.py:339-345 passes probability=aggregation.raw_aggregate (pre-calibration) as the inference input while passing those submitted-space pairs as isotonic_data. (4) calibration.py:70-80 fits fit_isotonic_logit on the submitted-space preds, then applies isotonic_transform to the raw_aggregate. The training axis (calibrated/human-adjusted probabilities) and the inference axis (raw aggregate) are different probability spaces, so the learned logit->outcome map is misapplied. The Platt path (calibration.py:62-69) has identical structure and inherits the same defect. Reachability confirmed: thresholds are min_resolved_for_isotonic=20, min_resolved_for_platt=50 (config.py:41-42) — normal operating ranges, not dead code. No inverse/normalization step reconciles the spaces; raw_aggregate is strictly upstream of calibrated_probability. Even the submitted-is-None fallback uses calibrated_probability (line 119), still post-calibration — so the predicted axis is never the raw quantity inference uses, reinforcing the mismatch. This is a genuine intent-vs-implementation gap: calibration is fit on a different quantity than it is applied to, which systematically distorts the correction (double-counting an already-applied correction).

### `BIZ:-153` — Persona 'rotation' help text is false on the UI path — extra runs are silently dropped, not rotated
- **confirmed ✓** · source: biz-logic · effort: small · file: `/Users/bjgilbert/Projects/Zoltar/sigma-predict/pages/2_Predict.py:198-203`
- **evidence:** The multiselect help text says: 'One persona will be used per run (rotated if fewer than num_runs)'. The UI always passes an explicit personas list to the orchestrator (pages/2_Predict.py:237, 254). In orchestrator.py, rotation (all_personas[i % len(all_personas)]) only happens when personas is None (orchestrator.py:101-106). When a list is passed, orchestrator.py:207 iterates personas[:num_runs] — pure truncation. So if the user selects 1 persona and sets num_runs=5, the loop runs once, not five times. If they select 2 personas and 5 runs, only 2 runs execute. The user pays for and believes they ran N runs; they got min(len(personas), num_runs). The 'rotated if fewer' claim never fires for any UI request.
- **recommendation:** Either (a) implement rotation in the UI before passing the list: personas_expanded = [sel[i % len(sel)] for i in range(num_runs)], or (b) pass personas=None when the user wants the default rotation and only pass an explicit list when they intend to cap runs, and fix the help text to state that num_runs is capped at the number of personas selected. Validate num_runs vs persona count and warn the user.
- **verifier:** Confirmed against actual code. Note the cited orchestrator.py path is wrong (actual: /Users/bjgilbert/Projects/Zoltar/sigma-predict/src/pipeline/orchestrator.py), but the line numbers and content match exactly there.

CLAIM (help text, pages/2_Predict.py:202): "One persona will be used per run (rotated if fewer than num_runs)".

WHAT THE CODE DOES:
- UI always builds an explicit non-empty personas list and passes it. Line 237: `personas = [SearchPersona(v) for v in sel_persona_vals] if sel_persona_vals else [SearchPersona.NEWS]`. Line 254 passes `personas` explicitly to the thread/orchestrator. So `personas` is NEVER None on the UI path.
- Orchestrator rotation (orchestrator.py:101-106) only executes `personas = [all_personas[i % len(all_personas)] for i in range(num_runs)]` when `personas is None`. That branch is unreachable from the UI.
- The forecast loop (orchestrator.py:207): `for i, persona in enumerate(personas[:num_runs])` — pure truncation, no expansion/rotation. Loop runs `min(len(personas), num_runs)` times.

CONSEQUENCE (verified by tracing): num_runs is a separate number_input (line 195) with no cross-validation against persona count. Select 1 persona + num_runs=5 → loop runs ONCE, not 5. Select 2 personas + num_runs=5 → 2 runs. The "rotated if fewer" promise never fires for any UI request, and num_runs silently does nothing beyond the persona count. This is a genuine intent-vs-implementation divergence and it is material: it silently undermines the documented multi-run ensembling behavior (the whole point of num_runs > 1 for independent estimation), and the help text actively misleads the user. Default-real=false rebuttal attempts all fail: I could not find any UI path that passes personas=None, nor any expansion of the list before the loop.

### `BIZ:-154` — Verification toggle mutates the cached global Config singleton, leaking across all sessions and threads
- **confirmed ✓** · source: biz-logic · effort: medium · file: `/Users/bjgilbert/Projects/Zoltar/sigma-predict/pages/2_Predict.py:234; app.py:33-35`
- **evidence:** get_config() is decorated @st.cache_resource (app.py:33), so Config is a single process-wide singleton shared across every browser session and the background pipeline threads. On launch, pages/2_Predict.py:234 does config.verification.enabled = verification_toggle, permanently mutating that shared object. A second user (or the same user on a later run) inherits the previous user's toggle state regardless of what their own toggle widget shows, because the widget default reads config.verification.enabled (line 207) which was already overwritten. The toggle also mutates state read by the in-flight thread of any concurrent prediction. The control 'works' for a single-user-single-run case but is a shared-mutable-state hazard and the displayed default can diverge from the true config.
- **recommendation:** Do not mutate the cached Config. Either pass an override flag through to the orchestrator/pipeline call (e.g. a dataclasses.replace(config, verification=...) copy per run), or deep-copy config before mutating it inside the run handler. Keep the cached singleton immutable.
- **verifier:** Confirmed by reading the code. get_config() is decorated @st.cache_resource (app.py:33-35), and Streamlit's cache_resource returns the same live object instance to every session and rerun by design (it does not copy, unlike cache_data) — so `config` at pages/2_Predict.py:32 is a true process-wide singleton. Config and VerificationConfig are plain mutable @dataclass types (src/config.py:45-53, 56-125), so in-place mutation sticks. Line 234 `config.verification.enabled = verification_toggle` mutates that shared singleton, and line 207 `value=config.verification.enabled` reads the same singleton as the widget default. Therefore the core claim is accurate: one user's toggle choice permanently overwrites the global config, which then becomes the silent default for other sessions and changes pipeline behavior for any code reading config.verification.enabled. The background thread receives the same Config object (line 254) and builds a per-thread Orchestrator(config) over it, so the concurrent-mutation hazard is also real (though narrower — depends on whether the in-flight Orchestrator re-reads the flag mid-run, and the launch button has a session-scoped idle gate that does not protect across sessions). The recommendation (pass an override or dataclasses.replace a per-run copy rather than mutating the cached singleton) is the correct fix. Two minor caveats: (1) the sub-claim that 'the displayed default can diverge from the true config' is the weakest point — within a single session Streamlit widget state takes over after first render, and on first render the default actually tracks the mutated singleton, so they stay consistent at instantiation; the genuine problem is the cross-session leak, not display divergence. (2) The concurrent-thread mutation is plausible but unverified as to whether the running pipeline re-reads the flag.

### `BIZ:-155` — Reject decision in UI does not propagate to the platform — rejected predictions are still submitted
- **confirmed ✓** · source: biz-logic · effort: small · file: `/Users/bjgilbert/Projects/Zoltar/sigma-predict/pages/2_Predict.py:363-377`
- **evidence:** When the user picks 'Reject', the UI builds HumanReview(human_reasoning='REJECTED') and then only calls cache._store.update(record) (line 376). It never un-submits or alters the already-submitted prediction. Because submission happened during the auto_submit thread (see finding 1), a rejected forecast has already been sent to the platform. Compare orchestrator.submit_after_review:416-419 which is the only place that honors a REJECTED reasoning (it saves without submitting) — but the UI never routes through that branch. The Reject control changes a stored string and nothing else.
- **recommendation:** After adopting predict_until_review + submit_after_review (finding 1), the REJECTED branch in submit_after_review will correctly skip submission. Until then, the Reject button is misleading and should be disabled or labeled as 'mark rejected (already submitted)'.
- **verifier:** Confirmed at all cited locations. UI path: pages/2_Predict.py:72-78 runs the pipeline in a background thread via orchestrator.predict(..., auto_submit=True). In orchestrator.py:492-499, auto_submit=True builds HumanReview(reviewed=False) (empty human_reasoning) and calls submit_after_review, which at line 416 only skips submission when human_reasoning startswith "REJECTED" — an empty reasoning falls through to platform_client.submit_prediction at line 430. So the forecast is submitted to the platform DURING the background thread, before the human ever sees the review UI. Later, when the user clicks Reject (pages/2_Predict.py:363-369), the UI builds HumanReview(human_reasoning="REJECTED"), assigns record.human_review, and at line 376 only calls cache._store.update(record). The submit_after_review REJECTED branch (orchestrator.py:416-419, the sole save-without-submit path) is never invoked — line 373 is an explicit TODO confirming the orchestrator call is stubbed out. Net: a rejected prediction has already been submitted and is never un-submitted; Reject merely mutates a stored string. Intent (a meaningful Reject control) vs implementation (no propagation) gap is real. The docstrings/TODOs corroborate this is known-interim wiring. Materiality is high: real submit_prediction calls exist for Polymarket/Metaculus/Kalshi. Minor non-refuting nuance: when submit_prediction raises NotImplementedError, submission is recorded as 'logged_only' (orchestrator.py:442-448), so on unimplemented platforms the live external effect is a log rather than a true platform submission — but the gap and the misleading control hold wherever submission is implemented, so this does not lower severity.

### `BIZ:-165` — Phase 2 Platt calibration is unreachable — fit_platt/save_platt_params are never called anywhere
- **confirmed ✓** · source: biz-logic · effort: medium · file: `src/pipeline/calibration.py:62, src/pipeline/orchestrator.py:336`
- **evidence:** calibrate() Phase 2 gate is `if n_resolved >= config.calibration.min_resolved_for_platt and platt_params:`. platt_params comes from load_platt_params(config) (orchestrator.py:336), which reads data/calibration/platt_global.json. But a repo-wide grep shows save_platt_params() and fit_platt() are defined in calibration.py and exercised only in tests — they are called by NO production code path: not in scripts/resolve.py (the resolution-processing entry point), not in the orchestrator, not in app.py/pages/components. The file is therefore never written, load_platt_params always returns None, and the `and platt_params` clause is always False. Phase 2 (the entire Platt-scaling branch, docstring 'Phase 2 (N>=50): Platt scaling on historical data') can never execute regardless of how many predictions resolve. The config knob `platt_refit_interval: int = 50` (config.py:40) is read by nothing (grep: only its definition appears).
- **recommendation:** Wire a refit step into scripts/resolve.py: after updating resolutions, if query.count_resolved() crosses platt_refit_interval and >= min_resolved_for_platt, call fit_platt(preds, outs) on get_calibration_pairs() and save_platt_params(). Until then, document that calibration tops out at Phase 1 (isotonic) and remove or mark min_resolved_for_platt/platt_refit_interval as inert.
- **verifier:** Confirmed against code. (1) calibration.py:62 gate is `if n_resolved >= config.calibration.min_resolved_for_platt and platt_params:` — requires non-None platt_params. (2) orchestrator.py:336 is the only production source of platt_params: `load_platt_params(self.config)`. (3) load_platt_params (calibration.py:283-284) returns None when platt_{label}.json does not exist. (4) Repo-wide grep proves save_platt_params (defined calibration.py:272) has ZERO call sites anywhere — not even in tests. fit_platt is called only in tests/test_calibration.py:72,78, never in production (not in scripts/resolve.py, orchestrator.py, dashboard.py, or app). (5) Therefore platt_global.json is never written, load_platt_params always returns None, the `and platt_params` clause is always False, and Phase 2 is unreachable for any n_resolved. (6) config.py:40 platt_refit_interval is read by nothing (grep: definition only) — inert. The module docstring (lines 3-5) and calibrate() docstring (lines 47-48) advertise 'Phase 2 (N>=50): Platt scaling on historical data' as live behavior, which it is not. This is a genuine, material intent-vs-implementation gap. Verified detail: the consumer/precedence logic is actually correct (Phase 2 checked before Phase 1 elif), so the sole defect is the missing producer-side fit/save wiring — exactly what the finding claims and the recommendation targets. Could not refute.

### `BIZ:-166` — Community-divergence review flag can never fire — signals stored under key 'metaculus' but read under 'metaculus_community'
- **confirmed ✓** · source: biz-logic · effort: trivial · file: `src/pipeline/orchestrator.py:547, src/review/human_review.py:340`
- **evidence:** _collect_platform_signals builds `signals = PlatformSignals()` with an empty prices_at_prediction_time dict, then does `platform_key = f"{question.platform.value}_community"` (=> 'metaculus_community') and `if platform_key in signals.prices_at_prediction_time: ... else: signals.prices_at_prediction_time[question.platform.value] = community`. Because the dict is freshly empty, the `if` is always False, so the community price is always stored under bare key 'metaculus'. But _generate_flags reads `community = ps.get("metaculus_community")` (human_review.py:340), which is always None. The 'Diverges from community by ...' flag — a named safeguard intended to catch predictions that disagree with the market — is therefore dead and never raised.
- **recommendation:** Make the writer and reader agree on one key. Simplest fix: store under f'{platform}_community' unconditionally in _collect_platform_signals and read the same key in _generate_flags. Add a test that asserts the divergence flag fires for a known mismatch.
- **verifier:** Confirmed by reading both cited locations and tracing the full data flow.

WRITER (src/pipeline/orchestrator.py:536-555, _collect_platform_signals):
- Line 542: signals = PlatformSignals() — freshly constructed. PlatformSignals.prices_at_prediction_time defaults to an EMPTY dict (src/models.py:133, Field(default_factory=dict)).
- Line 547: platform_key = f"{question.platform.value}_community". Platform.METACULUS = "metaculus" (src/models.py:14), so platform_key = "metaculus_community".
- Line 548: `if platform_key in signals.prices_at_prediction_time:` — tests membership against the just-created empty dict. Always False.
- Line 551 (else): stores community under bare question.platform.value == "metaculus". So the community price is ALWAYS stored under key "metaculus", never "metaculus_community".

READER (src/review/human_review.py:339-345, _generate_flags):
- Line 340: community = ps.get("metaculus_community") — the key that is never written. Always None.
- Lines 341-345: `if community is not None and abs(cal_p - community) > 0.15:` — the "Diverges from community by ..." flag. Guard is always False because community is always None. The flag is dead and can never fire.

NO MITIGATING PATH: orchestrator.py:362 is the only site that assigns record.platform_signals, and it calls _collect_platform_signals, which builds the dict from scratch. Nothing pre-seeds the dict, so the writer's `if` branch is unreachable and the reader's key never matches. The intent is explicit and unambiguous on both sides (writer constructs the "_community" key and has a branch for it; reader looks for "metaculus_community"), and the implementation defeats it via a logically-dead branch — a textbook intent-vs-implementation gap.

Severity = high is reasonable: this is a named safeguard (community-divergence flag) intended to catch predictions that disagree with the market, and it is silently inert. A human reviewer relying on these flags would never be warned of a market disagreement. Note one bound on impact: the raw price is still displayed (human_review.py:145-151 iterates all keys, including the bare "metaculus" key), so the data is not lost from the display table — only the automated flag is dead. This does not reduce the bug's reality, just clarifies that detection still partially exists via manual reading of the price table. The recommendation (make writer and reader agree on one key; the if/else in the writer is incoherent and should be replaced with an unconditional store under f"{platform}_community", plus a regression test) is correct.

### `CRIT-107` — decompose_question is called without warnings_out, so decomposition parse-failure is silently swallowed and the run anchors on a default 0.5 base rate with no trace
- **confirmed ✓** · source: critic · effort: trivial · file: `/Users/bjgilbert/Projects/Zoltar/sigma-predict/src/pipeline/orchestrator.py:141-144`
- **evidence:** decomposer.py:78-80 is explicitly designed to record the failure: `if warnings_out is not None: warnings_out.append("decomposition_parse_failure")` then `return Decomposition()`. But the orchestrator calls `decompose_question(question, self.config, router=self.router)` with NO warnings_out argument (orchestrator.py:141-143). On a malformed/unparseable decomposer LLM response the stage returns an empty Decomposition (base_rate=0.5, reference_class="", no actors, no sub-questions), the entire forecast proceeds anchored on a default 0.5 base rate, and record.pipeline_warnings contains nothing about it. The base_rate validator gate (validate_base_rate) passes 0.5 cleanly. This is a different defect from the already-found 'verification-unavailable warning never merged' — this is the decomposition stage's own designed warning channel left unwired.
- **recommendation:** Pass record.pipeline_warnings (or a local list that is then merged) as warnings_out= into decompose_question, and add a validator gate that flags an empty/default Decomposition (empty reference_class + zero sub_questions) as a degraded-input warning so downstream stages and human review see the signal.
- **verifier:** Mechanical core CONFIRMED. decompose_question (decomposer.py:24-29) accepts warnings_out and appends "decomposition_parse_failure" on JSON parse failure (lines 78-79) before returning an empty Decomposition() (line 80). The sole call site (orchestrator.py:141-143) passes question, config, router only — no warnings_out — so a decomposition parse failure is silently swallowed with no trace in record.pipeline_warnings.
