If LLM under-confidence is a real prior, it must be demonstrated on held-out resolved questions and then folded into the fitted Platt/isotonic stage, not hardcoded. At minimum gate it behind an explicit opt-in and flag it in record.pipeline_warnings.
- **verifier:** Confirmed against the code. apply_hedging_correction_scaled (src/pipeline/calibration.py:117-130) deterministically pushes every estimate away from 0.5 (distance-weighted: effective_shift = base_shift * 2 * distance), with base_shift default 0.04 (src/config.py:39, "shift away from 50% before Platt data exists"). It is the unconditional DEFAULT path: orchestrator.py:339 always calls calibrate(); with n_resolved=0 (<20) execution falls to Phase 0 (calibration.py:90-100), and it is also the isotonic fallback (calibration.py:81-89). There is no opt-in gate, and the result is NOT recorded in record.pipeline_warnings — only a console print at src/review/human_review.py:138.

The statistical claim is correct: extremizing a probability away from 0.5 strictly increases Brier/log loss whenever the input is already calibrated or overconfident. The justifying premise ("LLMs tend to hedge toward 50%") exists only in a docstring; I found no resolved-data file, calibration curve, Brier analysis, or any markdown anywhere in the repo (excluding .venv/node_modules) that derives the 0.04 magnitude or even validates the direction. So "applied before any resolved outcomes exist, with no resolved-data evidence" is accurate.

The finding actually UNDERSTATES the problem: the aggregation step already applies a logit-scale extremization (extremization_factor=1.5, _extremize at aggregator.py:133-148, wired at orchestrator.py:280) BEFORE calibration. The default new-deployment pipeline therefore stacks TWO unvalidated away-from-0.5 transforms, compounding the miscalibration risk on a user's very first predictions.

Minor evidence nit (does not affect verdict): the quoted snippet `min(0.99, p + effective_shift)` is only the p>0.5 branch; the p<0.5 branch subtracts toward 0.01. Both branches push away from 0.5, so the characterization is correct.

Recommendation is sound: Phase-0 default should be identity (or shrinkage toward base rate, the safe direction under uncertainty); any under-confidence prior must be demonstrated on held-out resolved questions and folded into the fitted Platt/isotonic stage, or at minimum gated behind explicit opt-in and surfaced in pipeline_warnings.

### `READ-240` — Temporal firewall on search-augmented base rate is inert — never excludes any result, contradicting its own docstring
- **confirmed ✓** · source: readiness:methodology · effort: medium · file: `src/pipeline/base_rate.py:136-158`
- **evidence:** The 180-day firewall reads `getattr(r, 'published_date', None) or getattr(r, 'date', None)` but SearchResult (search.py dataclass) has neither field, so pub_date_str is always None and the recency check is skipped for every result — every search-augmented result passes regardless of age. The docstring at lines 100-102 even states the firewall 'is not currently active', directly contradicting the live code at 136-158 that claims to apply it (and the user-facing context string at line 193 tells the LLM 'temporal firewall applied'). This is the one contamination control specifically meant to keep CURRENT events out of the BASE-RATE channel, and it is non-functional. Recent news can leak into the supposedly inside-view-free anchor.
- **recommendation:** Add published_date to SearchResult and populate it from the Tavily response, then make the firewall actually filter; or, until that exists, REMOVE the misleading 'temporal firewall applied' assertion from the prompt (line 193) and treat search-augmentation as recency-uncontrolled, flagging it in warnings. The current state asserts a contamination control that does not exist, which is worse than not claiming one.
- **verifier:** CONFIRMED. All mechanical claims verified against the code (source: src/pipeline/base_rate.py:136-158, src/pipeline/search.py:60-66, orchestrator.py:176-187).

1. SearchResult is a 4-field dataclass (search.py:60-66): title, url, content, score. No published_date, no date field. Confirmed absent everywhere in src/ via grep — the only matches for these tokens are the firewall code and docstring themselves; nothing ever sets them on a SearchResult.
2. SearchModule.search (search.py:88-124) sends Tavily payload with include_raw_content: False and parses ONLY title/url/content/score. No date is ever requested or stored.
3. base_rate.py:140 — getattr(r, 'published_date', None) or getattr(r, 'date', None) is therefore always None. The if pub_date_str: guard at 141 never fires, so the recency check at 146 is dead code and every result is appended at 158. The firewall excludes nothing, ever. The 'all results excluded' branch at 160-165 is unreachable. VERIFIED.
4. The augmented rate is live and load-bearing: orchestrator.py:176-187 overwrites record.decomposition.base_rate with the augmented value and replaces base_rate_source with the citation. The inert filter sits directly on the anchor-mutation path, so recency-uncontrolled material can propagate into the base-rate anchor that the docstring claims is inside-view-free.
5. The prompt at line 193 asserts to the extractor LLM that search results are 'historical only — temporal firewall applied,' which is false. This is the genuinely material part: it makes a contamination-control claim that the code does not deliver.

PARTIAL REFUTATION of framing (does not change real=true): The title says the code 'contradicts its own docstring.' That is inverted — the docstring at 100-101 is candid ('temporal firewall is not currently active'). It is the executable filter (which masquerades as active) and the prompt string at 193 (which asserts it is applied) that are misleading, not a code-vs-docstring contradiction. Also, the finding overstates 'this is THE one contamination control': the docstring lists four controls; control #1 (query restricted to '{reference_class} historical frequency statistics') is a real, functioning recency bias, and control #3 (separate model call, only numerical rate + citation fed back) genuinely limits what flows in. So the residual contamination risk is partially mitigated by working controls — a recent article would have to surface under a 'historical frequency statistics' query AND survive numerical-rate extraction. That lowers exploitability but does not eliminate it (recent news articles routinely cite historical base rates).

Net: the core defect — an asserted-but-inert contamination control whose false 'firewall applied' claim is fed to an LLM on the live anchor-mutation path — is real and material. The framing inaccuracies are cosmetic.

### `READ-257` — Verification token/cost accounting is silently dropped — cross_verify and challenge log tokens_in=0/tokens_out=0 and are never added to CostTracking
- **confirmed ✓** · source: readiness:ops-cost · effort: medium · file: `/Users/bjgilbert/Projects/Zoltar/sigma-predict/src/pipeline/llm_router.py:259-260,308-309,346-347`
- **evidence:** verify(), challenge(), and cross_verify() hardcode `tokens_in=0, tokens_out=0` in their audit log_call() invocations (lines 259-260, 308-309, 346-347) because the external sigma-verify client return path does not surface token counts. Consequently estimate_cost on those calls computes $0 and the audit log records zero tokens for every verification/challenge. More importantly, only `run_forecast` returns a CostTracking that the orchestrator folds into total_cost (orchestrator.py:219-230); decompose, base_rate, deliberation, verification, and challenge calls contribute NOTHING to record.cost. The reported `total_cost_usd` (orchestrator.py:375, predict.py:144, batch_predict.py:102) therefore systematically understates true spend — it counts only the forecaster + pre-mortem calls, omitting decomposition, both base-rate calls, deliberation, and the entire verification fan-out (up to 10 providers per verified stage). Any future spend cap built on record.cost would be calibrated against a fraction of real spend.
- **recommendation:** Make router.call/verify/challenge/cross_verify return token counts and a per-call cost, and have the orchestrator add every call's cost into total_cost (not just run_forecast's). For external providers that do not return token usage, estimate tokens from prompt/response length so cost is non-zero. Until then, label the displayed cost as 'forecaster-only (excludes verification/decomposition)'.
- **verifier:** CONFIRMED against actual code. Both sub-claims verified.

Claim 1 (audit token=0): VERIFIED at /Users/bjgilbert/Projects/Zoltar/sigma-predict/src/pipeline/llm_router.py:258-259 (verify), 308-309 (challenge), 346-347 (cross_verify). These hardcode tokens_in=0, tokens_out=0 AND omit cost_usd entirely (defaults to 0.0 in log_call). Root cause is upstream: /Users/bjgilbert/Projects/sigma-verify/src/sigma_verify/clients.py verify() returns a VerificationResult with no token fields (lines 308-316) and challenge() returns a parsed dict with no token counts (lines 335-344), discarding response.usage. So the router genuinely cannot surface tokens for these calls — the absent control truly does not exist anywhere.

Claim 2 (the material one — costs never reach record.cost): VERIFIED and not handled anywhere. Only run_forecast returns CostTracking (forecaster.py:100,261). Orchestrator accumulates ONLY run_cost (orchestrator.py:219-230). Confirmed by signatures: decompose_question -> Decomposition, estimate_base_rate -> tuple[float,str,str,int], search_augmented_base_rate -> tuple[float,str], deliberate -> tuple[list,Aggregation], verify_base_rate/verify_actors/verify_forecast_run -> StageVerification, challenge_forecast -> ChallengeEntry|None. None return cost. And these stages DO incur real spend: decomposer.py:61, base_rate.py:54 and :199, deliberation.py:127 all call router.call(...) via the local Anthropic paid path and discard token counts with `raw, _, _ =` — token counts are actually AVAILABLE on that path (AnthropicClient.call returns them) but are thrown away rather than threaded into cost. Verification stages call router.cross_verify/challenge and return no cost. Therefore record.cost.total_cost_usd (surfaced orchestrator.py:375, 456, and downstream predict.py/batch_predict.py) systematically undercounts: it excludes decomposition, both base-rate calls, deliberation, and the entire verification/challenge fan-out. A spend cap built on record.cost would be calibrated against a fraction of real spend.

This is a structural defect (missing return values across multiple stage signatures), not a config toggle, and exists in the current code.

### `READ-259` — Config validate() only checks for one LLM key — platform and search keys are never validated, so runs fail deep instead of fast
- **REFUTED ✗** · source: readiness:ops-cost · effort: small · file: `/Users/bjgilbert/Projects/Zoltar/sigma-predict/src/config.py:147-155`
- **evidence:** validate() (config.py:147-155) returns missing items ONLY for the LLM-provider key and primary_model. METACULUS_API_KEY / POLYMARKET_API_KEY / KALSHI_API_KEY / TAVILY_API_KEY are never validated. The platform clients omit the Authorization header entirely when their key is empty and proceed unauthenticated (per repo map: metaculus.py:67-69, polymarket.py:67-69, kalshi.py:88-90), and submit_prediction is a live WRITE path that only Metaculus guards (metaculus.py:133). So a predict run with auto_submit can: spend money on the full LLM pipeline, then fail or silently run unauthenticated at the platform-submit step — failing deep mid-run AFTER incurring cost, rather than failing fast at startup. The CLI checks config.validate() (predict.py:83, batch_predict.py:47) but validate() does not cover the keys the run actually needs.
- **recommendation:** Extend validate() to take the selected platform and whether search/submission is required, and assert the corresponding key is present before any paid work begins. Have the orchestrator validate platform/search prerequisites in __init__ or at the top of predict_until_review so the failure surfaces before the first LLM call.
- **verifier:** The finding's atomic facts are correct, but its central harm mechanism is refuted by the code it cites.

VERIFIED facts (all true):
- config.validate() (config.py:147-155) checks only LLM keys + primary_model; METACULUS/POLYMARKET/KALSHI/TAVILY keys are never validated.
- Both CLIs call config.validate() at the cited lines (predict.py:83, batch_predict.py:47).
- Clients omit the Authorization header when their key is empty (metaculus.py:67-69, kalshi.py:88-90; polymarket uses a public API with no auth at all).

REFUTED — the claimed harm ("spend LLM money, then fail deep / silently submit unauthenticated at the submit step") does not materialize:

1. Submit is guarded on every platform — the exact opposite of "silently run unauthenticated." Metaculus submit_prediction checks the key first and returns False with an error log if absent (metaculus.py:133-135) — the finding itself cites this guard. Polymarket and Kalshi submit_prediction raise NotImplementedError, which the orchestrator catches and records as "logged_only" (orchestrator.py:442-448). No submit path ever runs unauthenticated.

2. The cost/failure ordering is mislocated. The only operation that can fail on a missing platform key is the question READ, and it happens at predict.py:99 / batch_predict.py:62 — BEFORE the orchestrator runs any LLM (predict at predict.py:133). get_question raises ValueError on HTTP error (metaculus.py:124-129), so an auth-required read fails FAST, before LLM cost — not "deep after incurring cost." (Metaculus question reads are in fact publicly accessible, so typically no failure occurs at all.)

3. The "auto_submit" scenario the finding builds its severity on is not exercised by either CLI. Both call orchestrator.predict() WITHOUT auto_submit, so it defaults to False (orchestrator.py:467), routing through interactive present_for_review (console.input, human_review.py:191) before any submit. Submission is never unattended via the shipped entry points.

4. Missing TAVILY_API_KEY is a deliberate, documented graceful degradation: search.py:76-77 logs "search will return empty results (LLM knowledge only)" and search() returns [] (line 85-86). This is handled-by-design, not a failure.

Residual kernel: a missing search/platform key does let a run consume full LLM cost while silently degrading forecast quality (no search) — a fail-fast ergonomics improvement is defensible. But that is a LOW-severity quality/UX nit, not the HIGH-severity "spend money then submit unauthenticated mid-run" the finding describes. The load-bearing mechanism (unauthenticated writes + post-cost deep failure) is contradicted by the submit guards and the read-before-LLM ordering. Default to real=false: the genuine residual is already largely handled and does not support the claimed severity.

### `READ-261` — API keys held as plaintext dataclass fields with default repr — Config logging/serialization leaks all secrets
- **confirmed ✓** · source: readiness:ops-cost · effort: small · file: `/Users/bjgilbert/Projects/Zoltar/sigma-predict/src/config.py:58-64`
- **evidence:** All seven keys are ordinary str dataclass fields (config.py:58-64) on a @dataclass (line 14/55) with no custom __repr__ and no SecretStr wrapper. dataclass auto-generates a repr that includes every field value, so any `logger.debug(config)`, `print(config)`, `repr(config)`, or `dataclasses.asdict(config)` emits all API keys in cleartext. The Streamlit app caches this Config as a process-wide singleton (app.py:33 per repo map) and the audit logger only redacts keys it sees inside `params` dicts (llm_audit.py:27,34) — it does not protect a Config object logged elsewhere. This is a real exposure surface for a tool that already writes JSONL audit logs and runs a web UI.
- **recommendation:** Wrap key fields in a type with a redacting __repr__ (e.g. pydantic SecretStr, or a custom Secret str subclass), add `repr=False` on the field() definitions, and override Config.__repr__ to mask keys. Audit any logging path that could serialize Config.
- **verifier:** The structural claims are all code-verified TRUE: config.py:58-64 holds seven plaintext str API-key fields on a @dataclass (line 55) with no custom __repr__, no repr=False, and no SecretStr; Python auto-generates a __repr__ that includes every field value; app.py:33-35 caches Config as a @st.cache_resource singleton; llm_audit.py:27/34 redacts only keys it sees inside params dicts and does not protect a Config object. So the latent footgun (unguarded dataclass repr over secret fields) genuinely exists.

However, the finding's materiality framing overstates it. I grepped the entire repo (src/ + app.py) for every sink the finding names — repr(config), print(config), str(config), dataclasses.asdict, json.dumps of config, logger/logging calls taking a Config, and Streamlit display paths (st.write/st.json/st.code/st.exception/traceback) — and found ZERO. The config var (app.py:67) is passed into page functions but is never logged, serialized, or rendered anywhere. No code path triggers Config.__repr__. The keys are loaded from os.environ, so they already live in the process environment; the dataclass adds no new exposure absent a sink. The one component that actually writes JSONL to disk (llm_audit.py) is correctly protected: it redacts on the api_key/apikey/token/secret/password/auth pattern, hashes params, and per its docstring never writes raw values. The web UI never displays config.

So this is a real defense-in-depth / hardening gap, not an active high-severity production exposure. The 'high' rating depends on a leak path ('emits all API keys in cleartext', 'real exposure surface') that does not exist in the current code. The recommendation (SecretStr / repr=False / masking __repr__) is sound preventive hardening. Verdict: genuine but mis-severitied — it is a latent issue, not a live leak.

(2) Real test suite confirmed: 6 test files, 143 test functions total, covering the aggregator (27), calibration (28), cross_pollination (44), pipeline_contract (24), registry (13), and platform_clients (7) — i.e. the aggregation/calibration math and platform-client schema mappers the finding cites. (3) pytest is configured (pyproject.toml lines 36-38: testpaths=['tests'], asyncio_mode='auto') with a `dev` extra (lines 22-27: pytest, pytest-asyncio, pytest-httpx) but nothing invokes it. The recommendation's `pip install -e .[dev]` then `pytest` matches this config exactly. (4) v0.3.0 (d3f14b9) and prior commits landed on main with no automated gate. So the finding is genuine, material, and not already handled — regressions in the calibration/aggregation math would indeed only be caught by manual local runs.

Severity adjustment is warranted. The repo evidences a single-developer project: all commits are by one human (Brad Gilbert), one active branch (main), no other contributors. There is no PR/branch-protection workflow, so the recommendation to 'gate merges to main' presumes a collaboration model that does not exist — the author commits directly to main. There is also no production-deployment surface in the repo (Streamlit app, no Dockerfile, no deploy workflow, no infra config), so 'production-readiness' blast radius is limited. CI would still add real value (auto-running the existing 143 tests on push catches exactly the math/mapper regressions the finding identifies), but the operational risk is materially lower than a team/production context. The 'high' rating is calibrated for a multi-contributor or deployed-production setting the repo does not show.

### `READ-247` — No code/config version, git SHA, or schema_version stored on any record
- **confirmed ✓** · source: readiness:provenance · effort: medium · file: `src/models.py:205-230; pyproject.toml:3`
- **evidence:** PredictionRecord captures created_at but no code version, no git commit, no pyproject version, and no schema_version. grep across src/ found zero git/version capture; pyproject version is a static '0.1.0'. The aggregation method, trim_fraction, extremization_factor, calibration thresholds and hedging shift all live in Config (config.py) and can change between runs, but the record only stores the aggregation method name (models.py:115) — not trim_fraction, extremization_factor, or prior_hedging_shift. A record cannot be tied to the code/config that produced it, and a future field rename will silently mis-default historical JSONL on reload (store.py:49 does PredictionRecord(**json.loads(line))).
- **recommendation:** Add code_version (git SHA + dirty flag), schema_version, and a snapshot of the effective pipeline config (aggregation + calibration params, num_runs, personas) to PredictionRecord. Bump schema_version on any model change and migrate on load rather than silently defaulting.
- **verifier:** Confirmed against actual code. PredictionRecord (src/models.py:205-230) stores created_at but no code_version, git SHA, pyproject version, or schema_version — verified by reading the full model. Repo-wide grep across src/ and scripts/ for git/sha/version/schema_version/__version__/importlib.metadata found zero provenance-capture code; the only SHA reference (src/pipeline/llm_audit.py:53) is SHA-256 hashing of audit payloads, unrelated. pyproject version is a static '0.1.0'. The tunable pipeline params (trim_fraction=0.1, extremization_factor=1.5 in config.py:32-33; prior_hedging_shift=0.04 in config.py:39) are NOT persisted: the Aggregation model (models.py:114-123) stores only the method name plus computed outputs, and grep confirms none of these param names appear in any record/model persistence path. Records therefore cannot be tied to the code/config that produced them. The reload mis-default claim is also valid: store load does PredictionRecord(**json.loads(line)) (correct content) with only JSONDecodeError/ValueError caught, so a renamed/removed field silently reverts to its pydantic default rather than erroring.

Two minor accuracy issues that do not undermine the finding: (1) the cited path 'store.py:49' is actually 'src/registry/store.py:49' — line number and code are correct, only the directory is off; (2) RunResult.search_persona IS stored per-run (models.py:96), so persona is partially captured, though the pipeline-level config snapshot, num_runs, and numeric aggregation/calibration params are not.

Materiality is high: this system's value proposition is longitudinal calibration learning (isotonic/Platt fitting over historical resolved records). If aggregation or calibration parameters change between runs — which they trivially can, being plain config defaults — historical scores become non-comparable and silently contaminate the calibration fit with no way to segment by config version or detect the drift. That is a correctness-of-learning hazard for the core function, not cosmetic metadata hygiene.

### `READ-248` — Sampling parameters (temperature, max_tokens) are not persisted on the run record
- **confirmed ✓** · source: readiness:provenance · effort: small · file: `src/models.py:92-111; src/pipeline/forecaster.py:161-165,204-208`
- **evidence:** Every LLM call uses config.primary_model.temperature (default 0.7, config.py:19,78) and max_tokens, passed at forecaster.py:163-164 and 207. RunResult (models.py:92-111) stores model and provider but neither temperature nor max_tokens. The verification/challenge/deliberation calls likewise do not persist their sampling params. With temperature 0.7 the forecast is inherently nondeterministic, and the record does not even capture the temperature it ran at — so a reviewer cannot know whether a past forecast was greedy or high-variance, and cannot attempt a comparable replay.
- **recommendation:** Store temperature, max_tokens (and any top_p/seed if added) per call on RunResult and on verification/challenge entries. Consider supporting a seed where the provider allows it to make replay meaningful; at minimum record the params so the run is characterizable.
- **verifier:** Confirmed against the code. All factual claims hold:

1. Forecast and pre-mortem calls use config.primary_model.temperature (default 0.7) and max_tokens (4096) — src/pipeline/forecaster.py:163-164 and 206-207, with defaults defined at src/config.py:18-19,78.
2. RunResult (src/models.py:92-111) persists model and provider but NOT temperature, max_tokens, top_p, or seed.
3. VerificationEntry and ChallengeEntry (src/models.py:163-182) likewise persist only provider/model — no sampling params. The verify/challenge router paths (llm_router.py:229-318) delegate sampling entirely to external sigma-verify clients and never even pass/capture temperature.

Absent-control check — I specifically looked for any place the temperature value is persisted and found none. The one candidate, the audit log, does NOT rescue this: (a) it is disabled by default (Config.audit_path=None, config.py:109), and (b) even when enabled, llm_audit.py:30-48 writes only a STRUCTURAL summary — for a float it records `temperature:float` (the type), never the value 0.7 (line 38-39). By explicit design it stores no raw param values. So the actual sampling configuration is unrecoverable from any persisted artifact.

The forecaster temperature is config-driven (ModelConfig is mutable) and the record carries no version/commit field, so it is not recoverable from source-at-commit either. The finding's framing is correct: a stored forecast cannot state the temperature it ran at, so a reviewer cannot characterize whether it was greedy or high-variance.

Materiality: this is a forecasting/calibration system whose value rests on the provenance and characterizability of probabilistic estimates. A nondeterministic (temp 0.7) forecast with no recorded sampling configuration is a genuine provenance gap, spanning every persisted call type, with no recovery path. The recommendation is sound and cheap (add fields to RunResult and the verification/challenge entries).

### `READ-249` — Audit log cannot be correlated to any prediction or run — no prediction_id/run_id, and raw I/O is hashed not stored
- **confirmed ✓** · source: readiness:provenance · effort: medium · file: `src/pipeline/llm_audit.py:88-103; src/pipeline/llm_router.py:215-225`
- **evidence:** log_call writes ts, provider, model, method, params_hash, params_summary, result_hash, result_len, tokens, duration, cost (llm_audit.py:90-103). It does NOT write prediction_id or run_id, so audit entries cannot be tied back to the registry record they belong to. The prompt/response are only hashed (`result_hash`) and structurally summarized — the actual prompt and model output are never stored anywhere (by design, llm_audit.py:7-8). Combined with the prompts not being versioned, there is no path to reconstruct the exact request/response of a past call. The audit log is also disabled by default (config.audit_path defaults to None, config.py:109), so production runs likely have no per-call trail at all.
- **recommendation:** Thread prediction_id and run_id into log_call and write them on each entry. Provide an opt-in mode that stores full prompt+response (or a content-addressed store keyed by the existing hashes) for records that must be auditable. Default audit_path on for production deployments.
- **verifier:** PARTIALLY CONFIRMED — real core, overstated framing and severity. |source:src/pipeline/llm_audit.py:90-103, src/pipeline/llm_router.py:188-225, src/models.py:92-247, src/config.py:109|

Confirmed facts: (1) log_call writes ts/provider/model/method/params_hash/params_summary/result_hash/result_len/tokens/duration/cost and NOT prediction_id or run_id (llm_audit.py:90-103) — TRUE. (2) Raw prompt/response are only hashed, never stored (llm_audit.py:97, docstring line 7) — TRUE. (3) audit_path defaults to None/disabled (config.py:109) — TRUE. (4) Prompts are flat .md files with no version field (prompts/) — TRUE. (5) router.call() signature is (provider, model, system, user, temperature, max_tokens) with no run_id/prediction_id parameter (llm_router.py:188-196), so the router has no ID to log even though both run_id and prediction_id exist in the data model (models.py:93, 207) — TRUE. The correlation gap is genuine.

Where the finding overreaches: It conflates the audit log with the provenance record. The audit log is explicitly a lightweight cost/latency/security trail ("Ported from ollama-mcp-bridge audit pattern — simplified for sigma-predict's single-threaded, personal-tool context", docstring lines 11-12). The actual forecast provenance IS persisted in PredictionRecord/RunResult: reasoning_chain, adjustment_reasoning, base_rate_used, base_rate_source, sources_cited, search_queries, pre_mortem_scenario, falsification_anchors, plus per-run/per-prediction UUIDs (models.py:92-247). So the claim "there is no path to reconstruct the exact request/response of a past call" is misleading — the substantive reasoning and inputs are recoverable from the registry; only the verbatim wire-level prompt/response text is not, and that is by deliberate design.

The recommendation to "store full prompt+response" directly contradicts the module's stated security posture (llm_audit.py:7-8: 'raw prompt/system text is NEVER written to the log'). That part of the recommendation should be rejected, not adopted. Defaulting audit_path on is also defensible to leave opt-in given the personal-tool framing.

Net: the threading of run_id/prediction_id into log_call is a real, low-cost, non-controversial improvement that does not conflict with the security design — that is the legitimate kernel. The rest (no reconstruction path, store raw I/O, production-readiness framing) overstates a deliberate design choice in a self-described personal tool.

### `READ-251` — Isotonic-calibrated predictions store no calibration parameters — the transform cannot be reconstructed
- **confirmed ✓** · source: readiness:provenance · effort: small · file: `src/pipeline/calibration.py:70-89; src/models.py:126-129`
- **evidence:** CalibrationData.platt_params_used (models.py:127) is only populated for Phase-2 Platt calibration (calibrate returns platt_params.to_dict(), calibration.py:69). For Phase-1 isotonic calibration the function returns `(calibrated, None)` (calibration.py:80) and for Phase-0 hedging it returns `(calibrated, None)` (calibration.py:89,100). The record therefore stores calibrated_probability with platt_params_used=None and no record of the isotonic breakpoints, the input (predicted,outcome) pairs, or the hedging shift used. The isotonic fit is also recomputed at predict time from the live registry (orchestrator.py:336-345), so the breakpoints depend on whatever resolved records existed then — and none of that is captured. The calibration step of a past prediction is not reconstructable.
- **recommendation:** Store the calibration method actually applied (platt/isotonic/hedging), and for isotonic store the fitted breakpoints (or the exact input pairs + n_resolved + a fit hash); for hedging store the shift used. Make CalibrationData carry method + full params, not just optional Platt coefficients.
- **verifier:** Confirmed against the actual code at every claimed location. calibration.py:69 returns platt_params.to_dict() only for Phase-2 Platt; lines 80, 89, and 100 all return (calibrated, None) for isotonic and both hedging branches. models.py:126-129 CalibrationData carries only platt_params_used (Platt-specific {"a","b"}), calibrated_probability, and confidence_interval — no method field, no isotonic breakpoints, no hedging shift, no n_resolved. orchestrator.py:351-355 writes platt_params_used=platt_dict (None for non-Platt) plus the scalar cal_prob, capturing nothing else. orchestrator.py:335-345 confirms the isotonic fit is recomputed live at predict time from self.query.get_calibration_pairs() and count_resolved(), so both the breakpoints AND the phase selection depend on mutable registry state at that moment, none of which is stored.

The absent-control check holds: a repo-wide grep for isotonic/hedging/breakpoints/calibration_method outside calibration.py found no persistence of method or non-Platt params anywhere. The only consumer, human_review.py:135-138, cannot even distinguish isotonic from hedging — it mislabels every non-Platt case as "Prior hedging correction applied," which is factually wrong for Phase-1 isotonic predictions. That strengthens the provenance finding: the record loses the method identity itself, not just parameters.

Materiality is real for a provenance lens. The calibration step converts the raw aggregate into the submitted probability — load-bearing. Platt predictions are reconstructable (params stored + on-disk platt_*.json), but isotonic and hedging predictions are not, and these are exactly the early/growth-phase predictions the system makes most before accumulating 50+ resolutions. Because the isotonic breakpoints depend on registry state that mutates after every resolution, the transform cannot be reconstructed post hoc even in principle. Hedging is only partially recoverable (shift is a config constant) but the record stores neither the shift, the config version, nor reliably the input probability, so default drift silently breaks it.

### `READ-227` — search_augmented_base_rate is the ONLY external call site with graceful degradation — the asymmetry confirms degradation was never applied to the LLM forecasting path
- **REFUTED ✗** · source: readiness:resilience · effort: small · file: `/Users/bjgilbert/Projects/Zoltar/sigma-predict/src/pipeline/base_rate.py:198-209`
- **evidence:** search_augmented_base_rate wraps both the Tavily search (lines 126-130) AND its router.call (lines 198-209) in try/except that returns `(base_rate, '')` on failure — clean degradation. But estimate_base_rate's router.call in the SAME file (lines 54-61) has NO such guard. This proves the degradation pattern exists and the team knows how to apply it, yet it was applied to exactly one of the ~6 LLM call sites. The decompose, base_rate, forecast, pre-mortem, deliberation-trigger, verify, and challenge calls are inconsistently protected.
- **recommendation:** Apply the same try/except-and-degrade pattern used in search_augmented_base_rate to estimate_base_rate (degrade to 0.5 default, already the parse-failure behavior), decompose_question (degrade to Decomposition()), and the forecaster calls.
- **verifier:** The finding's central evidentiary claim is factually false. It asserts search_augmented_base_rate is "the ONLY external call site with graceful degradation" and uses that singularity to argue "degradation was never applied to the LLM forecasting path." Code inspection refutes this:

LLM call sites via router.call (src/pipeline, excluding router internals) = 6: base_rate.py:54 (estimate_base_rate), base_rate.py:199 (search_augmented_base_rate), forecaster.py:161 and :204, deliberation.py:127, decomposer.py:61.

Call-failure graceful degradation (try/except wrapping router.call) exists at TWO sites, not one:
1. search_augmented_base_rate (base_rate.py:198-209) — except → return (base_rate, '').
2. deliberation.py:126-149 — except Exception as e → degrades to default {"recommended_action": "accept_aggregate"} and continues.

So the "ONLY" premise is wrong, and the "asymmetry confirms it was never applied to the forecasting path" inference collapses: the pattern was independently applied in deliberation, which IS part of the forecasting flow. The finding also misreads the parse-failure guards (estimate_base_rate lines 81-86 catches JSONDecodeError/ValueError and degrades to 0.5; forecaster.py:173-176 and :214-216 degrade to base rate on parse failure) — these are degradation, just for malformed output rather than call exceptions.

There IS a real residual issue buried under the false framing: call-failure (exception) guarding is inconsistent — only 2 of 6 sites guard router.call against thrown exceptions, and the critical-path calls (decompose_question, estimate_base_rate, run_forecast) are unguarded with NO top-level try/except in orchestrator.run_pipeline (lines 138-204), so a transient API error aborts the entire prediction. But that genuine concern is a different, narrower claim than what was filed. The finding as written rests on a demonstrably false "ONLY one call site" premise and a wrong provenance inference ("never applied to the LLM forecasting path"), so the finding as stated is not real. The recommendation to add degradation to estimate_base_rate/decompose/forecast is directionally reasonable, but the justification (a unique pattern the team applied exactly once) is fabricated by the analysis.

Per instructions, default real=false when the finding as stated is refuted by code. A correctly-scoped resilience finding about unguarded critical-path calls + missing top-level handler would be legitimate and roughly medium severity, but that is not the finding submitted.

### `READ-228` — Tavily search has no retry or 429 handling; HTTP errors silently degrade to empty results, contaminating forecasts with no signal
- **confirmed ✓** · source: readiness:resilience · effort: medium · file: `/Users/bjgilbert/Projects/Zoltar/sigma-predict/src/pipeline/search.py:97-111`
- **evidence:** SearchModule.search creates a fresh `httpx.Client(timeout=30.0)` per call and catches httpx.HTTPStatusError and httpx.RequestError by returning `[]`. There is no retry, no backoff, and 429 (rate limit) is treated identically to a 404 — both silently yield zero results. search_with_persona (lines 144-152) fires 3-5 sequential queries per run; under Tavily rate limiting every query after the first 429 returns empty, so a persona that should gather 5 sources may gather 0, and the forecaster proceeds on LLM knowledge alone (search.py:31 'No search results available'). The forecast still completes and is recorded as a normal prediction — the search failure is invisible to the aggregate and to downstream scoring. With num_runs up to 10 (and 5 queries each = up to 50 Tavily calls per prediction), rate limiting is likely, not hypothetical.
- **recommendation:** Add bounded retry with backoff on 429/5xx/timeout (respecting Tavily's Retry-After). Distinguish 'search unavailable due to error' from 'search returned no hits' and propagate the former as a pipeline_warning and/or a reduced search_quality_score so the validator and human reviewer can see degraded evidence. Reuse a single httpx.Client instead of constructing one per query.
- **verifier:** Code-level claims all confirmed. search.py:97-111 builds a fresh httpx.Client(timeout=30.0) per call and catches HTTPStatusError/RequestError by returning []. Grep across src/ and pyproject.toml confirms no retry, backoff, Retry-After, tenacity, or 429-specific handling exists anywhere in the repo — the control is genuinely absent. search_with_persona (search.py:144-152) fires up to 5 sequential queries per persona, empty results degrade to "No search results available" (forecaster.py:31), and num_runs is parameterized (orchestrator.py:74,207) so the up-to-50-calls-per-prediction arithmetic holds. The same silent-degradation pattern repeats in the base_rate path (base_rate.py:127-133), making it systemic.\n\nThe finding overstates one claim: the failure is NOT fully "invisible to the aggregate and downstream scoring." validator.py:106-114 has a search_quality_gate that averages search_quality_score across runs and emits a pipeline_warning when avg < 3. The finding omits this control. However, the control is weak against this exact failure: (1) search_quality_score is LLM self-reported (forecaster.py:234), not derived from actual returned-source count, so an empty [] does not deterministically force a low score; (2) a missing JSON key defaults to 5, above the threshold of 3, so degraded runs can score as fine; (3) it is WARN-only, non-blocking by design; (4) partial degradation (some of 5 queries rate-limited) still returns some sources and a plausibly-mid self-score, evading the gate. So the recommendation's core ask — a deterministic "search unavailable due to error" signal distinct from "no hits," plus bounded retry on 429/5xx — addresses a real gap that the LLM-self-score gate does not reliably cover.\n\nThe resilience gap (no retry on transient 429/5xx/timeout, no deterministic error-vs-empty distinction, per-call client construction) is genuine and material for a forecasting pipeline whose call volume makes rate limiting likely.

### `READ-229` — Platform read clients swallow all errors (including 429/5xx/timeout) as empty/None — silent data loss feeds the pipeline
- **confirmed ✓** · source: readiness:resilience · effort: medium · file: `/Users/bjgilbert/Projects/Zoltar/sigma-predict/src/platforms/metaculus.py:111-116`
- **evidence:** get_questions in all three clients catches `except httpx.HTTPStatusError` and then bare `except Exception` and returns `[]` (metaculus.py:111-116, polymarket.py:101-106, kalshi.py:131-136). A 429 rate-limit, a 503, and a connection timeout are all indistinguishable from 'no questions matched'. get_community_prediction does the same returning None (metaculus.py:190-192, kalshi.py:175-177, polymarket.py:141-143), and orchestrator._collect_platform_signals (orchestrator.py:544-553) further swallows any exception with logger.debug. There is no retry/backoff anywhere, and the 30s timeout is the only control. Net effect: a transient platform outage during _collect_platform_signals produces a prediction with no community-price comparison and no warning surfaced to the human reviewer (human_review.py reads the missing key as absent).
- **recommendation:** Add bounded retry/backoff for 429/5xx/timeout in the platform clients. Narrow the bare `except Exception` so genuine transient network errors are retried and distinguished from empty result sets; emit a pipeline_warning when community-signal collection fails so degradation is visible rather than silent.
- **verifier:** Confirmed against the actual code; every substantive claim holds.

ABSENT CONTROL VERIFIED: grep across all of src/ and pyproject.toml for retry|backoff|tenacity|HTTPTransport|max_retries|Retry returns zero matches. No retry/backoff exists anywhere. The only resilience control is timeout=30.0 on each httpx.Client, exactly as the finding states.

ERROR SWALLOWING VERIFIED at all six cited sites:
- get_questions returns [] on both httpx.HTTPStatusError and bare except Exception: metaculus.py:111-116, polymarket.py:101-106, kalshi.py:131-136.
- get_community_prediction returns None on bare except Exception: metaculus.py:190-192, kalshi.py:175-177, polymarket.py:141-143.
A 429/503/connection-timeout is genuinely indistinguishable from an empty/no-match result in all of these. During question discovery a rate-limited list call returns "no questions" and the pipeline proceeds as if the platform had nothing.

ORCHESTRATOR DOUBLE-SWALLOW VERIFIED: _collect_platform_signals catches bare Exception and logs at logger.debug (actual path src/pipeline/orchestrator.py:552-553; finding cited orchestrator.py:544-553 — minor path imprecision, line numbers and behavior exact).

SILENT-TO-HUMAN VERIFIED and slightly stronger than stated. human_review.py:146 gates the price display on any(v is not None ...) so a failed fetch silently omits the section with no "unavailable" note. human_review.py:340-345 reads community = ps.get("metaculus_community") and the divergence-from-community human-review flag only fires if community is not None — so a transient fetch failure silently disables an explicit safety guardrail. No pipeline_warning is emitted, despite the codebase having a pipeline_warnings mechanism (models.py:216, validator.py) used for other degradations — so the omission is a real inconsistency, not a missing capability.

Severity nuance: this is a read-only signal-augmentation path, not the core prediction, and verification runs independently of platform signals — so it degrades a comparison/guardrail rather than corrupting the prediction. But the load-bearing harm (an explicit human-review divergence check silently self-disabling on transient platform failure, with no warning surfaced) is real and matches the resilience lens precisely.

### `SWEE-1` — Search-augmented base rate update is never propagated to the forecaster runs
- **confirmed ✓** · source: sweep · effort: trivial · file: `/Users/bjgilbert/Projects/Zoltar/sigma-predict/src/pipeline/orchestrator.py:184-228`
- **evidence:** After search augmentation, the code rebinds the local `base_rate` and updates `record.decomposition.base_rate_source = citation_note` (lines 184-186). But the forecast loop passes the STALE `base_source` variable, not the updated source: `run_forecast(... base_rate=base_rate, base_rate_source=base_source, ...)` (line 224). `base_source` was assigned at line 162 from `estimate_base_rate` and is never reassigned, so forecasters see the augmented numeric base_rate but the original (pre-augmentation) source string. The augmented numeric value IS passed (base_rate was rebound), so the prompt shows e.g. base_rate=0.42 with source='LLM estimate' instead of the 'STATISTICAL REFERENCE DATA...' citation that justifies that number.
- **recommendation:** Pass the effective source: rebind `base_source = citation_note` inside the `if citation_note:` block (alongside the existing record update), so the forecast loop uses the augmented source string.
- **verifier:** Confirmed by direct code inspection. In /Users/bjgilbert/Projects/Zoltar/sigma-predict/src/pipeline/orchestrator.py: line 162 assigns local `base_source` from estimate_base_rate; lines 183-187 (the `if citation_note:` block) rebind `base_rate = augmented_rate` and update `record.decomposition.base_rate_source = citation_note` but DO NOT update the local `base_source`. The forecast loop at line 219-228 passes `base_rate=base_rate` (the augmented numeric value, correct) and `base_rate_source=base_source` (the stale, pre-augmentation source string). `base_source` is never reassigned between 162 and 223. In src/pipeline/forecaster.py:144-147 the prompt renders both together under a "Base Rate (LOCKED)" section: "**Base rate:** {base_rate:.3f}" and "**Source:** {base_rate_source}". So the forecaster sees the augmented number paired with the original source label. The citation_note (src/pipeline/base_rate.py:244-247) is substantive — "STATISTICAL REFERENCE DATA: {stat_rate} from '{citation}' (confidence=..., blended with LLM estimate ...)" — and that grounding never reaches the forecaster. The recommended fix (rebind base_source = citation_note inside the if-block) is correct and minimal. The finding's mechanism and evidence are fully accurate, including the detail that the numeric value IS correctly propagated while only the source string is stale.

### `SWEE-13` — Kalshi/Polymarket price normalization can emit probabilities >1.0 or silently mangle valid prices
- **confirmed ✓** · source: sweep · effort: small · file: `src/platforms/kalshi.py:30`
- **evidence:** community_pred = val / 100.0 if val > 1.0 else val  (mirrored at kalshi.py:174). The heuristic assumes any value <=1.0 is already a probability and any value >1.0 is cents. A legitimate Kalshi cents price of 1 (1 cent = $0.01 = 0.01 probability) fails the `val > 1.0` test and is returned as 0.01... wait, no: it returns `val` = 1.0, i.e. a 1-cent market becomes probability 1.0 (certainty). Conversely a price of exactly 100 cents -> 1.0 correctly, but 100.0 is the only safe upper value: there is no clamp, so any malformed/garbage numeric (e.g. 250 from a buggy or hostile response) yields community_prediction = 2.5, which is an invalid probability fed downstream into PlatformSignals with no range validation.
- **recommendation:** Normalize against the documented unit (Kalshi prices are integer cents 0-100) explicitly rather than guessing by magnitude, and clamp the result to [0.0, 1.0]. The 1-cent-becomes-1.0 collision is a real correctness bug for low-probability markets.
- **verifier:** Confirmed against the code. src/platforms/kalshi.py:30 reads `community_pred = val / 100.0 if val > 1.0 else val` and is mirrored verbatim at kalshi.py:174. The developer intent is documented: Kalshi prices are integer cents 0-100 (comment line 29; test test_platform_clients.py:87 maps `last_price: 65` -> 0.65). 

Sub-claim A (the 1-cent collision) is a genuine correctness bug. For an integer-cents price of exactly 1 (1 cent = 1% probability), `1.0 > 1.0` is False, so the code returns `val` = 1.0, i.e. it reports a 1% longshot as 100% certainty — a maximal-magnitude error at a single boundary value. The finding's prose stumbles mid-sentence ('returns 0.01... wait, no') but lands on the correct conclusion. The boundary is the only collision point: 0 cents -> 0.0 and 100 cents -> 1.0 are both handled correctly; only `val == 1` is wrong.

Sub-claim B (no clamp -> values like 2.5 from garbage/hostile input flow downstream unvalidated) is also accurate: there is no clamp, and PlatformSignals.prices_at_prediction_time (models.py:133) is an unvalidated `dict[str, float | None]`. But this requires a malformed API response, so it is a defensive-coding gap rather than a live bug.

Materiality is real but narrower than 'high' implies. The normalized value flows into get_community_prediction -> orchestrator._collect_platform_signals -> PlatformSignals.prices_at_prediction_time, which is consumed by review/human_review.py as a comparison/anomaly signal — NOT the actual submitted probability. So worst case is a misleading human-review comparison for the narrow slice of markets priced at exactly 1 cent, not a corrupted submitted forecast. The recommendation (normalize against the documented integer-cents unit explicitly and clamp to [0,1]) is correct and cheap. The same defect pattern would also benefit from a unit test at the val==1 boundary, which the existing test suite does not cover (it only tests 65).

### `SWEE-15` — Metaculus get_resolution treats continuous/numeric resolutions as binary probabilities
- **confirmed ✓** · source: sweep · effort: medium · file: `src/platforms/metaculus.py:170`
- **evidence:** outcome = float(resolution_value); return Resolution(outcome=outcome, ...). For binary questions Metaculus resolution is 0/1, but _parse_question_type maps continuous questions to QuestionType.NUMERIC and group questions to MULTIPLE_CHOICE. For a continuous question the raw `resolution` field is a value in the question's range (could be any magnitude), and for ambiguous/annulled resolutions Metaculus returns sentinel values like -1 (annulled) or string codes. float(-1.0) is silently accepted as outcome=-1.0. Resolution.outcome (models.py:154) is documented '0|1 for binary, value for numeric' but downstream Brier/log scoring (calibration.py) assumes 0/1. A -1 or out-of-[0,1] outcome corrupts Brier/log-score computation.
- **recommendation:** Branch on question_type before coercing: only accept 0/1 (and known sentinels mapped to None) for binary; carry numeric values separately. Reject Metaculus annulled/ambiguous sentinels (negative codes) rather than float()-ing them into a score input.
- **verifier:** Confirmed against the code. metaculus.py:170-173 does outcome = float(resolution_value) with no branch on question_type and no range/sentinel check — it only catches TypeError/ValueError, so any numeric Metaculus resolution value (including ambiguous/annulled sentinels like -1/-2, or a continuous question's in-range magnitude) is silently accepted as Resolution.outcome.

The downstream contamination is real and reachable, not theoretical: scripts/resolve.py:94 calls metaculus.get_resolution(), then lines 106-110 feed outcome unguarded into brier_score (scoring.py:8-10, (predicted-actual)^2), log_score (scoring.py:13-19, treats actual>=0.5 as the positive outcome), and classify_error (resolve.py:23-64, e.g. abs(base_rate-outcome)>0.5 which is always true for outcome=-1). The contaminated outcome is then persisted to the record and re-consumed by registry/query.py:120/152/196 (Brier decomposition by domain/horizon) and scripts/dashboard.py:59/82. A -1 or out-of-[0,1] outcome corrupts the core accuracy metrics the calibration system depends on. No tests exercise get_resolution and no validation exists anywhere in the chain.

Two minor inaccuracies in the finding that do not change the substance: (1) it cites calibration.py for Brier/log scoring, but scoring lives in scoring.py and is invoked from resolve.py — location imprecision only; (2) the exact sentinel code (-1 vs -2) is an API detail, but the load-bearing claim — that any non-{0,1} value passes through float() unguarded into scoring — is demonstrably true. Scope note: resolve.py only calls get_resolution for platform=='metaculus' records (others are skipped), so corruption is currently Metaculus-scoped, but that is the only resolution path actually wired up.

Recommendation is sound: branch on question_type and reject/map sentinels (and out-of-range values) to None before they reach score inputs.

### `SWEE-25` — log_score silently mislabels numeric questions as binary, producing meaningless scores
- **confirmed ✓** · source: sweep · effort: small · file: `/Users/bjgilbert/Projects/Zoltar/sigma-predict/src/scoring.py:13-19`
- **evidence:** def log_score(predicted, actual): p = max(0.001, min(0.999, predicted)); if actual >= 0.5: return -math.log(p) else: return -math.log(1.0 - p). Resolution.outcome is documented as '0|1 for binary, value for numeric' (models.py:154). resolve.py:106-109 calls brier_score/log_score on whatever outcome the platform returns, with no question_type check. A numeric question that resolves to e.g. 42 is treated as actual>=0.5 -> binary 'yes', and brier_score returns (predicted-42)**2 which is nonsensical for a probability forecast.
- **recommendation:** Guard scoring by question_type: only compute Brier/log score for binary (and clamp outcome to {0,1}). For numeric questions either skip these scores or use an appropriate proper score. At minimum assert/validate outcome in [0,1] before calling these functions.
- **verifier:** CONFIRMED. Verified the full chain in actual code (resolve.py is at scripts/resolve.py, not the cited src/resolve.py — minor path error, but the cited line numbers 106-109 match exactly).

1. scoring.py:13-19 — log_score collapses any actual>=0.5 to a binary "yes" via -math.log(p); brier_score (line 8-10) returns (predicted-actual)**2. Both assume actual is a 0/1 probability outcome. |source:scoring.py| VERIFIED

2. models.py:154 — Resolution.outcome documented as "0|1 for binary, value for numeric", so a single float field carries both probability outcomes and raw numeric values. |source:models.py| VERIFIED

3. metaculus.py:156-182 — get_resolution does `outcome = float(resolution_value)` with NO question_type check. For a continuous question, Metaculus's resolution field is the resolved numeric value, passed verbatim. The platform layer explicitly models QuestionType.NUMERIC (metaculus.py:25-26) and MULTIPLE_CHOICE, so non-binary questions are first-class in the data model. |source:metaculus.py| VERIFIED

4. resolve.py:104-110 — calls brier_score/log_score on `outcome` with no question_type guard, even though the record carries question_type. A numeric resolve of 42.0 -> log_score returns -log(0.999) (treated as "yes") and brier returns (p-42)**2, which is meaningless for a probability forecast. The failure is SILENT: no error, no skip, no validation. |source:resolve.py| VERIFIED

5. predict.py/batch_predict.py have NO question_type filter, so numeric questions can flow end-to-end into stored records that resolve.py will mis-score. The corrupted brier/log scores then feed aggregate calibration stats across pages/1_Dashboard, 4_Scoreboard, components/charts, polluting system-wide accuracy metrics. VERIFIED

The recommendation (guard scoring by question_type, validate outcome in [0,1] for binary, skip/use proper score for numeric) is sound.

### `SWEE-27` — Single malformed registry line causes permanent data loss on next update()
- **confirmed ✓** · source: sweep · effort: medium · file: `/Users/bjgilbert/Projects/Zoltar/sigma-predict/src/registry/store.py:43-54,79-107`
- **evidence:** load_all() catches JSONDecodeError/ValueError and just logs 'Skipping malformed record on line %d'. update() calls records = self.load_all(), mutates that list, then _write_all(records) rewrites the WHOLE file via os.replace. Any line that failed to parse (corruption, schema drift, partial write) is silently dropped from the in-memory list and therefore erased from disk on the very next update. The 'atomic rewrite' guarantees the corrupted-but-recoverable lines are atomically destroyed.
- **recommendation:** Preserve unparseable lines verbatim and re-emit them during _write_all, or refuse to rewrite when any line failed to parse (fail loud). At minimum back up the file before os.replace.
- **verifier:** Confirmed against the actual code at /Users/bjgilbert/Projects/Zoltar/sigma-predict/src/registry/store.py.

Mechanism verified line-by-line:
- load_all() (lines 42-54) iterates lines, and on (json.JSONDecodeError, ValueError) at line 50 it logs "Skipping malformed record on line %d" and continues. Malformed lines never enter the `records` list.
- update() (lines 79-94) does `records = self.load_all()`, mutates the list (replace or append), then calls `_write_all(records)`.
- _write_all() (lines 101-107) writes ONLY the in-memory records to a temp file and `os.replace(tmp_path, self._path)` atomically overwrites the original. The previously-skipped malformed lines are therefore permanently erased on the next update(). No backup is taken.

Materiality confirmed: update() is called in real production paths, not dead code — scripts/resolve.py:113 (batch resolution run that loops over predictions and calls store.update(record) per resolved item) and pages/3_Review.py:288 (Streamlit review edit). So the destructive rewrite is routinely exercised.

Realistic trigger paths for a malformed line:
- save() (lines 31-35) appends with no locking/fsync/durability guard; a crash, kill, disk-full, or concurrent writer can leave a partial/corrupt line.
- Schema drift is the strongest real trigger: PredictionRecord is a pydantic model; tightening a field or adding a required field makes historical records raise ValueError at line 50, get skipped, and then get erased on the next resolve.py run. Silent and irreversible.

The data at stake is the resolved-prediction track record (Brier/log scores) — the core, non-reconstructable asset of a prediction system. Loss is silent (only a WARNING log) and irreversible (atomic overwrite, no backup). The defect is genuine and the recommendation (preserve unparseable lines verbatim and re-emit, or fail loud, or back up before os.replace) is appropriate.

I attempted to refute on the grounds that update() might be unused — it is not; it is on the primary resolution path. The conditional nature of the trigger (a malformed line must exist) is the only mitigating factor, but given save() has zero durability protection and schema evolution is normal, the condition is plausible over the system's lifetime.

### `SWEE-36` — Pipeline error messages are silently swallowed by the progress fragment
- **confirmed ✓** · source: sweep · effort: small · file: `/Users/bjgilbert/Projects/Zoltar/sigma-predict/pages/2_Predict.py:269-292`
- **evidence:** On a pipeline exception, _run_pipeline does q.put(f"ERROR: {e}"), then completed_slot.clear(), then finally q.put(None). In pipeline_progress_fragment the drain loop appends each message to msgs, but when it pulls the None sentinel it sets state and calls st.rerun(); return BEFORE reaching the 'if msgs:' display block at line 289. Because ERROR and None can be drained in the same 1-second tick, the ERROR string is collected into msgs and then discarded by the early return. The user sees the pipeline silently revert to 'idle' with no error shown and no record produced.
- **recommendation:** Capture the error before the None sentinel handling: store the last ERROR message in the completed_slot or session_state and render st.error() when slot is empty after completion. At minimum, display accumulated msgs before the rerun, or write the error to session_state for display on the next render.
- **verifier:** Confirmed code-accurate at /Users/bjgilbert/Projects/Zoltar/sigma-predict/pages/2_Predict.py.

_run_pipeline (lines 82-86): on exception it does q.put(f"ERROR: {e}") (83), completed_slot.clear() (84), then finally q.put(None) (86). The ERROR and None are enqueued back-to-back with no intervening delay.

pipeline_progress_fragment (lines 270-292) drains the queue in a while-True loop: each plain message is appended to msgs (286). When it pulls the None sentinel (273), it enters the completion block. Because completed_slot was cleared on error, slot is empty, so it sets KEY_PIPELINE_STATE = "idle" (283), calls st.rerun() and returns at line 285 — BEFORE reaching the 'if msgs:' display block at line 289. The accumulated ERROR string in msgs is therefore discarded.

Timing: the fragment runs every 1 second. Since ERROR and None are put consecutively in the thread, by the next fragment tick both are sitting in the queue and get drained in the same pass. This is the deterministic normal path for any pipeline exception, not a rare race.

I checked for alternate error-surfacing paths: grep for st.error/st.warning shows error displays only for the manual search/fetch tools (lines 115, 123, 158, 167, 187) and client init (51). There is NO error display tied to the pipeline failure / idle revert at line 283. So the user genuinely sees the UI silently return to idle with no error and no record. The mechanism in the finding is correct in every particular.

One refutation attempt that fails: one might argue the ERROR could be drained in an earlier tick before None arrives. But the thread puts both with no sleep between, so they are effectively simultaneous relative to the 1s polling interval; the overwhelmingly common outcome is same-tick drain. Even in the rare split case, the ERROR would only flash for ~1s inside an st.status block then vanish on the idle rerun with no persistence — still effectively swallowed. So the finding holds either way.

The recommendation (persist the last ERROR to session_state / completed_slot and render st.error when slot is empty after completion) is sound and minimal.

### `SWEE-37` — Pipeline queue and completed-slot are global @st.cache_resource singletons shared across all sessions
- **confirmed ✓** · source: sweep · effort: medium · file: `/Users/bjgilbert/Projects/Zoltar/sigma-predict/app.py:49-57`
- **evidence:** get_pipeline_queue() and get_completed_slot() are decorated @st.cache_resource, which in Streamlit returns ONE shared object for the entire server process across all browser sessions/users. Two users (or two tabs) running predictions concurrently share the same queue.Queue and the same single-item list. _run_pipeline does completed_slot.clear(); completed_slot.append(record) (2_Predict.py:80-81), so user B's completed record overwrites user A's, and progress strings from both threads interleave in the one queue. The 'thread→UI bridge' comment (app.py:56) assumes single-user.
- **recommendation:** Make the queue and completed-slot per-session (store in st.session_state, created on first use) rather than @st.cache_resource. If multi-user is not a goal, document the single-user assumption explicitly; the current code presents no such warning.
- **verifier:** CONFIRMED — code-grounded and accurate. Verified at the cited locations:

- app.py:49-57: get_pipeline_queue() returns queue.Queue() and get_completed_slot() returns [], both decorated @st.cache_resource. Per documented Streamlit semantics, @st.cache_resource returns ONE shared object for the entire server process across all browser sessions/users (it intentionally does not copy, unlike @st.cache_data). So the queue and the single-item list ARE process-global singletons, exactly as claimed.

- 2_Predict.py:80-81: _run_pipeline does completed_slot.clear(); completed_slot.append(record). With two concurrent pipeline runs, the second writer's clear()+append() destroys the first user's record before it is read. Confirmed.

- 2_Predict.py:240-247: the Run Pipeline handler drains the shared queue (q.get_nowait loop) and clears the shared slot before launching — so user B launching wipes user A's in-flight bridge state, and the single shared queue interleaves progress strings from both threads.

- 2_Predict.py:277: the progress fragment reads slot[0] back into per-session st.session_state. The session_state keys (KEY_PIPELINE_*) ARE per-session and correctly isolated; the defect is exclusively the cache_resource queue/slot, which are not. This refutes the obvious counterargument (that Streamlit session isolation saves it) — the shared-singleton layer is precisely the unprotected one.

- No README and no docs exist (no *.md, no README*), and no in-code comment documents a single-user assumption. The only adjacent comment ('thread→UI bridge', app.py:56) implicitly assumes single user. The recommendation (move to st.session_state, or document the assumption) is appropriate.

Could not refute the technical substance. It is a genuine cross-session race/data-corruption bug.

### `SWEE-45` — resolve.py crashes formatting brier_score when outcome is None
- **confirmed ✓** · source: sweep · effort: trivial · file: `/Users/bjgilbert/Projects/Zoltar/sigma-predict/scripts/resolve.py:107`
- **evidence:** Line 107 guards score computation: `if outcome is not None:` ... `resolution.brier_score = round(...)`. But lines 116-120 print unconditionally: `click.echo(f"  Resolved: ... brier={resolution.brier_score:.4f}")`. When a Metaculus question is 'resolved' but `data.get('resolution')` yields a value that maps to `outcome=None` (or any future path leaving outcome None), brier_score stays None and `{None:.4f}` raises `TypeError: unsupported format string passed to NoneType.__format__`. The record has already been written via store.update() at line 113, so the crash aborts the loop mid-run after a partial registry write.
- **recommendation:** Move the echo inside the `if outcome is not None:` block, or guard the format: `brier={resolution.brier_score:.4f}` only when not None. Also handle the outcome=None case explicitly (it was treated as resolved enough to set record.resolution but produced no score).
- **verifier:** PARTIALLY CONFIRMED, severity overstated. |source:scripts/resolve.py:107-120, src/platforms/metaculus.py:156-182, src/models.py:153-156|

The code matches the cited evidence exactly. Line 107 guards scoring with `if outcome is not None:`, brier_score defaults to None (models.py:156), and lines 116-120 echo `brier={resolution.brier_score:.4f}` unconditionally. store.update() at line 113 fires before the echo at 119. So the structural inconsistency the finding describes is genuine: IF a Resolution with outcome=None ever reached line 107, `f"{None:.4f}"` would raise TypeError after a partial registry write. The Resolution model explicitly permits `outcome: float | None = None`, and the line-107 guard proves the author anticipated None.

BUT the finding's concrete trigger is refuted by the actual code. resolve.py only reaches the scoring/echo block for platform=="metaculus" (lines 93-97; all other platforms `continue`). MetaculusClient.get_resolution (metaculus.py:156-182) NEVER returns a Resolution with outcome=None: it returns None outright when data.get("resolution") is None (line 167-168) or when float() conversion fails (line 170-173). When it does return a Resolution, outcome is always a successfully-converted float. Line 87 also skips records that already carry a resolution, so resolve.py only processes freshly-fetched Resolutions. Therefore on every current input path, `outcome is not None` is true whenever a Resolution exists, brier_score is always set before the echo, and the TypeError is NOT reachable. The finding itself hedges ("or any future path leaving outcome None"), conceding it is a latent gap rather than a live crash.

VERDICT: real=true as a latent defensive-coding inconsistency (the guard at 107 and the unguarded format at 119 disagree about whether outcome/brier can be None — a real maintenance hazard if a future platform client or numeric-question path returns outcome=None). NOT real as a currently-triggerable high-severity crash. No reproducible input exists today.

### `SWEE-46` — resolve.py and scoring silently corrupt numeric/non-binary questions
- **confirmed ✓** · source: sweep · effort: small · file: `/Users/bjgilbert/Projects/Zoltar/sigma-predict/scripts/resolve.py:108`
- **evidence:** `Resolution.outcome` is documented `# 0|1 for binary, value for numeric` (models.py:154) and QuestionType has BINARY, NUMERIC, MULTIPLE_CHOICE (models.py:19-22). MetaculusClient.get_resolution does `outcome = float(resolution_value)` for ANY resolution with no type check. resolve.py then unconditionally computes `brier_score(submitted_p, outcome)` = `(predicted - actual)**2`. For a numeric question resolving to e.g. 50000, this stores `(0.5 - 50000)**2 ~ 2.5e9` as a 'Brier score'. log_score branches on `actual >= 0.5` treating everything >=0.5 as a positive binary outcome. dashboard.py then averages these into the global and per-domain/per-platform Brier numbers (lines 49-51, 69, 93). Nothing in resolve.py, scoring.py, or query.py filters on question_type.
- **recommendation:** Filter resolve scoring and all dashboard/query calibration aggregation to `question_type == 'binary'` (and outcome in {0,1}), or clamp/skip numeric outcomes. Brier and log scores are only meaningful for binary outcomes in [0,1].
- **verifier:** Confirmed against the actual code. Mechanism is real and complete: src/platforms/metaculus.py:171 does `outcome = float(resolution_value)` with NO question_type check (it only returns None when float() raises, which drops string/multiple-choice resolutions but accepts any numeric one). scripts/resolve.py:108-109 then unconditionally computes brier_score and log_score on that outcome. src/scoring.py:8-19 is the naive (predicted-actual)**2 and an `actual >= 0.5` binary branch — both only meaningful for outcome in {0,1}. I verified there is NO question_type filtering anywhere in the pipeline: predict.py:102 and batch_predict.py:62 call get_questions with no type filter; metaculus._parse_question_type (metaculus.py:19-30) explicitly maps Metaculus 'continuous' to QuestionType.NUMERIC; nothing downstream (forecaster, orchestrator, resolve, scoring, query, dashboard) ever filters on it. Aggregation contamination confirmed: dashboard.py:49-51,69,93 average brier_score over all resolved pairs filtered only on `outcome is not None`; query.py:104-121 get_calibration_pairs and query.py:127-201 Brier-decomposition functions do the same. Notably query.py:108 docstring claims 'Only includes resolved binary predictions' but the code does NOT filter on type — a documented-intent-vs-implementation divergence that reinforces the finding. So a NUMERIC question resolving to a non-{0,1} value is stored as a bogus Brier score, fed into classify_error (resolve.py:48 CALIBRATION_FAILURE threshold), and silently averaged into global/per-domain/per-platform calibration numbers. This silently corrupts the system's central output with no error raised. One factual correction to the evidence: Metaculus's API `resolution` field for continuous questions is typically a normalized/scaled value (commonly near [0,1]), not the raw 50000, so the cited ~2.5e9 magnitude overstates the realistic case — but the defect holds regardless, since any non-binary outcome poisons Brier/log scores and compute_brier_decomposition (which assumes outcomes in [0,1]).

### `BIZ:-116` — Extremization is dead under default config — single provider always yields n_distinct_providers == 1
- **confirmed ✓** · source: biz-logic · effort: medium · file: `/Users/bjgilbert/Projects/Zoltar/sigma-predict/src/pipeline/aggregator.py:90-94`
- **evidence:** aggregate_runs applies `_extremize` only when `n_distinct_providers >= 2` (lines 91, 102). The orchestrator computes n_distinct_providers from `set(r.provider for r in valid_runs)` (orchestrator.py:274). But run_forecast always stamps every run with the single `config.primary_model.provider` (forecaster.py:111, prov=provider or config.primary_model.provider), and the orchestrator's run loop (orchestrator.py:207-228) varies only persona, never provider/model. So all runs in a default invocation share one provider, n_distinct_providers is always 1, and the configured extremization_factor=1.5 (config.py:33) is never applied. The 'extremized_trimmed_mean' method silently degrades to a plain trimmed mean. The method name and the config knob both advertise a transform that the default execution path can never trigger.
- **recommendation:** Either vary provider/model across runs in the orchestrator loop (true multi-model ensemble) so the gate can fire, or gate extremization on a more meaningful independence signal (e.g. effective_n or distinct personas) and rename the method honestly. At minimum surface a warning when extremized_trimmed_mean is requested but n_distinct_providers<2 so the user knows extremization was skipped.
- **verifier:** Confirmed at code level. The gate and the dead config knob both check out:

1. aggregator.py:90-94 — extremization in "extremized_trimmed_mean" only runs when n_distinct_providers >= 2; otherwise raw_aggregate = trimmed_mean (plain trimmed mean). The fallback branch at lines 102-105 behaves identically. So the transform the method name advertises is gated on multi-provider.

2. orchestrator.py:274-275 — n_distinct_providers = max(1, len(set(r.provider for r in valid_runs ...))). It is derived purely from the provider field stamped on each run.

3. forecaster.py:110-111, 230 — run_forecast sets prov = provider or config.primary_model.provider and stamps provider=prov on the RunResult. The single caller is orchestrator.py:219, which passes NO provider/model_id argument, so prov always resolves to the single config.primary_model.provider.

4. orchestrator.py:207-228 — the run loop iterates personas[:num_runs] and varies only the search persona. Nothing in the loop varies provider or model. Verified run_forecast has exactly one caller (grep: only forecaster.py def + orchestrator.py:219). deliberation.py also calls only config.primary_model.provider (lines 128-129) and returns the same runs without adding differently-provisioned runs.

5. config.py:31-33 — default method="extremized_trimmed_mean", extremization_factor=1.5. Both default and the auto-detected primary_model resolve to a single provider.

Net: in every default invocation, all runs share one provider, n_distinct_providers is always 1, and the _extremize call (and thus extremization_factor=1.5) is never reached. The method silently degrades to a plain trimmed mean. test_aggregator.py:78 (test_single_provider_no_extremization) confirms this is the as-coded behavior. No warning is surfaced when extremization is skipped — verified extremization_factor is referenced nowhere except aggregator internals and the config default; nothing logs/warns on the skip.

This is a genuine intent-vs-implementation gap: the config knob and the method name advertise a transform the default execution path cannot trigger, with no user-facing signal. To actually get extremization, a caller must supply num_runs with multiple distinct providers via run_forecast's provider/model_id params, which the orchestrator never does. The finding's evidence chain (every cited line) is accurate.

### `BIZ:-117` — Deliberation can never fire under default config (num_runs=1) and the configured threshold is ignored
- **confirmed ✓** · source: biz-logic · effort: small · file: `/Users/bjgilbert/Projects/Zoltar/sigma-predict/src/pipeline/orchestrator.py:319-324`
- **evidence:** Deliberation is gated by `should_deliberate(...) and num_runs > 1` (orchestrator.py:324). predict_until_review defaults num_runs=1 (orchestrator.py:74), and the single-run aggregation path hard-codes inter_model_agreement=1.0 and stdev=0.0 (aggregator.py:64-72), so even without the num_runs guard the disagreement condition could not trip on a single run. Separately, should_deliberate uses module-level constants _INTER_MODEL_AGREEMENT_THRESHOLD=0.6 and _PRE_MORTEM_DELTA_THRESHOLD=0.1 (deliberation.py:22-23) and never reads config.aggregation.deliberation_stdev_threshold (config.py:34, value 0.15) — that config option is referenced nowhere in the codebase and is purely decorative. Also the trigger docstring says 'inter_model_agreement < threshold' but the orchestrator logs 'Deliberation triggered (stdev=%.3f)' (line 325), conflating two different metrics.
- **recommendation:** Make deliberation reachable for the common single-model multi-run case (it currently requires >1 run but the agreement metric only varies with multiple runs anyway, so confirm the path is exercised), and either wire deliberation_stdev_threshold into should_deliberate or delete it. Reconcile the log message with the actual trigger metric.
- **verifier:** Verified all cited claims against the code.

(1) orchestrator.py:324 gates deliberation with `should_deliberate(...) and num_runs > 1` — CONFIRMED.
(2) Default num_runs=1 at orchestrator.py:74 — CONFIRMED. All three real callers default to 1: scripts/predict.py:50 (default=1), scripts/batch_predict.py:32 (default=1), pages/2_Predict.py:195 (value=1). So under default config deliberation never fires.
(3) aggregator.py:64-72 hard-codes inter_model_agreement=1.0, stdev=0.0 for n==1 — CONFIRMED. With agreement 1.0 the agreement-based condition (threshold 0.6) cannot trip on a single run.
(4) deliberation_stdev_threshold (config.py:34, value 0.15) is referenced NOWHERE else — CONFIRMED via grep (only the definition line matches). should_deliberate (deliberation.py:26-62) uses module-level constants _INTER_MODEL_AGREEMENT_THRESHOLD=0.6 and _PRE_MORTEM_DELTA_THRESHOLD=0.1 and never reads config.aggregation.deliberation_stdev_threshold. The config option is purely decorative dead config.
(5) Log/metric conflation — CONFIRMED. Trigger conditions are inter_model_agreement, challenge vulnerability, and pre_mortem_delta; none is stdev. Yet orchestrator.py:325 logs "Deliberation triggered (stdev=%.3f)" and deliberation.py:89 repeats the same misleading stdev log. stdev is hard-coded to 0.0 on single runs and is not a trigger input.

One imprecision in the finding (does not change the verdict): the finding implies the agreement metric is the only thing that could trip the condition and that it "only varies with multiple runs anyway." In fact two of the three should_deliberate conditions are run-count-independent — pre_mortem_delta is computed per run (orchestrator.py:316-318) and challenge vulnerability == 'high' — so should_deliberate CAN return True on a single run. The reason deliberation is unreachable under default config is the explicit `and num_runs > 1` guard, not the agreement metric alone. The recommendation already hedges this ("confirm the path is exercised").

Net: the intent-vs-implementation gap is real and material. Default config makes deliberation unreachable, a documented/configured threshold is silently ignored (dead config), and the log message reports a metric that is neither the trigger nor meaningful (0.0) on single runs. All three sub-claims are code-grounded.

### `BIZ:-128` — tournament_id, tags, and search parameters are accepted but silently ignored by Kalshi and Polymarket clients
