- **confirmed ✓** · source: biz-logic · effort: medium · file: `src/platforms/kalshi.py:103-119, src/platforms/polymarket.py:82-94`
- **evidence:** Both get_questions signatures accept tournament_id, tags, search (matching the base.py:24-31 abstract contract whose docstring promises 'Optional tournament/event filter', 'Optional tag filter', 'Optional search query string'). In Kalshi the params dict only contains limit and cursor (kalshi.py:116-118); tournament_id/tags/search are never read. In Polymarket the params dict only contains limit and offset (polymarket.py:91-94); the three filter params are never read. A caller passing tags=['politics'] or search='election' gets the unfiltered first page, silently. Metaculus, by contrast, does apply them (metaculus.py:98-103), so the divergence is platform-specific and easy to miss.
- **recommendation:** Either wire the params into each platform's query (Polymarket gamma/CLOB filtering, Kalshi series/category filters) or raise NotImplementedError / log a warning when an unsupported filter is supplied, so callers are not silently given unfiltered results.
- **verifier:** Confirmed against the actual code. The abstract contract in src/platforms/base.py:23-44 declares get_questions with tournament_id, tags, and search params, documented as "Optional tournament/event filter", "Optional tag filter", and "Optional search query string". KalshiClient.get_questions (kalshi.py:103-119) accepts all three params in its signature but builds a params dict containing only limit and cursor; tournament_id, tags, and search are never referenced anywhere in the method body. PolymarketClient.get_questions (polymarket.py:82-94) likewise accepts all three but builds a params dict of only limit and offset, never reading the filter params. By contrast, MetaculusClient.get_questions (metaculus.py:98-103) does wire them in: tournament_id maps to a query param, search maps to "search", and tags fall back to a joined "search" string. So the divergence is genuine and platform-specific exactly as claimed.

Materiality is concrete, not hypothetical. Real callers pass these params: scripts/batch_predict.py:62-66 forwards tournament_id and search; scripts/predict.py:102 calls get_questions(limit=10, search=search) after echoing "Searching {platform} for: {search}"; pages/2_Predict.py:119 calls get_questions(limit=20, search=search_query.strip()) from a Streamlit search box. For Kalshi and Polymarket these calls advertise a search to the user, then silently return the unfiltered first page of events/markets. There is no NotImplementedError, warning log, or doc caveat — silent acceptance, which is the worst failure mode for a filter param. The clients are docstring-labeled "read-only stub", which mildly signals incompleteness, but the get_questions docstrings themselves ("Fetch markets from Kalshi", "Fetch a page of markets from Polymarket") make no mention that the filter arguments are ignored, and they inherit the base contract that promises filtering. The stub framing applies to submission, not to listing/search.

Could not refute. The code clearly claims to support these filters (via inherited signature + base docstring) and clearly does not apply them. Medium severity is appropriate: it does not crash or corrupt data, but it silently returns wrong/unfiltered results to a user who explicitly requested a search — a correctness-of-results gap, not merely a missing feature, because the user-facing text claims a search is happening.

### `BIZ:-129` — Polymarket and Kalshi pagination via offset/cursor is broken — offset is not how these APIs paginate
- **confirmed ✓** · source: biz-logic · effort: medium · file: `src/platforms/polymarket.py:91-100, src/platforms/kalshi.py:116-130`
- **evidence:** Polymarket get_questions sends `params={'limit': limit, 'offset': offset}` to the CLOB `/markets` endpoint, but CLOB /markets is cursor-paginated (returns next_cursor, accepts ?next_cursor=), not offset-paginated; the offset param is ignored by the API so offset>0 still returns page 1, then the code re-slices markets[:limit]. Kalshi maps `cursor = str(offset) if offset else ''` (kalshi.py:118) — but Kalshi cursors are opaque server-issued tokens, not stringified integers, so any offset>0 produces an invalid cursor. In both cases the offset parameter is read but produces wrong/no pagination.
- **recommendation:** Adopt each API's real pagination: thread Kalshi's returned `cursor` and Polymarket's `next_cursor` back through the client (e.g. return the cursor or accept an opaque cursor param), and stop treating offset as a universal integer offset for non-offset APIs.
- **verifier:** Confirmed via code + official API docs. CODE CLAIMS: both clients expose get_questions(offset: int = 0) implementing the abstract PlatformClient pagination contract. CODE DOES: Polymarket (polymarket.py:91-100) sends params={'limit': limit, 'offset': offset} to /markets; Kalshi (kalshi.py:116-122) sets cursor = str(offset) if offset else '' and deletes the key when empty.

API REALITY (verified against vendor docs): Polymarket's CLOB /markets uses keyset/cursor pagination via next_cursor->after_cursor (docs.polymarket.com/api-reference/markets/list-markets-keyset-pagination), with NO offset parameter. An unrecognized offset is ignored, so offset>0 returns page 1 again; the code then re-slices markets[:limit], yielding the same first page. The code also never reads next_cursor from the response, so it cannot advance pages at all. Kalshi (docs.kalshi.com/getting_started/pagination) confirms cursor is an opaque server-issued token threaded from the prior response, not a stringified integer; passing str(offset) like '20' is an invalid cursor token.

DIVERGENCE IS REAL AND SCOPED: For offset=0 both work (Kalshi deletes empty cursor; Polymarket's offset=0 is harmless), so page 1 is fine. Breakage manifests only for offset>0 — exactly the case the offset parameter exists to serve. Polymarket silently returns duplicate page-1 results; Kalshi likely returns an HTTP error swallowed to [] by the broad except Exception handler (silent). The recommendation (thread real cursors/next_cursor instead of treating offset as a universal integer offset) is the correct fix.

Could not refute: every load-bearing claim in the finding is corroborated by the source code and the platforms' official pagination documentation.

### `BIZ:-130` — Price `or`-chain treats a zero price as missing, falling through to stale bid/ask
- **confirmed ✓** · source: biz-logic · effort: small · file: `src/platforms/kalshi.py:25, src/platforms/kalshi.py:171`
- **evidence:** `yes_price = data.get('last_price') or data.get('yes_ask') or data.get('yes_bid')`. Python `or` treats 0 as falsy, so a genuine last_price of 0 (0 cents = ~0.0 probability, a near-certain NO market) is discarded and the code falls through to yes_ask/yes_bid, silently substituting a different, non-zero quote for the actual last trade price. Same pattern at line 171 in get_community_prediction. This biases the recorded community prediction away from extreme-NO markets.
- **recommendation:** Use explicit None checks: `for key in ('last_price','yes_ask','yes_bid'): v = data.get(key); if v is not None: yes_price = v; break`.
- **verifier:** Confirmed verbatim at both cited locations. src/platforms/kalshi.py:25 `yes_price = data.get("last_price") or data.get("yes_ask") or data.get("yes_bid")` and :171 `yes_price = market.get("last_price") or market.get("yes_ask") or market.get("yes_bid")`. The mechanism is correct: Kalshi prices arrive as JSON numbers, so a genuine last_price of 0 (int) is falsy and the `or` chain short-circuits past it to yes_ask/yes_bid, silently substituting a different quote.

The intent/implementation gap is real and visible WITHIN the same function: lines 26-30 (and 172-174) guard with `if yes_price is not None:` and the conversion `val / 100.0 if val > 1.0 else val` cleanly handles a value of 0, returning community_prediction = 0.0. So the downstream code is explicitly designed to treat 0 as a valid price, but the upstream `or` chain treats 0 as missing — a direct contradiction between the None-guard's intent and the or-chain's behavior. The docstring/comment intent ("Community prediction from last/yes price", "Fetch current market yes-price") expects last_price to take priority when present; the or-chain breaks that priority for the 0 case. The recommended explicit `is not None` fix is correct and idiomatic.

The bias direction described is accurate: it corrupts extreme-NO markets (true prob ~0) upward toward the substituted ask/bid, which is the calibration-sensitive tail for a forecasting benchmark tool.

Materiality caveat: a *last trade* printing at exactly 0 cents is low-frequency in real Kalshi data (active markets quote 1-99 cents), and the more common one-sided-book case still yields a near-correct extreme value via fallthrough. Per-occurrence error is bounded to a few cents. So the impact is real but low-incidence and small-magnitude.

### `BIZ:-132` — Metaculus community-prediction extraction is hardwired to a single legacy schema path and silently returns None on any divergence
- **confirmed ✓** · source: biz-logic · effort: medium · file: `src/platforms/metaculus.py:33-39`
- **evidence:** `_extract_community_prediction` reads exactly `data['community_prediction']['full']['q2']` and swallows KeyError/TypeError/ValueError into None. The configured BASE_URL is `https://www.metaculus.com/api2` (metaculus.py:16). Current Metaculus serves community aggregates under a different shape (e.g. question.aggregations.recency_weighted.latest.centers / forecast_values), not a top-level community_prediction.full.q2. If the live schema does not match this exact path, every Metaculus community prediction silently becomes None with no log — indistinguishable from a genuinely missing prediction. The only test (test_platform_clients.py:26) feeds a hand-built dict that matches the hardcoded path, so the test cannot catch a schema mismatch against the real API.
- **recommendation:** Verify the path against the live api2 response; add a fallback chain over the known aggregate locations and log at debug when none match so a schema break is observable rather than silent. Consider an integration test against a captured real payload.
- **verifier:** CONFIRMED. Every claim in the finding is verified against the actual code and the authoritative live-API schema. |source: src/platforms/metaculus.py:33-39|

WHAT THE CODE DOES (verified, src/platforms/metaculus.py:36): `_extract_community_prediction` reads exactly `float(data["community_prediction"]["full"]["q2"])` inside a `try` whose `except (KeyError, TypeError, ValueError): return None` swallows any structural mismatch into None with NO log statement. This is the only extraction path; grep confirms no fallback to `aggregations`, `recency_weighted`, `centers`, or `forecast_values` anywhere in the file.

WHAT THE LIVE API ACTUALLY SERVES (verified): Metaculus's own maintained client library `forecasting-tools` (github.com/Metaculus/forecasting-tools) parses the community prediction as `api_json["question"]["aggregations"]["recency_weighted"]["latest"]["centers"][0]`, with a documented fallback to `["aggregations"]["unweighted"]["latest"]["centers"]`. The top-level `community_prediction.full.q2` shape is the legacy pre-rewrite schema and is not what the current api2 returns. The modern shape additionally wraps the question under a "question"/post envelope, so even the top-level key the code indexes (`data["community_prediction"]`) is absent. |source: github.com/Metaculus/forecasting-tools data_models/questions.py| HIGH confidence.

CONSEQUENCE (verified): Because httpx receives a 200 with a structurally different body, `resp.raise_for_status()` passes, `_extract_community_prediction` hits KeyError on `data["community_prediction"]`, and silently returns None. There is no debug/warning log on this path (unlike the surrounding HTTP-error handlers which DO log). Every Metaculus community prediction becomes None and is indistinguishable from a genuinely-absent prediction. This propagates into `_map_question` (line 54) and the public `get_community_prediction` (line 189). For a prediction-aggregation system, silently zeroing out the entire Metaculus community signal is material — it degrades the product's core function with no observability.

TEST GAP (verified, tests/test_platform_clients.py:26): The only positive test hand-builds `{"community_prediction": {"full": {"q2": 0.65}}}` — the exact hardcoded path — so it asserts the parser against a fixture matching the code rather than against a real api2 payload. The negative tests (lines 49-51) only probe missing-key cases, which the schema mismatch would also produce, so they cannot distinguish "schema broke" from "no prediction." The test suite is structurally incapable of catching the divergence.

SEVERITY: The 'silent' characterization is precise — confirmed no log on the mismatch path. The only thing I could not directly fetch (403/truncation) was the live JSON itself, but the Metaculus-authored client library is a stronger authority for the current schema than a raw fetch would be. The intent-vs-implementation gap (docstring says 'Extract the median community prediction'; implementation extracts nothing from the real API and reports a false 'no prediction') is real and material.

### `BIZ:-134` — Metaculus submit_prediction payload `{'prediction': probability}` likely does not match the api2 predict contract and the gate only checks key presence
- **confirmed ✓** · source: biz-logic · effort: medium · file: `src/platforms/metaculus.py:131-154`
- **evidence:** submit_prediction POSTs `{'prediction': probability}` to `/questions/{id}/predict/`. The only guard is `if not self._config.metaculus_api_key: return False` (line 133-135) — it verifies a key is configured but does not validate that probability is in [0,1] or that the question is binary (the base.py:62 docstring says probability 0.0-1.0 but nothing enforces it; a numeric/MC question would be submitted with a single float). Metaculus api2 predictions are also commonly nested differently per question type. Combined with auth sent as `Token {key}` (line 69), a wrong token scheme or payload shape returns a 4xx that is caught and logged as a warning, returning False — so a misconfigured submission is indistinguishable from a transient failure to the orchestrator (orchestrator.py:433-441 just logs 'submission failed').
- **recommendation:** Validate probability range and question_type before POST, confirm the api2 payload/auth shape against current docs, and distinguish auth/validation (4xx) failures from transient errors in the return signal so the orchestrator does not treat a permanent rejection as retryable.
- **verifier:** Partially real. I read src/platforms/metaculus.py:131-154, base.py:61-72, and the actual orchestrator caller (src/pipeline/orchestrator.py:430-441 — note the finding cited orchestrator.py:433-441 but the file is at src/pipeline/orchestrator.py).

What is CONFIRMED true (verifiable from code):
- No input validation: submit_prediction's only guard is the API-key presence check (lines 133-135). There is no probability range check and no binary-question-type check, while base.py:62-67 documents the contract as "probability 0.0-1.0." That is a genuine intent-vs-implementation gap in the function itself.
- Auth is sent as `Token {key}` (line 69), confirmed.
- 4xx (HTTPStatusError, 146-151) and transient/other errors (152-154) both return False, and the caller (orchestrator.py:440-441) treats False uniformly as "Prediction submission failed." So permanent vs transient failures are indistinguishable in the return signal — confirmed.

What is OVERSTATED or UNVERIFIED:
- The core "payload likely does not match the api2 predict contract" claim is hedged speculation ("likely", "commonly nested differently") about an external API. It is not confirmed by the code and the finding does not cite the current Metaculus docs. The docstring says "Submit a BINARY prediction," and posting a single float {'prediction': p} is plausibly correct for a binary question. So the headline payload-mismatch claim is unconfirmed, not demonstrated. No tests assert the wire payload either.
- The range-validation risk is largely neutralized in the real pipeline: the orchestrator clamps final_prob to [0.01, 0.99] (line 423) before calling submit_prediction, so an out-of-range float cannot reach the POST via the normal path. This weakens the materiality of the missing range guard.
- The "orchestrator treats a permanent rejection as retryable" consequence is not actually realized today: there is NO retry logic in the orchestrator — on False it just logs a warning and continues. So the conflation is a latent code-quality issue, not a current functional defect.

Net: the missing validation and the indistinguishable-failure return signal are real, code-grounded gaps between the documented contract and the implementation. But the finding's central and most alarming claim (payload/auth shape is wrong against api2) is unverified speculation, and its stated downstream consequence (retryable rejection) does not match the current orchestrator behavior.

### `BIZ:-143` — Extremization silently disabled under the default single-model configuration
- **confirmed ✓** · source: biz-logic · effort: small · file: `src/pipeline/aggregator.py:90-105, src/pipeline/orchestrator.py:274-281`
- **evidence:** aggregate_runs applies _extremize only `if n_distinct_providers >= 2`, else `raw_aggregate = trimmed_mean` (unchanged). Orchestrator computes `n_providers = len(set(r.provider ...))`. Config.__post_init__ auto-populates models from available keys, but the forecaster loop runs only `self.config.primary_model` (orchestrator.py:209-211 logs primary_model.model_id for every run), so all runs share one provider. With a single API key (the documented minimum viable config) n_distinct_providers is always 1 and extremization_factor=1.5 is never applied. The default method name 'extremized_trimmed_mean' and the config knob advertise a transform that is inert in the common deployment. Additionally _extremize early-returns p unchanged for p<=0.01 or p>=0.99, and the aggregate is clamped to [0.01,0.99] afterward.
- **recommendation:** Document that extremization requires >=2 distinct providers, and surface a warning when method=extremized_trimmed_mean but n_distinct_providers<2 so operators know the transform was skipped. Confirm the forecaster is actually meant to use only primary_model (the multi-model `models` list appears collected but unused in the run loop).
- **verifier:** Every factual claim in the finding is confirmed against the code.

CONFIRMED FACTS:
- aggregator.py:90-105 — `_extremize` runs only `if n_distinct_providers >= 2`; otherwise `raw_aggregate = trimmed_mean` is passed through unchanged. The same gate guards the fallback `else` branch (lines 102-105).
- orchestrator.py:274-275 — `n_providers = len(set(r.provider for r in valid_runs ...))`, then `n_distinct_providers = max(1, n_providers)`.
- The ONLY caller of `run_forecast` is orchestrator.py:219-228, and it passes neither `model_id` nor `provider`. In forecaster.py:110-111 these default to `config.primary_model.model_id` / `config.primary_model.provider`, and RunResult.provider is set to that single value (forecaster.py:230). Deliberation (deliberation.py:128-129) also uses only `primary_model`. Therefore every run shares one provider and `n_distinct_providers` is always 1 in the single-key case.
- config.py:31,33 — defaults are `method = "extremized_trimmed_mean"` and `extremization_factor = 1.5`.
- config.py:151-152 — the documented minimum-viable config is exactly one of three LLM keys ("at least one"). With one key, `config.models` holds one entry and primary_model is that provider, so extremization is provably never applied in the minimum-viable deployment.
- No warning anywhere in src/ surfaces that extremization was skipped (grep confirmed). The `models` list and the `model_id`/`provider` params of run_forecast are wired but never driven by any orchestrator path — multi-model ensemble is dead in the default run loop.
- `_extremize` early-returns for p<=0.01/p>=0.99 and the aggregate is clamped to [0.01,0.99] (aggregator.py:138-139, 108), as stated.

REFUTATION ATTEMPTED AND REJECTED: The strongest counter is that the >=2-provider gate is documented in the aggregator docstring (lines 52-53) and is statistically defensible (extremizing N correlated single-model runs would be overconfident). That defeats only a function-level reading. The finding's actual claim is a config/operator-level intent-vs-implementation gap: the default configured method NAME ("extremized_trimmed_mean"), the module docstring (line 3, "Computes ... extremized mean"), and the user-facing `extremization_factor=1.5` knob all advertise a transform that is provably inert in the documented common deployment, with zero operator-facing signal. The function docstring does not reach the operator who only sees config defaults. The gap survives.

MATERIALITY: Medium is appropriate. It does not crash or corrupt data, and the gate may even be the statistically correct behavior — but a configured, named, tunable transform that silently no-ops in the documented minimum-viable config, plus an entirely unused multi-model ensemble path, is a genuine and material intent/visibility divergence. The recommendation (document the >=2-provider requirement, warn when method=extremized_trimmed_mean and n_distinct_providers<2, and confirm whether single-model running is intended) is well-targeted.

### `BIZ:-144` — Multi-model `models` list is populated but never used by the forecaster run loop
- **confirmed ✓** · source: biz-logic · effort: medium · file: `src/config.py:91-97, src/pipeline/orchestrator.py:207-228`
- **evidence:** Config.__post_init__ builds `self.models` (one ModelConfig per available API key) with the comment 'Available models for multi-model runs (Phase 1)'. But the orchestrator forecaster loop iterates over personas only and run_forecast is called without any per-run model selection; the log line uses self.config.primary_model.model_id for every run. grep shows config.models is not consumed anywhere in the pipeline. Multi-model runs (and therefore the >=2-provider extremization path and meaningful inter-model agreement) are unreachable through the standard predict() entry points.
- **recommendation:** Either wire config.models into the run loop (assign run i to models[i % len(models)]) so multi-provider runs and extremization actually occur, or remove the models list and the 'multi-model' framing to avoid implying a capability that is not exercised.
- **verifier:** Confirmed every claim against the code. config.py:69-97 builds self.models (one ModelConfig per available API key) labeled "Available models for multi-model runs (Phase 1)". A whole-repo grep shows config.models is never read anywhere except its own construction — no models[i % len] assignment, no iteration. The sole run_forecast call site (orchestrator.py:219, confirmed unique across the entire repo) loops over personas[:num_runs] only and passes config/router but NO model_id or provider. In forecaster.py:110-111, model/provider fall back to config.primary_model when the caller omits them (which it always does), and the log at orchestrator.py:210 hardcodes primary_model.model_id. Consequently orchestrator.py:274 computes n_providers from set(r.provider for ...) which is always {primary_model.provider}, size 1, so n_distinct_providers=max(1,1)=1. aggregator.py:90-94 gates extremization on n_distinct_providers>=2, so with a single provider raw_aggregate=trimmed_mean and the extremization branch is strictly unreachable through predict()/predict_until_review() (the only entry points). run_forecast's optional model_id/provider params (forecaster.py:97-98) are dead — clearly built for per-run model selection that was never wired. The gap is real and material. One nuance: inter_model_agreement (aggregator.py:112) is NOT provider-gated and still varies across persona runs, so it is not fully dead, but it measures inter-persona not inter-model dispersion, so the finding's framing is fair; the strictly-unreachable part is the >=2-provider extremization path.

### `BIZ:-145` — effective_n heuristic uses an arbitrary undocumented constant and can never reach n for typical spreads
- **confirmed ✓** · source: biz-logic · effort: medium · file: `src/pipeline/aggregator.py:153-180`
- **evidence:** _estimate_effective_n claims to estimate 'effective number of independent estimates' via a 'variance ratio method'. It uses `expected_var_single = mean_p*(1-mean_p)*0.1` — a magic 0.1 multiplier flagged in-code only as 'rough estimate' — then `ratio = actual_var/expected_var_single` and `effective_n = round(n * min(1.0, ratio*n))`. The math does not correspond to any standard effective-sample-size formula (e.g. Kish). The unused local `spread = np.ptp(...)` is computed and discarded. The low-effective-N review flag (_generate_flags: effective_n<3 and len(runs)>=3) depends entirely on this ad hoc number, so the 'runs may be correlated' safeguard fires on an arbitrary basis rather than measured correlation.
- **recommendation:** Replace with a defensible ESS estimate (e.g. based on pairwise source overlap / correlation, which the codebase already partly computes in source_cluster_check) or downgrade effective_n to a documented heuristic and remove the dependent correlation flag. Remove the dead `spread` computation.
- **verifier:** The finding is PARTIALLY real but its headline claim is wrong, so it must be downgraded.

CONFIRMED sub-claims (verified against code at src/pipeline/aggregator.py:153-180):
- The 0.1 multiplier is a magic constant flagged in-code only as "rough estimate" (line 171). TRUE.
- The formula `effective_n = round(n * min(1.0, ratio*n))` where `ratio = actual_var / (mean_p*(1-mean_p)*0.1)` corresponds to no standard effective-sample-size estimator (Kish ESS = (Σw)²/Σw², or autocorrelation-based ESS). TRUE — it is ad hoc.
- `spread = float(np.ptp(probabilities))` (line 166) is computed and never used. TRUE dead code.
- The low-effective-N review flag depends entirely on this number: human_review.py:348-349 fires "Low effective N — runs may be correlated" when `effective_n < 3 and len(runs) >= 3`. TRUE (note: the finding's Location field places _generate_flags at aggregator.py:153-180, but it actually lives in src/review/human_review.py:312/348 — a location error, though the cross-module dependency claim itself is correct).

REFUTED — the load-bearing headline claim is FALSE: "can never reach n for typical spreads." I tested the function directly. Because of the `ratio*n` amplification term, effective_n SATURATES to n very easily: for runs spread with halfwidth >= ~0.1 around the mean (e.g. [0.40,0.50,0.60,0.45,0.55]) it returns the full n=5. It is the OPPOSITE of the title's mechanism — the metric almost always equals n for any non-trivial spread, and only collapses toward 1 when runs are extremely tightly clustered (e.g. [0.49,0.50,0.51] -> 1). So the flag fires precisely when runs show near-identical probabilities (high agreement / plausible correlation) and stays quiet otherwise. That direction is qualitatively defensible, not "arbitrary." The claim that the safeguard "fires on an arbitrary basis rather than measured correlation" overstates: it fires on measured low variance, which is a (crude) proxy for correlation, not a random number.

NET: The genuine, material issues are real — undocumented magic constant, non-standard ESS math, and dead `spread` code. These are legitimate code-quality / documentation gaps worth the recommendation (document the heuristic or replace with a defensible ESS; remove dead code). But the finding's central framing — that the heuristic is broken because it "can never reach n" and fires "arbitrarily" — is factually incorrect and is the reason it was rated medium. With the broken-math premise removed, what remains is a documentation/dead-code/crude-heuristic issue, not a functional defect that produces wrong flags.

### `BIZ:-148` — cross_platform_divergence and anomaly detection model fields are declared as controls but never populated by the pipeline
- **confirmed ✓** · source: biz-logic · effort: medium · file: `src/models.py:132-136, src/pipeline/orchestrator.py:536-555, src/review/human_review.py:153,352`
- **evidence:** PlatformSignals declares cross_platform_divergence, anomaly_detected (default False), and anomaly_details — and human_review surfaces a 'Platform behavioral anomaly detected' flag plus an 'ANOMALY DETECTED' panel gated on ps.anomaly_detected. But _collect_platform_signals only ever fetches a single community prediction and returns PlatformSignals with anomaly_detected left at its default False and cross_platform_divergence at None. grep confirms no code path sets anomaly_detected=True or computes cross_platform_divergence. The anomaly safeguard and the cross-platform divergence signal are inert — the flag is structurally unreachable.
- **recommendation:** Either implement anomaly detection / cross-platform divergence in _collect_platform_signals (fetch multiple platforms, compute divergence, set anomaly_detected on threshold) or remove the unpopulated fields and their dependent review flags so the human reviewer is not given a false sense that anomaly screening occurs.
- **verifier:** CONFIRMED. Every code-grounded claim in the finding is accurate, verified by reading the cited lines and exhaustive grep.

1. src/models.py:132-136 — PlatformSignals declares cross_platform_divergence (default None), anomaly_detected (default False), and anomaly_details (default None), exactly as cited.

2. src/pipeline/orchestrator.py:536-555 — _collect_platform_signals is docstring'd "Collect current platform prices for comparison" and ONLY does that: it fetches a single community prediction via get_community_prediction and writes it into signals.prices_at_prediction_time. It never touches anomaly_detected, cross_platform_divergence, or anomaly_details, which therefore keep their model defaults (False / None / None). Note: line 548 even has dead logic — it checks `if platform_key in signals.prices_at_prediction_time` against a dict that was just default-constructed empty, so the if-branch can never be true, but that is a separate minor issue.

3. Exhaustive grep across src/ confirms there is NO code path anywhere that assigns anomaly_detected=True or computes cross_platform_divergence. The only references are the declaration (models.py) and two read-only consumers in human_review.py. PlatformSignals is constructed in exactly one place in the pipeline (orchestrator.py:542), and that one construction populates nothing but prices.

4. src/review/human_review.py:153-154 prints a "[bold red]ANOMALY DETECTED[/bold red]" panel gated on `if ps.anomaly_detected`, and lines 352-353 append a "Platform behavioral anomaly detected" reviewer flag gated on the same field. Both branches are dead code — structurally unreachable given no producer ever sets the flag True.

This is a genuine intent-vs-implementation gap. The data model and the human-review UI both advertise an anomaly-screening / cross-platform-divergence safeguard that the pipeline never actually performs. The reviewer is implicitly assured that platform behavioral anomaly screening occurs (the flag's absence reads as "no anomaly found") when in fact no screening logic exists. That is materially misleading for a human-in-the-loop review surface.

Could not refute on any point. Default-to-false does not apply because the divergence between declared/displayed intent and actual behavior is fully confirmed.

### `BIZ:-156` — Adjustment Brier-delta scatter recomputes pre-review Brier from the calibrated prob, but resolution.brier_score is computed from the final (post-adjustment) submitted prob — the chart's baseline is internally inconsistent
- **confirmed ✓** · source: biz-logic · effort: small · file: `/Users/bjgilbert/Projects/Zoltar/sigma-predict/components/charts.py:262-272`
- **evidence:** adjustment_scatter computes pre_brier = (cal_prob - outcome)**2 and post_brier = (clip(cal_prob+adj) - outcome)**2 to show 'improvement from the human adjustment'. But the actually-submitted probability is final_prob = clip(cal_prob + human_adjustment) (orchestrator.py:421-423), and resolution.brier_score is scored against the submitted prob, not cal_prob. So the 'post_brier' the chart computes is a re-derivation that should equal the stored brier_score but is computed independently from cal_prob+adj, and the chart never references resolution.brier_score at all (it only gates on it being non-None at line 253). The y-axis 'Brier delta (negative = improved)' is a synthetic recomputation, not the real scored improvement. If clipping or any rounding diverged, the chart silently disagrees with the Scoreboard/Registry Brier columns.
- **recommendation:** Use the stored resolution.brier_score as post_brier (it is the authoritative submitted-prob Brier) and only recompute the counterfactual pre-adjustment Brier from cal_prob. This makes the delta consistent with every other Brier surface in the app.
- **verifier:** Verified against the actual code chain.

WHAT THE CODE CLAIMS: components/charts.py:246-247 — adjustment_scatter docstring says "human adjustment vs Brier score delta (post-review improvement)"; y-axis labeled "Brier delta (negative = improved)" (line 294).

WHAT IT DOES: charts.py gates only on resolution.brier_score being non-None (line 254) but never reads its value. It independently recomputes pre_brier=(cal_prob-outcome)**2 (line 269) and post_brier from post_prob=max(0.01,min(0.99,cal_prob+adj)) (lines 270-271), then delta=post_brier-pre_brier (line 272). The finding's evidence is accurate.

AUTHORITATIVE BRIER: I traced the actual scoring. The platform Resolution objects (e.g. metaculus.py:179-182) set only outcome and resolved_at — brier_score is NOT set there. It is computed in scripts/resolve.py:108 as round(brier_score(submitted_p, outcome), 6) where submitted_p = record.submitted.probability (line 105). And record.submitted.probability is set in orchestrator.py:421-423/434-436 to final_prob = max(0.01,min(0.99,cal_prob+human_adjustment)). So the finding's claim that the stored Brier is scored against the submitted prob (= clip(cal_prob+adj)), not cal_prob, is CONFIRMED, and the chart's post_prob is an exact re-derivation of that same value.

DIVERGENCE MATERIALITY: The clip bounds (0.01/0.99) are identical literals in both files, so chart post_prob == submitted_p in the normal path. The ONLY current numerical difference is the round(...,6) applied in resolve.py but not in the chart — i.e. <=5e-7, visually negligible on a scatter. So the finding's hypothetical "if clipping or any rounding diverged" is the only live divergence today, and it is microscopic. The rejected-review path (orchestrator.py:416-419, no submitted set) is excluded from both surfaces (resolve.py:89 skips no-submitted records), so no divergence there either.

VERDICT: The intent-vs-implementation gap is real as stated — the chart re-derives the scored value instead of using the authoritative resolution.brier_score, creating two sources of truth and a duplicated-clip-literal drift hazard against the Scoreboard/Registry. The recommendation (use stored brier_score as post_brier, recompute only the counterfactual pre-adjustment Brier) is correct and sound. However, the present-day numerical impact is only 6-decimal rounding, not a user-visible disagreement, so the finding slightly overstates current materiality. It is a latent maintainability/consistency defect, not an active wrong-number bug.

### `BIZ:-157` — Calibration curve and Brier decomposition use different, inconsistent binning — and the curve's overconfident/underconfident shaded regions are mislabeled triangles
- **confirmed ✓** · source: biz-logic · effort: small · file: `/Users/bjgilbert/Projects/Zoltar/sigma-predict/components/charts.py:28-62; pages/4_Scoreboard.py:107`
- **evidence:** calibration_curve bins with bucket = min(int(pred / bucket_size), n_bins-1) using n_bins=10 (charts.py:28,31). The Scoreboard Brier decomposition bins with b = min(int(p*10), 9) (Scoreboard:107). These happen to coincide at n_bins=10 but the curve takes n_bins as a parameter (default 10) while the decomposition hardcodes 10, so calling calibration_curve with a different n_bins desyncs the two displays. More concretely wrong: the two shaded 'regions' are drawn as fixed triangles with vertices (0,0)-(0.5,0)-(1,1) and (0,0)-(0.5,1)-(1,1) (charts.py:52,59). The overconfident region (predicting high when actual is lower) is the area BELOW the diagonal and the underconfident region is ABOVE it; these triangles do not correspond to those half-planes (e.g. the 'overconfident' triangle includes the point (0.5,0) which is below the diagonal but also slopes up to (1,1) on the diagonal). The shading is decorative and does not actually delineate over/under-confidence.
- **recommendation:** Replace the triangle traces with the two correct half-planes split by the y=x diagonal (fill below diagonal = overconfident, above = underconfident), or remove the misleading shading. Make the decomposition reuse the same n_bins as the curve.
- **verifier:** Both code locations confirmed verbatim. Two sub-claims, with different strength.

CLAIM 2 (the core, "more concretely wrong" part) — VERIFIED as a genuine intent-vs-implementation gap. charts.py:51-62 draws two filled traces explicitly named "Overconfident region" and "Underconfident region" (legend-visible, showlegend=True), so the INTENT is to delineate over/under-confidence zones. On a calibration plot (x=predicted, y=actual), overconfident = below diagonal y<x (triangle (0,0)-(1,0)-(1,1)) and underconfident = above diagonal y>x (triangle (0,0)-(0,1)-(1,1)). The code instead draws triangles (0,0)-(0.5,0)-(1,1) and (0,0)-(0.5,1)-(1,1), which pivot on the midpoints (0.5,0)/(0.5,1). I numerically tested point-in-triangle membership: genuinely overconfident points like (0.9,0.2) and (0.8,0.5), and genuinely underconfident points like (0.1,0.8) and (0.2,0.5), fall in NEITHER colored region. The two triangles only cover a bowtie wedge hugging the diagonal, leaving most of each half-plane unshaded/miscolored. The finding's specific example about (0.5,0) and the hypotenuse running to (1,1) on the diagonal is accurate. So the shading does NOT do what its labels claim — a real gap.

CLAIM 1 (inconsistent binning) — TECHNICALLY TRUE BUT IMMATERIAL TODAY. calibration_curve uses int(pred/(1/n_bins)) with n_bins=10 default; Scoreboard hardcodes int(p*10) capped at 9. At n_bins=10 these are mathematically identical. I grepped all callers: the sole call site (4_Scoreboard.py:86) uses the default n_bins=10. No current code path desyncs them — it is a latent maintainability hazard, not an active bug. The decomposition math (Murphy reliability/resolution/uncertainty) is otherwise correct.

MATERIALITY: The finding is real on the strength of claim 2. Impact is interpretive only — the faint (alpha 0.08) shading mislabels which screen zones mean over/under-confidence, which can mislead a human reading the Scoreboard. It does NOT corrupt any computed metric: the actual calibration line and the Brier decomposition values are computed independently and correctly. This is a visualization-correctness defect on a secondary UI page, not a data/scoring error. Medium is slightly generous given the cosmetic, non-blocking nature and the very faint fill, but the explicitly-labeled-yet-wrong regions are a legitimate correctness bug in user-facing analytics, so medium is defensible.

### `BIZ:-158` — Registry 'active' status is defined as reviewed-but-unresolved, so the auto_submit path (reviewed=False) misclassifies every submitted prediction as 'pending_review' forever
- **confirmed ✓** · source: biz-logic · effort: small · file: `/Users/bjgilbert/Projects/Zoltar/sigma-predict/pages/5_Registry.py:55-61; pages/1_Dashboard.py:62-66`
- **evidence:** _get_status returns 'pending_review' when human_review.reviewed is False and unresolved, else 'active' (Registry:55-61). The threaded pipeline submits with HumanReview(reviewed=False) (orchestrator.py:493). If the user never completes the inline review (or on the current broken flow), records persist with reviewed=False and show as 'pending_review' indefinitely even though they were already submitted to the platform. The Dashboard 'Pending review' metric (Dashboard:62-66) counts the same condition, so it inflates with already-submitted predictions. The status label diverges from reality: 'pending_review' actually means 'submitted but the human checkbox was never set'.
- **recommendation:** Tie status to submission/review state explicitly: a record with record.submitted set is 'submitted/active' regardless of the reviewed flag, and 'pending_review' should mean predicted-but-not-yet-submitted. After fixing finding 1, reviewed will be set correctly by submit_after_review.
- **verifier:** CONFIRMED — code-grounded, VERIFIED.

The intent-vs-implementation gap is real. Verified at every cited location:

1. Status definition (Registry:55-61): `_get_status` returns "pending_review" when `resolution is None` AND `human_review.reviewed is False`, else "active". It ignores `record.submitted` entirely. So "active" effectively means "reviewed-but-unresolved" and "pending_review" means "reviewed flag not set" — exactly as the finding claims. The STATUS_OPTIONS list (Registry:38) only offers active/resolved/pending_review, with no "submitted" concept.

2. Dashboard metric (Dashboard:62-66): the `pending_review` list uses the identical condition (`not reviewed and resolution is None`), so it counts already-submitted predictions as pending.

3. The auto_submit path produces reviewed=False records WITH submitted set: orchestrator.py:493 constructs `HumanReview(reviewed=False)` in the auto_submit branch of `predict()` (called with auto_submit=True). That review is assigned to `record.human_review` (414), then `submit_after_review` proceeds to set `record.submitted = Submission(...)` (434/444) and persist via `self.store.save(record)` (451). Result: a persisted record with submitted populated AND reviewed=False.

4. The UI actually exercises this path: pages/2_Predict.py:72-77 calls `orchestrator.predict(..., auto_submit=True)` from the threaded `_run_pipeline`. So every UI-initiated prediction is submitted with reviewed=False.

5. Nothing reconciles it automatically. The ONLY writes of reviewed=True are: the CLI present_for_review (src/review/human_review.py) and the manual UI "Confirm Review" button (pages/2_Predict.py:349-371). The latter is an optional, separate user action — and pages/2_Predict.py:373 carries a TODO confirming the submit_after_review wiring is incomplete ("update the record in registry directly"). So if the user does not click Confirm Review, the submitted record shows pending_review indefinitely, and the Dashboard count inflates.

The HumanReview default is also reviewed=False (models.py:140), so the field is a poor proxy for submission state by design — the model has a dedicated `submitted: Submission | None` field (models.py:227) that the status logic should use but does not.

This is a genuine divergence between the label's intent ("pending_review" = awaiting review/submission) and reality ("reviewed checkbox never set, possibly already submitted").

### `BIZ:-159` — URL fetch mode uses a naive 'last path segment' ID extraction that silently produces wrong IDs for real platform URLs
- **confirmed ✓** · source: biz-logic · effort: small · file: `/Users/bjgilbert/Projects/Zoltar/sigma-predict/pages/2_Predict.py:169-187`
- **evidence:** The URL input mode does qid_from_url = url.rstrip('/').split('/')[-1] (line 179) and feeds it to client.get_question. Metaculus URLs are of the form https://www.metaculus.com/questions/12345/some-slug/ — the last segment is the slug ('some-slug'), not the numeric ID. The comment calls it a 'platform-specific heuristic' (line 172) but it is not platform-specific at all; it is one generic rule applied to all three platforms. For a typical Metaculus URL with a trailing slug, get_question(slug) will 404 and surface 'Fetch failed' (metaculus.py:126). The feature claims URL support but works only for URLs that happen to end in the bare ID.
- **recommendation:** Implement per-platform URL parsing (e.g. regex for /questions/(\d+) on Metaculus) or extract the numeric segment. At minimum, document that only ID-terminal URLs work and validate the extracted token before fetching.
- **verifier:** Confirmed against the code. At /Users/bjgilbert/Projects/Zoltar/sigma-predict/pages/2_Predict.py:178-182 the URL mode does exactly `qid_from_url = url.rstrip("/").split("/")[-1]` and feeds it straight to `client.get_question(qid_from_url)`. The comment at line 172 calls it a "platform-specific heuristic," but the implementation is a single generic rule applied identically regardless of the selected platform (Metaculus/Polymarket/Kalshi per src/models.py:13-16). That is a genuine intent-vs-implementation divergence: the name/comment claims platform awareness; the code has none.

Materiality confirmed via the platform clients. Metaculus get_question (src/platforms/metaculus.py:118-126) builds the API path `/questions/{question_id}/` and on a non-numeric/unknown ID raises `ValueError("Question {id} not found on Metaculus")`, which the UI catches and renders as "Fetch failed: ...". Canonical Metaculus URLs are `https://www.metaculus.com/questions/12345/some-slug/` — after rstrip the last segment is the slug, not the numeric ID, so the lookup 404s. Polymarket get_question (polymarket.py:108-116) hits `/markets/{question_id}` expecting a condition ID, while public Polymarket URLs end in an event slug — also a mismatch. So for the normal, canonical URL form on at least the primary platform (Metaculus) and Polymarket, the feature does not work. It only works for the atypical case where a URL happens to end in the bare API ID. The evidence quotes are verbatim-accurate and the cited line numbers (179, 172, metaculus.py:126) are correct.

The finding is real. The one correction: the failure is NOT silent — it surfaces a visible "Fetch failed" Streamlit error, and the working "ID" input mode is right next to it as a trivial workaround. The title's "silently produces wrong IDs" is somewhat overstated; the IDs are wrong but the failure is loud and recoverable. This is a usability defect in an optional convenience input path, not a data-integrity or pipeline-correctness bug.

### `BIZ:-167` — Dead branch in _collect_platform_signals — the 'if key in dict' guard is always False on a freshly-constructed PlatformSignals
- **confirmed ✓** · source: biz-logic · effort: trivial · file: `src/pipeline/orchestrator.py:548-551`
- **evidence:** signals is created one line earlier as PlatformSignals() (line 542) whose prices_at_prediction_time defaults to an empty dict (models.py:133). The conditional `if platform_key in signals.prices_at_prediction_time:` (line 548) can never be True at this point, so the branch assigning `signals.prices_at_prediction_time[platform_key] = community` is unreachable. Only the else branch (line 551) ever runs. This is the mechanism behind the inert divergence flag above.
- **recommendation:** Delete the dead if/else and assign the intended canonical key directly. The branch suggests an earlier design where signals were pre-populated; that no longer holds.
- **verifier:** Confirmed by reading the cited code. The mechanism is exactly as claimed:

1. orchestrator.py:542 constructs `signals = PlatformSignals()`. models.py:133 (in src/models.py, NOT src/pipeline/models.py — the finding mislabels the path, but the line number and content are correct) defines `prices_at_prediction_time: dict[str, float | None] = Field(default_factory=dict)`, so the dict is empty at construction.

2. No code mutates `signals.prices_at_prediction_time` between line 542 and the membership check at line 548 (verified by grepping all references in orchestrator.py). The only writes are the two branches at 549/551 themselves.

3. Therefore `if platform_key in signals.prices_at_prediction_time:` (line 548, platform_key = f"{question.platform.value}_community") is provably always False. Line 549 is dead/unreachable; line 551 (the else) always executes, storing the community price under the bare key `question.platform.value` (e.g. "metaculus") and discarding the computed `_community` key.

Intent-vs-implementation gap is genuine: the developer computes `platform_key = f"...community"` (line 547) clearly intending it to be the storage key, but the guard that would use it can never fire. The code reads as if it distinguishes a pre-populated vs. fresh case, but on a freshly-constructed object only one case ever occurs.

Materiality is HIGHER than the finding's own framing suggests. The downstream consumer human_review.py:339-340 does `ps.get("metaculus_community")` — it looks up exactly the `_community`-suffixed key that the dead branch was supposed to populate. Because the bug stores under "metaculus" instead, `.get("metaculus_community")` always returns None, so the "Large divergence from community" reviewer flag (lines 341-345) NEVER fires. This is a silent suppression of a human-review safety flag in a prediction pipeline — not merely cosmetic dead code. The finding correctly identifies this as the mechanism behind an "inert divergence flag."

The recommendation ("assign the intended canonical key directly") is correct: the canonical key should be the `_community` form to match the consumer.

### `BIZ:-168` — PlatformSignals anomaly detection is declared but never computed — anomaly flag and cross_platform_divergence are inert
- **confirmed ✓** · source: biz-logic · effort: medium · file: `src/models.py:134-136, src/review/human_review.py:153,352, src/pipeline/orchestrator.py:536`
- **evidence:** PlatformSignals declares anomaly_detected: bool = False, anomaly_details, and cross_platform_divergence: float | None = None. Grep across src/ and scripts/ shows these are only ever read (human_review.py:153 'ANOMALY DETECTED', :352 flag 'Platform behavioral anomaly detected') and never written. _collect_platform_signals (orchestrator.py:536-555) only fetches one community price and never sets anomaly_detected or cross_platform_divergence. So anomaly_detected is permanently False and both the review-panel anomaly display and the _generate_flags anomaly flag are unreachable controls. This aligns with monitor.py honestly declaring anomaly/cross-platform monitoring as an unimplemented Phase 3 feature.
- **recommendation:** Either implement divergence/anomaly computation in _collect_platform_signals (compare calibrated prob or cross-platform prices and set the fields) or annotate the model fields and the two human_review code paths as Phase 3 placeholders so reviewers don't assume the safeguard is active.
- **verifier:** Code-verified at all cited locations. models.py:132-136 declares PlatformSignals with cross_platform_divergence (default None), anomaly_detected (default False), anomaly_details (default None). A grep across src/ and scripts/ confirms these three fields are NEVER assigned anywhere — they appear only at their declaration (models.py) and at three read sites (human_review.py:153-154 panel display, :352-353 flag generation). _collect_platform_signals (orchestrator.py:536-555) is the only producer of PlatformSignals; it constructs PlatformSignals() with defaults and only writes prices_at_prediction_time, never the anomaly/divergence fields. Therefore anomaly_detected is permanently False, and both the 'ANOMALY DETECTED' review-panel display and the 'Platform behavioral anomaly detected' flag are unreachable dead code. monitor.py:1,17,19-21 corroborates by openly declaring anomaly/cross-platform monitoring an unimplemented Phase 3 feature. The intent-vs-implementation gap is real and material: the model fields and the two human_review read paths carry no placeholder annotation, so a reviewer would reasonably believe an active anomaly safeguard exists when it can never trigger. I could not refute it — no deserialization path, no kwargs construction, and no other writer exists.

### `BIZ:-172` — resolve.py only checks Metaculus; --check-all silently skips all Polymarket/Kalshi predictions, and Metaculus resolution outcome is taken raw without binary validation
- **confirmed ✓** · source: biz-logic · effort: small · file: `scripts/resolve.py:93-97, src/platforms/metaculus.py:166-182`
- **evidence:** main() instantiates only MetaculusClient and, for any non-metaculus record, prints 'resolution check not implemented' and continues (resolve.py:93-97). PolymarketClient.get_resolution and KalshiClient.get_resolution exist (they return None / are stubs) but are never invoked because resolve.py hard-codes the metaculus branch. So --check-all's promise to 'Check all unresolved predictions' (docstring/help line 68) is partial: it only resolves Metaculus. Additionally MetaculusClient.get_resolution does `outcome = float(resolution_value)` (metaculus.py:171) with no clamp/validation — a Metaculus 'annulled'/'ambiguous' numeric or an out-of-[0,1] continuous resolution would flow straight into brier_score = (p - outcome)**2 (resolve.py:108) and pollute the dashboard's Brier averages, since brier_score does no domain checking (scoring.py:8-10).
- **recommendation:** Either route get_resolution through the record's platform client generically (store a client map keyed by record.platform) or update --check-all help to say 'Metaculus only'. Add a guard rejecting non-binary or out-of-range outcomes before scoring, and skip annulled resolutions.
- **verifier:** Both halves of the finding are code-grounded, but the evidence overstates parts. Verdict: REAL, with severity lowered to LOW-MEDIUM.

CLAIM 1 (multi-platform skip) — CONFIRMED but loud, not silent. resolve.py:93-97 hard-codes `if record.platform == "metaculus"`, else echoes "resolution check not implemented for {platform}" and continues. predict.py:27-31 and batch_predict.py:54-56 explicitly accept --platform polymarket/kalshi, and PredictionRecord.platform (models.py:209) is a free string, so non-metaculus records genuinely exist and never get resolution-checked. The --check-all help text "Check all unresolved predictions" (line 68) is unqualified, so an intent-vs-implementation gap is real. BUT: (a) the skip is explicitly echoed per record, not silent as the title claims; (b) PolymarketClient.get_resolution (polymarket.py:125-127) and KalshiClient.get_resolution (kalshi.py:160-162) are genuine stubs returning None — routing them generically would resolve nothing anyway. So the practical impact is a misleading help string plus dead non-metaculus records, not lost resolutions. This is documentation/UX accuracy, not data corruption.

CLAIM 2 (no outcome validation) — PARTIALLY CONFIRMED, evidence example is wrong. metaculus.py:170-173 wraps float(resolution_value) in try/except (TypeError, ValueError) returning None on failure, and resolve.py:99-102 skips None. The finding's stated example — "annulled/ambiguous" — resolves to a STRING on the Metaculus API, so float() raises ValueError and the record is correctly skipped. The evidence's claim that annulled/ambiguous "would flow straight into brier_score" is FALSE for those cases. The genuine residual risk is narrower: a numeric-but-out-of-[0,1] resolution (e.g. Metaculus uses -1.0 for annulled in some API shapes, and continuous resolutions are floats) passes float(), is stored unvalidated (Resolution.outcome at models.py:154 has no validator), and flows into brier_score=(p-outcome)**2 (scoring.py:8-10, no clamp) — producing a garbage Brier that pollutes dashboard averages. That specific path is real and unguarded. The recommendation (guard rejecting out-of-[0,1] outcomes before scoring) is appropriate.

Net: the divergence between intent (resolve all platforms / score valid binary outcomes) and implementation (metaculus-only / unbounded float scoring) genuinely exists and is worth a guard plus a help-text fix. But the title's "silently skips" is inaccurate (it echoes), and the headline annulled/ambiguous example is technically wrong (handled by the existing try/except). Real, materially worth fixing, but lower impact than "medium" implies given the explicit echo and the fact that poly/kalshi resolution is unimplemented end-to-end.

### `CRIT-109` — Community-divergence flag in human_review reads key 'metaculus_community' that the orchestrator never writes — the flag is dead for every platform, including Metaculus
- **confirmed ✓** · source: critic · effort: small · file: `/Users/bjgilbert/Projects/Zoltar/sigma-predict/src/review/human_review.py:339-345`
- **evidence:** _generate_flags does `community = ps.get("metaculus_community")` (human_review.py:340). But _collect_platform_signals writes the key as the bare `question.platform.value` (orchestrator.py:551 — e.g. 'metaculus', 'kalshi', 'polymarket'); the '<platform>_community' branch (line 548) is unreachable because signals.prices_at_prediction_time starts empty. So the lookup `ps.get("metaculus_community")` is always None and the divergence-from-community flag NEVER fires, even for a Metaculus question whose price is actually stored under 'metaculus'. Prior passes flagged the dead '_community' branch and noted the human_review hardcoded key, but did not connect that this makes the entire divergence guard inert end-to-end (and additionally that the key is hardcoded to metaculus regardless of the question's real platform, so Kalshi/Polymarket divergence is never flaggable by design). This guard is the primary anomaly signal surfaced to the human reviewer.
- **recommendation:** Make producer and consumer agree on one key: have _generate_flags read `f"{record.question.platform.value}"` (or whatever orchestrator writes) rather than the literal 'metaculus_community', and add a contract test asserting the key written by _collect_platform_signals is the key read by _generate_flags.
- **verifier:** CONFIRMED on mechanics, with severity framing partially refuted. |source:code-read|

Producer (src/pipeline/orchestrator.py:542-551, NOT the cited src/review/orchestrator.py — citation path is wrong but the code exists): signals = PlatformSignals() starts with an empty prices_at_prediction_time dict (models.py:133, default_factory=dict). The guard `if platform_key in signals.prices_at_prediction_time` (line 548) tests whether 'metaculus_community' is already in an empty dict — always false — so the else branch (line 551) always runs and writes under the BARE key question.platform.value ('metaculus'/'kalshi'/'polymarket'). The '_community'-suffixed key (line 549) is unreachable dead code.

Consumer (src/review/human_review.py:340): community = ps.get('metaculus_community'). Grep confirms NOTHING anywhere writes 'metaculus_community' (only writers to prices_at_prediction_time are orchestrator.py:549 and :551). So ps.get('metaculus_community') is always None, and the divergence-from-community flag (lines 341-345) NEVER fires for any platform, including Metaculus whose price is stored under 'metaculus'. The two bugs compound: fixing either side alone does not restore the guard. VERIFIED.

Additional confirmed point: even if the key matched, the consumer hardcodes 'metaculus', so Kalshi/Polymarket divergence is unflaggable by design. VERIFIED.

NOVELTY — accept as new. The finding is honest that prior passes separately flagged (a) the dead _community branch and (b) the hardcoded key. Each of those in isolation reads as a cosmetic key-naming nit. The new, material contribution is the end-to-end conclusion: the divergence guard — a human-review safety flag — is fully inert in production for every platform. That is a distinct impact-level claim, not a mechanical restatement, and it changes the assessment from 'minor inconsistency' to 'dead safety flag'. This clears the 'merely restates prior passes' bar.

SEVERITY — lower to low/medium. Two overstatements in the writeup: (1) It calls the divergence flag 'the primary anomaly signal surfaced to the human reviewer.' It is one of ~8 flags in _generate_flags; independent paths exist (anomaly_detected line 352, external-model disagree/dispute flags lines 360-375). It is not 'primary.' (2) The community price IS still displayed to the reviewer in the Platform Prices section (human_review.py:143-151, which reads the bare key correctly), so the human can still see the community number and eyeball divergence manually — only the automated alert is dead, not the information. This is a degraded-not-blind failure mode. Real bug worth fixing (the recommended contract test is sound), but medium overstates blast radius; low-to-medium is accurate.

### `CRIT-110` — Streamlit UI never configures logging, so all library logger.error/warning output from the background pipeline thread is silently discarded
- **REFUTED ✗** · source: critic · effort: trivial · file: `/Users/bjgilbert/Projects/Zoltar/sigma-predict/app.py:1`
- **evidence:** Only the CLI scripts call logging.basicConfig (resolve.py:74, predict.py:74, batch_predict.py:44). app.py and all pages/ contain no logging configuration (grep for 'logging'/'basicConfig' in app.py + pages/ returns NONE). The pipeline modules log failures via module loggers (e.g. platform clients' `except Exception: logger.warning(...)`, llm_router verification failures llm_router.py:264, search.py:107). With no root handler configured in the Streamlit process, those records hit the default last-resort handler at WARNING+ to stderr at best and DEBUG-level diagnostics (e.g. decomposer 'Raw response' at decomposer.py:77, provider-init failures llm_router.py:139/153) are dropped entirely. Combined with the already-found UI behavior of swallowing pipeline errors into a queue string and resetting to idle, a failed prediction in the UI produces zero diagnostics anywhere. This is a whole observability class the prior passes did not examine.
- **recommendation:** Add logging.basicConfig (or a Streamlit-friendly handler) in app.py at startup so background-thread pipeline logs are captured to a file/console, and surface ERROR-level records to the UI rather than only a generic 'ERROR:' queue token.
- **verifier:** The finding's factual premises about the code are correct, but its central claim and severity driver are FALSE, refuted empirically.

VERIFIED FACTS: app.py (full file read, 82 lines) configures no logging; grep across app.py/pages/components/src confirms zero basicConfig/dictConfig/addHandler/StreamHandler anywhere except the three CLI scripts (scripts/predict.py:74, batch_predict.py:44, resolve.py:74). The pipeline runs in a daemon thread (pages/2_Predict.py:252-256) and modules log via getLogger(__name__) (llm_router.py:264 warning, search.py:107 error, decomposer.py:77 debug, etc.). All citations check out.

WHERE THE FINDING IS WRONG (load-bearing): The title asserts 'all library logger.error/warning output ... is silently discarded.' I tested this empirically in the actual .venv. (1) Importing streamlit 1.55.0 attaches NO handler to the root logger; root stays at WARNING with no handlers. (2) Inspected streamlit.logger.get_logger source: it attaches its StreamHandler only to specific named loggers (streamlit, tornado.*) and sets propagate=False — it never touches root and never configures the app's src.pipeline.* namespace. (3) Ran a process that imports streamlit then logs at all four levels via logging.getLogger('src.pipeline.llm_router'): WARNING and ERROR records BOTH printed to stderr via Python's logging.lastResort handler (a StderrHandler at WARNING level). Only DEBUG and INFO were dropped.

So warning/error pipeline diagnostics are NOT silently discarded — they reach stderr, which `streamlit run` surfaces in its console/logs. The finding's own evidence even hedges this ('to stderr at best'), contradicting its own title. Critically, search.py:107 — which the finding cites as lost output — is logger.error and DOES reach stderr; the finding miscategorizes its own evidence.

The severity-justifying claim 'a failed prediction in the UI produces zero diagnostics anywhere' is therefore false: pipeline exceptions that surface as logger.error/warning go to stderr.

OVERLAP: The finding concedes the queue-swallowing/idle-reset behavior was already found by prior passes and leans on it for impact. Its claimed-new 'whole observability class' reduces, once corrected, to a narrow true sliver: DEBUG/INFO records (decomposer.py:77 Raw response, llm_router.py:139/153 provider-init debug) are dropped, and the UI process lacks the timestamped/formatted handler the CLI scripts set. That is a real but minor diagnostic-verbosity gap, not the 'zero diagnostics' observability hole described, and it does not rise to medium. The finding as written should be refuted because its headline and impact rationale are factually incorrect.

### `CRIT-111` — Persona search template.format(question=...) crashes the search stage when an untrusted market title contains literal brace characters
- **REFUTED ✗** · source: critic · effort: trivial · file: `/Users/bjgilbert/Projects/Zoltar/sigma-predict/src/pipeline/search.py:144-146`
- **evidence:** `query = template.format(question=question)` is called on every persona template with the question text, which originates from untrusted third-party market titles (PlatformQuestion.title flows in via orchestrator.py:214 search_with_persona). str.format treats any '{...}' in the substituted question text's surrounding template literally, but more importantly if the question string itself is passed as a positional-less template OR a template author error occurs it raises; concretely, a market title such as 'Will model {gpt} ship?' substituted then re-evaluated, or any template whose only field is {question} where the question value contains an unmatched brace, produces KeyError/ValueError/IndexError at format time. There is no try/except around search_with_persona's format loop, so a hostile or merely brace-containing title can crash the forecaster's search stage. This is an untested malformed-input failure mode distinct from the prompt-injection finding (that one is about LLM instruction injection, not a Python format() crash) and from the search.py Tavily-key finding.
- **recommendation:** Build queries with safe substitution (e.g. template.replace('{question}', question) or a defensive str.format with try/except that falls back to a raw search) and add a test with a title containing '{' and '}'.
- **verifier:** REFUTED on the central technical claim. The finding asserts that an untrusted market title containing brace characters, when passed through `template.format(question=question)` at search.py:145, gets "substituted then re-evaluated" and "produces KeyError/ValueError/IndexError at format time." This is false and reflects a misunderstanding of Python's str.format semantics.

I verified the code: at orchestrator.py:214-215 the untrusted `question.title` (PlatformQuestion.title, models.py:237) is concatenated and passed as the `question` argument to search_with_persona, which loops `template.format(question=question)` with no try/except (search.py:144-146). The data-flow portion of the finding is accurate.

However, str.format only parses the TEMPLATE string for `{...}` placeholders. The substituted VALUE is inserted as inert data and is NEVER re-parsed. I tested this directly:
- `'{question} scholarly research'.format(question='Will model {gpt} ship?')` -> 'Will model {gpt} ship? scholarly research' (no crash)
- Unmatched single `{` or `}` in the value -> no crash
- Even a value of `'{missing_key} and {0} and {}'` -> inserted verbatim, no crash

There is no double-format / no recursive evaluation anywhere in the code. The templates in _PERSONA_QUERY_STRATEGIES are static, author-controlled, and contain only the well-formed `{question}` field supplied by keyword — so format() cannot raise on them regardless of title content. The only way this line raises is a developer typo in a template literal (an author error, not an untrusted-input crash), which is not a runtime vulnerability and not what the finding claims.

Because the claimed crash mode does not exist, the finding is not genuine. Its NEW-ness relative to the prompt-injection and Tavily-key findings is moot since the underlying bug is not real. Severity should be dropped to none.

### `SECU-55` — Tavily API key transmitted in JSON request body instead of an auth header
- **confirmed ✓** · source: framed · effort: trivial · file: `/Users/bjgilbert/Projects/Zoltar/sigma-predict/src/pipeline/search.py:88-104`
- **evidence:** payload = {
    "api_key": self._api_key,
    "query": query,
    ...
}
...
response = client.post(TAVILY_API_URL, json=payload, headers={"Content-Type": "application/json"})
The secret is part of the serialized request body. Request bodies are more frequently captured by debug logging, error trackers, and intermediary proxies than Authorization headers, which are commonly redacted by default. Tavily supports header-based auth (Authorization: Bearer).
- **recommendation:** Send the key as an Authorization header (Bearer) rather than in the JSON body, matching the header-based pattern already used by the platform clients (metaculus.py:69). Reduces exposure surface in logs/proxies.
- **verifier:** Code-grounded verification confirms every load-bearing claim. At src/pipeline/search.py:88-95 the Tavily api_key is placed inside the JSON `payload` dict, and at line 101 it is serialized into the request body via `json=payload`, with only a `Content-Type` header set (line 102). I attempted to refute the claim that Tavily supports header auth and that the body is the wrong approach, but Tavily's official docs confirm the opposite of a refutation: the documented/recommended method is `Authorization: Bearer <token>`, and the api_key is NOT meant to be in the request body. The cross-reference to header-based auth elsewhere in the codebase also holds — metaculus.py:69 does set `headers["Authorization"] = f"Token {api_key}"`. The security rationale (Authorization headers are redacted by proxies/APM/error trackers by convention, request bodies are not) is sound and standard. One minor inaccuracy: the recommendation cites `metaculus.py:69` but the actual path is `src/platforms/metaculus.py`, not `src/pipeline/`; the line number and content are correct, so this is cosmetic and does not undermine the finding.

### `SECU-57` — Cross-session leak via global cache_resource mutable singletons and Config mutation
- **confirmed ✓** · source: framed · effort: medium · file: `/Users/bjgilbert/Projects/Zoltar/sigma-predict/pages/2_Predict.py:234`
- **evidence:** st.cache_resource singletons are shared across ALL browser sessions in a Streamlit process. The completed-record bridge is one such singleton: get_completed_slot() returns a single-element list (app.py:54-57) and get_pipeline_queue() a single Queue (app.py:49-51). Two concurrent users running predictions race on the same slot/queue, so one user can receive another user's PredictionRecord. Separately, line 234 does `config.verification.enabled = verification_toggle` on the process-wide cached Config singleton (app.py:33 get_config), so one user's toggle silently changes verification behavior for every other session and for the background thread. Combined with no auth, this is a confidentiality and integrity issue across users.
- **recommendation:** Make the thread→UI bridge per-session (store the queue/slot in st.session_state, not cache_resource), and never mutate the shared Config singleton — pass a per-run copy (dataclasses.replace) into the thread. If the app is strictly single-user localhost, document that constraint explicitly.
- **verifier:** Confirmed against the code. app.py:49-57 define get_pipeline_queue() and get_completed_slot() as @st.cache_resource singletons (a single Queue and a single-element list), and get_config() at app.py:33-35 is likewise a @st.cache_resource singleton. st.cache_resource objects are shared process-wide across all browser sessions — this is documented Streamlit behavior. 2_Predict.py confirms both halves of the finding: (1) the thread->UI bridge uses these shared singletons — _run_pipeline does completed_slot.clear()/append(record) (lines 80-84) and the per-session progress fragment reads slot[0] (line 277), so two concurrent users race on one shared slot/queue and User B can read User A's PredictionRecord; (2) line 234 does `config.verification.enabled = verification_toggle` on the shared Config singleton, and that same shared config object is passed into the background thread at line 254 (args=(config, ...)) where Orchestrator(config) consumes it. So one user's toggle changes verification behavior for every session and for in-flight threads. There is no auth, no .streamlit/config.toml, and no README/docs declaring a single-user or localhost constraint (verified via find/grep — only prompt .md files and a pytest README exist). The Config mutation half is non-racy and certain; the record-leak half is timing-dependent but genuinely possible. The recommendation (per-session st.session_state bridge + dataclasses.replace copy into the thread) is correct and idiomatic. The only weakness in the finding is framing: the entire impact is conditional on multi-user deployment, which is plausible (the app's threading + per-second fragment polling architecture targets a server model) but not proven. That makes it a concurrency-isolation correctness defect that rises to a security concern under a realistic deployment, rather than a guaranteed cross-user breach. That nuance does not refute the finding — the underlying code defect is unambiguous.

### `SECU-58` — Unbounded JSON parsing of remote responses (no max response size, no redirect/TLS hardening)
- **REFUTED ✗** · source: framed · effort: small · file: `/Users/bjgilbert/Projects/Zoltar/sigma-predict/src/platforms/kalshi.py:91-95`
- **evidence:** self._client = httpx.Client(base_url=BASE_URL, headers=headers, timeout=30.0) — same pattern in metaculus.py:70-74 and polymarket.py:70-74. No max response size, no explicit redirect policy. Every read path then calls resp.json() on the full body (kalshi.py:126, metaculus.py:108, polymarket.py:98) and search.py:105 does response.json() with no size limit. A hostile or compromised endpoint (or a redirect to one) can return an arbitrarily large body and exhaust memory; httpx follows the configured default redirect behavior with no restriction to the original host.
- **recommendation:** Cap response size (stream + enforce a byte limit before .json()), set an explicit follow_redirects policy (or follow_redirects=False) so responses cannot be redirected off the intended host, and keep verify=True explicit. Apply to all three platform clients and the Tavily search call.
- **verifier:** Read all four cited locations: kalshi.py:91-95, metaculus.py:70-74, polymarket.py:70-74, and search.py (actually at src/pipeline/search.py:98-105). The code matches the quoted snippets — httpx.Client with base_url/timeout only, and .json() called on full response bodies with no byte cap.

However, the finding's security framing rests on a load-bearing factual error. The evidence states "httpx follows the configured default redirect behavior with no restriction to the original host." This is wrong: httpx defaults to follow_redirects=False since v0.20 (project pins httpx>=0.27.0; installed 0.28.1, confirmed). So redirects are NOT followed by default — a 3xx response is returned as-is and no off-host fetch occurs. The recommended fix (follow_redirects=False) is a no-op against current behavior. The "redirect to a hostile host" scenario, which is what would elevate this from a self-DoS to a real boundary issue, does not exist.

The TLS sub-claim is also non-substantive: httpx defaults to verify=True and the code never disables it; "keep verify=True explicit" is purely cosmetic.

What remains literally true is the absence of a max-response-size guard before .json(). But materiality is weak: all four base URLs are hardcoded HTTPS constants to trusted first-party APIs (api.elections.kalshi.com, www.metaculus.com, clob.polymarket.com, Tavily). Grep confirms no user-controlled or dynamic URL ever reaches these clients; question_id path segments are appended to fixed HTTPS bases and cannot redirect off-host. For unbounded-body memory exhaustion to fire, an established provider would have to turn malicious or the TLS-verified connection would have to be MITM'd (defeated by verify=True). For a local CLI/pipeline tool calling trusted market APIs, this is a defensive-hardening nicety (self-inflicted DoS resilience), not a medium-severity security vulnerability. The medium rating and the threat model both depend substantially on the invalid redirect/TLS claims, so the finding as written does not hold.

### `SECU-63` — Registry persistence has no file locking; concurrent UI/CLI writes can silently drop records (lost update)
- **confirmed ✓** · source: framed · effort: small · file: `/Users/bjgilbert/Projects/Zoltar/sigma-predict/src/registry/store.py:73-107`
- **evidence:** update() does load_all() (read whole file) then _write_all() (rewrite whole file via temp + os.replace), with no fcntl/flock anywhere in registry/. save() is a bare append (store.py:33). The Streamlit UI writes through this on every human review (2_Predict.py:376 cache._store.update, 3_Review.py:288) and the orchestrator persists records as a side effect, while CLIs (resolve.py:113) also call update() in a loop. With concurrent processes/sessions — explicitly enabled by the no-auth multi-session UI plus background threads — an append that lands between another process's load_all and os.replace is overwritten and lost. This is a data-integrity issue, not just performance.
- **recommendation:** Add advisory file locking (fcntl.flock) around the read-modify-write in update() and around save(), or serialize all writes through a single owner. At minimum document the single-writer assumption and prevent concurrent writers in the UI. The atomic os.replace protects against torn writes but not against lost updates.
- **verifier:** CONFIRMED against the code. store.py:73-107 shows update() does load_all() (read whole file, lines 37-54) then _write_all() which writes a temp file and os.replace (101-107), with no locking. save() (line 33) is a bare "a"-mode append. A grep for fcntl/flock/filelock across src/ finds nothing relevant (only an unrelated threading.Lock comment in llm_router.py). The cited call sites all exist: pages/2_Predict.py:376 (cache._store.update), pages/3_Review.py:288 (store.update), scripts/resolve.py:113 (store.update in a loop), and orchestrator save() at orchestrator.py:418/451.

The concurrency premise is real and in fact stronger than the finding states. Orchestrator.__init__ (orchestrator.py:66) builds RegistryStore(config.registry_path) from a path string, not a shared object. The Streamlit Predict page launches the pipeline in a daemon background thread (2_Predict.py:252-257) whose _run_pipeline creates a brand-new per-thread Orchestrator (line 65), hence a separate RegistryStore writing the same file. @st.cache_resource singletons (app.py:43-46) are shared across all browser sessions in the one server process, and resolve.py / dashboard.py are independent OS processes. So multiple genuinely-independent writers can hit one file with no advisory lock.
