The lost-update mechanism — writer B's append or os.replace landing between writer A's load_all() and writer A's os.replace() — silently discards a record. os.replace is atomic against torn writes but not against this read-modify-write race. This is data integrity, not just performance, so the finding's framing is correct.

One overstatement to flag: WITHIN a single session the flow is serialized — the daemon thread save() completes and posts a sentinel before the state moves to awaiting_review and the human's update() runs (2_Predict.py:274-285 then 376). So the default solo-operator path is not itself racy. The race requires concurrent actors: two browser sessions, the resolve.py/dashboard CLI running while the Streamlit server is up, or two sessions' pipeline threads finishing near-simultaneously. These are plausible given the multi-session no-auth UI plus separate CLIs, but they are not the single-user default the finding implies.

The recommendation (fcntl.flock around the read-modify-write and append, or a single-writer owner, or at minimum documenting/enforcing single-writer) is sound and matches the actual gap.

### `QUAL-66` — _extract_json raises unguarded ValueError on unterminated code fences
- **confirmed ✓** · source: framed · effort: trivial · file: `/Users/bjgilbert/Projects/Zoltar/sigma-predict/src/pipeline/utils.py:10-27`
- **evidence:** When the input contains an opening ```json or ``` but no closing fence, `end = text.index("```", start)` (lines 13, 17) raises ValueError, not JSONDecodeError. Every caller (forecaster.py:171-175, base_rate.py:62-64, decomposer.py:71-75) wraps only `json.loads` in `try/except json.JSONDecodeError`, so a truncated/malformed LLM response with a dangling fence crashes the run instead of degrading to the parse-failed fallback path. This is the exact failure mode the parse_failed handling was built to absorb.
- **recommendation:** Use `text.find("```", start)` and handle the -1 case (fall through to brace-scan), or wrap the fence extraction in try/except and fall back to the `{...}` slice. Callers should also catch ValueError alongside JSONDecodeError.
- **verifier:** Confirmed against the code and reproduced empirically.

utils.py:10-27: `_extract_json` uses `text.index("```", start)` at lines 14 and 18. Python's str.index raises ValueError when the substring is absent. I reproduced both cited cases: input with an opening ```json fence and no closing fence raises `ValueError: substring not found`, and a bare opening ``` with no closing fence does the same.

Caller exception handling: Every call site calls `_extract_json(...)` OUTSIDE and BEFORE the try block, and the try blocks catch json.JSONDecodeError:
- forecaster.py:171 (try at 173, `except json.JSONDecodeError` at 175)
- decomposer.py:71 (try at 73, `except json.JSONDecodeError` at 75)
- base_rate.py:62 (try at 64, `except (json.JSONDecodeError, ValueError)` at 81)
Since ValueError is a base class of JSONDecodeError but the extract call is outside the try, the ValueError from _extract_json escapes in all cases and crashes the run rather than degrading to the parse_failed / Decomposition() / 0.5-default fallback that this handling was explicitly built to absorb.

One correction to the finding: it claims base_rate.py catches "only json.JSONDecodeError" — actually base_rate.py:81 catches `(json.JSONDecodeError, ValueError)`. But this does not save it, because the _extract_json(raw) call at line 62 is before the try at line 64. So the substantive bug holds at that site too.

The finding also UNDERSTATES blast radius: there are 5 vulnerable call sites, not 3. forecaster.py:213 (pre-mortem, except json.JSONDecodeError at 216) and base_rate.py:211 (search-augmented, except json.JSONDecodeError at 214) follow the identical unguarded pattern.

Trigger realism: the most plausible real-world trigger is max_tokens truncation mid-response — the LLM emits ```json\n{... and gets cut off before the closing fence. base_rate uses max_tokens=1024 and forecaster/decomposer use configured max_tokens, so truncation is a live possibility. This is a genuine production crash path that defeats the existing resilience design.

### `QUAL-67` — Per-field float()/int() coercion of LLM JSON crashes the run instead of degrading
- **confirmed ✓** · source: framed · effort: small · file: `/Users/bjgilbert/Projects/Zoltar/sigma-predict/src/pipeline/forecaster.py:181,221,234,243`
- **evidence:** Only the top-level `json.loads` is guarded (lines 173-179). After that, `probability = float(data.get("probability", base_rate))` (181), `pm_delta = float(pm_data.get("pre_mortem_delta", 0.0))` (221), `int(data.get("search_quality_score", 5))` (234), `int(data.get("confidence_self_score", 5))` (243) all coerce values that came from the LLM JSON. If the model emits `"probability": "high"` or `"confidence_self_score": "5/10"`, the float()/int() raises ValueError and aborts the whole forecast run — the JSON parsed fine, so parse_failed is never set and there is no graceful fallback.
- **recommendation:** Add a small helper `_coerce_float(value, default)` / `_coerce_int(value, default)` that try/excepts ValueError/TypeError and returns the default (logging a warning), then route each field through it. base_rate.py:66-70 has the same exposure but at least catches ValueError at the block level — make the pattern consistent.
- **verifier:** Confirmed against the code. forecaster.py lines 181, 221, 234, 243 contain unguarded float()/int() coercions of LLM-supplied JSON values; only the top-level json.loads (173-179) is wrapped in try/except, and that handler is what sets parse_failed. If json.loads succeeds but a field holds a non-numeric value, the coercion raises and parse_failed is never set, so the intended degradation path is bypassed.

No outer safety net: run_forecast is invoked in a bare for-loop in orchestrator.predict_until_review (orchestrator.py:219) with no surrounding try/except in that method (the try blocks at lines 429/529/544 are in unrelated methods). A raised ValueError/TypeError propagates up and aborts the prediction rather than degrading one run.

The bypassed degradation is intentional and real: orchestrator.py:251-252 explicitly filters out parse_failed runs before aggregation, so the design expects a defaulted run, not a crash.

Trigger plausibility holds: llm_router.py enforces no response_format/JSON schema; JSON is pulled from free-form text via _extract_json. Nothing guarantees probability/scores are numeric. Verified empirically: float('high') and int('5/10') raise ValueError; float(None) (from JSON null) raises TypeError — all parse as valid JSON first.

Consistency claim verified: base_rate.py:64-86 wraps the identical float()/int() coercions (lines 66, 70) in a block-level except (json.JSONDecodeError, ValueError) that returns a safe default — the exact pattern forecaster.py lacks. Genuine intra-codebase inconsistency.

One minor evidence imprecision (not enough to refute): the null case raises TypeError, not ValueError as the evidence narrative implies, but the recommendation correctly says catch ValueError/TypeError, so the proposed fix is sound. I could not refute this finding.

### `QUAL-68` — _parse_deliberation_json duplicates _extract_json logic verbatim
- **confirmed ✓** · source: framed · effort: trivial · file: `/Users/bjgilbert/Projects/Zoltar/sigma-predict/src/pipeline/deliberation.py:155-179`
- **evidence:** Lines 157-170 reimplement the exact code-fence-then-brace-scan extraction already in utils.py:_extract_json (10-27). utils.py was explicitly created to consolidate this ('Shared pipeline utilities — extracted from decomposer, base_rate, forecaster' — utils.py:1), but deliberation.py was not migrated and keeps its own divergent copy. Drift risk: a fix to the extraction logic (e.g. the ValueError bug above) must be applied in two places and one will be missed.
- **recommendation:** Replace lines 157-170 with `text = _extract_json(text)` (import from src.pipeline.utils) and keep only the deliberation-specific fallback dict in the JSONDecodeError handler.
- **verifier:** Confirmed against code. deliberation.py:157-170 (_parse_deliberation_json) reimplements the same code-fence-then-brace-scan JSON extraction that utils.py:_extract_json (10-27) was created to consolidate. utils.py's docstring explicitly states the consolidation intent ('Shared pipeline utilities — extracted from decomposer, base_rate, forecaster'), and grep confirms decomposer.py, base_rate.py, and forecaster.py all import and call _extract_json, while deliberation.py imports only json/Config/models and keeps its own copy. So deliberation.py is the lone module that was not migrated. The drift-risk argument is valid: any fix to the extraction pattern (e.g. the text.index('```', start) ValueError when a closing fence is missing) must be made in two places.

The finding overstates one point: the duplication is NOT 'verbatim.' The two code-fence branches are identical, but the no-fence branch diverges — utils.py has an early `if text.startswith('{'): return text` shortcut and a trailing `return text`, which the deliberation copy lacks. This divergence actually reinforces the drift concern (the copies have already drifted) rather than weakening it. The recommended fix (replace 157-170 with `text = _extract_json(text)`, keep the JSONDecodeError fallback dict) is functionally sound because both implementations fall through to the same json.loads on the no-fence path.

This is genuine maintainability debt, but it is a single isolated 10-line duplication at one call site with no runtime defect today.

### `CORR-80` — Temporal firewall in search_augmented_base_rate is permanently inert — never excludes any result
- **confirmed ✓** · source: framed · effort: small · file: `src/pipeline/base_rate.py:136-158`
- **evidence:** The code builds `cutoff = date.today() - timedelta(days=180)` and filters results via `pub_date_str = getattr(r, 'published_date', None) or getattr(r, 'date', None)` (line 140). But SearchResult (search.py dataclass) has neither a published_date nor a date field, so getattr always returns None, the `if pub_date_str:` block never runs, and every result is appended unconditionally (line 158). The docstring at lines 100-101 even admits 'temporal firewall is not currently active', directly contradicting the 30 lines of firewall code below it and the user-facing label 'temporal firewall applied' injected into the LLM prompt at line 193. The contamination-control guarantee the code advertises does not hold: recent (post-question) statistics can leak into the locked base-rate anchor.
- **recommendation:** Either add a published_date field to SearchResult and populate it from the Tavily response, or delete the dead firewall loop and stop emitting the misleading 'temporal firewall applied' label into the prompt. Do not advertise a control that is inert.
- **verifier:** Confirmed against the code. SearchResult (src/pipeline/search.py:59-66) is a dataclass with exactly four fields: title, url, content, score. It has no published_date and no date attribute. Therefore in base_rate.py line 140, `getattr(r, "published_date", None) or getattr(r, "date", None)` is always None for every result. With pub_date_str always None, the `if pub_date_str:` guard at line 141 is never entered, the exclusion `continue` at line 151 is unreachable, and `filtered_results.append(r)` at line 158 runs unconditionally for every result. The temporal-firewall loop (lines 136-158) is a functional no-op pass-through that never excludes anything.\n\nThe audit's supporting claims also check out: the docstring at lines 100-101 explicitly states 'temporal firewall is not currently active — SearchResult has no published_date field,' which directly contradicts the firewall code below it; and line 193 injects 'historical only — temporal firewall applied' into the LLM user prompt, a label asserting a control that does not run. So the advertised contamination guarantee does not hold and the prompt tells the model recency filtering occurred when it did not.\n\nThe recommendation is sound (either populate published_date from the Tavily response, or delete the dead loop and remove the misleading label). Nothing in the Tavily parsing (search.py:113-122) populates a date field, so populating it would require an API/parse change.</parameter>
<parameter name="severity_adjustment">keep — medium is appropriate. Mitigating factor: the query is restricted to 'historical frequency statistics' (line 124) and only a single numeric rate is extracted via a separate isolated model call, so the real contamination surface is narrower than a general news search, pushing toward low. But this is offset by the active harm of advertising an inert safety control — the misleading 'temporal firewall applied' label fed to the LLM and the docstring/code contradiction create false assurance, which is itself a correctness defect. Net: medium, sitting at the medium/low boundary.</parameter>
</invoke>


### `CORR-81` — Single-provider multi-run setup silently disables extremization, contradicting the configured method
- **confirmed ✓** · source: framed · effort: small · file: `src/pipeline/aggregator.py:90-94; src/pipeline/orchestrator.py:274-275`
- **evidence:** aggregate_runs only extremizes when `n_distinct_providers >= 2` (line 91). orchestrator computes `n_providers = len(set(r.provider for r in valid_runs if hasattr(r,'provider')))` then `n_distinct_providers = max(1, n_providers)` (lines 274-275). The default pipeline runs N independent runs all on `config.primary_model` (orchestrator.py:210, forecaster always sets provider to config.primary_model.provider, forecaster.py:111,230), so every run shares one provider, n_distinct_providers=1, and extremization is silently skipped even though config.aggregation.method == 'extremized_trimmed_mean'. The configured aggregation method is effectively a plain trimmed mean for the common single-model multi-run case, with no warning surfaced. The `hasattr(r,'provider')` guard is also dead — RunResult always has provider.
- **recommendation:** Decide intentionally: if extremization should apply across independent runs regardless of provider count, gate on n_runs>=2 instead; if provider-diversity is truly required, log a pipeline_warning when method=='extremized_trimmed_mean' but n_distinct_providers<2 so the silent downgrade is visible.
- **verifier:** Every code-grounded claim in the finding is confirmed:

1. aggregator.py:90-94 — extremization only applies when `n_distinct_providers >= 2`; otherwise it silently falls back to `raw_aggregate = trimmed_mean` (line 94). Confirmed verbatim.

2. orchestrator.py:274-275 — `n_providers = len(set(r.provider for r in valid_runs if hasattr(r, "provider")))` then `n_distinct_providers = max(1, n_providers)`. Confirmed verbatim.

3. Single-provider claim confirmed: there is exactly one `run_forecast` call site (orchestrator.py:219) and it does not pass a `provider` argument. forecaster.py:111 sets `prov = provider or config.primary_model.provider` and line 230 sets `provider=prov` on the RunResult. So every run in the default pipeline shares one provider → n_providers=1 → n_distinct_providers=max(1,1)=1 → extremization skipped.

4. Default method confirmed: config.py:31 sets `method = "extremized_trimmed_mean"`. So the default config requests extremization but the common multi-run-single-model path silently delivers a plain trimmed mean.

5. No warning is surfaced: I checked the warning appends around aggregation (parse-failure exclusion, source-clustering gate) and none covers the extremization downgrade. The downgrade is silent, as claimed.

6. Dead-guard claim confirmed: RunResult is a Pydantic BaseModel with `provider: str = ""` (models.py:95) always defined, so `hasattr(r, "provider")` is always True — dead code.

The only scoping nuance: num_runs defaults to 1, and a single run hits the early `single_run` return (aggregator.py:59-72) where extremization is moot. The silent downgrade only manifests for num_runs>=2 on a single model. But that is precisely the scenario the persona loop (orchestrator.py:105-106) is built for and exactly what the finding describes as the common single-model multi-run case. So this strengthens rather than refutes the finding — it is a realistic standard usage mode, not a corner case.

I could not refute the finding. It is genuine and material.

### `CORR-82` — Forecaster crashes the run on non-numeric LLM probability/score fields instead of degrading
- **confirmed ✓** · source: framed · effort: small · file: `src/pipeline/forecaster.py:181,221,234,243`
- **evidence:** Only the top-level json.loads is wrapped in try/except (lines 173-179). After that, `probability = float(data.get('probability', base_rate))` (181), `pm_delta = float(pm_data.get('pre_mortem_delta', 0.0))` (221), `search_quality_score=int(data.get('search_quality_score', 5))` (234), `confidence_self_score=int(data.get('confidence_self_score', 5))` (243) coerce LLM-provided values with no per-field guard. If the model returns e.g. `"probability": "high"` or `"confidence_self_score": "5/10"` (valid JSON, wrong type), float()/int() raises ValueError and the entire run crashes — defeating the parse_failed graceful-degradation design (which only catches malformed JSON, not malformed field types). base_rate.py:70 has the same pattern for instance_count but is at least inside the broad try/except.
- **recommendation:** Wrap each numeric coercion in a local try/except that falls back to the default and sets parse_failed=True (or logs a field-level warning), so a single bad field degrades the run rather than throwing.
- **verifier:** Confirmed against the code. All cited lines match verbatim: src/pipeline/forecaster.py:181 `probability = float(data.get("probability", base_rate))`, :221 `pm_delta = float(pm_data.get("pre_mortem_delta", 0.0))`, :234 `search_quality_score=int(data.get("search_quality_score", 5))`, :243 `confidence_self_score=int(data.get("confidence_self_score", 5))`. The only try/except is around json.loads at lines 173-179 (forecast) and 214-217 (pre-mortem), each catching ONLY json.JSONDecodeError — type-coercion ValueErrors fall through.

The crash claim holds end-to-end: in orchestrator.py the per-run loop (line 219) calls run_forecast with no try/except, and its callers predict_until_review/predict (lines 70, 461) do not wrap it either, so a ValueError propagates out and aborts the entire prediction — not just one run. This directly defeats the parse_failed graceful-degradation design, which excludes parse_failed runs from aggregation (orchestrator lines 251-272) but never gets a chance to run because the exception is thrown before parse_failed is meaningfully handled for type errors.

The reachability is genuine, not theoretical: llm_router.py uses plain text completion with no response_format/json_schema/structured-output enforcement (only temperature/max_tokens are passed), and JSON is scraped from free-form text via _extract_json. So a model can emit syntactically valid JSON with wrong-typed values ("probability": "high", "confidence_self_score": "5/10", "~0.3", or 5.0-as-string), which is well-documented LLM behavior — int("5/10") and float("high") both raise ValueError. The int() calls at 234/243 are especially fragile.

The base_rate.py cross-reference is also accurate: line ~66-70 does float(data.get("base_rate", 0.5)) and int(raw_count) but inside a broad try block (line 64), so that module degrades gracefully while forecaster.py does not — supporting the inconsistency the finding notes.

Could not refute. The recommendation (per-field try/except → default + parse_failed=True) is appropriate.

### `CORR-84` — log_score and Brier decomposition silently produce meaningless numbers for non-binary outcomes
- **confirmed ✓** · source: framed · effort: small · file: `src/scoring.py:13-19; src/registry/query.py:57`
- **evidence:** log_score treats `actual >= 0.5` as outcome=1 (scoring.py:16) with no check that actual is in {0,1}; a NUMERIC question whose Resolution.outcome is e.g. 42.0 (models.py:154 allows free float) scores as if it were outcome=1. compute_brier_decomposition computes `uncertainty = bar_o*(1-bar_o)` (query.py:57) and bins on `int(pred/bin_width)`, both of which assume outcomes in [0,1]; a 42.0 outcome makes uncertainty negative and the Murphy identity check meaningless. The models support QuestionType.NUMERIC/MULTIPLE_CHOICE (models.py:20-22) and get_calibration_pairs/brier groupers ingest any resolved record regardless of type (query.py:111-120,142-153), so non-binary resolutions flow straight into binary-only math with no filter.
- **recommendation:** Filter calibration/scoring inputs to binary questions (or clamp/validate outcome in {0,1}) before feeding brier_score, log_score, and compute_brier_decomposition; raise or skip non-binary records explicitly rather than scoring them silently.
- **verifier:** Confirmed against the code at all cited locations.

scoring.py:13-19: log_score does `if actual >= 0.5: return -log(p)` with no check that actual ∈ {0,1}. brier_score (line 8-10) likewise does raw (predicted-actual)^2. Verified empirically: log_score(0.7, 42.0) returns 0.357 (silently scoring a numeric 42.0 as outcome=1); brier_score(0.7, 42.0) returns 1705.69.

query.py:57: uncertainty = bar_o*(1-bar_o) and binning via int(pred/bin_width) both assume outcomes in [0,1]. Verified empirically: a pairs list containing one (0.5, 42.0) outcome yields uncertainty = -48.75 (negative — impossible for a valid Murphy decomposition where uncertainty ∈ [0,0.25]) and a meaningless brier_check of -1.04.

The data model permits the non-binary value by design: models.py:154 `outcome: float | None = None  # 0|1 for binary, value for numeric`, and QuestionType.NUMERIC/MULTIPLE_CHOICE exist (models.py:20-22). metaculus.py:171 does `outcome = float(resolution_value)` with no {0,1} clamp — a numeric question's resolution flows straight through.

No filter exists at any stage: predict.py accepts any --question-id/--url with no question_type gate (the pipeline submits a single probability regardless of type); scripts/resolve.py:86-110 has no question_type check before calling brier_score/log_score; query.py get_calibration_pairs (110-120) and the by_domain/by_horizon groupers (142-153, 181-196) ingest every resolved record with a non-None outcome regardless of type. The docstrings claim "binary predictions" (query.py:107) but nothing enforces it. The finding's evidence and all line citations check out.

I could not refute it.

### `DEPS-92` — Declared dependency floors are stale and understate what the code is actually tested against
- **REFUTED ✗** · source: framed · effort: trivial · file: `/Users/bjgilbert/Projects/Zoltar/sigma-predict/pyproject.toml:8-9`
- **evidence:** openai floor is `>=1.50.0` but the working install is 2.26.0 — a clean install satisfying the floor (openai 1.50) would pull a pre-2.0 SDK whose client surface differs from what llm_router.py uses, so the declared range admits a config the code likely does not support. Same pattern for anthropic (`>=0.40.0` vs installed 0.84.0). The egg-info metadata is also stale: sigma_predict.egg-info/requires.txt lists only 8 deps and is MISSING google-genai, sigma-verify, python-dotenv, streamlit, and plotly entirely — i.e. the last built package metadata does not match the current pyproject, so any artifact built from that metadata ships an incomplete dependency set.
- **recommendation:** Raise floors to the minimum versions actually exercised (e.g. `openai>=2.0`, `anthropic>=0.84` or whatever the code requires) so the declared range matches reality. Rebuild the package so egg-info/dist metadata regenerates with the full current dependency list, or delete the stale egg-info from the tree.
- **verifier:** The finding fails on both of its claims when checked against the code.

CLAIM 1 — "stale floors understate / admit unsupported configs" (the load-bearing part of the medium severity): REFUTED.
- The openai mechanism is factually wrong. The audit asserts a clean install at openai 1.50 "would pull a pre-2.0 SDK whose client surface differs from what llm_router.py uses." But grep across src/ and scripts/ shows ZERO direct openai usage anywhere in sigma-predict. OpenAI is reached only through sigma_verify.PROVIDERS (src/pipeline/llm_router.py:121,175). sigma-predict's own code never touches the openai client surface, so its declared openai floor cannot "admit a config the code does not support." Any openai version concern belongs to sigma-verify's pin, not this repo's.
- The anthropic mechanism is also unsupported. The only direct LLM surface used is anthropic.Anthropic().messages.create(model=, max_tokens=, temperature=, system=, messages=[...]) with response.content[0].text and response.usage.input_tokens/output_tokens (llm_router.py:58-70). This is the stable messages API available since ~anthropic 0.18; the floor >=0.40.0 fully supports it. The audit identifies no 0.84-only feature the code requires — it hedges with "or whatever the code requires," which is conjecture. A floor sitting below the installed version is normal, intentional library practice that widens compatibility; the audit presents no config in the declared range that actually breaks.

CLAIM 2 — "egg-info is stale and missing deps": FACTUALLY TRUE but IMMATERIAL.
- requires.txt (dated Mar 13) does list only 8 deps and omits google-genai, sigma-verify, python-dotenv, streamlit, plotly that are in the current pyproject. That part of the observation is correct.
- But the consequence the audit draws is wrong. The egg-info is gitignored and NOT tracked in git (git check-ignore confirms IGNORED; git ls-files shows no egg-info). There is no dist/ dir. egg-info is a local, build-time-generated artifact that is regenerated from pyproject.toml (the authoritative source) on every install/build. Artifacts are built FROM pyproject, not FROM egg-info, so "any artifact built from that metadata ships an incomplete dependency set" does not happen. PKG-INFO version (0.1.0) matches pyproject, so no real version drift in shipped metadata.

Additional accuracy problem: the cited location for the supporting evidence is wrong — the audit references llm_router.py at top level, but it actually lives at src/pipeline/llm_router.py.

Net: the central technical mechanism (floors admit a broken config) is unsupported by the code, and the one true sub-observation (stale egg-info) describes a gitignored local artifact with no shipping or correctness impact. This does not rise to a genuine, material medium issue.

### `DEPS-93` — Internal dep pinned to a mutable floating reference, not an immutable revision
- **confirmed ✓** · source: framed · effort: small · file: `/Users/bjgilbert/Projects/Zoltar/sigma-predict/pyproject.toml:10`
- **evidence:** `sigma-verify>=0.1.0` resolves to a private repo (github.com/coloradored13/sigma-verify) currently at an editable checkout. sigma-verify itself declares Requires: google-genai, openai (pip show) — it transitively widens the external-provider attack/version surface. With only a `>=0.1.0` floor and no committed revision, the verification provider code (sigma_verify.PROVIDERS, consumed by llm_router.py) can change underneath sigma-predict with no version signal, and config.py verification_providers must stay manually in sync with that external registry.
- **recommendation:** Pin to an immutable tag/SHA via a direct git reference (see the dependency-confusion finding) and bump sigma-verify's own version on each provider-registry change so sigma-predict can pin a floor that means something. Add a startup check that config verification_providers names exist in sigma_verify.PROVIDERS to fail fast on drift.
- **verifier:** Code-grounded verification confirms the substantive core of this finding, though the security framing is overstated.

CONFIRMED:
- pyproject.toml:10 is exactly `sigma-verify>=0.1.0` — a floating floor with no tag/SHA/immutable pin.
- sigma-verify is installed as an EDITABLE install from a local path. `importlib.metadata` direct_url.json returns `{"dir_info": {"editable": true}, "url": "file:///Users/bjgilbert/Projects/sigma-verify"}` at version 0.1.0. This makes the `>=0.1.0` floor genuinely meaningless: the code under that path can change arbitrarily while the version string stays 0.1.0, so there is no version signal whatsoever.
- The consumption coupling is real. src/pipeline/llm_router.py imports `from sigma_verify import PROVIDERS` (lines 121, 128, 175, 179) and `classify_error, VerificationError` (266, 357). PROVIDERS is the live registry driving which external verification providers get instantiated.
- The manual-sync claim is accurate. src/config.py:99-105 declares `verification_providers` as a hardcoded list that must mirror PROVIDERS names; llm_router.py:126 filters PROVIDERS by this `allowed` set. Verified PROVIDERS currently exposes 13 keys; config lists 10 — already a partial divergence by design.
- The drift-failure concern is well-founded and partly SILENT: in `_init_external_providers` (lines 128-153) a config-listed provider that no longer exists in PROVIDERS is silently skipped (loop never yields it), degrading the verification pool with no error. A hard `ValueError("Unknown provider")` only fires later at call-time in `_get_or_create_client` (line 181), not at startup. So the recommended startup cross-check is justified.

OVERREACH (refuted portions):
- The "external-provider attack surface" / dependency-confusion framing is weak. The dependency resolves to a LOCAL editable filesystem path on the developer's machine, not a public index — pip is not resolving `>=0.1.0` against PyPI today, so there is no live dependency-confusion vector from this line.
- The transitive-widening point (google-genai, openai) is immaterial: sigma-predict already declares both directly at pyproject.toml:8-9, so sigma-verify adds no new external surface.

Net: the finding is genuine and material as a dependency-hygiene / reproducibility / silent-drift issue, but its security/attack-surface justification does not hold up.

### `TEST-100` — Verification agreement scoring (_compute_agreement) and outlier gate (should_verify_run) are pure logic but untested
- **confirmed ✓** · source: framed · effort: small · file: `/Users/bjgilbert/Projects/Zoltar/sigma-predict/src/pipeline/verification.py:40-50,181-210`
- **evidence:** _compute_agreement returns 'no_data'/'unanimous'/'none'/'partial' based on set cardinality of assessments among successful entries (lines 46-50) — four distinct branches, none covered. should_verify_run has branches for 'none' (False), 'all' (True), <2 runs (True), and the numpy outlier test `abs(run.probability - mean_p) > outlier_threshold` (lines 196-210). test_cross_pollination covers router caching and config but not these scoring functions. A regression in the agreement set logic would silently mislabel cross-model consensus (which feeds deliberation and human-review flags).
- **recommendation:** Add unit tests building lists of VerificationEntry with mixed status/assessment to cover all four _compute_agreement outcomes (including all-failed -> no_data), and RunResult lists to cover should_verify_run none/all/single/outlier-above/outlier-below.
- **verifier:** CONFIRMED. Code-grounded verification:

(1) The cited functions exist exactly as described. /Users/bjgilbert/Projects/Zoltar/sigma-predict/src/pipeline/verification.py:40-50 — `_compute_agreement` has four branches: `no_data` (no successes, line 44), `unanimous` (1 distinct assessment, line 47), `none` (all distinct, line 49), `partial` (fallthrough, line 50). Lines 181-210 — `should_verify_run` has branches: `none`->False (197), `all`->True (199), `<2 runs`->True (204), and the numpy outlier test `bool(deviation > outlier_threshold)` (210). VERIFIED against source.

(2) Untested claim is accurate. `grep -rn` across ./tests/ for `_compute_agreement`, `should_verify_run`, and any `verification` import returned nothing for the function calls. The two test files touching verification symbols only construct `VerificationEntry` as a data model (test_pipeline_contract.py:183-193) and exercise verification config/router availability (test_cross_pollination.py). Neither invokes the two scoring functions. Both functions are pure (no I/O, deterministic), so they are trivially testable in isolation — there is no reason coverage should be absent. VERIFIED.

(3) Materiality is partially overstated but still real. The audit claims agreement "feeds deliberation and human-review flags." I checked the consumers: deliberation (deliberation.py:40) triggers on `aggregation.inter_model_agreement`, a SEPARATE metric from the aggregator, NOT on `_compute_agreement`'s output. Human-review FLAGS (human_review.py:355-383) iterate over raw `entry.assessment` values ("disagree"/"partial"), NOT over the `stage.agreement` string. The `_compute_agreement` output actually drives the human-review DISPLAY color-coding (human_review.py:247-255) and is stored on StageVerification. So a regression in `_compute_agreement` would mislabel the displayed consensus level, not silently corrupt the deliberation/flag decisions as the evidence implies.

(4) `should_verify_run` is the more material of the two: it directly gates whether expensive cross-model verification runs at orchestrator.py:235. A regression in the numpy outlier comparison would change which forecasts get externally verified — a genuine behavioral control with no coverage.

Net: the finding is real (two untested pure-logic functions with multiple branches, one of which gates real verification behavior). The recommendation (parametrized unit tests covering all four agreement outcomes and the none/all/single/outlier branches) is sound and cheap to implement.
