REFUTED / OVERSTATED (this is why severity should drop):
- The finding's key evidence claim "no test feeds a value >1.0 to confirm the /100 branch" is FACTUALLY WRONG. tests/test_platform_clients.py:87-95 (test_market_with_price) passes last_price: 65 (>1.0) and asserts community_prediction == 0.65, which directly exercises the `val / 100.0 if val > 1.0` branch at kalshi.py:30. The auditor's headline example is already covered.
- The get_resolution citation is imprecise for Kalshi: Kalshi's get_resolution (kalshi.py:160-162) unconditionally returns None with no try/except — it does not "silently swallow schema drift," so it is not one of the cited degradation paths.
- "Metaculus missing alternate keys not covered" is weak: test_missing_community_prediction (lines 48-51) already tests missing/None/empty community_prediction via _extract_community_prediction.

MATERIALITY: These are read-only stub clients in a prediction-aggregation tool; the untested paths degrade gracefully (empty list / None) by design, so blast radius is low. The genuine remaining gaps (untested error-swallowing on schema drift, borderline 1.0 heuristic, missing-price mapping) are real but minor coverage gaps in non-critical code. Combined with a demonstrably incorrect central evidence statement, medium is too high.

### `TEST-103` — fit_isotonic_logit (isotonic fitting) imported but never exercised; only the lookup transform is tested
- **confirmed ✓** · source: framed · effort: small · file: `/Users/bjgilbert/Projects/Zoltar/sigma-predict/src/pipeline/calibration.py:133-169; /Users/bjgilbert/Projects/Zoltar/sigma-predict/tests/test_calibration.py:10`
- **evidence:** test_calibration imports fit_isotonic_logit (line 10) but grep shows no call site in the test body — only isotonic_transform (the interpolation lookup) is tested via TestIsotonicTransform. The fitting itself (logit transform of clipped preds, scipy isotonic_regression, breakpoint capping at max(3, n//5) and uniform subsampling lines 157-162) is unverified. The Phase-1 calibration path (n_resolved 20-49) calls fit_isotonic_logit on live data, so a regression in monotonicity or breakpoint capping would silently mis-calibrate every Phase-1 prediction.
- **recommendation:** Add a test calling fit_isotonic_logit on a monotone-increasing dataset asserting the returned breakpoints are sorted by logit_x and y-values are non-decreasing (isotonic invariant), and that len(breakpoints) <= max(3, n//5).
- **verifier:** The finding is partially correct but its central factual claim is wrong. CLAIM: "fit_isotonic_logit imported but never exercised." VERIFIED FALSE — grep confirms it appears in tests only at the import (line 10) with no direct call, BUT test_phase1_uses_isotonic (tests/test_calibration.py:161-170) calls calibrate(0.7, config, n_resolved=25, isotonic_data=[(0.3,0.0)]*5 + [(0.7,1.0)]*5). With n_resolved=25 >= min_resolved_for_isotonic (20) and 10 isotonic pairs (>=5), this routes through calibration.py:70-74 and DOES call fit_isotonic_logit at runtime. Moreover max_bps = max(3, 25//5) = 5 and len(iso_probs)=10 > 5, so the subsampling branch (lines 158-162) the finding specifically calls out as "unverified" is in fact EXECUTED. So the function runs end-to-end (clip, logit, scipy isotonic_regression, uniform subsampling) — there is no import-only-no-execution gap.

WHAT IS REAL: No test asserts anything about fit_isotonic_logit's OUTPUT correctness. test_phase1_uses_isotonic only asserts 0.01<=prob<=0.99 and params_dict is None. The bounds assertion is satisfied by isotonic_transform's own clamp (calibration.py clamps to [0.01,0.99]) regardless of whether fitting is correct, so it cannot detect a monotonicity or breakpoint-capping regression. The isotonic invariant (non-decreasing y), logit-x sorting, and the cap len <= max(3, n//5) are never asserted. The recommended test would add genuine behavioral coverage. So a real test-quality gap exists.

NET: genuine gap, but mis-described. The accurate framing is "fit_isotonic_logit is executed via calibrate but its output is never asserted (only bounds/routing are checked)," not "imported but never exercised / fitting unverified by execution." The "silently mis-calibrate every Phase-1 prediction" risk is overstated: the output is range-clamped and the path is exercised, so catastrophic crashes/range violations are already covered; only subtle correctness regressions slip through.

### `IMPR-176` — Forecast call and pre-mortem call run back-to-back when the pre-mortem is deliberately decoupled from the forecast result
- **confirmed ✓** · source: improvement · effort: small · file: `/Users/bjgilbert/Projects/Zoltar/sigma-predict/src/pipeline/forecaster.py:161-208`
- **evidence:** run_forecast issues the main forecast (line 161) and then the pre-mortem (line 204) as two sequential LLM round-trips. The pre-mortem is explicitly contamination-controlled to NOT see the forecast's probability or reasoning (lines 186-202 strip them; pre_mortem_user is built only from question + sub_questions + actor analysis, all known before the forecast call). So the pre-mortem call has no data dependency on the forecast call's response — they are forced sequential only by code structure. The only coupling is at line 222 where pm_delta is applied to `probability`, which happens after both return.
- **recommendation:** Issue the forecast call and the pre-mortem call concurrently (both inputs are available up front), then combine results. Halves per-run LLM latency. Composes with the run-level parallelization above. If full parallelism feels risky, this is the lower-effort subset that still cuts each run's critical path roughly in half.
- **verifier:** Verified against /Users/bjgilbert/Projects/Zoltar/sigma-predict/src/pipeline/forecaster.py and llm_router.py. The claimed current state is accurate:

- Line 161 issues the main forecast via router.call(); line 204 issues the pre-mortem via a second router.call(). Two sequential LLM round-trips, same provider/model.
- The pre-mortem's input (pre_mortem_user, lines 189-202) is built ONLY from question.title, question.resolution_criteria, decomposition.sub_questions, and _format_actor_analysis(decomposition). All of these are available before line 161. The contamination-control comment (lines 186-188) confirms this is deliberate: the pre-mortem must NOT see the forecast's probability/reasoning.
- Confirmed the pre-mortem call does not consume `result`, `data`, or `probability`. The only coupling is post-return: pm_delta applied to probability at lines 220-223. So the two calls have no forward data dependency and are forced sequential purely by code structure. The latency claim (roughly halving each run's two-call critical path) is arithmetically sound.

Not reductive — no deletion safety check needed.

Caveats that justify lowering priority rather than refuting:
1. Thread-safety is a real prerequisite, and the router already documents it (llm_router.py:82-84: "Add a threading.Lock around _client_cache access if concurrent calls are introduced"). The Anthropic primary path creates a fresh client per call (line 58) so the cache risk is low there, but the shared self._audit.log_call (line 215) is not documented thread-safe. So the "lower-effort subset" framing understates the work.
2. The cleaner, larger win is run-level parallelization: orchestrator.py:207-228 runs num_runs forecasts strictly sequentially in a for-loop with no concurrency anywhere in the codebase (grep confirms no ThreadPool/asyncio/threading outside the docstring). Parallelizing at the run level captures most wall-clock benefit. Layering intra-run concurrency ON TOP adds a second concurrency layer and multiplies the thread-safety surface (cache + audit logger) for a smaller incremental gain. The proposal itself frames this as a subset of run-level parallelization, which is the right ordering — do the run level first.

The opportunity is genuine and grounded in the code, not speculative or churn.

### `IMPR-177` — Calibration step scans and re-parses the entire registry twice in immediate succession
- **confirmed ✓** · source: improvement · effort: trivial · file: `/Users/bjgilbert/Projects/Zoltar/sigma-predict/src/pipeline/orchestrator.py:335-337`
- **evidence:** `n_resolved = self.query.count_resolved()` (line 335) calls get_resolved() -> store.load_all(), which opens registry.jsonl, reads every line, and constructs a full PredictionRecord via Pydantic for each. Immediately after, `pairs = self.query.get_calibration_pairs()` (line 337) calls get_resolved() -> load_all() AGAIN — a second full file read and full Pydantic re-parse of every record. count_resolved() is literally `len(self.get_resolved())` (query.py:123-125), so the two calls compute over the identical materialized list. Pydantic model construction over hundreds-to-thousands of records is the expensive part, paid twice per prediction.
- **recommendation:** Materialize resolved records once and derive both values: `resolved = self.query.get_resolved(); n_resolved = len(resolved); pairs = [...derive from resolved...]`. Or have get_calibration_pairs accept an optional pre-loaded list. Cuts registry I/O and Pydantic parsing in the calibration step by 50% with a trivial refactor and zero behavior change.
- **verifier:** The described current state is verified exactly against the code. In src/pipeline/orchestrator.py, the calibration step runs `n_resolved = self.query.count_resolved()` (line 335) then `pairs = self.query.get_calibration_pairs()` (line 337) back-to-back. In src/registry/query.py, count_resolved() is `len(self.get_resolved())` (lines 123-125) and get_calibration_pairs() iterates `self.get_resolved()` (line 111). get_resolved() (lines 81-85) calls self._store.load_all(). In src/registry/store.py, load_all() (lines 37-54) has no caching: it opens registry.jsonl, reads every line, runs json.loads, and constructs a PredictionRecord Pydantic model per line on every call. So the two adjacent calls do perform a full file read + full Pydantic re-parse of every record twice. The redundancy is real and the claim is accurate.

The fix is trivial and SAFE (not a reductive/deletion proposal). There are exactly two callers (the two adjacent lines at 335/337 — confirmed by grep), so materializing once — `resolved = self.query.get_resolved(); n_resolved = len(resolved)` and deriving pairs from `resolved` (or passing resolved into get_calibration_pairs as an optional arg) — is zero-behavior-change and breaks nothing. get_calibration_pairs already contains the inline derivation logic, so refactoring it to accept a pre-loaded list is straightforward.

However the PERFORMANCE payoff is overstated. (1) This runs once per prediction, not in a tight loop. The surrounding pipeline (predict_until_review) is dominated by multiple LLM/network calls — decompose, base-rate estimate, search, aggregation, optional deliberation — each costing seconds. A second JSONL scan + Pydantic parse of hundreds-to-low-thousands of records is sub-millisecond to low-millisecond; it is a rounding error against LLM latency. (2) The store's own design (update() rewrites the entire file via _write_all; docstring explicitly states 'hundreds-to-low-thousands of records') shows the project deliberately accepts O(n) full-file operations at this scale, so the double-read is consistent with the codebase's accepted performance posture, not an anomaly. (3) The framing that 'Pydantic construction over hundreds-to-thousands of records is the expensive part, paid twice per prediction' is the inflated piece — it is paid twice, but the absolute cost is negligible relative to surrounding work. This is a clean-code / redundant-work removal worth shipping because it is free, not a meaningful performance optimization.

### `IMPR-179` — A fresh httpx.Client (new TCP/TLS connection) is created and torn down for every individual search query
- **confirmed ✓** · source: improvement · effort: small · file: `/Users/bjgilbert/Projects/Zoltar/sigma-predict/src/pipeline/search.py:97-104`
- **evidence:** search() wraps each request in `with httpx.Client(timeout=30.0) as client:` — a new client (and therefore a new connection pool, new TCP handshake, new TLS negotiation) per call. search_with_persona (lines 144-150) loops over 3-5 persona templates calling self.search() each time, so a single persona run pays the full connect+TLS cost 3-5 times against the same api.tavily.com host. Across an N-run prediction that is num_runs × ~5 cold connections.
- **recommendation:** Hold one httpx.Client as a SearchModule instance attribute (created in __init__, closed in a close()/context-manager) so all persona queries reuse a keep-alive connection pool to api.tavily.com. Eliminates repeated TLS handshakes. If/when run-level parallelization lands, the persona queries within a run can also be issued concurrently against that shared pool. Reductive note: this removes per-call client construction rather than adding a layer.
- **verifier:** Current state confirmed against the code. search() at search.py:97-104 wraps every request in `with httpx.Client(timeout=30.0) as client:`, so each call performs a fresh TCP+TLS handshake and tears the pool down. search_with_persona (lines 144-150) loops over 3-5 persona templates calling self.search() each time, and orchestrator.py:207 loops that over num_runs personas, plus one more search in search_augmented_base_rate (base_rate.py:127). So per prediction the cold-connection count is num_runs×(3-5)+1, exactly as claimed. There is no existing close()/context-manager on SearchModule, so this is not already done.

Reductive-safety check: only three files reference SearchModule (orchestrator.py, base_rate.py, search.py). grep over tests/ returns ZERO references to SearchModule, httpx, or search — no test pins the per-call client construction, and no caller depends on it. Moving the client to __init__ is safe; the only caller-side requirement is adding a .close()/context-manager at the two short-lived instantiation sites (orchestrator.py:175 and :205) so sockets aren't leaked. That is a minor addition, not a break.

Where the proposal is inflated: the dominant per-query wall-time cost is Tavily's server-side search execution (hundreds of ms to seconds), not the ~50-200ms TLS/TCP setup. Connection reuse only saves the handshake on calls 2..N within one SearchModule's lifetime — a real but secondary saving. The actual large win is that a shared keep-alive pool is a PREREQUISITE for the run-level/persona-level concurrency the proposal lists as a follow-on; the multiplicative latency reduction comes from concurrency, not from reuse alone. As a standalone change it is correct, safe, and low-risk, but modest in payoff.

### `IMPR-181` — Cross-model verification calls every external provider sequentially
- **confirmed ✓** · source: improvement · effort: small · file: `/Users/bjgilbert/Projects/Zoltar/sigma-predict/src/pipeline/llm_router.py:332-369`
- **evidence:** cross_verify iterates `for client in self._external_clients():` and calls `client.verify(finding, context)` (a network LLM round-trip) one provider at a time, accumulating into results. This is invoked per verified stage — verify_base_rate, verify_actors, and verify_forecast_run all call router.cross_verify (verification.py:75,127,164), and verify_forecast_run can fire once per outlier run (orchestrator.py:234-241). With multiple external providers, each verified stage waits for provider1 then provider2 then provider3 serially, when the providers are fully independent.
- **recommendation:** Fan out the per-client verify() calls in cross_verify concurrently (ThreadPoolExecutor over self._external_clients()), preserving result ordering on collection. Verification latency per stage drops from sum-of-providers to max-of-providers. Highest impact when verification is enabled with 2-3 providers across several stages.
- **verifier:** VERIFIED. The current state is exactly as described. cross_verify (llm_router.py:322-369) iterates `for client in self._external_clients():` and calls the network LLM round-trip `client.verify(finding, context)` one provider at a time, appending to `results`. All three verified stages call router.cross_verify (verification.py:75, 127, 164), and verify_forecast_run fires once per qualifying run inside the orchestrator loop (orchestrator.py:233-241). The per-client verify() calls are fully independent — separate provider instances, separate network endpoints, no shared mutable state between iterations — so fanning them out with a ThreadPoolExecutor and collecting in order is sound, and latency per stage drops from sum-of-providers to max-of-providers.

The payoff is actually UNDERSTATED by the proposal. It assumes "2-3 providers," but the default config (config.py:102-106) ships verification_providers with 10 entries (openai, google, llama, gemma, nemotron, deepseek, qwen, devstral, glm, kimi). _external_clients() excludes only the primary, so cross_verify can serialize up to ~9-10 LLM round-trips per stage. With verify_forecasts defaulting to "outliers" and one cross_verify per outlier run plus base_rate and (conditionally) actors, the serial cost compounds across stages. This is a real, high-value latency win on the default configuration.

Concurrency safety of the proposed fix: cross_verify does NOT touch the _client_cache — it iterates already-instantiated clients held in _external_providers, so the docstring warning at llm_router.py:80-84 ("add a threading.Lock around _client_cache access if concurrent calls are introduced") does not apply to this path. The one genuine shared-state hazard the implementer must handle is the audit logger: _audit.log_call mutates a shared list buffer and may trigger flush() (llm_audit.py:104-106), and it is explicitly documented as single-threaded (llm_audit.py:11-12). The fan-out must serialize the log_call/append step (e.g., collect results from futures and log on the main thread, or guard with a lock) rather than calling log_call from inside worker threads. This is a normal, well-understood implementation detail, not a blocker.

Not a reductive proposal, so no removal-safety concern. No test exercises cross_verify or pins sequential ordering (grep of tests/ found only a verification output-shape assertion with verification disabled in test_pipeline_contract.py:144), so the refactor will not break the suite.

One scoping note for the implementer: per-stage fan-out parallelizes within a stage, but the orchestrator still calls stages and per-run verifications sequentially; for the "outliers across several runs" case, the larger win would be parallelizing the per-run verify_forecast_run calls in the orchestrator loop too. That is out of scope for this specific opportunity (which targets cross_verify internals) but worth noting as the natural follow-on.

### `IMPR-183` — Registry update() rewrites and re-parses the entire file on every single-record change
- **REFUTED ✗** · source: improvement · effort: small · file: `/Users/bjgilbert/Projects/Zoltar/sigma-predict/src/registry/store.py:73-107`
- **evidence:** update() calls load_all() (full read + Pydantic parse of every record), mutates one element, then _write_all() (full re-serialize + rewrite of every record). resolve.py calls update() once per resolved record inside a loop (resolve.py:113), making batch resolution O(n^2) in both parse and serialize cost. The UI also calls store.update() on every human review and every lesson-add (2_Predict.py:376, 3_Review.py:288), each rewriting the whole file. model_dump_json over every record is paid on each call.
- **recommendation:** For batch flows, expose an update_many(records) that loads once, applies all changes, and writes once — eliminating the O(n^2). resolve.py would call it after the loop instead of inside it. For single UI updates the full rewrite is acceptable at stated scale, but update_many removes the quadratic blowup in the CLI batch path with one method and a one-line caller change. Reductive angle: this collapses N read-modify-write cycles into one, reducing total I/O rather than adding caching machinery.
- **verifier:** The described mechanism is accurate but the performance payoff is not credible at the stated scale, so this is churn dressed up as an optimization.

VERIFIED facts (current state is as described):
- store.update() (src/registry/store.py:79,94) does load_all() (full read + Pydantic parse of every record) then _write_all() (full re-serialize via model_dump_json over every record). Correct.
- The batch caller is scripts/resolve.py:113 (the claim says resolve.py:113 — minor path inaccuracy), called inside the loop at lines 86-114. Correct.
- UI callers exist: pages/2_Predict.py:376 (cache._store.update) and pages/3_Review.py:288. Correct.
- update() is matched by prediction_id and rewrites the whole file. Correct.

REFUTING evidence (payoff is inflated / not credible):
1. Network latency dominates by 2-3 orders of magnitude. Every record that reaches store.update() at resolve.py:113 first made a BLOCKING HTTP round-trip to Metaculus at resolve.py:94 (metaculus.get_resolution -> self._client.get, src/platforms/metaculus.py:159). update() is only called for records that newly flip to resolved this run (guards at lines 87/89/99 skip the rest), so the count of update() calls K equals the count of network calls. A remote API round-trip is ~100ms-1s; re-reading and re-serializing a few hundred-to-low-thousand JSONL records is single-digit ms. Collapsing K file rewrites into one saves negligible wall-clock time when each iteration is already paying a network round-trip.
2. The "O(n^2)" framing only bites when K (resolved-this-run) approaches N (total), and even then the quadratic file cost sits behind K blocking network calls that dwarf it.
3. The store's own docstring (store.py:16-21) explicitly states full-file rewrite is "safe for the expected volume of hundreds-to-low-thousands of records." The full rewrite is a deliberate, documented design choice for the documented scale.
4. The "reductive" framing is a stretch: update_many is an ADDITIVE new method, not a deletion. update() cannot be removed — it is exercised by tests (tests/test_registry.py:94,104) and both UI pages. The change adds a method plus a buffering-and-flush in resolve.py; net line count is roughly flat, not reduced.

SAFETY note (per reductive-proposal rule): nothing is being deleted, so no caller/test breakage risk — but that also means the "collapses N cycles into one, reducing I/O rather than adding machinery" pitch overstates it; it does add a method and a loop-accumulator.

Bottom line: real mechanism, non-credible payoff. The hot path is network-bound, not I/O-bound, and the design is explicitly scoped for small volumes. This is optimization for a cost that is invisible at the stated scale.

### `IMPR-188` — Silent fallbacks in base-rate search augmentation and platform-signal collection leave no signal in the record
- **confirmed ✓** · source: improvement · effort: small · file: `/Users/bjgilbert/Projects/Zoltar/sigma-predict/src/pipeline/base_rate.py:128`
- **evidence:** search_augmented_base_rate returns `base_rate, ""` unchanged on four distinct failure paths (search exception 128-130, no results 132-134, LLM call exception 207-209, parse failure 214-216) — all only logger.warning/info, none recorded on the PredictionRecord. From the registry alone you cannot distinguish 'search augmentation ran and found nothing' from 'search augmentation crashed' from 'never attempted'. Similarly _collect_platform_signals swallows the community-prediction fetch error at DEBUG (orchestrator.py:552-553), so a persistently-failing platform price fetch silently yields empty platform_signals with no flag — and human_review._generate_flags depends on that community price being present. estimate_cost silently maps unknown models to Sonnet pricing (llm_router.py:409) with no warning, so cost telemetry for a renamed/new model is wrong-but-plausible and nothing signals it.
- **recommendation:** Route these soft failures into the existing `record.pipeline_warnings` list (already the established channel — see orchestrator.py:200,245,292) rather than only logging. e.g. append 'base_rate_search_augmentation_failed: <reason>' and 'platform_signal_fetch_failed'. For estimate_cost, add a one-time logger.warning when a model misses the pricing table. This reuses existing infrastructure — no new layer.
- **verifier:** VERIFIED against the actual code. The proposal's claims are substantially accurate, and verification surfaced additional corroborating defects that strengthen (not weaken) the case.

CONFIRMED CLAIMS:
1. search_augmented_base_rate (base_rate.py:89-255) returns `base_rate, ""` on four distinct soft-failure paths — search exception (128-130), no results (132-134), LLM call exception (207-209), parse failure (214-216) — plus two more the proposal did not enumerate: all-results-excluded-by-temporal-firewall (160-165) and no-numerical-rate-extracted (222-227). Every one is logger.warning/info only. Critically, the function signature has NO warnings channel parameter at all (unlike its sibling estimate_base_rate at line 26 and decompose_question at decomposer.py:28, both of which already take `warnings_out`). So the orchestrator caller (orchestrator.py:176-187) genuinely cannot distinguish "ran, found nothing" from "crashed" from "never attempted" — exactly as claimed. The fix is well-scoped: add a `warnings_out` param mirroring the established sibling pattern. |source: src/pipeline/base_rate.py| VERIFIED HIGH

2. _collect_platform_signals (orchestrator.py:544-553) swallows the fetch error at logger.debug with no flag — confirmed verbatim. human_review._generate_flags DOES depend on the community price (human_review.py:340-344: divergence flag keyed on it). VERIFIED.

3. estimate_cost (llm_router.py:409) silently maps unknown models to Sonnet pricing `(3.0, 15.0)` with an inline comment and no warning — confirmed verbatim. Called from llm_router.py:214 (every API call) and forecaster.py:252. A renamed/new model produces wrong-but-plausible cost telemetry silently. VERIFIED.

ADDITIONAL DEFECTS FOUND that reinforce the thesis (the area is genuinely under-instrumented):
- DEAD LOCAL WARNINGS LIST: orchestrator.py:112 creates a local `pipeline_warnings = []`, appends the verification-unavailable warning at line 134, and NEVER copies it to record.pipeline_warnings. That signal is silently dropped — a pre-existing observability bug in the very channel the proposal wants to reuse. Worth fixing in the same pass.
- KEY MISMATCH (latent): _collect_platform_signals stores the community price under `question.platform.value` = "metaculus" (the `else` branch at line 551, since the dict starts empty so the `if platform_key in ...` is always False on first write), but _generate_flags reads `ps.get("metaculus_community")` (human_review.py:340). So the divergence flag never fires even on SUCCESSFUL fetch. This means the platform-signals path is doubly broken: silent on failure AND mis-keyed on success. The proposed platform_signal_fetch_failed warning addresses the failure half; the key mismatch is a separate bug the reviewer should be told about.

PAYOFF IS CREDITABLE: This is a registry-recorded analytical pipeline where the record IS the deliverable. Silent fallbacks that alter the base rate (a locked anchor fed to all forecast runs) or zero out platform signals (which gate human-review flags) without leaving a trace directly degrade auditability of predictions. pipeline_warnings is the established, already-wired channel (orchestrator.py:200,245,261,271,292,359; validator.py built around it). Routing soft failures there reuses existing infrastructure with no new layer — low cost, real benefit.

NOT REDUCTIVE — purely additive instrumentation, no deletion-safety concern.

ONE CORRECTION to the proposal's framing: it lists four failure paths in search_augmented_base_rate; there are actually six. Minor — strengthens the case.

### `IMPR-189` — Calibration phase and inputs are logged but never persisted — registry record cannot explain how a calibrated probability was produced
- **confirmed ✓** · source: improvement · effort: small · file: `/Users/bjgilbert/Projects/Zoltar/sigma-predict/src/pipeline/calibration.py:62`
- **evidence:** calibrate() chooses among Platt / isotonic / scaled-hedging based on n_resolved (calibration.py:62-100) and logs which phase fired at INFO. But the returned CalibrationData (models.py:126-129) stores only platt_params_used + calibrated_probability + CI. The phase ('platt'/'isotonic'/'hedging'/'isotonic_fallback'), n_resolved at calibration time, and the raw->calibrated delta are not saved. resolve.py's classify_error later flags CALIBRATION_FAILURE (resolve.py:47-50) on a bad outcome but has no way to know the prediction was made under Phase 0 hedging with n_resolved=3 vs Phase 2 Platt with n_resolved=200 — which completely changes whether the failure is expected noise or a real calibration regression. The diagnostic context is computed and discarded.
- **recommendation:** Add `phase: str` and `n_resolved_at_calibration: int` fields to CalibrationData and set them from calibrate()'s return (return the phase string alongside the dict). This is a few model fields + plumbing the value already computed at orchestrator.py:335. Makes every calibrated prediction self-describing without re-running anything.
- **verifier:** Grounded in the actual code (note: real path is src/models.py, not src/pipeline/models.py — the cited line numbers 126-129 are correct).

CURRENT STATE CONFIRMED: calibrate() (calibration.py:62-100) selects Phase 2 (Platt) / Phase 1 (isotonic) / isotonic_fallback / Phase 0 (scaled hedging) by n_resolved and logs each at INFO, but returns only tuple(calibrated_probability, platt_params_dict_or_None). CalibrationData (models.py:126-129) persists only platt_params_used, calibrated_probability, confidence_interval. The phase string and n_resolved-at-calibration are computed then discarded — verified by grep: no `phase` or `n_resolved_at` field exists anywhere.

DIAGNOSTIC GAP REAL: classify_error (resolve.py:23-64) flags CALIBRATION_FAILURE from brier>0.25 AND abs(cal_prob-base_rate)>0.4, reading record.calibration.calibrated_probability and record.decomposition.base_rate. Nothing in the record tells it which calibration regime produced cal_prob, so a Phase-0 hedge at n_resolved=3 and a Phase-2 Platt fit at n_resolved=200 are indistinguishable at resolution time — exactly as claimed. This materially changes whether a high-Brier outcome is expected small-sample noise or a real regression.

PLUMBING COST AS CLAIMED (LOW): one production caller (orchestrator.py:339), one CalibrationData construction (orchestrator.py:351), and n_resolved already computed at orchestrator.py:335. Changing the return to a 3-tuple touches that caller plus 6 test call sites (tests/test_calibration.py:157-201). Adding two Pydantic fields with defaults is backward-compatible with existing registry JSON. Not reductive — purely additive observability, so no deletion-safety concern.

ONE OVERSTATEMENT IN THE PROPOSAL: it implies the phase is fully unrecoverable. In fact platt_params_used is non-None only for Phase 2, so Platt-vs-not-Platt IS already recoverable. The genuine gap is (a) Phase 1 isotonic / Phase 0 hedging / isotonic_fallback all collapse to platt_params_used=None and are mutually indistinguishable, and (b) n_resolved is lost in every case. So the gap is narrower than 'completely changes' suggests but still real and worth closing.

### `IMPR-190` — No calibration-drift metric — dashboard reports lifetime-average Brier only, masking recent regression
- **REFUTED ✗** · source: improvement · effort: small · file: `/Users/bjgilbert/Projects/Zoltar/sigma-predict/scripts/dashboard.py:47`
- **evidence:** dashboard.py computes a single lifetime `avg_brier` over all resolved pairs (dashboard.py:47-52) plus by-domain/by-platform breakdowns. There is no time-windowed view: a model that calibrated well over 200 historical predictions but has degraded over the last 20 (e.g. after a model version bump or prompt change) is invisible — the lifetime average absorbs it. resolve.py computes per-record Brier but no rolling comparison. For a forecasting system whose entire value is calibration, the absence of a 'recent vs baseline Brier' signal means calibration drift — the single most important failure mode — has no detector.
- **recommendation:** Add a rolling-window Brier comparison to dashboard.py: last-N (or last-30-days) Brier vs lifetime Brier, flagging when recent exceeds baseline by a threshold. The records already carry created_at/resolved_at and submitted.probability; query.py already has get_calibration_pairs. This is a reductive-friendly add — a single derived metric computed from existing data, not new instrumentation in the hot path. Could also replace the by-platform table if that is rarely actionable.
- **verifier:** The proposal's load-bearing claim — that calibration drift "has no detector" and is "invisible" because the system reports only lifetime-average Brier — is false for the system as a whole. The user-facing observability surface is the Streamlit prediction hub added in v0.2.0/v0.3.0 (app.py + pages/), not the CLI dashboard.py. That hub already contains exactly the requested signal:

1. components/charts.py:85 `brier_timeline(records, window=7)` computes a rolling-average Brier over time (default 7-prediction window) and plots it as a line against individual per-prediction Brier markers. It is rendered on pages/4_Scoreboard.py:140-142. A rising rolling line vs the flat lifetime baseline IS the "recent vs baseline Brier" drift view the proposal asks for.
2. pages/4_Scoreboard.py:42 has a `created_at` date-range filter, so the entire calibration view (including Brier) can be scoped to any recent window.
3. pages/1_Dashboard.py:31 `_in_current_month` already slices a recent (current-month) view.

So the premise is contradicted by existing code. The data fields the proposal cites (created_at, resolution.resolved_at — confirmed at src/models.py:217 and :155) do exist, and get_calibration_pairs exists (query.py), but they are already leveraged for time-windowed views in the Scoreboard.

The narrow residual that IS true: the standalone CLI scripts/dashboard.py (the cited location, line 47-52) computes only lifetime avg_brier plus by-domain/by-platform breakdowns with no time window. But (a) the CLI is not the primary observability surface, and (b) adding a rolling metric there duplicates what the Scoreboard already renders — low-value churn, not the "single most important failure mode has no detector" emergency described.

The one genuinely missing piece is an automatic threshold ALERT/FLAG (recent exceeds baseline by X) — both the timeline and date filter require a human to eyeball them. That is a legitimate but minor enhancement, far narrower than the proposal's framing, and it belongs on the Scoreboard (which already has the rolling data wired) rather than the CLI dashboard. The proposal does not describe this; it describes building a detector that substantially already exists.

The "could replace the by-platform table" reductive aside is speculative with no evidence the table is unactionable; refute that too. Not safe/justified as a reduction.

### `IMPR-192` — Streamlit background pipeline thread collapses all failures to a single 'ERROR:' string with no stack/context
- **confirmed ✓** · source: improvement · effort: trivial · file: `/Users/bjgilbert/Projects/Zoltar/sigma-predict/pages/2_Predict.py:82`
- **evidence:** _run_pipeline wraps the entire Orchestrator.predict() in `except Exception as e: q.put(f"ERROR: {e}")` and clears the slot (2_Predict.py:82-84). The exception type, traceback, and which pipeline stage failed are all discarded — only str(e) reaches the UI queue. There is no logger.exception call in the thread, and Streamlit threads do not inherit the script's logging config, so the stack trace is lost entirely. A failure deep in a provider call surfaces to the operator as a one-line message with no way to diagnose, and per the repo map (2_Predict.py:282-283) an empty slot can even reset to idle making it look like 'nothing happened'.
- **recommendation:** In the except branch add `logger.exception("pipeline thread failed")` (or traceback.format_exc() into the queue message) so the full context is captured to stderr/log even though the UI shows the short message. One added line; converts an undebuggable UI failure into a traceable one.
- **verifier:** Verified against the actual code. All load-bearing claims hold.

CURRENT STATE (confirmed exactly):
- pages/2_Predict.py:82-84 reads `except Exception as e: q.put(f"ERROR: {e}") / completed_slot.clear()`. Only str(e) is captured; type, traceback, and failing stage are discarded.
- No `import logging` and no `logger` anywhere in pages/2_Predict.py (grep: zero matches).
- The pipeline does extensive structured logging: src/pipeline/orchestrator.py uses `logger = logging.getLogger(__name__)` with ~30 info/warning calls across the 7 stages — exactly the context that is lost.
- logging.basicConfig is called ONLY in CLI scripts (scripts/predict.py, resolve.py, batch_predict.py), never in app.py or any page. So in the Streamlit app no root handler/config is configured. The proposal's framing ("threads don't inherit the script's logging config") is slightly imprecise — the real root cause is the Streamlit app never configures logging at all — but the conclusion (stack trace is lost) is identical and correct.

PAYOFF (credible, verified empirically): I ran a simulation with no basicConfig and a fresh module logger; `logger.exception('pipeline thread failed')` still emits the full traceback to stderr via Python's last-resort handler (logs at ERROR > WARNING threshold). So the proposed one-liner genuinely converts a context-free failure into a traceable one on the Streamlit server console, with no config changes required.

UNDERSTATED, IF ANYTHING: The line 282-283 "reset to idle" claim is real (slot is cleared on error, so the None-sentinel branch hits the else and sets state to idle). It is actually worse than described: in the progress fragment (lines 270-289), when the None sentinel is drained, the code `return`s at line 285 BEFORE the `if msgs:` render block at line 289. So if the `ERROR:` string and the None sentinel are drained in the same 1-second fragment tick, the ERROR message is collected into msgs but never written to the UI. The failure can surface as a pure silent reset-to-idle with neither a UI error nor a logged trace. This strengthens, not weakens, the case.

REDUCTIVE CHECK: N/A — this is purely additive (one logging line). No removal, no callers/tests at risk.

This is a genuine observability gap, accurately described, with a correct and minimal fix.

### `IMPR-197` — verify_base_rate / verify_actors / verify_forecast_run are three near-identical wrappers around cross_verify — collapse to one
- **confirmed ✓** · source: improvement · effort: small · file: `src/pipeline/verification.py:53-178`
- **evidence:** All three functions follow the identical 5-line skeleton: build finding string, build context string, results = router.cross_verify(finding, context), entries = [_result_to_entry(r) for r in results], agreement = _compute_agreement(entries), log, then return StageVerification(stage=..., finding_summary=..., verifications=entries, agreement=agreement). The only per-stage variation is the stage label, the finding_summary text, and the finding/context strings. ~125 lines encode roughly one function plus three string builders. verify_actors adds a guard (returns None if not level_2_3_present) but the orchestrator already gates that at orchestrator.py:148 before calling.
- **recommendation:** Introduce one helper verify_stage(stage, finding_summary, finding, context, router) that does the cross_verify -> entries -> agreement -> StageVerification flow. Keep the three small string-builders (or inline them at the call sites in the orchestrator, which already branch per stage). This is net-reductive (collapses ~3x duplicated control flow into 1) and makes adding a new verifiable stage a one-liner instead of a copy-paste of 40 lines.
- **verifier:** Verified against src/pipeline/verification.py:53-178 and orchestrator.py.

CONFIRMED claims:
- All three functions (verify_base_rate L53, verify_actors L92, verify_forecast_run L144) share an identical control-flow skeleton at L75-89, L127-141, L164-178: results = router.cross_verify(finding, context) -> entries = [_result_to_entry(r) for r in results] -> agreement = _compute_agreement(entries) -> logger.info(...) -> return StageVerification(stage=, finding_summary=, verifications=entries, agreement=agreement). This is real, genuine duplication.
- Per-stage variation is exactly as described: stage label, finding_summary text, finding/context strings (plus a slightly different log message).
- The config parameter is accepted by all three but never used inside them (grep confirms config is only read in should_verify_run at L194/L210). So a verify_stage helper would not even need config.
- verify_actors guard redundancy is TRUE: orchestrator.py:147-148 gates on v_config.verify_actors AND level_2_3_present before calling, verify_actors:103 re-checks the same and returns None, and orchestrator.py:156 re-checks is not None. Triple-guarded; the internal guard is defensively redundant.

SAFETY (reductive check): SAFE. grep across the repo shows the only callers are orchestrator.py (3 sites, plus imports at L48-50). NO test references verify_base_rate/verify_actors/verify_forecast_run/_compute_agreement/_result_to_entry (searched tests/ — test files are aggregator, calibration, cross_pollination, pipeline_contract, platform_clients, registry). The proposal keeps the three public functions / string-builders and only extracts the shared tail, so no caller or test breaks. Net-reductive and non-breaking.

CAVEAT that tempers magnitude: the bulk of the 125 lines is per-stage finding/context string construction, which is NOT duplication and remains after the refactor. The genuinely duplicated core is ~5 lines x3 = ~15 lines; realistic net savings are ~10-15 lines, not the "125 lines -> one function" the title implies. verify_actors also has real per-stage logic (filtering actors >= level 2 to build actor_summaries, L108-113) so it cannot collapse to a pure string template. The "new stage = one-liner" benefit is real but speculative (only 3 stages exist, no evidence of churn adding more).

Verdict: real, grounded, and safe — but a modest, cosmetic DRY cleanup, not an architectural win. Worth doing if touching this file; not worth a dedicated effort.

### `IMPR-198` — Verification audit-logging boilerplate is copy-pasted across call(), verify(), challenge(), cross_verify()
- **confirmed ✓** · source: improvement · effort: medium · file: `src/pipeline/llm_router.py:212-225,245-262,295-311,332-350`
- **evidence:** Each of the four dispatch methods repeats the same t0 = time.monotonic() ... if self._audit is not None: duration_ms = (time.monotonic()-t0)*1000; self._audit.log_call(provider=..., model=..., method=..., params=..., result_text=..., ...) block, with only method name and params differing. The VerificationError-construction fallback (from sigma_verify import classify_error, VerificationError; return/append VerificationError(... finding[:100], provenance_tag=f'|source:external-...|')) is also duplicated verbatim between verify() (lines 265-277) and cross_verify() (lines 356-368).
- **recommendation:** Wrap the timing+audit in a single context manager or helper (e.g. with self._timed_audit(method, params) as rec: ... rec.record(text, tokens_in, tokens_out)), and extract one _verification_error(client, e, tool) helper for the duplicated error path. Net-reductive: removes ~4 copies of the audit block and 2 copies of the error block. Also fixes the latent inconsistency where only some methods feed real token counts to the audit log.
- **verifier:** Verified against src/pipeline/llm_router.py and sigma_verify introspection.

REAL but OVERSTATED. Two distinct claims:

1) Error-fallback duplication (verify 265-277, cross_verify 356-368): GENUINELY duplicated, differing only in tool_attempted ("verify_finding" vs "cross_verify") and return-vs-append. Extracting _verification_error(client, e, tool) is a clean, safe ~12-line reduction. This is the one solid win.

2) Audit-block duplication: PARTIALLY real but inflated. The pattern recurs across all four methods, but call() (212-225) is structurally DIFFERENT — it computes cost via estimate_cost() and passes cost_usd plus real token counts, with different result_text handling. The other three (verify 248-261, challenge 299-311, cross_verify 336-349) are the near-identical ones. So the honest reduction is collapsing 3 similar blocks, not 4 identical ones; a single context manager spanning all four needs branching for cost/tokens, eroding the claimed simplicity.

FALSE sub-claim — the headline "also fixes the latent inconsistency where only some methods feed real token counts": refuted. VerificationResult (return type of every external verify()) has NO token fields — confirmed by dataclass introspection: model, provider, assessment, reasoning, confidence, counter_evidence, provenance_tag, status. challenge() returns a plain dict, also no token usage. The tokens_in=0/tokens_out=0 in verify/challenge/cross_verify are not an oversight; those clients simply return no usage data. A rec.record(text, tokens_in, tokens_out) API would have nothing real to record for 3 of 4 methods. This payoff does not exist.

SAFETY (reductive proposal): the proposed helpers are net-additive; no caller or test depends on the inlined structure. test_cross_pollination.py (44 tests, exercises router.call with mocked _audit) passes at baseline and would survive a faithful refactor. The reduction is safe.

NET: cosmetic DRY cleanup in a non-hot, well-tested file. The error-helper is worth doing; the audit-block unification is smaller than advertised and one of its two stated benefits is bogus. Not an architecture-level improvement.

### `IMPR-199` — Orchestrator.predict_until_review is a 300-line linear procedure mixing 7 stages, verification gating, warning accumulation, and cost math
- **confirmed ✓** · source: improvement · effort: small · file: `src/pipeline/orchestrator.py:70-382`
- **evidence:** predict_until_review inlines all of: persona defaulting, record init, verification-report setup, decompose+verify-actors, base-rate+search-augment+verify, the forecast loop, run verification, parse-failure exclusion logic (lines 251-273), provider counting, aggregation, source-clustering warning, challenge, deliberation gating, calibration + the heuristic CI math (lines 347-349), platform signals, and cost finalize. The verification gate 'if v_enabled and v_config.<flag>' repeats 4 times (lines 147,190,233,297). The parse-failure exclusion and CI computation are non-trivial logic embedded mid-procedure with no unit-test seam.
- **recommendation:** Do NOT add an abstraction layer/stage-registry framework — that would be additive churn for a single linear pipeline. Instead extract the two genuinely self-contained, testable blobs as free functions in their existing modules: _select_valid_runs(runs) -> (valid_runs, warnings) into aggregator.py (the parse-failure exclusion, orchestrator.py:251-273) and _confidence_interval(cal_prob, stdev) into calibration.py (orchestrator.py:347-349). That shrinks the orchestrator, gives both pieces a test seam, and removes the duplicated provider-counting hasattr guard (line 274 — RunResult always sets provider, forecaster.py:230). Keep the linear flow; the win is moving logic to where it is testable, not wrapping it.
- **verifier:** Every concrete claim checks out against the code. predict_until_review spans orchestrator.py:70-382 (~313 lines), a single linear procedure inlining all seven stages plus verification gating, warning accumulation, and cost math — matching the description. The verification gate `if v_enabled and v_config.<flag>` repeats exactly 4 times at lines 147, 190, 233, 297 (verified). The two extraction targets are real and self-contained: (1) parse-failure exclusion at lines 251-273 — non-trivial branching (n_valid==0 fallback, n_valid<2 wording, two warning messages) with no current unit-test seam; (2) CI heuristic at lines 347-349 — inline math (cal_prob ± 1.96*stdev, clamped to [0.01,0.99], with a ±0.05 fallback when stdev==0). Both destination modules (aggregator.py, calibration.py) already exist AND already have test files (test_aggregator.py, test_calibration.py), so extracted functions immediately gain a tested home — this is the load-bearing payoff. The proposed names `_select_valid_runs`/`_confidence_interval` do not collide with anything in src/ or tests/.

The provider-counting simplification is also correct and SAFE: line 274 `len(set(r.provider for r in valid_runs if hasattr(r, "provider")))` — the hasattr guard is always True because RunResult is a Pydantic BaseModel with `provider: str = ""` (models.py:95) and run_forecast always sets `provider=prov` (forecaster.py:230, citation exact). Removing the guard cannot break callers or tests; it's a dead defensive check, not behavior anyone depends on.

This is an additive/relocating refactor (extract + import + drop a dead guard), not a reductive deletion that strips a called API — so the 'safe removal' failure mode (breaking the test suite) does not apply. The proposal is well-calibrated: it explicitly REJECTS a stage-registry abstraction framework as additive churn and scopes the win to testability rather than overclaiming correctness fixes. It is honest that the CI heuristic itself is unchanged.

One caveat worth noting but not disqualifying: the CI extraction makes a statistically questionable heuristic (using forecast dispersion `aggregation.stdev` as a proxy for calibration uncertainty) testable, not correct — the proposal already acknowledges this scope. The net change is modest in size; this is a low-to-medium maintainability/testability win, not a high-severity correctness fix.

### `IMPR-203` — router param is optional in pipeline-stage functions, with each able to silently construct its own LLMRouter — a leaky boundary
- **confirmed ✓** · source: improvement · effort: small · file: `src/pipeline/forecaster.py:99,113-115`
- **evidence:** run_forecast (and the same pattern is implied across stage functions) accepts router=None and falls back to constructing a fresh LLMRouter(config) inside the function (forecaster.py:113-115). The orchestrator always passes its own self.router, so this fallback is never exercised in the real pipeline, but it means a stray call path could spin up a second router (new client cache, new audit logger writing to the same file, duplicated provider init). It blurs the ownership boundary: the orchestrator is meant to own the single router, but each stage can opt out.
- **recommendation:** Make router a required parameter on the stage functions (drop the =None default and the inline LLMRouter construction). The orchestrator already always supplies it; tests can pass a fake. This removes the hidden second-router hazard and the duplicated 'from src.pipeline.llm_router import LLMRouter' lazy import. Net deletion plus a clearer single-owner contract for the router/audit-logger.
- **verifier:** VERIFIED for the named target; SCOPE OVERSTATED for the generalization.

Current state (confirmed at src/pipeline/forecaster.py:97-115): run_forecast accepts `router=None` and, when None, lazily imports and constructs `LLMRouter(config)` inside the function. Claim is accurate to the line.

Caller analysis: `run_forecast` has exactly ONE caller — orchestrator.py:219 — which always passes `router=self.router` (orchestrator constructs the single router at line 65). Grep across src/, scripts/, and the whole repo confirms no other call path. No test imports or calls run_forecast (tests/test_pipeline_contract.py and test_cross_pollination.py touch decomposer, deliberate, and LLMRouter directly, never the forecaster). So the `router=None` fallback in run_forecast is genuinely dead in production AND not kept alive by any test. Removing the default + the inline `from src.pipeline.llm_router import LLMRouter` / `LLMRouter(config)` is SAFE for this function: it breaks no caller and no test. Net deletion, clearer single-owner contract. The hazard described (a stray path spinning up a second router with its own _client_cache and its own LLMAuditLogger — confirmed at llm_router.py:90,105 — whose buffered audit entries would never be flushed since orchestrator only flushes its own router) is real in principle but latent, never exercised. So this is a defensive/clarity cleanup, not an active-bug fix.

WHERE THE PROPOSAL OVERREACHES (the part to refute): "the same pattern is implied across stage functions" and "make router required on the stage functions" is not uniformly safe. Two counter-cases the verifier task explicitly warns about:
1. verification.py (lines 57,95,147,216) ALREADY uses required `router: LLMRouter` with no default — the boundary is already fixed in the newest module, so the "each stage can opt out" framing is partly stale.
2. deliberation.py:69,87-92 uses `router=None` but with SKIP-and-warn semantics, NOT silent construction — different and intentional. And tests/test_cross_pollination.py:612-620 (test_deliberate_passes_through_when_router_none) explicitly exercises the `router=None` path and asserts pass-through. Dropping deliberate's default would break that test. Per the reductive-safety rule: if the proposal is read as "all stage functions," it is UNSAFE for deliberate and would recur the prior break-the-suite mistake.

Net: the improvement is real, worthwhile, and safe SCOPED TO run_forecast (and, by the same caller-checked reasoning, decomposer.py and base_rate.py which share the silent-construct pattern — though those each need their own grep before touching; decomposer IS called in tests but always with a mock router passed). It is NOT safe as a blanket "all stage functions" change. The proposal correctly names forecaster.py as the location, which is the safe case.

### `IMPR-206` — No README / quickstart — onboarding requires reverse-engineering CLI invocations from module docstrings
- **confirmed ✓** · source: improvement · effort: small · file: `/Users/bjgilbert/Projects/Zoltar/sigma-predict/pyproject.toml:1`
- **evidence:** `ls *.md` returns no matches — there is no README, CONTRIBUTING, or docs file anywhere at repo root. A new developer must discover that the app launches via `streamlit run app.py` (never stated), that CLIs run as `python -m scripts.predict` (only in per-file docstrings), and that data/ paths and .env layout are required. The pyproject defines a `sigma-predict` console script but nothing documents it. The two parallel UIs (Streamlit hub vs scripts/dashboard.py Rich console) are undocumented, so a user does not know which to use.
- **recommendation:** Add a concise README: one-paragraph what-it-is, the .env keys (mirroring .env.example), the four working commands (streamlit run app.py; python -m scripts.predict ...; python -m scripts.resolve --check-all; python -m scripts.dashboard), and a note that monitor is a stub. Pays off immediately for every new contributor and for the user returning after a gap.
- **verifier:** Confirmed against the actual repo. (1) No markdown files exist anywhere — root `ls *.md` returns "no matches" and there is no README/CONTRIBUTING/docs. (2) The app launches via `streamlit run app.py` — app.py is a Streamlit entry point (st.set_page_config, components/state imports) and `grep "streamlit run"` across all .py/.toml files returns zero hits, so the launch command is genuinely never stated anywhere. (3) CLIs run as `python -m scripts.predict` / `--check-all` / `python -m scripts.dashboard`, documented only inside per-file module docstrings (verified in predict.py, resolve.py, dashboard.py). (4) monitor.py is verifiably a stub: its main() prints "Platform monitor is a Phase 3 feature and not yet implemented." (5) Two parallel UIs confirmed — Streamlit (app.py + pages/1_Dashboard..5_Registry) and Rich console (scripts/dashboard.py), with no doc steering a user to either. (6) pyproject.toml defines `sigma-predict = "scripts.predict:main"` console script, undocumented. (7) .env.example exists with keys to mirror.

This is a purely additive DX improvement (not reductive), so there is no removal-safety concern — nothing to break. The payoff is credible: a new contributor or the solo owner returning after a gap must currently reverse-engineer usage from app.py's body, three script docstrings, pyproject [project.scripts], and .env.example. A short README collapses that to one read. The proposal is concrete and grounded, not speculative churn.

One caveat the proposal gets slightly wrong: it says to mirror .env.example for the env keys, but .env.example marks TAVILY_API_KEY as "Required" while the project explicitly made Tavily optional (commit 2910120). A README that blindly mirrors .env.example would propagate that staleness. Minor — the README should describe Tavily as optional, and ideally .env.example should be corrected too. Does not undermine the opportunity.

### `IMPR-207` — Config.validate() only checks LLM keys — predict runs fail deep in network calls instead of failing fast with a clear message
- **REFUTED ✗** · source: improvement · effort: small · file: `/Users/bjgilbert/Projects/Zoltar/sigma-predict/src/config.py:147-155`
- **evidence:** validate() returns missing items only for LLM keys (line 151) and primary_model. It never checks TAVILY_API_KEY (search backend default is tavily, config.py:24) nor the platform key for the platform actually being used. So `python -m scripts.predict --platform metaculus` with no METACULUS_API_KEY passes validate() and proceeds; the metaculus client then silently runs unauthenticated (per repo map, auth fails open) and the failure surfaces as an empty result or a deep error rather than 'METACULUS_API_KEY not set'. resolve.py:76 and dashboard.py:26 don't call validate() at all.
- **recommendation:** Make validate() accept the platform/search context (or add a validate_for(platform, needs_search) variant) and warn when the selected platform's key or TAVILY_API_KEY is empty — non-fatal warning is enough since the system tolerates running unauthenticated. Surface it as a single clear stderr line in predict.py/batch_predict.py. Small change, big reduction in confusing late-stage failures.
- **verifier:** The current-state CLAIMS are mostly accurate, but the PAYOFF is overstated to the point that the opportunity does not hold up.

Verified accurate: validate() (config.py:147-155) only checks LLM keys + primary_model; it does not check TAVILY_API_KEY or any platform key. Search default is tavily (config.py:24). resolve.py and dashboard.py do not call validate() (only predict.py:83 and batch_predict.py:47 do). Metaculus client fails open on auth (metaculus.py:67-69 adds the Authorization header only if a key is present).

Why the payoff is not credible:

1. TAVILY already has the exact warning the proposal asks for. src/pipeline/search.py:76-77 logs 'No TAVILY_API_KEY — search will return empty results (LLM knowledge only)'. Duplicating this in validate() is churn, not a 'big reduction in confusing failures'.

2. The 'metaculus silently fails deep' scenario is largely fictional for the predict path. predict.py only READS questions (get_question / get_questions), and Metaculus's public /api2 read endpoints do not require auth — an unauthenticated read succeeds, it does not produce an empty result or a deep error. When a question genuinely is not found, get_question already raises a clear ValueError('Question {id} not found on Metaculus') (metaculus.py:126). The only path that truly needs the platform key is submit_prediction, and it ALREADY fails fast with the precise message the proposal wants: 'Cannot submit prediction: METACULUS_API_KEY not set' (metaculus.py:133-134).

So both halves of the 'fail fast with a clear message' benefit already exist in the codebase (Tavily warning in search.py, Metaculus submit warning in the client). The remaining delta — a startup heads-up that a selected platform's key is unset before a submission flow — is real but marginal DX, not the 'small change, big reduction' the title claims. The proposal's framing rests on a failure mode (read runs silently dying deep in network calls with no key) that the code does not actually exhibit.

### `IMPR-208` — resolve.py crashes on the format string when a resolution exists but outcome is None
- **confirmed ✓** · source: improvement · effort: trivial · file: `/Users/bjgilbert/Projects/Zoltar/sigma-predict/scripts/resolve.py:107-119`
- **evidence:** brier_score is only assigned inside `if outcome is not None:` (lines 107-111). The unconditional echo at line 119 does `brier={resolution.brier_score:.4f}`. If a platform returns a resolution object with outcome=None (e.g. annulled/ambiguous question), brier_score keeps its model default; if that default is None, `f'{None:.4f}'` raises TypeError and aborts the entire resolve loop mid-batch (and store.update already ran for this record, so state is partially written). This is a latent crash on a realistic input.
- **recommendation:** Guard the echo: only print the brier line when outcome is not None, otherwise print a 'resolved without scorable outcome' message. Two-line fix. Also consider moving store.update to run once after the loop or wrapping per-record in try/except so one bad record can't abort a batch resolution run.
- **verifier:** The mechanical core of the claim is accurate: Resolution.brier_score defaults to None (src/models.py:156), brier_score is only assigned inside `if outcome is not None:` (resolve.py:107-110), the echo at resolve.py:119 unconditionally formats `resolution.brier_score:.4f`, and `f'{None:.4f}'` does raise TypeError (verified). store.update (line 113) runs before the echo, so a crash would leave state partially written — the partial-write concern is real.

However, the claim's premise that this is "a latent crash on a realistic input" is INFLATED. The triggering condition — a platform returning a Resolution with outcome=None — cannot occur with the current code. The ONLY Resolution(...) constructor in the entire codebase is MetaculusClient.get_resolution (metaculus.py:179), which always passes a valid float outcome. The annulled/ambiguous case the claim cites is precisely the branch that returns None instead (metaculus.py:170-173: float() raises on string resolutions like "annulled", caught, returns None), which resolve.py then skips at lines 99-102 before reaching the echo. resolve.py only calls get_resolution for platform=="metaculus" (lines 93-97); Kalshi and Polymarket get_resolution are stubs returning None. Records with an existing Resolution are skipped at line 87, so a stored outcome=None never re-enters the echo. No test exercises get_resolution at all, so removing/guarding touches nothing.

So the bug is genuinely latent but currently UNREACHABLE — it only becomes live if a future platform implements get_resolution to construct Resolution(outcome=None) (plausible: a market could resolve as void/annulled and a future author might model that as outcome=None rather than returning None). The proposed two-line guard is correct, cheap, and defensible defensive hardening; the per-record try/except for batch robustness is a legitimate secondary improvement. This is NOT a reductive proposal, so no deletion-safety concern.

Verdict: real as a defensive-hardening / DX improvement, but the "realistic input / active crash" framing overstates current exposure.

### `IMPR-209` — Multi-platform support is asymmetric: resolve.py only fetches resolutions for Metaculus, so Polymarket/Kalshi predictions can be made but never scored
- **confirmed ✓** · source: improvement · effort: medium · file: `/Users/bjgilbert/Projects/Zoltar/sigma-predict/scripts/resolve.py:93-97`
- **evidence:** predict.py and batch_predict.py route to all three platforms, but resolve.py hardcodes `if record.platform == 'metaculus': ... else: skip`. Any prediction made on Polymarket or Kalshi is permanently unscored — it never gets a Brier/log score, never enters get_calibration_pairs(), and therefore never contributes to the calibration feedback loop. The forecasting tool's value depends on closing the resolve loop, and two-thirds of its supported platforms are excluded from it.
- **recommendation:** Either implement get_resolution for Polymarket/Kalshi (the clients already exist), OR — if those platforms' resolution endpoints aren't ready — make the gap loud: surface a count of skipped-by-platform records in the resolve summary and on the Dashboard so the user knows those predictions are accumulating unscored. The current silent per-record skip hides a systematic gap.
- **verifier:** Verified against the code. The core structural claim is REAL and accurate.

Confirmed:
- resolve.py:93-97 hardcodes `if record.platform == "metaculus": metaculus.get_resolution(...)` else echo a skip and `continue`. Exactly as described.
- predict.py (lines 24-33, 40) and batch_predict.py (lines 26, 54-56) both route to all three platforms (metaculus/polymarket/kalshi) via click.Choice and client factories. So predictions CAN be made on all three.
- The calibration loop is genuinely closed off: src/registry/query.py get_calibration_pairs (line 104), the binned-calibration function (~line 143), and the horizon function (~line 182) all early-`continue` when `record.resolution is None`. Polymarket/Kalshi records never get a resolution, so they never enter any calibration computation. count_resolved / get_resolved (line 82) likewise filter on resolution is not None. The "never enters get_calibration_pairs, never contributes to feedback loop" claim is correct.
- The Dashboard (pages/1_Dashboard.py) splits records into active vs resolved by `resolution is None` and shows a per-row Platform column, but has NO platform-level breakdown of structurally-unscoreable predictions. Polymarket/Kalshi predictions accumulate in "active" indistinguishable from Metaculus questions genuinely awaiting resolution. The "silent systematic gap" framing is accurate.

ONE FACTUAL CORRECTION to the proposal that materially affects scoping: the proposal says Option A is easy because "the clients already exist." This is half-true and misleading. The CLIENT CLASSES exist (PolymarketClient, KalshiClient) and both already declare get_resolution — but both are deliberate stubs that just `return None` with docstring "Resolution retrieval not yet implemented" (polymarket.py:125-127, kalshi.py:160-162). So the resolution-fetching logic genuinely does NOT exist; it must be built against each platform's resolution endpoint. Option A is real work, not wiring-up-existing-code. This actually strengthens the proposal's fallback (Option B: make the gap loud) as the pragmatic near-term fix, since Option A requires real endpoint integration.

Not a reductive proposal — nothing to delete, no safety/removal concern. This is additive (implement resolution OR surface skip counts).

Worthwhile: yes. A forecasting tool's entire value proposition is the closed resolve→score→calibrate loop. Two of three supported platforms being permanently excluded, silently, is a genuine product-integrity gap, not churn. The minimum viable fix (Option B: count skipped-by-platform in the resolve summary line ~122 and surface it on the Dashboard) is low-effort and directly addresses the "silent" problem even before endpoint work lands.

### `IMPR-213` — Cost transparency gap: no spend confirmation or per-run cost estimate before launching the paid pipeline (CLI and UI)
- **confirmed ✓** · source: improvement · effort: small · file: `/Users/bjgilbert/Projects/Zoltar/sigma-predict/pages/2_Predict.py:232`
- **evidence:** Run Pipeline (2_Predict.py:232) and batch_predict.py:88 trigger real LLM + Tavily spend with num_runs up to 10 (2_Predict.py:195) and no pre-flight estimate or confirmation. The system ALREADY has cost machinery — llm_router has estimate_cost and CostTracking — but it's only used to report cost AFTER the spend. A user has no way to know a 10-run + verification run will cost ~$X before committing. batch_predict can fan out across many questions with the same blind spend.
- **recommendation:** Surface the existing estimate_cost up front: show 'Estimated cost: ~$X for N runs' next to the Run button (UI) and a 'proceeding with N predictions, est. $X' line before the batch loop (CLI), optionally gated behind a confirm for batches. Reuses existing cost code; no new estimation logic. Materially improves the tool's usability as a budget-aware forecasting instrument.
- **verifier:** PARTIALLY REAL with a load-bearing inaccuracy in the proposed mechanism. Verified against actual code (not the claimed paths — see corrections below).

CURRENT STATE — mostly accurate:
- pages/2_Predict.py:232 "Run Pipeline" button launches the paid pipeline immediately, no pre-flight cost estimate or confirmation. CONFIRMED.
- num_runs max=10 at 2_Predict.py:195. CONFIRMED.
- Verification toggle (line 205-208) adds external-model calls on top of runs. CONFIRMED — multiplies spend.
- Batch CLI is at scripts/batch_predict.py, NOT root batch_predict.py as claimed. Loop at line 82-98, predict() at line 88. It prints "Proceeding with N predictions (runs=R each)" at line 76 (a count, no cost) and reports total_cost only AFTER the loop (lines 93, 102). CONFIRMED no pre-flight estimate/confirm.
- CostTracking (src/models.py:198) and estimate_cost (src/pipeline/llm_router.py:388) exist and are used post-spend only (llm_router.py:214 for audit logging; batch CLI sums record.cost after each run). CONFIRMED.

LOAD-BEARING FLAW IN THE PROPOSAL: The claim "Surface the existing estimate_cost up front ... Reuses existing cost code; no new estimation logic" is FALSE. estimate_cost(provider, model, tokens_in, tokens_out) is a pure pricing function that REQUIRES actual token counts. Those counts only exist AFTER a call completes (it is called at llm_router.py:214 precisely after _anthropic.call returns tokens_in/tokens_out). There is no pre-flight token estimator, no historical-average machinery, nothing in src/ to feed estimate_cost before a run. Producing a credible "~$X for N runs" up front would require genuinely NEW logic to estimate per-run token usage (variable prompt + search-result sizes, persona count, verification call count, decomposition depth). So the proposal cannot be implemented as described.

ASSESSMENT: The cost-transparency gap is real and worth closing for a budget-aware tool. But the proposed implementation path is wrong. An honest version would show a per-run cost from a HISTORICAL AVERAGE over past registry records (which do carry record.cost.total_cost_usd) times N runs, with an explicit "estimate, actuals vary" caveat — not a reuse of estimate_cost. Pre-flight token estimation for a forecasting pipeline is inherently rough, so the estimate is advisory, not precise. The CLI already has the question count, so adding a cost line + a confirm gate for batches is genuinely cheap; the UI side is a small markdown line next to the button. real=true because the gap and the value exist, but the proposal's central claim of zero new estimation logic is refuted and must be corrected before any build.

### `IMPR-214` — No way to forecast an ad-hoc / free-text question — the tool is locked to the three platform APIs
- **confirmed ✓** · source: improvement · effort: medium · file: `/Users/bjgilbert/Projects/Zoltar/sigma-predict/pages/2_Predict.py:107`

The full decompose->base-rate->forecast->calibrate machinery would work on an arbitrary question.
- **recommendation:** Add a 'Custom question' input mode that constructs a PlatformQuestion from user title/description/resolution_criteria and runs the pipeline in log-only mode (no submission). Mostly reuses existing models and the existing _logged_only path. This significantly broadens the tool's value beyond mirroring existing markets — it becomes a general forecasting instrument, not just a market-shadowing one.
- **verifier:** The opportunity is real and grounded, but the proposal's effort/reuse claim is materially optimistic.

VERIFIED current state:
- CLI is at /Users/bjgilbert/Projects/Zoltar/sigma-predict/scripts/predict.py (the proposal says predict.py — minor path inaccuracy). It does require --question-id/--search/--url and sys.exit(1)s otherwise (lines 120-122). Confirmed.
- UI: /Users/bjgilbert/Projects/Zoltar/sigma-predict/pages/2_Predict.py:107 has input_mode = st.radio("Find question by", ["Search","ID","URL"]). All three branches fetch an existing question via a platform client. No custom/free-text path. Confirmed.
- PlatformQuestion (src/models.py:233-247) is a plain Pydantic model exposing title, description, resolution_criteria, etc. — constructible by hand. Confirmed.
- The forecasting core is genuinely platform-agnostic: predict_until_review only touches platform_client via _collect_platform_signals (orchestrator.py:362→536), which is wrapped in try/except (lines 544-553), so a None client survives that step.

CRITICAL inaccuracy in the proposal (load-bearing): it claims submission 'already degrades gracefully to _logged_only' and the custom path 'mostly reuses the existing _logged_only path.' That path (orchestrator.py:442-448) ONLY catches NotImplementedError. A custom question has no real platform, so the natural construction passes platform_client=None; with auto_submit=True (what the UI uses, 2_Predict.py:77) submit_after_review reaches line 430 None.submit_prediction(...) and raises an UNCAUGHT AttributeError. The pipeline thread would error out, not log gracefully. So the custom path needs real work: a stub/no-op client, a None-guard before submission, or an auto_submit=False + UI-side skip — not a free reuse of _logged_only.

Secondary understated friction: Platform is a closed 3-value enum (models.py:13-16). A custom question must still be assigned metaculus/polymarket/kalshi, or the enum (and code that switches on it / stores question.platform.value) must gain a CUSTOM member. 'Mostly reuses existing models' undersells this.

Net: the gap is real, the value claim (general forecasting instrument vs. market mirror) is credible, and this is a net-new feature (not reductive, so removal-safety analysis is N/A). But it is NOT the near-zero-effort reuse the proposal implies — the _logged_only graceful-degradation premise is false for the None-client case.

- **confirmed ✓** · source: improvement · effort: trivial · file: `/Users/bjgilbert/Projects/Zoltar/sigma-predict/src/pipeline/calibration.py:103-114`

- **recommendation:** Delete apply_hedging_correction (lines 103-114). Nothing imports or calls it. If a test references it, delete that test too — it is testing a function with no production caller. One fewer near-duplicate hedging function reduces the chance a future edit picks the wrong one.

Both calibrate() callers (lines 82, 92) use apply_hedging_correction_scaled. So the reduction target is real.

HOWEVER, the proposal's stated current state is FALSE. It claims grep "finds exactly one reference: the definition itself." ### `IMPR-224` — Binary-only scoring is silently misapplied to NUMERIC/MULTIPLE_CHOICE — remove the unsupported question types rather than carry inert generality
- **REFUTED ✗** · source: improvement · effort: medium · file: `/Users/bjgilbert/Projects/Zoltar/sigma-predict/src/scoring.py:13-19`
- **evidence:** log_score treats actual>=0.5 as outcome=1 (scoring.py:16) and (per repo map) Brier decomposition assumes binary (query.py:57). models.py declares QuestionType.NUMERIC and MULTIPLE_CHOICE (models.py:20-22) and Resolution.outcome is a free float, but no code path actually forecasts or scores non-binary questions — the whole pipeline (base_rate, aggregation, calibration, scoring) is binary-probability end to end. The enum values are generality added for needs that never materialized; their presence makes the models look more capable than the pipeline is and lets non-binary resolutions flow into binary scoring to produce silently meaningless numbers.
- **recommendation:** Until non-binary forecasting is actually built, reduce QuestionType to BINARY only (models.py:20-22) and reject non-binary questions at platform-mapping time with a clear skip, rather than carrying MULTIPLE_CHOICE/NUMERIC branches that no code honors. This makes the binary assumption baked into scoring honest and explicit instead of an undocumented trap. Smaller surface, no silently-wrong scores.
- **verifier:** The proposal's reductive prescription — "reduce QuestionType to BINARY only (models.py:20-22)" — is UNSAFE and mislocates its own evidence, so I refute it as framed (real=false), while noting it contains a real latent-bug kernel that warrants a DIFFERENT, smaller fix.

SAFETY (the decisive issue): Deleting NUMERIC/MULTIPLE_CHOICE breaks live callers and a test. src/platforms/metaculus.py:26 and :29 (`_parse_question_type`) return QuestionType.NUMERIC and QuestionType.MULTIPLE_CHOICE directly — removing the enum members is an immediate AttributeError. tests/test_platform_clients.py:39-46 (`test_numeric_question`) asserts `_parse_question_type(data) == QuestionType.NUMERIC`; that test would fail to collect. This is precisely the "deletion that breaks the test suite" failure mode the task flagged. The proposal mentions rejecting non-binary at mapping time but does not account for the existing parser logic or its test, presenting the change as a clean enum reduction.

EVIDENCE ERRORS in the claim: (a) Cited `query.py:57` does not exist; real file is src/registry/query.py. (b) Line 57 there is `uncertainty = bar_o * (1 - bar_o)` and the Murphy decomposition operates on generic (predicted, outcome) float pairs — it does NOT 'assume binary' at that line as claimed. (c) The headline location scoring.py:13-19 does not reference QuestionType at all; scoring.py is fully decoupled from the enum, operating on raw floats. So the link between the enum and scoring is looser than stated.

REAL KERNEL (why this isn't pure churn but still not the right fix): The "binary end-to-end" observation is accurate. No code branches on question_type — forecaster.py:136, decomposer.py:53, orchestrator.py:119, human_review.py:47 only interpolate question_type.value into strings; polymarket/kalshi hardcode BINARY; only Metaculus emits non-binary. A non-binary Metaculus resolution would flow into binary log_score (actual>=0.5 threshold) and produce meaningless numbers — a genuine silent trap. But (1) the trap is at the SCORING/INGEST boundary, not the enum, and the safe remedy is a skip/reject guard that KEEPS the enum and _parse_question_type for detection; (2) the inert generality is downstream handling, not the parser, which is working tested code that correctly classifies; (3) practical risk is low — non-binary ingestion via Metaculus is possible but there's no evidence it's happening, and the auto-detection exists precisely so such questions could be filtered.

Net: the proposed deletion breaks callers + a test (refute), and its supporting citations are wrong. The valuable, safe action is the secondary guard it mentions (reject non-binary at platform-mapping / pipeline entry), not collapsing the enum.
