### `READ-235` — Extremization formula extremizes the mean of probabilities rather than pooling log-odds — not the estimator the literature justifies
- **confirmed ✓** · source: readiness:methodology · effort: medium · file: `src/pipeline/aggregator.py:133-150`
- **evidence:** _extremize computes `logistic(factor * logit(p))` where p is the trimmed mean of raw PROBABILITIES (aggregator.py:75,83-84,92). The extremizing-aggregator results (Satopaa et al. 2014; Baron/Mellers/Tetlock) are derived for the AVERAGE OF LOG-ODDS (geometric mean of odds), i.e. extremize after pooling on the logit scale, not after arithmetic-mean pooling on the probability scale. logistic(a * logit(mean_p)) is a different, ad hoc transform with no calibration-improvement guarantee. The extremization_factor=1.5 (config.py:33) is a fixed magic constant with no fit to this system's resolved track record.
- **recommendation:** If extremization is retained, pool on the log-odds scale first (mean of logit(p_i)) then apply the factor, matching the derivation. Treat the factor as a parameter to be empirically fit against resolved Brier scores once N is sufficient, not a hardcoded 1.5. Document that with correlated same-model runs the independence assumption underpinning extremization is violated regardless of scale.
- **verifier:** CONFIRMED, code-grounded. All mechanical claims verified against source:

1. `_extremize(p, factor) = logistic(factor * logit(p))` — exact, aggregator.py:142-148.
2. It is applied to `trimmed_mean`, which is the arithmetic mean of raw PROBABILITIES (aggregator.py:80-84 trims/means probabilities; line 92/103 passes trimmed_mean into _extremize). So the code computes logistic(a·logit(mean_p)).
3. The literature's extremizing aggregator (Satopaa et al. 2014; Baron/Mellers/Tetlock) is derived for pooling on the logit scale FIRST, i.e. logistic(a·mean(logit p_i)) — extremize the log-odds pool. By Jensen's inequality logit(mean p) != mean(logit p) unless all p_i are equal, so the code implements a genuinely different transform than the one its docstring and the cited results justify. The finding is mathematically correct on this distinction.
4. Absent-control check: grep across src confirms NO log-odds pooling exists anywhere. Pooling is always arithmetic mean / trimmed mean / median on the probability scale. The logit usage in calibration.py is a separate post-hoc Platt/isotonic calibration stage, not pooling — it does not implement or substitute for log-odds pooling. The claimed gap is real and unhandled.
5. extremization_factor=1.5 is a hardcoded default (actual location src/config.py:33, finding said config.py:33 — correct file is src/config.py, minor path imprecision but the line and value are exact) with no fit to resolved Brier scores. Confirmed.

The recommendation (pool on logit scale before applying the factor; treat the factor as an empirically-fit parameter; document the independence-violation caveat for correlated same-model runs) is sound and directly addresses the gap.

Note for whoever fixes it: the system already has a downstream Platt/isotonic calibration layer that fits the logit scale once N>=20/50. A hardcoded extremization factor applied to the logit before that stage partly double-manipulates the logit scale and can be partially absorbed/fought by the fitted Platt slope — this strengthens the case for either fitting the factor or folding extremization into the calibration stage rather than hardcoding it.

Severity: keep at medium. It is a methodology-correctness gap, not a crash/invalid-output bug. Impact is bounded: output is clamped to [0.01,0.99], the path is gated behind n_distinct_providers>=2, the qualitative direction (push away from 0.5) is preserved, and a downstream calibration stage can partially absorb a fixed multiplicative logit distortion. Not high (no broken output, partly recoverable), not low (the estimator does not match its cited derivation and the constant is genuinely unfit).

### `READ-237` — _estimate_effective_n uses a dimensionally-incoherent variance-ratio formula with magic constant 0.1
- **confirmed ✓** · source: readiness:methodology · effort: medium · file: `src/pipeline/aggregator.py:153-180`
- **evidence:** `expected_var_single = mean_p*(1-mean_p)*0.1` (0.1 is unexplained), then `ratio = actual_var / expected_var_single` and `effective_n = max(1, min(n, int(round(n * min(1.0, ratio * n)))))`. The `ratio * n` term has no statistical meaning: the standard effective-sample-size correction for correlated estimates is n_eff = n / (1 + (n-1)*rho), driven by the inter-run CORRELATION, not by comparing observed dispersion to an arbitrary fraction of the Bernoulli variance of the mean. As written, larger observed variance (MORE disagreement) drives effective_n UP toward n, which inverts the intended semantics for correlated draws of one model: high agreement (low variance) should mean LOW effective independence, but here low variance yields low effective_n only incidentally via the same term that also caps at n. The self-described 'rough heuristic' is not a valid effective-N estimator.
- **recommendation:** Effective-N from N runs of the SAME model is not recoverable from the dispersion of the point estimates alone — there is no independent-replication structure to estimate rho from. Either drop effective_n for single-model multi-run setups (report n with a caveat), or estimate it only across genuinely distinct providers using an intraclass-correlation-based n/(1+(n-1)*rho). Remove the magic 0.1 and the ratio*n term.
- **verifier:** Confirmed against src/pipeline/aggregator.py:153-180. The core defects are real: (1) the magic constant 0.1 on line 171 (`expected_var_single = mean_p*(1-mean_p)*0.1`) is undocumented and underived (comment literally says "rough estimate"); (2) line 176 computes `n * min(1, ratio*n)` = effectively `n^2 * actual_var / expected_var_single` (capped), which has no statistical basis — the standard ESS correction `n/(1+(n-1)*rho)` is driven by inter-run correlation, and grep confirms NO rho/ICC estimator exists anywhere in the repo; `_estimate_effective_n` is the sole estimator. Simulation confirms the n^2 scaling makes the saturation threshold scale as ~evs/n (e.g. stdev 0.091 at n=3 vs 0.050 at n=10 to pin eff_n to n), i.e. an arbitrary, n-dependent calibration. So "dimensionally-incoherent, magic 0.1, meaningless ratio*n" is accurate and material.

HOWEVER, the finding's central evidence sentence is WRONG and self-contradictory. It claims the formula "inverts the intended semantics," arguing "high agreement (low variance) should mean LOW effective independence." My simulation shows the code DOES return effective_n=1 for identical estimates and effective_n=n for high disagreement — i.e. low variance -> low effective_n, exactly what the finding says SHOULD happen and what the docstring (lines 156-157, 116) intends. The monotonic direction is the conventionally correct one (more spread = more effective independence for repeated draws of one model). There is no inversion. The genuine defect is calibration/magnitude (the n^2 + arbitrary 0.1), not direction. The finding's headline and recommendation are sound; its key supporting argument is incorrect.

Materiality is bounded: effective_n only feeds a human-review heuristic flag (human_review.py:348: flag when effective_n<3 and runs>=3, "runs may be correlated"). It does not enter the probability output. A miscalibrated estimator yields spurious/missed correlation flags for human reviewers — a real but contained QA-hygiene issue. The recommendation (drop effective_n for single-model multi-run, or use ICC-based n/(1+(n-1)*rho) only across distinct providers; remove the 0.1 and ratio*n) is methodologically correct.

### `READ-238` — Confidence interval combines a calibrated center with uncalibrated run-to-run dispersion treated as estimator standard error
- **confirmed ✓** · source: readiness:methodology · effort: small · file: `src/pipeline/orchestrator.py:347-349`
- **evidence:** `ci_low = cal_prob - 1.96*stdev`, `ci_high = cal_prob + 1.96*stdev`, where stdev is np.std(run probabilities, ddof=1) (aggregator.py:77) — the spread of the individual run point estimates, NOT the sampling SE of the aggregate. Multiplying run-to-run dispersion by 1.96 conflates 'how much the runs disagreed' with 'a 95% interval on the true probability', which are different quantities; the SE of a mean would be stdev/sqrt(n_eff), and even that would not be a Bayesian interval on the event probability. The center is the calibrated probability while the width comes from the RAW pre-calibration spread, so the interval is not internally consistent. Single-run fallback is an arbitrary +/-0.05.
- **recommendation:** Either drop the CI or define it explicitly as a heuristic dispersion band (label it as such, e.g. 'inter-run spread', not a 95% CI). If a genuine interval is wanted, derive it on a consistent scale (e.g. logit-space SE using effective_n) and apply calibration to the endpoints too, not just the center.
- **verifier:** Confirmed against code, not refuted. orchestrator.py:347-349 computes ci_low/ci_high = cal_prob +/- 1.96*stdev where cal_prob is the post-calibration probability and stdev (aggregator.py:77) is np.std(run probabilities, ddof=1) — the sample SD of the individual run POINT ESTIMATES, i.e. inter-run disagreement, not a sampling SE of the aggregate. The methodology critique is sound on three independent grounds: (1) it is not even an SE-of-the-mean (that would be stdev/sqrt(n)), so as a frequentist interval on the aggregate it is ~sqrt(n)x too wide; (2) it is not a Bayesian/predictive interval on the binary event probability (would need beta-binomial or logit-space treatment); (3) scale inconsistency — calibrated center with an uncalibrated, raw pre-calibration width, endpoints never passed through calibration. The single-run fallback is indeed a hardcoded +/-0.05 (else branch, lines 348-349).

Material because the value is surfaced to end users under the label 'Confidence interval' in both the Streamlit UI (pages/2_Predict.py:312) and the human review output (src/review/human_review.py:140) — the 1.96 multiplier deliberately invokes the normal 95% CI convention with no docstring/comment framing it as a heuristic spread, so the 'mislabeled / internally inconsistent' characterization holds and it is not a known intentional heuristic. The 'correct' ingredient is conspicuously available but unused: effective_n is computed (aggregator.py:118) yet never feeds the CI. Recommendation (relabel as inter-run spread, or derive a consistent logit-space SE using effective_n and calibrate endpoints) is feasible and correct.

Refutation attempts that failed: (a) no alternate CI control exists anywhere — grep confirms the only CI construction is at orchestrator.py:348-349; (b) it is not framed as an intentional dispersion band anywhere in code/comments; (c) the calibrated headline point estimate is unaffected, so this does not corrupt the core output.

### `READ-239` — Proper-scoring and Brier decomposition silently produce meaningless numbers for non-binary resolutions
- **confirmed ✓** · source: readiness:methodology · effort: small · file: `src/scoring.py:13-19`
- **evidence:** log_score does `if actual >= 0.5: return -log(p) else -log(1-p)` — it binarizes a free float at 0.5. Resolution.outcome is `float | None` documented as '0|1 for binary, value for numeric' (models.py:154), and QuestionType supports NUMERIC/MULTIPLE_CHOICE. A numeric outcome of e.g. 0.7 (a continuous resolution) is scored as outcome=1 with no error. compute_brier_decomposition likewise hard-codes binary uncertainty `uncertainty = bar_o*(1-bar_o)` (query.py:57) and bins on [0,1]. No clamp/validation that outcome is in {0,1} anywhere before these run.
- **recommendation:** Guard the scoring/decomposition entrypoints to assert outcome in {0,1} (or that question_type==BINARY) and raise/skip otherwise, rather than coercing. Surface a flag when a non-binary resolution reaches a binary scorer so contaminated scores are not silently entered into the calibration set.
- **verifier:** Confirmed and material. Code-grounded verification:

1. scoring.py:13-19 — log_score binarizes a free float at 0.5 (`if actual >= 0.5: -log(p) else -log(1-p)`) with no validation that actual is in {0,1}. brier_score (line 8-10) computes (p-actual)^2 against any float. Verified exactly as quoted.

2. Resolution.outcome (models.py:154) is `float | None` with the literal comment "0|1 for binary, value for numeric". No Pydantic field_validator, no ge=0/le=1 constraint anywhere on the field (confirmed by grep — no validators in models.py).

3. compute_brier_decomposition (src/registry/query.py:9-64, not bare query.py — minor location error in finding) hard-codes binary uncertainty `uncertainty = bar_o*(1-bar_o)` at line 57 and bins predictions on [0,1] (line 37-42). Verified.

4. The control truly does not exist anywhere: 
   - scripts/resolve.py:104-110 scores any non-None outcome with NO question_type guard.
   - get_calibration_pairs (query.py:104-121), get_brier_decomposition_by_domain (141-158), and by_horizon (181-199) all filter ONLY on `outcome is None` — never on question_type. Notably the get_calibration_pairs docstring (line 107) explicitly claims "Only includes resolved binary predictions" — a documented-intent violation the code does not honor, which strengthens the finding.
   - No question_type filtering exists in orchestrator.py or any script before forecast/submit/resolve.

5. The path is reachable: metaculus.py:25-26 maps continuous Metaculus questions to QuestionType.NUMERIC, and get_resolution (line 171) does `float(resolution_value)` with no normalization/clamp — a continuous resolution of 0.7 (or even outside [0,1]) becomes outcome and is scored as if binary, silently entering the calibration set.

The only inaccuracy is location: finding cites "query.py:57" but the file is src/registry/query.py:57 (line number correct within that file). Substance fully verified. This is a silent-contamination failure (no crash), which is the most dangerous class — meaningless proper-scores and Brier decompositions get fed into calibration without any flag.

### `READ-241` — Search-augmentation blend weights (0.25/0.40/0.50) are magic constants keyed to a self-reported LLM confidence string
- **confirmed ✓** · source: readiness:methodology · effort: medium · file: `src/pipeline/base_rate.py:232-240`
- **evidence:** `if confidence=='low': augmented = 0.75*base_rate + 0.25*stat_rate; elif medium: 0.60/0.40; else: 0.50/0.50`. The blend weight is driven entirely by an LLM-emitted 'high'|'medium'|'low' token (line 220), and the numeric weights are unjustified. A linear-pool weight between a prior (LLM base rate) and an empirical statistic should reflect the relative precision/sample size of each, not a 3-level self-assessed confidence label that the model has no calibration incentive to produce honestly. The empirical statistic is capped at 50% weight even when it is a hard frequency from an authoritative source, which under-weights real data; conversely a confidently-wrong 'high' label gives a hallucinated rate equal weight to the reasoned prior.
- **recommendation:** Anchor the blend on the reference_class_instance_count (already captured at base_rate.py:69-70) via a precision-weighted update (e.g. Beta-Binomial: treat the statistical rate as k/n pseudo-counts against the prior), rather than discretized self-reported confidence. If that data is unavailable, document the weights as a deliberate conservative heuristic and flag low-confidence blends.
- **verifier:** Confirmed against the code. base_rate.py:232-240 contains exactly the described blend: confidence=='low' -> 0.75/0.25, 'medium' -> 0.60/0.40, else -> 0.50/0.50. The blend is gated entirely on the LLM-emitted confidence token parsed at line 220 (system prompt at lines 180-188 instructs the model to self-report 'high'|'medium'|'low'). The numeric weights have no derivation in code or comments — they are hardcoded heuristics. The model has no calibration incentive to report confidence honestly, so a confidently-wrong 'high' gives a hallucinated rate equal weight to the reasoned prior. This is a genuine methodological weakness.

Materiality confirmed downstream: orchestrator.py:176-187 calls search_augmented_base_rate and, when a citation is returned, executes base_rate = augmented_rate (line 184) and overwrites record.decomposition.base_rate. Per the module docstring (base_rate.py:3-6), this base rate is the locked anchor fed to all estimation runs, so the blend output propagates into every forecast. The flaw is not cosmetic.

The finding's core recommendation is well-supported by the codebase itself: reference_class_instance_count is captured (base_rate.py:69-70, threaded through orchestrator.py:172) and is already treated as a reliability signal by validator.py:59-83, which warns at N<10 (±30pp CI) and N<20. So the precision data the finding says should drive the blend exists in the same record and is acknowledged elsewhere as the right reliability measure — yet the blend ignores it. That internal inconsistency strengthens the finding.

Two corrections that temper but do not refute it: (1) The finding frames stat_rate as 'a hard frequency from an authoritative source.' It is not — stat_rate is itself LLM-extracted from 300-char search excerpts (lines 170-206) with no verification, so capping its weight at 50% is partly defensible conservatism rather than a pure flaw. (2) The recommended Beta-Binomial update is only partially implementable as written: instance_count is the prior's pseudo-count, but the statistic's own sample size (the 'n' behind stat_rate) is never extracted, so a true precision-weighted pooling of the two would also require capturing the statistic's n. The directional fix (anchor on captured instance data and flag low-confidence blends rather than gating on a self-reported label) holds and is sound.

Severity medium is correct. It affects the locked anchor for all runs (argues against 'low'), but the blend is conservative-biased — it caps stat weight at 50% and the stat itself is LLM-noisy — so the downside is bounded, and the existing instance-count validator already emits reliability warnings that partially mitigate (argues against 'high').

### `READ-242` — Calibration set mixes submitted vs. calibrated probabilities, biasing the fitted curve
- **confirmed ✓** · source: readiness:methodology · effort: medium · file: `src/registry/query.py:114-120`
- **evidence:** get_calibration_pairs uses `predicted = record.submitted.probability if record.submitted is not None else record.calibration.calibrated_probability`. The same fallback is repeated in the Brier-decomposition groupers (query.py:145-149, 184-188). These pairs feed fit_platt / fit_isotonic_logit and the decomposition. Submitted probabilities are POST-calibration (and post-human-review adjustment), while calibrated_probability is the pipeline's calibrated output; mixing the two in one fit means the calibrator is partly being fit to already-calibrated (or human-adjusted) inputs. Fitting a calibration map on outputs that have themselves been calibrated/adjusted does not recover the raw->true mapping and can drive the fit toward identity or worse.
- **recommendation:** Calibration must be fit on the RAW aggregate (pre-calibration) probability paired with the outcome, consistently across all resolved records. Persist the raw aggregate explicitly and use it (not submitted/calibrated) for fit_platt/fit_isotonic and for the reliability term of the decomposition. Keep submitted-vs-calibrated only for separate post-hoc performance reporting.
- **verifier:** CONFIRMED via code. |source: src/registry/query.py:104-120, src/pipeline/orchestrator.py:337-345,407,421-435, src/pipeline/calibration.py:62-100,133-206|

The finding's core mechanism is verified end-to-end:

1. get_calibration_pairs() (query.py:116-119) returns (submitted.probability if present else calibrated_probability, outcome). The same fallback is repeated in both Brier-decomposition groupers (query.py:145-148, 184-187). VERIFIED.

2. submitted.probability is unambiguously POST-calibration and post-human-adjustment: orchestrator.py:407 cal_prob = record.calibration.calibrated_probability; line 422 final_prob = cal_prob + review.human_adjustment; line 435 record.submitted = Submission(probability=final_prob). So the training-x is calibrated output plus a manual human nudge. calibrated_probability is the pipeline's calibrated output. Both fallback branches are post-calibration variants — the title's "mixes submitted vs calibrated" is literally accurate. VERIFIED.

3. These pairs feed the calibration fit: orchestrator.py:337-344 passes pairs as isotonic_data into calibrate(), which at calibration.py:72-75 calls fit_isotonic_logit(preds, outs) and then applies isotonic_transform to aggregation.raw_aggregate (orchestrator.py:340). This is the load-bearing defect: the isotonic map is LEARNED on the calibrated/adjusted distribution but APPLIED to the raw (pre-calibration) aggregate — a domain mismatch. If historical calibration was roughly working, submitted probs are near-calibrated, so the learned map is ~identity on the calibrated scale; applying it to raw under-corrects raw miscalibration, exactly the "drives toward identity" concern. VERIFIED and material.

Absent-control confirmation: grep across the whole repo shows raw_aggregate is ONLY ever used as the transform input (orchestrator.py:340, aggregator.py:257/289 for submission probability), NEVER as training-x. No raw->outcome calibration path exists anywhere. The dashboard reuses the identical get_calibration_pairs() for post-hoc Brier reporting (dashboard.py:47,59) — there submitted IS correct, which is why a single function serving both fit and reporting is the root cause, matching the recommendation.

Refutations considered and rejected: (a) "intentional recursive calibration" — no, the output is applied to raw, not to a calibrated value, so it is not coherent feedback calibration; (b) "raw not persisted, no choice" — false, aggregation.raw_aggregate is a persisted field on every PredictionRecord, so the fix is directly implementable today (the recommendation's "persist the raw aggregate" is slightly redundant since it's already persisted, but the substantive fix — fit on raw_aggregate, not submitted — is correct and feasible); (c) "negligible" — the bias is systematic, not random, and defeats the purpose of the calibration stage in a system whose value proposition is calibration.

Two minor evidence inaccuracies that do NOT undermine the finding: (1) the finding says pairs feed "fit_platt / fit_isotonic_logit" — in production fit_platt and save_platt_params are NEVER called (only in tests; load_platt_params reads a file the pipeline never writes), so the only data-driven calibration path that actually executes is Phase-1 isotonic, which uses these mismatched pairs. This makes the live impact worse, not better. (2) human_adjustment contamination adds non-reproducible manual noise to training-x — an additional leakage vector the finding correctly flags.

### `READ-243` — Isotonic breakpoint subsampling can pair sorted logits with mis-aligned isotonic values, distorting the calibration map
- **REFUTED ✗** · source: readiness:methodology · effort: medium · file: `src/pipeline/calibration.py:154-168`
- **evidence:** scipy.optimize.isotonic_regression(acts_sorted).x returns one fitted value PER input point (length N), monotone in input order. The code then subsamples both logits_sorted and iso_probs at the SAME uniform indices (lines 158-165) and zips them. This is only coherent because both arrays are already in logit-sorted order — fine. But the deeper issue: it fits isotonic regression of OUTCOMES against logit-RANK order and then interpolates by logit VALUE (isotonic_transform, lines 200-204). Pool-adjacent-violators on rank order, then linear interpolation on value, is not a standard isotonic calibration (which maps predicted->observed-frequency directly). With as few as 5 points (line 72) and breakpoints capped at max(3, N//5)=3 for N<20, the resulting map is fit on 5-19 noisy binary outcomes — far too few for a reliable nonparametric calibrator, and isotonic on tiny N overfits to the empirical step function.
- **recommendation:** Raise min_resolved_for_isotonic well above 20 (nonparametric isotonic calibration is unreliable below ~50-100 points), or keep Phase 1 as a shrinkage-to-identity rather than full isotonic. Verify the value-vs-rank interpolation matches intended isotonic-regression-of-frequency semantics, ideally via sklearn IsotonicRegression(out_of_bounds='clip') on the probability scale as a reference check.
- **verifier:** REFUTED. The finding bundles a false headline mechanism with a mild, mis-framed residual concern.

(1) Misalignment claim (the title) is false and self-refuted in its own evidence ("fine"). In src/pipeline/calibration.py:148-168, logits_sorted and acts_sorted are both indexed by the same argsort `order`; iso_probs = _isotonic(acts_sorted).x is in that same order; subsampling at the same `indices` keeps logit_x and prob_y paired. No mis-alignment occurs; each breakpoint correctly maps a logit to its fitted value.

(2) "PAV on rank order then interpolate by value is not standard isotonic calibration" is incorrect. scipy.optimize.isotonic_regression IS the standard L2 PAV (verified: fit of [0,1,0,1,1,0,1,1,1] -> [0,.5,.5,.667,.667,.667,1,1,1]). Because logits are sorted ascending before the call, PAV-on-outcomes-in-logit-order yields fitted values identical to value-based isotonic regression (sklearn IsotonicRegression(logits, outcomes)); x-values only determine ordering, not fitted values. Linear interpolation between (logit_x, prob_y) breakpoints on the logit scale is a legitimate, standard isotonic calibration map. The recommended sklearn cross-check would confirm equivalence, not expose a bug.

(3) The only genuine kernel is the generic "small-N nonparametric isotonic is noisy/overfits." This is real but mild and is already structured into the design: Platt is gated to N>=50, isotonic only to the N=20-49 window, breakpoints capped at max(3, N//5), and logit-scale linear interpolation smooths the step function. The finding also mis-frames the magnitude: it leans on "as few as 5 points" and "3 breakpoints for N<20," but the Phase 1 isotonic branch (calibration.py:70-75) only executes when n_resolved >= min_resolved_for_isotonic (20, confirmed src/config.py:42). isotonic_data = get_calibration_pairs() (orchestrator.py:337) is the resolved set, so length ~= n_resolved ~ 20-49, giving 4-9 breakpoints; the >=5 guard at line 72 is a secondary belt-and-suspenders check, not the operative floor. The N<20/3-breakpoint regime the finding describes is effectively unreachable.

Default to real=false: the claimed distortion mechanism does not exist, and the surviving concern is a known/accepted tradeoff that is already mitigated and is quantitatively overstated.

### `READ-258` — estimate_cost silently defaults unknown models to Sonnet pricing — no error, wrong cost for any model not in the hardcoded table
- **confirmed ✓** · source: readiness:ops-cost · effort: small · file: `/Users/bjgilbert/Projects/Zoltar/sigma-predict/src/pipeline/llm_router.py:409`
- **evidence:** `rates = pricing.get(model, (3.0, 15.0))  # default to Sonnet pricing` — any model id not in the 10-entry hardcoded table (lines 397-408, comment says 'approximate, March 2026') is priced as claude-sonnet-4-6 with no warning or log. The configured verification_providers include llama, gemma, nemotron, deepseek, qwen, devstral, glm, kimi (config.py:103-104) — none of which appear in the pricing table, so any cost they DID report would be mispriced as premium Sonnet rates. Cost governance built on these numbers is unreliable and the error is silent.
- **recommendation:** Log a warning (once per unknown model) when falling back, and return a sentinel/None cost rather than a confidently-wrong Sonnet estimate so downstream accounting can flag 'cost unknown' instead of fabricating a number. Move the pricing table to config so it can be updated without a code change.
- **verifier:** The CODE MECHANISM is confirmed at llm_router.py:409 — `rates = pricing.get(model, (3.0, 15.0))  # default to Sonnet pricing` does silently fall back to Sonnet rates for any model id not in the 10-entry exact-match table, with no warning or log. No control exists anywhere: I grepped the repo and estimate_cost has exactly two callers (llm_router.py:214 and forecaster.py:252), neither guards against unknown models. So the silent-fabrication behavior is real.

HOWEVER the finding's MATERIALITY ARGUMENT is substantially wrong and overstated. The finding's central claim — that verification_providers (llama, gemma, nemotron, deepseek, qwen, devstral, glm, kimi from config.py:103-104) would be 'mispriced as premium Sonnet rates' — does not hold against the code:

1. Those 8 providers reach the audit log ONLY via verify() (llm_router.py:247) and challenge() (line 297). Both call log_call with hardcoded tokens_in=0, tokens_out=0 and NEVER invoke estimate_cost. log_call's cost_usd defaults to 0.0 (llm_audit.py:86). So these models are never priced at all, let alone as Sonnet. The finding's evidence sentence is therefore incorrect.

2. estimate_cost is reached only through router.call() (line 214) and forecaster.py:252. Every router.call() caller (forecaster.py:110-111, deliberation.py:127, decomposer.py:61, base_rate.py:54/199) passes the PRIMARY model (config.primary_model.provider/model_id), which is anthropic/openai/google — i.e. the models the table actually covers (claude-sonnet/opus/haiku, gpt-4o/mini, gpt-5.1, o4-mini, gemini variants). The local Ollama models that drive the finding's materiality are zero-marginal-cost and never hit this path.

RESIDUAL REAL RISK (why real=true, not false): the fallback is still genuinely unsafe as defense-in-depth. If a primary model_id not matching a table key exactly is configured — a future gpt-5.2, claude-opus-4-7, or an id with a date/version suffix that doesn't string-match — cost is silently fabricated as Sonnet rates. The 'approximate, March 2026' comment plus exact-string-match keys make drift likely over time, and cost tracking is surfaced via CostTracking.total_cost_usd. The recommendation (log-once on fallback, return sentinel/None, move table to config) is sound. But the blast radius is the single configured primary model, not the 8 verification providers the finding emphasizes.

### `READ-260` — resolve.py and dashboard.py construct Config() but never call validate() — no fail-fast at all
- **REFUTED ✗** · source: readiness:ops-cost · effort: trivial · file: `/Users/bjgilbert/Projects/Zoltar/sigma-predict/scripts/resolve.py:76`
- **evidence:** scripts/resolve.py:76 and scripts/dashboard.py:26 both do `config = Config()` with no subsequent validate() call (confirmed via grep — only `Config()` appears, no `validate`). resolve.py then makes authenticated platform calls to fetch resolutions (resolve.py:93-97); with a missing/empty METACULUS_API_KEY it will run unauthenticated and degrade to empty results via the clients' broad except-and-warn paths, producing a silent no-op resolution run rather than a clear 'missing key' error. predict.py/batch_predict.py at least call validate(); these two diverge.
- **recommendation:** Call config.validate() at the top of resolve.main and dashboard.main and exit with a clear message on missing required keys, matching predict.py:83-87. Centralize the validate-and-exit boilerplate so all four scripts behave identically.
- **verifier:** REFUTED. The finding's literal observation is true but trivial; its stated mechanism and proposed fix are both wrong, making it non-material.

Confirmed true (grep + reads): scripts/resolve.py:76 and scripts/dashboard.py:26 call Config() with no validate(); predict.py:83 and batch_predict.py:47 do call validate(). So the bare "they never call validate()" claim holds.

But the finding collapses on its causal mechanism and remediation:

1. validate() does NOT check METACULUS_API_KEY. src/config.py:147-155 only checks LLM provider keys (Anthropic/OpenAI/Google) and primary_model. The recommended fix ("call config.validate() and exit on missing required keys, matching predict.py:83-87") would therefore NOT detect or guard a missing Metaculus key at all. The remediation does not address the failure it describes — the two are unrelated. Even predict.py's validate() call does not catch a missing Metaculus key.

2. The "missing METACULUS_API_KEY -> silent no-op resolution" mechanism is largely fictional. get_resolution (metaculus.py:156-182) reads the public /questions/{id}/ endpoint; Metaculus resolution data is publicly readable and does not require auth. The Authorization header (metaculus.py:66-69) only matters for writes. submit_prediction already has its own explicit guard (metaculus.py:133-135: logs an error and returns False when the key is missing). So the key-related concern is already handled where it actually matters, and resolution reads work fine unauthenticated.

3. The proposed fix would be actively wrong for these two scripts. Neither resolve.py nor dashboard.py imports an LLM client or Orchestrator (confirmed by import grep) — they are registry reads plus, for resolve, public Metaculus resolution reads. Adding validate() (which checks LLM keys) would make `dashboard` and `resolve` fail-fast on a missing LLM key they never use — introducing a spurious failure mode rather than fixing one.

Net: the divergence between scripts is intentional and correct (LLM-using scripts validate LLM keys; non-LLM scripts don't). There is no real fail-fast gap, no real silent-no-op resolution path tied to the missing key, and the proposed fix is misdirected. Per the default-real=false guidance for already-handled / unconfirmable mechanisms, this is not a genuine, material ops-cost issue.

### `READ-263` — No linter, formatter, or type-checker configured despite type-annotated codebase
- **confirmed ✓** · source: readiness:ops-cost · effort: small · file: `/Users/bjgilbert/Projects/Zoltar/sigma-predict/pyproject.toml:22-27`
- **evidence:** grep for ruff|mypy|black|flake8|pylint|coverage|pyright across all .toml/.cfg/.ini/.yaml/.yml (excluding .venv) returns ZERO matches. pyproject.toml dev extras (lines 23-27) contain only pytest, pytest-asyncio, pytest-httpx — no static-analysis tooling. The code is heavily type-annotated (`from __future__ import annotations`, union types, dataclasses throughout) and uses fragile float()/int() coercion of LLM JSON (forecaster.py:181,234,243 per repo map) that a type checker plus tests would help guard. Nothing enforces style or catches type errors.
- **recommendation:** Add ruff (lint+format) and mypy (or pyright) to the dev extras and a [tool.ruff]/[tool.mypy] config block in pyproject.toml; wire both into the CI workflow above. Optionally add pytest-cov with a coverage floor.
- **verifier:** CONFIRMED with caveats. |source:grep+pyproject.toml+forecaster.py| Status: VERIFIED.

The absent-control core claim is real and reproducible. grep -rniE 'ruff|mypy|black|flake8|pylint|coverage|pyright|isort|pre-commit|pytest-cov' across all .toml/.cfg/.ini/.yaml/.yml/.txt (excluding .venv) returns ZERO matches. pyproject.toml dev extras (lines 23-27) contain only pytest, pytest-asyncio, pytest-httpx. No [tool.ruff] or [tool.mypy] config blocks exist. The codebase IS heavily type-annotated: `from __future__ import annotations` appears in 10+ src files. The float()/int() LLM-JSON coercions are real at forecaster.py:181, 221, 234, 243. So nothing enforces style or checks types in a type-annotated codebase — the control truly does not exist anywhere in the repo.

Two findings against the report's framing (severity should LOWER, not stay at medium):

1. The materiality rationale is technically wrong. The finding argues a type checker "would help guard" the fragile float()/int() coercion. It would not. mypy/pyright treat `data.get(...)` (from json.loads) as `Any` and emit no error. The actual runtime risk is an unguarded ValueError — e.g. float("high") at line 181 is NOT wrapped in the surrounding try/except (which only guards json.loads at 173-179). A type checker catches none of this; the real fix is runtime validation, not static typing. The finding overstates what mypy buys here.

2. The recommendation is internally inconsistent / templated. It says "wire both into the CI workflow above" — but there is NO CI workflow in this repo. No .github/ directory, no yaml/yml files anywhere (find confirms zero). The recommendation references infrastructure that does not exist, indicating the finding text was not fully grounded against this repo.

Context for severity: this is a v0.1.0 single-maintainer research/prediction tool with no CI whatsoever. Adding ruff+mypy is reasonable hygiene, but the gap is low-stakes ops debt, the type-checker materiality argument is incorrect, and the recommendation cites a phantom CI block. The control-absence is genuine; the impact framing is inflated.

### `READ-264` — No dependency lockfile — unpinned >= ranges make builds non-reproducible
- **confirmed ✓** · source: readiness:ops-cost · effort: small · file: `/Users/bjgilbert/Projects/Zoltar/sigma-predict/pyproject.toml:6-20`
- **evidence:** No poetry.lock / uv.lock / requirements.txt / Pipfile.lock exists (ls returned exit 1 / no matches). Every dependency in pyproject.toml is a floor-only constraint (`anthropic>=0.40.0`, `openai>=1.50.0`, `streamlit>=1.37.0`, `numpy>=2.0.0`, etc., lines 7-19). Two installs days apart can resolve to different versions of the LLM SDKs — exactly the dependencies whose API surface (response.usage, message shape) the cost-accounting and router code depends on (llm_router.py:67-69). A breaking minor release in anthropic or sigma-verify would silently change behavior with no pinned baseline to roll back to.
- **recommendation:** Generate and commit a lockfile (uv lock / pip-compile to requirements.txt) and install from it in CI and deployment. Keep pyproject ranges for libraries but pin the application's resolved set.
- **verifier:** CONFIRMED. Every factual claim in the finding verifies against the repo (Tier 2 finding, source: direct file inspection).

Lockfile absence VERIFIED: `git ls-files | grep -iE 'lock|requirements|Pipfile|pdm|constraints'` returns nothing (exit 1). `find` across the working tree finds no poetry.lock / uv.lock / requirements*.txt / Pipfile.lock / pdm.lock. `git check-ignore` confirms no lockfile is being silently gitignored — so the control truly does not exist anywhere in the repo, not even untracked-but-present. There is also no CI workflow, Dockerfile, Makefile, or deploy config anywhere (git ls-files grep exit 1) — so there is no other layer that could be pinning a resolved set at install/deploy time. The absent-control claim is fully substantiated.

Unpinned ranges VERIFIED: pyproject.toml lines 6-20 are all floor-only `>=` constraints exactly as quoted (anthropic>=0.40.0, openai>=1.50.0, streamlit>=1.37.0, numpy>=2.0.0, sigma-verify>=0.1.0, etc.). Dev deps (24-27) are also floor-only. No upper bounds anywhere.

API-surface dependency VERIFIED: the cited router code is at src/pipeline/llm_router.py (finding's path '/sigma-predict/.../llm_router.py' is correct relative to repo; the bare 'llm_router.py' shorthand resolves there). Lines 67-70 directly access response.content[0].text, response.usage.input_tokens, response.usage.output_tokens — concrete structural coupling to the anthropic SDK response object that token/cost accounting depends on. (Finding cites 67-69; actual is 68-69 for usage. Off-by-one, immaterial.) A breaking change in the anthropic SDK's usage object (which has historically changed shape across minor versions) would silently alter cost accounting with no pinned baseline to diff or roll back to.

Materiality for the ops-cost lens: this is the production-readiness concern the lens exists to catch. Builds are genuinely non-reproducible — two `pip install -e .` runs days apart can resolve different anthropic/openai/streamlit/numpy versions. numpy>=2.0.0 with no ceiling is itself a known reproducibility hazard (NumPy 2.x introduced ABI/behavioral breaks vs scipy). And sigma-verify>=0.1.0 is an unusually loose floor on a sibling internal package whose provider response shape the router also consumes — a 0.x package making no compatibility guarantees, pinned only at its earliest possible version.

Could not refute. The one weak spot in a refutation attempt — 'maybe a lockfile is generated in CI/deploy' — is itself disproven: there is no CI or deploy layer at all. Default-to-false does not apply because the control is confirmed absent and the failure mode (silent cost-accounting drift on SDK upgrade) is concrete and code-grounded.

Severity: KEEP at medium. Reasoning for not raising: no evidence of an active incident, the app is pre-1.0 (version 0.1.0) and appears to be single-developer/local-run (a committed .venv exists, .env present, no deploy infra), so blast radius today is one machine, not a fleet. Reasoning for not lowering: the recommendation is cheap and standard (uv lock / pip-compile + install-from-lock), the coupling to a volatile SDK surface is real, and 'medium' correctly reflects a real but non-urgent production-readiness gap rather than a live defect. Medium is calibrated.

### `READ-265` — No README or operator documentation — setup, run, and operate knowledge is undocumented
- **confirmed ✓** · source: readiness:ops-cost · effort: small · file: `/Users/bjgilbert/Projects/Zoltar/sigma-predict/README.md (absent)`
- **evidence:** No README* and no docs/ directory exist (ls returned exit 1). The project ships four CLI scripts (predict, batch_predict, resolve, dashboard), a Streamlit hub (app.py + 5 pages), required env vars across 7 keys, and a data/ layout the code assumes (config.py:128-133 derives data/prompts/predictions/calibration paths relative to source). A new operator has only .env.example (which lists keys but no run instructions) and docstring usage examples buried in individual scripts. There is no documented answer to: how to install, which keys are truly required vs optional, how to run the UI, where the registry lives, or how cost behaves. Deployability for anyone but the original author is poor.
- **recommendation:** Add a README covering install (with the lockfile), required vs optional env keys (reconcile with .env.example which marks TAVILY required but config.validate treats it optional), how to run each CLI and the Streamlit app, the data/ directory contract, and a cost/spend warning. Document that the UI has no auth and triggers paid spend.
- **verifier:** CONFIRMED. Every load-bearing claim verified against code. (1) No README* and no docs/ at repo root — only .md files in-project are prompt templates under prompts/ and vendor files under .venv/.pytest_cache; none are operator docs. (2) Four CLI scripts present (scripts/predict.py, batch_predict.py, resolve.py, dashboard.py — plus an additional monitor.py, so finding undercounts rather than overstates). (3) Streamlit app.py + exactly 5 pages (1_Dashboard..5_Registry) confirmed. (4) Seven *_KEY env vars confirmed in .env. (5) config.py:128-133 derives data_dir/prompts_dir from source location; predictions/calibration/registry paths derive from data_dir (lines 136-145) — the data/ layout is an undocumented implicit contract. (6) Only .env.example exists for setup, with terse comments and no run instructions. (7) The TAVILY required/optional discrepancy is REAL and material: .env.example line 6 lists TAVILY_API_KEY under '# Required', but config.validate() (lines 147-155) only requires one of ANTHROPIC/OPENAI/GOOGLE and never checks TAVILY. (8) No auth anywhere in app.py or pages/ (grep for auth|password|login|st.secrets returned nothing), and 2_Predict.py:59-72 spawns a background thread invoking Orchestrator.predict(), which drives paid LLM + Tavily calls — so the no-auth + paid-spend warning is grounded. One minor inaccuracy in the recommendation: there is NO lockfile (no uv.lock/poetry.lock/requirements.txt, none git-tracked), so 'install with the lockfile' is slightly off — but this strengthens the deployability concern (deps are unpinned in pyproject), it does not refute the finding. Could not refute; the finding is genuine, code-grounded, and material to anyone other than the original author standing this up.

### `READ-250` — Intermediate stage outputs (decomposer, base-rate, pre-mortem, deliberation) keep only parsed fields — raw LLM reasoning is discarded
- **confirmed ✓** · source: readiness:provenance · effort: medium · file: `src/pipeline/forecaster.py:170-247; src/pipeline/orchestrator.py:141-186`
- **evidence:** run_forecast parses the forecast JSON and keeps only selected fields (probability, adjustment_reasoning, reasoning_chain, etc., forecaster.py:227-247); the raw model response `result` is logged at debug level only on parse failure (forecaster.py:177) and otherwise dropped. The decomposer and base_rate stages (orchestrator.py:141-186) likewise persist only the parsed Decomposition fields — the raw decomposition/base-rate model outputs are not stored. Deliberation results are stored as an opaque dict (models.py:121 deliberation_result: dict|None) with no schema guarantee. If a parsed field looks wrong post-hoc, there is no raw output to audit against.
- **recommendation:** Persist the raw LLM response text (or its hash plus a stored copy) for each stage that affects the final number — at minimum the forecast and base-rate calls — so parsed values can be checked against the source generation.
- **verifier:** Confirmed against code. forecaster.py run_forecast parses the model output (_extract_json + json.loads) and keeps only selected fields on RunResult (lines 227-247); the raw `result` is logged at logger.debug only on parse failure (line 177) and otherwise dropped, and the pre-mortem raw `pm_result` is likewise discarded after parsing (lines 213-217). orchestrator.py:141-186 persists only parsed Decomposition fields and scalar base-rate values — no raw decomposer/base-rate model text is retained. models.py:121 confirms `deliberation_result: dict | None = None` is an unschematized opaque dict. So the core claim — raw LLM reasoning for the number-affecting stages is discarded — is accurate.

I checked whether an audit control exists anywhere in the repo (the finding implies absence). There IS one: src/pipeline/llm_audit.py (LLMAuditLogger), wired into LLMRouter.call/verify/challenge/cross_verify and flushed at orchestrator.py:369. It records result_hash (SHA-256 truncated to 16 hex chars) + result_len per call. This is the 'hash' half of the recommendation but explicitly NOT the 'stored copy' half — the module docstring states raw text is NEVER written, and log_call never persists result_text. A 16-char truncated hash cannot be used to check parsed values against the source generation (no recoverable text, not even a usable diff/integrity anchor for content). Additionally audit_path defaults to None (config.py:109) with no config file enabling it, so by default there is zero retention — not even the hash. Therefore the recommended control (raw response text, or hash + stored copy, for at least forecast and base-rate calls) is genuinely absent. The finding's recommendation should note the existing audit logger as the natural insertion point — extend it with an opt-in raw-text capture rather than building net-new.

Material: post-hoc, if a parsed field looks wrong there is no source generation to audit against. Mitigating context (does not refute, slightly tempers): this is a personal tool with human-in-the-loop review pre-submission, and several reasoning-bearing parsed fields ARE retained on RunResult (adjustment_reasoning, reasoning_chain, pre_mortem_scenario, falsification_anchors, sources_cited), so the audit gap is narrower than 'nothing retained' — it's the unparsed remainder plus the decomposition/base-rate narrative that are lost.

### `READ-252` — Platt parameters loaded from a single mutable global file that is overwritten on refit, with no versioning
- **confirmed ✓** · source: readiness:provenance · effort: small · file: `src/pipeline/calibration.py:272-287; src/pipeline/orchestrator.py:336`
- **evidence:** load_platt_params reads data/calibration/platt_global.json (calibration.py:280-287) and save_platt_params overwrites it in place (calibration.py:272-277). Even when a record stores platt_params_used, there is no fit identifier, fit date, or training-set fingerprint linking those coefficients to the data they were fit on. Refitting (CalibrationConfig.platt_refit_interval) silently replaces the file, so two records claiming Platt calibration may have used different global params with no way to tell which fit was active. The stored {a,b} are reconstructable only because they happen to be inlined, but the provenance of that fit (which resolved records, when) is lost.
- **recommendation:** Version Platt fits: write platt_<timestamp_or_hash>.json, never overwrite, and store the fit identifier (plus n_resolved and training-set hash) on the record alongside {a,b}.
- **verifier:** Core provenance defect CONFIRMED but mechanism MISCHARACTERIZED. |source:src/models.py:126-129| CalibrationData stores only platt_params_used ({a,b}), calibrated_probability, confidence_interval — no fit_id, fit_date, n_resolved, or training-set hash. |source:src/pipeline/calibration.py:272-287| save_platt_params writes platt_<label>.json with open(path,"w") (overwrite) and load_platt_params defaults label="global" -> platt_global.json. |source:src/pipeline/orchestrator.py:336,352| load_platt_params(self.config) feeds calibrate(); record stores platt_dict with no lineage. So the absent control (fit versioning/lineage) genuinely does not exist anywhere in src/ (grep for fit_id/fit_hash/fingerprint/fit_timestamp returns nothing). VERIFIED.

However the finding overstates the live mechanism. REFUTED sub-claims: (1) save_platt_params is NEVER called anywhere in the repo (grep across all .py: only the definition + tests calling fit_platt). There is no refit loop, scheduler, or persistence path. (2) platt_refit_interval (config.py:40) is dead config — referenced by zero executing code; the finding cites it as driving "silent" refits that overwrite the file, but no code reads it. (3) The "two records used different global params with no way to tell" scenario cannot occur in current code because nothing ever writes/rewrites the file; it requires out-of-band manual editing. (4) The entire Platt phase (calibration.py:62, gated on platt_params truthy AND n_resolved>=50) is currently unreachable in production since platt_global.json is never produced (data/calibration/ is empty).

Conclusion: the provenance gap is genuine and the recommendation (version fits, store fit-id + n_resolved + training hash on the record) is correct and worth doing. But the described active harm (overwrite-on-refit contaminating live records) does not exist today. The defect is latent — it bites only once someone builds the refit/persistence path, which is the same moment the fix would naturally be added.

### `READ-255` — Submitted vs calibrated probability ambiguity in calibration/scoring read paths undermines what a record 'predicted'
- **REFUTED ✗** · source: readiness:provenance · effort: small · file: `src/registry/query.py:116-120,145-149,184-188`
- **evidence:** get_calibration_pairs and the Brier-decomposition groupers use record.submitted.probability when present and otherwise fall back to record.calibration.calibrated_probability (query.py:116-120, 145-149, 184-188). submitted.probability can differ from calibrated_probability because of the human_review adjustment applied at orchestrator.py:421-423 (final_prob = cal_prob + human_adjustment). So the scoring set silently mixes human-adjusted submitted numbers with model-calibrated numbers depending on whether each record was submitted, and the record does not flag which probability is the canonical 'prediction'. This makes the calibration curve itself non-reproducible/ambiguous.
- **recommendation:** Define and store one canonical prediction probability per record (with provenance: which stage produced it), and have scoring/calibration consume that single field rather than branching on submitted-vs-calibrated.
- **verifier:** REFUTED. The finding's code-location claims are accurate (query.py:116-120, 145-149, 184-188 do branch submitted.probability vs calibration.calibrated_probability; orchestrator.py:421-423 does set final_prob = cal_prob + human_adjustment and store it as submitted.probability). But the core claim — that the calibration/scoring set "silently mixes human-adjusted submitted numbers with model-calibrated numbers depending on whether each record was submitted" — does not hold, because the precondition for the mixing can never occur.

Decisive evidence: scripts/resolve.py:112 (`record.resolution = resolution`) is the ONLY place in the entire codebase (verified across src/ and scripts/) that ever attaches a Resolution to a record. That assignment is gated at resolve.py:89 by `if not record.submitted: continue`. Therefore every record with `resolution is not None` was necessarily submitted. All three query.py read paths first gate on `record.resolution is None or record.resolution.outcome is None` before selecting the probability, so for every record that reaches the probability-selection branch, `record.submitted is not None` is always true and the `submitted.probability` branch is always taken. The fallback to `calibration.calibrated_probability` (query.py:119/148/187) is unreachable dead code for resolved records. No mixing of the two sources can occur in the scoring set.

The human-adjustment concern is also a non-issue: when a human adjustment is applied, final_prob (= cal_prob + adjustment) is stored as submitted.probability, AND resolve.py:105-108 computes the canonical resolution.brier_score from that same submitted.probability. So the read path (query.py) and the stored canonical score agree on the same number. The canonical 'prediction' is unambiguous: it is submitted.probability (human-adjusted where applicable). There is no provenance ambiguity and the calibration curve is reproducible — it is consistently driven by submitted.probability.

The only residual is a minor code smell: a defensive `else` fallback branch that is currently unreachable. That is not a medium-severity provenance defect and does not undermine what a record 'predicted'. The recommendation (store one canonical field) is defensible as future-proofing but solves a problem that does not exist under current invariants.

### `READ-230` — get_question raises on first transient error with no retry — search-then-fetch flow can abort on a single timeout
- **confirmed ✓** · source: readiness:resilience · effort: small · file: `/Users/bjgilbert/Projects/Zoltar/sigma-predict/src/platforms/metaculus.py:118-129`
- **evidence:** get_question converts any error (including transient 429/5xx/timeout) into a raised ValueError with no retry (metaculus.py:124-129, polymarket.py:114-119, kalshi.py:149-154). This is the entry point the orchestrator and CLI use to load the question being predicted; a single transient timeout here aborts the run before it starts. Unlike the list methods which degrade to [], this path is fail-hard with no backoff, so a brief platform blip during question fetch is unrecoverable for that invocation.
- **recommendation:** Add a small bounded retry with backoff for transient classes (timeout, 429, 5xx) before raising ValueError, so a momentary blip does not abort the run. Keep raising on genuine 404/not-found.
- **verifier:** Confirmed against the actual code. In all three platform clients, get_question catches every exception (including transient timeout/429/5xx) and re-raises as ValueError with no retry or backoff: metaculus.py:124-129, polymarket.py:114-119, kalshi.py:149-154. The asymmetry the finding describes is real — the corresponding get_questions list methods degrade to [] (metaculus.py:111-116, polymarket.py:101-106, kalshi.py:131-136) while get_question is fail-hard.

Absent-control claim VERIFIED: a repo-wide search (grep over all .py files plus pyproject.toml) found zero retry/backoff/tenacity, no httpx transport-level retries (clients are plain httpx.Client with timeout=30.0 and no transport=), and no manual retry loops. The control genuinely does not exist anywhere.

Caller analysis (materiality): get_question is invoked at scripts/predict.py:99 with NO surrounding try/except — a transient error aborts the CLI run with an uncaught traceback, matching the finding's 'abort before it starts.' However, the Streamlit callers at pages/2_Predict.py:162 and :182 BOTH wrap the call in try/except and surface 'Fetch failed: {e}', so the interactive path already degrades gracefully and recovery is a single re-click.

So the finding is genuine and not already handled in the CLI path, but the practical blast radius is modest: the failure occurs at the cheapest point in the flow (before any LLM/pipeline work), there is no data loss or partial-state corruption, the Streamlit path self-recovers, and the CLI remedy is re-running the command. A bounded retry on transient classes is reasonable hardening, but this does not rise to medium given the low cost of failure and the existing interactive handling.

### `READ-231` — External verification/challenge providers catch exceptions but never retry — a transient 429 is recorded as a permanent VerificationError, silently degrading agreement scoring
- **confirmed ✓** · source: readiness:resilience · effort: medium · file: `/Users/bjgilbert/Projects/Zoltar/sigma-predict/src/pipeline/llm_router.py:263-277`
- **evidence:** verify() (lines 263-277), challenge() (lines 313-320), and cross_verify() (lines 351-368) catch `except Exception` and turn it into a VerificationError / failed-dict / None. This is better than the primary path (it does not crash), but there is NO retry/backoff: a transient 429 or timeout on an external provider is permanently recorded as an error_class failure. With multiple external providers iterated sequentially in cross_verify (line 332), a shared rate-limit window can fail several providers at once, collapsing the verification pool and skewing inter-model agreement — yet the pipeline reports verification as 'enabled' (orchestrator.py:127-130) with no signal that providers transiently dropped.
- **recommendation:** Add bounded retry/backoff for transient classes (429/5xx/timeout) before recording a terminal VerificationError, and surface a count of transient-vs-permanent verification failures so a degraded verification pool is distinguishable from genuine disagreement.
- **verifier:** PARTIALLY CONFIRMED — the absent control is real, but the finding's stated impact mechanism is substantially wrong, warranting a severity reduction.

CONFIRMED claims (code-grounded):
- No retry/backoff exists. verify() (llm_router.py:263-277), challenge() (313-320), and cross_verify() (351-368) catch `except Exception` and immediately record a terminal VerificationError/failed-dict/None. grep across both src/ and the sigma-verify package (/Users/bjgilbert/Projects/sigma-verify/src) found no retry/backoff/tenacity for transient API errors. The only time.sleep (handlers.py:58) is Ollama-startup polling, not API-call retry. So a transient 429/timeout/5xx is permanently recorded as an error_class failure. This control truly does not exist anywhere in the repo.
- cross_verify iterates providers sequentially (llm_router.py:332), so a shared rate-limit window can fail multiple providers in one run, shrinking the surviving pool.

REFUTED / OVERSTATED claims:
- "Skewing inter-model agreement": FALSE as stated. inter_model_agreement (aggregator.py:112) is computed purely from forecast-run probability stdev and has zero dependency on verification results. The finding conflates it with the separate per-stage verification agreement.
- "Silently degrading agreement scoring": Largely refuted. _compute_agreement (verification.py:40-50) filters to status=="success" BEFORE scoring. A VerificationError carries status=="failed" (sigma-verify clients.py:104) and is EXCLUDED — it is never miscounted as disagree, so it cannot flip unanimous→partial or inflate disagreement. If all providers fail, agreement correctly becomes "no_data" (distinguishable from genuine consensus), not a false score.
- "No signal that providers transiently dropped": Mostly refuted. classify_error (clients.py:33-48) tags rate-limit/timeout/network-error (transient) vs auth-error/token-limit (permanent), and human_review.py:261-265 prints "FAILED (error_class)" per failed provider. The transient-vs-permanent distinction the recommendation asks for already exists at per-entry granularity; only an aggregate count is missing.

Residual real harm: on a transient rate-limit window, the verification pool silently shrinks (smaller N → weaker consensus robustness) with no aggregate "degraded pool" signal, and no automatic recovery via retry. That is a genuine but minor resilience gap — not the scoring-corruption the finding describes. The "verification enabled" report (orchestrator.py:127-130) is truthful: it reflects configured/available providers, and per-call transient failure does not make that flag a lie.

### `READ-232` — float()/int() coercion on LLM JSON fields can raise ValueError and crash a run even when JSON parses successfully
- **confirmed ✓** · source: readiness:resilience · effort: small · file: `/Users/bjgilbert/Projects/Zoltar/sigma-predict/src/pipeline/forecaster.py:181`
- **evidence:** After json.loads succeeds, the code does `probability = float(data.get('probability', base_rate))` (line 181), `int(data.get('search_quality_score', 5))` (line 234), `int(data.get('confidence_self_score', 5))` (line 243), and `float(pm_data.get('pre_mortem_delta', 0.0))` (line 221) with no try/except. A malformed-but-valid-JSON response (e.g. "probability": "high" or null) raises ValueError/TypeError that escapes the parse_failed handling (which only guards json.loads) and crashes the run. base_rate.py:70 has the same exposure: `int(raw_count)` is inside the try that catches ValueError, but forecaster.py's coercions are NOT. This is a partial/malformed-response resilience gap distinct from connection failures.
- **recommendation:** Wrap each numeric coercion in a helper that falls back to the default and sets parse_failed/a warning on failure, so a malformed field degrades gracefully rather than crashing the run.
- **verifier:** CONFIRMED. Code-grounded verification of every load-bearing claim in the finding:

1. forecaster.py:181 `probability = float(data.get("probability", base_rate))` — VERIFIED. Outside any try/except. Sits after the json.loads try block (173-179) closes.
2. forecaster.py:221 `float(pm_data.get("pre_mortem_delta", 0.0))` — VERIFIED, outside try (the pm json.loads try at 214-217 has already closed).
3. forecaster.py:234 `int(data.get("search_quality_score", 5))` and 243 `int(data.get("confidence_self_score", 5))` — VERIFIED, both inside the RunResult(...) constructor call, no guard.
4. parse_failed claim — VERIFIED. parse_failed is set True ONLY inside `except json.JSONDecodeError` (175-179). It guards json.loads alone; the coercions run unconditionally afterward.
5. base_rate.py comparison — VERIFIED. base_rate.py:70 `int(raw_count)` is inside a try that catches `(json.JSONDecodeError, ValueError)` at line 81, so base_rate handles this; forecaster does not. The asymmetry the finding cites is real and notable (it shows the codebase already knows the pattern but applied it inconsistently).

Crash mechanism is real: a valid-JSON-but-wrong-type field triggers it. `"probability": "high"` -> float() ValueError; `"probability": null` -> get() returns None -> float(None) TypeError; `"search_quality_score": "n/a"` -> int() ValueError; null -> int(None) TypeError. These escape the JSONDecodeError handler. The coercions feed the RunResult constructor before Pydantic validates, so Pydantic does not catch them. No earlier validation exists. The absent-control claim (no defensive coercion helper) is confirmed — grep shows none, and the pattern present in base_rate.py is not reused here.

Resilience framing is legitimate, not contrived: LLMs do emit string/null numerics under real conditions, which is exactly the malformed-but-parseable response class this lens targets. Distinct from connection-failure resilience.

SEVERITY ADJUSTMENT — recommend LOWER from medium to low. Blast radius is narrower than the finding's "crash a run" wording implies. I traced all three call sites:
- scripts/batch_predict.py:96-98 wraps orchestrator.predict() in `except Exception: continue` — a malformed field loses ONLY that one question and the batch proceeds.
- pages/2_Predict.py:61-82 wraps the predict call in try/except Exception — Streamlit surfaces an error, does not crash the app.
- scripts/predict.py:133 has NO try/except — here it does abort the single-question CLI process. This is the only true uncaught path, and it is the lowest-stakes context (one-off single-question CLI run, immediately visible to the operator).
Additionally there is no silent-corruption risk: the failure is a loud, immediate exception, not a bad number entering the forecast. The recommendation (graceful degradation via a coercion helper that sets parse_failed) is still sound and improves the batch case (a malformed-but-parseable response currently discards the whole run rather than degrading to base_rate like a true parse failure would). But because outer handlers already contain the blast radius in the two multi-question paths, medium overstates impact. Low is the calibrated severity.

### `SWEE-2` — Verification-unavailable warning is collected into a local list that is never merged into the record
- **confirmed ✓** · source: sweep · effort: trivial · file: `/Users/bjgilbert/Projects/Zoltar/sigma-predict/src/pipeline/orchestrator.py:112-134`
- **evidence:** A local list is created: `pipeline_warnings: list[str] = []` (line 112). The 'Verification enabled in config but no external providers available' message is appended to THIS local list (line 134). Every other warning in the method uses `record.pipeline_warnings.append(...)` (lines 200, 245, 261, 271, 292, 359). The local `pipeline_warnings` is never copied into `record.pipeline_warnings`, so this warning silently vanishes from the persisted record and any UI reading record.pipeline_warnings.
- **recommendation:** Append directly to `record.pipeline_warnings` (the record exists by line 127), or merge the local list into the record before returning. Remove the unused local list to avoid confusion.
- **verifier:** Confirmed by reading the code. At orchestrator.py:112 a local `pipeline_warnings: list[str] = []` is created with the comment "Accumulate pipeline warnings for downstream injection". At line 134 the "Verification enabled in config but no external providers available" message is appended to this LOCAL list. Grep of all `pipeline_warnings` references in the method (lines 112, 134, 200, 245, 261, 271, 292, 359) shows every OTHER warning is appended to `record.pipeline_warnings`, while the local list is written only once (134) and never read again. The method returns `record` at line 382 with no merge of the local list. The local variable is unreachable outside the function scope, so the message never reaches the persisted/serialized field `record.pipeline_warnings` (models.py:216), which is the field tests and any consumer read. So the finding's core claim — this one warning silently vanishes from the persisted record — is accurate and the location/line numbers are correct.

Two caveats that bear on severity: (1) The message is NOT entirely silent — it is also emitted via logger.warning at line 133, so it appears in logs. (2) The underlying condition is fully recoverable from the persisted record through other fields: record.verification (lines 127-130) persists enabled=False and providers_available=[] whenever this branch fires, so a UI/consumer can detect "verification was requested but unavailable" without the warning string. The finding overstates impact by saying it vanishes from "any UI reading record.pipeline_warnings" without noting the condition is observable elsewhere. The recommendation (append directly to record.pipeline_warnings or merge the local list, and remove the unused local) is correct and low-risk.

Net: genuine real bug (a warning that the code clearly intends to persist is dropped, inconsistent with all sibling warnings), but the practical consequence is muted by the parallel log line and the recoverable verification fields. This is a borderline low/medium — I'd lower to low.

### `SWEE-3` — Default extremization is silently never applied because all runs use one model
- **confirmed ✓** · source: sweep · effort: medium · file: `/Users/bjgilbert/Projects/Zoltar/sigma-predict/src/pipeline/aggregator.py:90-94`
- **evidence:** Extremization only fires when `n_distinct_providers >= 2` (aggregator.py:91). In the orchestrator forecast loop every run calls `run_forecast(...)` with no `model_id`/`provider` arguments (orchestrator.py:219-228), so all runs default to `config.primary_model` (forecaster.py:110-111). `n_distinct_providers = max(1, len(set(r.provider...)))` is therefore always 1 (orchestrator.py:274-275). With the default method `extremized_trimmed_mean`, the extremization branch is never taken — `extremization_factor` config and the `_extremize()` function are dead in normal operation. The aggregation name and config knob imply behavior that does not occur.
- **recommendation:** Either wire multi-model runs (vary provider/model_id per run from config.models) so extremization can engage, or document that extremization requires multi-provider runs and surface a warning when method='extremized_trimmed_mean' but only one provider is present.
- **verifier:** Confirmed and code-grounded at every cited point. aggregator.py:90-94 gates extremization on `n_distinct_providers >= 2`. The only non-test caller of run_forecast is orchestrator.py:219-228, which passes neither model_id nor provider, so forecaster.py:110-111 defaults every run to config.primary_model (single provider). orchestrator.py:274-275 computes n_distinct_providers = max(1, len(set of providers)), which is therefore always 1. RunResult does carry a real `provider` field (models.py:95) populated by the forecaster (forecaster.py:230), but since all runs use one provider the set has size 1. With the default method `extremized_trimmed_mean` (aggregator.py:38), the >=2 branch (line 91) is never taken, so _extremize() and the extremization_factor config knob are dead in normal operation. The deliberation path also uses config.primary_model (deliberation.py:128-129), so it cannot introduce a second provider either. config.models exists and is explicitly labeled 'Available models for multi-model runs (Phase 1)' (config.py:70, 91-97) but is never iterated — confirming the multi-model feature is wired into config but unused, i.e. incomplete rather than deliberately single-model. The method name and config knob imply behavior that does not occur. Recommendation (wire multi-model runs or document + warn) is appropriate. I could not refute any element of the finding.

### `SWEE-4` — Single verify()/challenge() can route back to the primary model (self-verification)
- **confirmed ✓** · source: sweep · effort: small · file: `/Users/bjgilbert/Projects/Zoltar/sigma-predict/src/pipeline/llm_router.py:413-420`
- **evidence:** `_get_external_client(provider=None)` returns `next(iter(self._external_providers.values()))` with NO exclusion of the primary provider. `cross_verify()` correctly filters the primary via `_external_clients()` (lines 422-436), but `verify()` (line 240) and `challenge()` (line 290) use `_get_external_client`. `challenge_forecast` calls `router.challenge(claim, evidence)` with no provider (verification.py:236). If the primary provider (e.g. openai when openai is primary) is also present in `_external_providers`, the pre-mortem challenge is performed by the same model that produced the forecast — defeating the cross-model premise. The whole module docstring promises 'External clients only (Anthropic is the host model)' but only guards Anthropic, not a non-Anthropic primary.
- **recommendation:** In `_get_external_client(None)`, pick the first client from `_external_clients()` (which excludes the primary) rather than from the raw `_external_providers` dict.
- **verifier:** Confirmed at the code level and through the consumer path.

CODE: llm_router.py:413-420 — `_get_external_client(provider=None)` returns `next(iter(self._external_providers.values()))` with NO primary exclusion. By contrast `_external_clients()` (422-436) explicitly filters out `self.config.primary_model.provider` (with a fallback to include it if nothing else remains). So the two helpers diverge: cross_verify() is guarded, verify()/challenge() are not.

CONSUMER: verification.py:236 — `challenge_forecast` calls `router.challenge(claim, evidence)` with no provider, routing to the unguarded `_get_external_client(None)`. This is wired into the live pipeline (orchestrator.py:300).

TRIGGER IS THE DEFAULT, NOT AN EDGE CASE: The init loop (llm_router.py:142-153) adds a non-Anthropic provider to `_external_providers` whenever it is in `config.verification_providers` and available. The Anthropic-specific guard (129-140) only excludes Anthropic-as-primary; it does NOT exclude a non-Anthropic primary. Default `verification_providers` (config.py:102-105) includes "openai" and "google". When no Anthropic key is set, primary auto-detects to openai (config.py:80-84). I verified sigma_verify.PROVIDERS order: ['openai','google',...] — openai is FIRST. So with openai primary and openai in the external dict, `next(iter(...))` returns the openai client = the primary model. The pre-mortem challenge is then performed by the same model that produced the forecast, defeating the cross-model premise and contradicting the module docstring's 'External clients only' promise.

SCOPE LIMIT (why not higher): The verification stages (verify_stage, verify_forecast_run) all use cross_verify() / _external_clients(), which correctly exclude the primary — they are NOT affected. Blast radius in the live pipeline is the single challenge() / pre-mortem stage. router.verify() (240) shares the same defect but has no production caller (only cross_verify is used by the orchestrator). The contamination is silent (the ChallengeEntry records provider/model, so it is auditable post-hoc, but nothing prevents or warns at runtime).

The recommendation is correct and matches the existing intended pattern: in the None branch, draw from _external_clients() rather than the raw _external_providers dict.

### `SWEE-14` — Polymarket community_prediction passes token price straight through with no range clamp
- **confirmed ✓** · source: sweep · effort: small · file: `src/platforms/polymarket.py:140`
- **evidence:** price = token.get("price"); if price is not None: return float(price)  (and the _map_market path at line 31). Whatever the remote returns in `price` is float()'d and returned as a probability. Polymarket CLOB prices are 0-1 strings normally, but there is no clamp or sanity check — a malformed or out-of-range value (negative, >1) flows directly into community_prediction, which PlatformQuestion (models.py:245) types only as `float | None` with no validator.
- **recommendation:** Clamp to [0.0, 1.0] and reject NaN/inf before returning. Apply a Pydantic validator on PlatformQuestion.community_prediction so no client can populate an out-of-range probability.
- **verifier:** The cited code is accurate. polymarket.py:140 (get_community_prediction) and :31 (_map_market) both do float(price) with no range clamp or NaN/inf check, and PlatformQuestion.community_prediction (models.py:245) plus PlatformSignals.prices_at_prediction_time (models.py:133) are typed float|None with no Pydantic validator. So the literal defect — no clamp, no validator — exists as described.

However, the finding overstates materiality. I traced where the Polymarket community value actually flows: it lands in PlatformSignals.prices_at_prediction_time under key "polymarket"/"polymarket_community". That dict is consumed in exactly two places: (1) human_review.py:149-151, which only displays it as f"{price:.3f}" — display-only, no corruption; and (2) the divergence flag at human_review.py:340-341, which reads ps.get("metaculus_community") — hardcoded to Metaculus, so the Polymarket value is never used in any computation. There is no aggregation, scoring, or calibration path that consumes the Polymarket value. An out-of-range value would only print a misleading line, not corrupt analysis.

Additional context the finding got wrong: the recommendation's framing ("so no client can populate an out-of-range probability") implies an untrusted-input boundary, but this is a read-only client consuming a price from the trusted Polymarket CLOB API, where prices are normally well-formed 0-1 strings. The finding also missed that the same missing-clamp pattern exists in metaculus.py:36 — which is actually the path used in the divergence math, the one place a bad value could matter. So the finding identified a genuine defensive-coding gap but at the wrong location for real impact and with an inflated severity.

### `SWEE-17` — Broad `except Exception` around .json() swallows parse failures and returns empty/raises generic, hiding upstream API changes
- **confirmed ✓** · source: sweep · effort: medium · file: `src/platforms/metaculus.py:114`
- **evidence:** Every list method ends with `except Exception as exc: logger.warning(...); return []` (metaculus.py:114-116, kalshi.py:134-136, polymarket.py:104-106). resp.json() raising on a non-JSON body (HTML error page, gateway timeout page, truncated stream), or _map_* raising a KeyError (e.g. metaculus _map_question does data["id"] at line 44 with no default — a missing id raises KeyError), is indistinguishable from 'no results'. The caller gets [] and proceeds as if the platform simply had no questions. A schema change at the remote silently degrades to empty results with only a WARN log.
- **recommendation:** Narrow the catch to httpx/json.JSONDecodeError/mapping errors and distinguish 'empty result set' from 'failed to parse'. At minimum surface parse failures at ERROR, and consider re-raising so the pipeline can flag a platform_warning rather than treating degraded data as authoritative-empty.
- **verifier:** Confirmed at all three cited locations. metaculus.py:114-116, kalshi.py:134-136, and polymarket.py:104-106 each end their list method with `except Exception as exc: logger.warning(...); return []`. The data flow makes the failure modes the finding describes genuinely reachable and indistinguishable from a legitimately empty result:

1. Body parse failure: `data = resp.json()` (metaculus:108, kalshi:126, polymarket:98) raises `json.JSONDecodeError` on a non-JSON body (HTML error page, gateway timeout, truncated stream). `json.JSONDecodeError` is NOT a subclass of `httpx.HTTPStatusError`, so it skips the specific handler and falls through to the broad `except Exception` → `return []`.

2. Mapping failure: `_map_question` does `str(data["id"])` at metaculus.py:44 with no default (and again at line 55 in the url fallback).
