A schema change dropping `id` raises KeyError, also caught by the broad handler → `return []`. Note other fields use `.get()` with defaults, so `id` is the specific brittle key.

Caller behavior confirms materiality — empty result is treated as authoritative-empty, not as failure:
- scripts/batch_predict.py:68 — `if not questions: click.echo("No questions found."); sys.exit(0)`.
- pages/2_Predict.py:119-124 — wraps the call in its own try/except, but because the client already swallows the parse error internally and returns `[]`, the page's handler never fires; it just renders an empty results table. So the page's defensive catch is defeated by the client's broad catch.

The HTTP layer is handled correctly and distinctly (HTTPStatusError caught separately, logged WARN for lists / ERROR for single fetch), so the gap is narrowly the body-parse + mapping layer — exactly what the finding scopes. The recommendation (narrow the catch, log parse failures at ERROR, distinguish empty-set from parse-failure, optionally surface a platform_warning) is sound and matches the actual code shape.

Could not refute. The only nuance is that the most common remote breakage (5xx/4xx) is already surfaced, which limits blast radius, but silent schema drift / non-JSON bodies degrading a prediction pipeline to 'no questions' with only a WARN is a genuine observability/correctness gap.

### `SWEE-18` — Metaculus _map_question crashes on payload missing 'id' despite other fields using .get defaults
- **confirmed ✓** · source: sweep · effort: small · file: `src/platforms/metaculus.py:44`
- **evidence:** question_id=str(data["id"]) and url=...f"...{data['id']}/" use direct subscripting while every other field uses data.get(..., default). A question payload without `id` (or a results entry that is not a dict) raises KeyError/TypeError. In get_questions this is caught and turns the whole page into [] (see prior finding); in get_question it's caught and re-raised as a misleading 'Question not found' ValueError even when the question was found but the schema differed.
- **recommendation:** Use data.get("id") with an explicit guard; if id is absent, skip that record (in list context) rather than failing the whole batch, and raise a distinct error in single-fetch context.
- **verifier:** Code-grounded verification of src/platforms/metaculus.py confirms the core finding. Line 44 (question_id=str(data["id"])) and line 55 (url default f-string "...{data['id']}/") both use direct subscripting on "id", while every other field in _map_question uses data.get(..., default) — title, description, resolution_criteria, fine_print, created_at, close_time, resolve_time, url, tags. A question payload missing "id" raises KeyError; a non-dict results entry raises TypeError. The downstream consequences are real: in get_questions, lines 114-116 catch broad Exception and return [] for the entire page, so a single malformed entry silently wipes the whole batch (the list comprehension on line 110 is fully inside the try). In get_question, a KeyError is caught by the generic except Exception (line 127) and re-raised as a ValueError masking the true cause. Two inaccuracies in the finding, neither load-bearing: (1) it quotes the re-raised message as "Question not found", but the path a KeyError actually takes is line 129's "Failed to fetch question {id} from Metaculus"; the "...not found" message is only on line 126's HTTPStatusError branch, which a KeyError would not reach. The misleading-error point still stands, just via the wrong line. (2) It misses that the line 55 f-string default is eagerly evaluated even when url is present, but line 44 fails first so this is moot. Materiality: Metaculus API v2 always returns id on question objects (confirmed by test fixtures using integer id), so this only fires on a schema regression, API change, or malformed/non-dict entry — it is defensive robustness, not a crash on normal payloads. The inconsistency (one field non-defensive among many defensive ones) plus the silent full-batch loss in get_questions is a genuine, fixable robustness defect, and the recommendation (guard id, skip-record in list context, distinct error in single-fetch) is sound.

### `SWEE-19` — Polymarket get_questions params include offset/limit the CLOB /markets endpoint does not honor (cursor pagination)
- **confirmed ✓** · source: sweep · effort: medium · file: `src/platforms/polymarket.py:91`
- **evidence:** params = {"limit": limit, "offset": offset}; self._client.get("/markets", params=params). The Polymarket CLOB /markets endpoint paginates via a `next_cursor` token, not limit/offset. Sending offset is ignored server-side, so offset!=0 returns the same first page; the code then re-slices with markets[:limit] (line 100) over whatever was returned. Pagination through this client silently returns duplicate first-page data for every offset, so any 'fetch all open markets' loop built on get_questions(offset=...) will loop over the same markets.
- **recommendation:** Implement cursor-based pagination (read next_cursor from the response, pass it back) or document that only the first page is reachable. The offset parameter is currently a no-op that gives the false impression of working pagination.
- **verifier:** VERIFIED at code level, but materiality is overstated. |source: src/platforms/polymarket.py:91-100| read directly.

CONFIRMED — code facts:
- Lines 91-94: params = {"limit": limit, "offset": offset} sent to GET /markets.
- Line 96: self._client.get("/markets", params=params).
- Line 100: return [_map_market(m) for m in markets[:limit]] — re-slices client-side.

CONFIRMED — API behavior claim: The Polymarket CLOB GET /markets endpoint uses cursor pagination (next_cursor query param; response shape {limit, count, next_cursor, data:[...]}). It does not honor an offset query param, so offset is a server-side no-op. The code never reads next_cursor (line 99 only unwraps data/markets), so only the first page is ever reachable. This part of the finding is accurate.

REFUTED — the claimed material impact. The finding asserts "any 'fetch all open markets' loop built on get_questions(offset=...) will loop over the same markets." I grepped every call site: scripts/predict.py:102 (limit=10), pages/2_Predict.py:119 (limit=20), scripts/batch_predict.py:62 (limit, tournament_id, search). NONE of them ever pass offset — all use the default offset=0. There is no pagination loop anywhere in the codebase. The duplicate-data scenario is purely hypothetical for the current code. The bug is latent (a footgun for a future caller), not an active defect.

Net: genuine latent issue (offset is a dead/misleading param, cursor pagination unimplemented, only first page reachable), but the described concrete harm does not occur in this codebase today. Also worth noting tangentially: search, tags, and tournament_id are likewise silently dropped from params in this client — broader stub incompleteness, though outside the finding's scope.

### `SWEE-20` — Kalshi get_questions cursor pagination misuses numeric offset as an opaque cursor token
- **confirmed ✓** · source: sweep · effort: medium · file: `src/platforms/kalshi.py:116`
- **evidence:** params = {"limit": limit, "cursor": str(offset) if offset else ""}. Kalshi's `cursor` is an opaque server-issued token returned in the response as `cursor`, not a row offset. Passing str(offset) like "20" is not a valid cursor; Kalshi will reject it or ignore it, so offset-based paging never advances. The response's own `cursor` field is never read or threaded back.
- **recommendation:** Read `cursor` from the response body and pass it back on the next call; change the public signature semantics (offset is meaningless here) or store/return the cursor. As written, paging past the first page is broken.
- **verifier:** CONFIRMED with adjustment. |source: src/platforms/kalshi.py:116-121 (read directly)|

The cited line matches exactly: `"cursor": str(offset) if offset else ""` (line 118). The core mechanism is correct and code-grounded:

1. Kalshi's /trade-api/v2/events endpoint uses opaque cursor-token pagination. The `cursor` query param expects the server-issued token returned in the prior response's `cursor` field, not a numeric row offset. Passing str(offset) like "20" is not a valid token. VERIFIED (factual Kalshi API behavior).

2. The response `cursor` field is never read or threaded back — grep across the file shows no `data.get("cursor")`; line 127 only reads `events`. So there is no mechanism to advance pages. VERIFIED.

3. Therefore offset-based paging past page 1 cannot work: cursor="20" yields a rejected/ignored request, not "skip 20 rows."

Refutation attempts (all partial, none fatal):
- First page is fine: when offset=0 (the default), cursor is set to "" then deleted (lines 120-121), so the param is omitted and page 1 is correct. The bug is confined to offset>0.
- Dead path: NO caller in the repo passes a non-zero offset. scripts/predict.py (limit=10), scripts/batch_predict.py (limit/tournament/search only), pages/2_Predict.py (limit=20) all rely on offset=0. The broken branch is currently never exercised.
- Failure is graceful: an invalid-cursor 400 is caught at line 131-133 (HTTPStatusError -> return []), so the manifestation is a silent empty list, not a crash or corrupted data.

Net: the issue genuinely exists (the offset parameter is semantically dead/broken for Kalshi, and the base class advertises offset as "Pagination offset" so the abstraction is violated), but it is a latent bug on a dead path in a module the docstring itself labels a "read-only stub," with a caught/silent failure mode. That does not rise to medium impact.

### `SWEE-26` — Configured deliberation_stdev_threshold is dead — deliberation uses a hardcoded constant with a different meaning
- **confirmed ✓** · source: sweep · effort: small · file: `/Users/bjgilbert/Projects/Zoltar/sigma-predict/src/config.py:34`
- **evidence:** AggregationConfig.deliberation_stdev_threshold = 0.15 is defined and never read anywhere (grep finds only the definition line). deliberation.py:22 instead hardcodes _INTER_MODEL_AGREEMENT_THRESHOLD = 0.6 and gates on aggregation.inter_model_agreement < 0.6. Since aggregator.py:112 sets inter_model_agreement = max(0, 1 - 2*stdev), the real trigger is stdev > 0.2, not the configured 0.15. Tuning the config value has zero effect, and the effective threshold silently differs from what the config claims.
- **recommendation:** Either wire deliberation.py to read config.aggregation.deliberation_stdev_threshold, or delete the dead config field. Reconcile the 0.15 vs effective-0.2 discrepancy.
- **verifier:** Confirmed against actual code. config.py:34 defines AggregationConfig.deliberation_stdev_threshold = 0.15. A repo-wide grep returns ONLY that definition line — the field is never read anywhere. The actual deliberation gate is in src/pipeline/deliberation.py (the finding's path "deliberation.py:22" omits the src/pipeline/ prefix, but line numbers and content match exactly): line 22 hardcodes _INTER_MODEL_AGREEMENT_THRESHOLD = 0.6, and should_deliberate() at line 40 gates on aggregation.inter_model_agreement < 0.6. The config object IS passed into should_deliberate(aggregation, config, ...) but is never used to read the threshold — it relies solely on the module-level constant. aggregator.py:112 computes inter_model_agreement = max(0.0, 1.0 - 2.0 * stdev), so the gate inter_model_agreement < 0.6 algebraically reduces to stdev > 0.2, not the configured 0.15. Every quantitative claim in the finding checks out: the config field is dead, tuning it has zero effect, and the effective stdev trigger (0.2) silently differs from the configured value (0.15). The docstring/comments referencing "threshold" reinforce the misleading impression that config drives behavior. This is a genuine dead-config + silent-divergence defect.

### `SWEE-29` — effective_n heuristic relies on an undocumented magic constant and can mis-scale
- **confirmed ✓** · source: sweep · effort: medium · file: `/Users/bjgilbert/Projects/Zoltar/sigma-predict/src/pipeline/aggregator.py:171-176`
- **evidence:** expected_var_single = mean_p * (1 - mean_p) * 0.1  # rough estimate, then ratio = actual_var / expected_var_single and effective_n = max(1, min(n, int(round(n * min(1.0, ratio * n))))). The 0.1 factor is arbitrary and unjustified; the formula multiplies ratio by n then by n again (n * (ratio*n)), so effective_n scales with n^2 before clamping. For modest real spread this saturates to n almost immediately, and for near-identical runs collapses to 1, making the 'low effective N' flag (human_review.py:348) effectively binary rather than a graded measure of correlation.
- **recommendation:** Replace the ad-hoc heuristic with a defensible correlation/ESS estimate, or document the constant and the intended n-scaling. Add unit tests pinning expected effective_n for known inputs.
- **verifier:** Confirmed by reading the code and empirically reproducing the heuristic's behavior.

CODE MATCH: aggregator.py:171-176 matches the finding verbatim. Line 171: `expected_var_single = mean_p * (1 - mean_p) * 0.1  # rough estimate`. Line 176: `effective_n = max(1, min(n, int(round(n * min(1.0, ratio * n)))))`. The 0.1 factor is undocumented beyond "rough estimate"/"rough heuristic" comments (lines 169-171). VERIFIED.

N^2 SCALING: Confirmed. The expression is `n * min(1.0, ratio * n)` — the inner `ratio * n` is multiplied by the outer `n`, so pre-clamp the effective_n grows as ratio*n^2. The `min(1.0, ...)` clamps the inner multiplier to 1, so as soon as `ratio >= 1/n` the result jumps to the full n. VERIFIED.

BINARY/SATURATION BEHAVIOR: Confirmed empirically. For n=5, mean=0.5: std<=0.02 -> eff_n=1; std=0.03 -> eff_n=2; std>=0.05 -> eff_n=5 (saturated). The intermediate band is only ~std 0.03-0.06 wide. For mean=0.9 saturation is even faster (std=0.05 already gives full n). So the supposedly graded correlation measure is effectively a step function, making the human_review.py:348 flag (`effective_n < 3`) essentially binary. VERIFIED.

DOWNSTREAM FLAG: human_review.py line 348 exact match: `if record.aggregation.effective_n < 3 and len(record.runs) >= 3:`. The finding's path omitted the `/review/` subdir (actual: src/review/human_review.py) but the line number is exact — minor citation imprecision, not material.

TESTS: test_aggregator.py only asserts the degenerate cases (effective_n <= 1 / == 1). No test pins an intermediate graded value, so the heuristic's poor calibration is unguarded. The 'add unit tests' recommendation is warranted.

All technical claims hold. This is a real quality issue: a correlation/ESS estimator that is mathematically ad hoc (arbitrary 0.1, n^2 scaling) and degenerates into a near-binary signal, undermining the 'low effective N' review flag it feeds.

### `SWEE-30` — Brier decomposition silently bins numeric and out-of-range predictions as if they were probabilities
- **confirmed ✓** · source: sweep · effort: small · file: `/Users/bjgilbert/Projects/Zoltar/sigma-predict/src/registry/query.py:39-57,104-121`
- **evidence:** get_calibration_pairs() and the decomposition functions append (predicted, float(record.resolution.outcome)) for every resolved record with no question_type filter. compute_brier_decomposition bins by k = min(int(pred / bin_width), n_bins-1) assuming pred in [0,1], and uncertainty = bar_o*(1-bar_o) assumes outcomes in {0,1}. A numeric question's outcome (e.g. 42) poisons bar_o, reliability, resolution, and the calibration fit (fit_platt is later fed these pairs). The docstring at query.py:108 claims 'only resolved binary predictions' but nothing enforces binary.
- **recommendation:** Filter to record.question_type == 'binary' (and outcome in {0,1}) in get_calibration_pairs and both decomposition builders, matching the documented contract.
- **verifier:** CONFIRMED, source: src/registry/query.py, src/models.py, src/platforms/metaculus.py, src/pipeline/orchestrator.py, src/pipeline/calibration.py, scripts/resolve.py. Severity MEDIUM, status VERIFIED.

The finding is accurate on every cited claim:

1. No type filter (query.py:104-121, 127-158, 160-201). get_calibration_pairs() and both decomposition builders iterate resolved records and append (predicted, float(record.resolution.outcome)) with no question_type == 'binary' guard and no outcome in {0,1} check. The only filters are resolution is None / outcome is None.

2. Math assumes probabilities/binary (query.py:9-64). compute_brier_decomposition bins by k = min(int(pred/bin_width), n_bins-1) (pred in [0,1] assumed) and computes uncertainty = bar_o*(1-bar_o) (outcomes in {0,1} assumed). A numeric outcome like 42 directly inflates bar_o, reliability, resolution, and uncertainty.

3. Reachable end-to-end. models.py defines NUMERIC and MULTIPLE_CHOICE; Resolution.outcome is float|None with comment '0|1 for binary, value for numeric'. metaculus.py:19-30 parses real 'continuous' -> NUMERIC and group -> MULTIPLE_CHOICE. The orchestrator (orchestrator.py:119) records question_type but NEVER filters non-binary questions out of forecasting — it produces a submitted.probability in [0,1] regardless. metaculus.py:156-182 get_resolution does outcome = float(resolution_value) with NO normalization/clamp, and scripts/resolve.py:94-113 writes that resolution back to the registry. So a numeric record with probability ~0.x and outcome 42.0 is persistable.

4. Calibration poisoning is real, not just a display metric. The pairs feed calibrate(..., isotonic_data=pairs) (orchestrator.py:337-344) -> fit_isotonic_logit (calibration.py:133-169), where outcomes (acts) are passed to scipy isotonic_regression and returned as prob_y breakpoints used by isotonic_transform for interpolation. A 42.0 outcome corrupts the calibration curve applied to all future forecasts. The finding's claim about fit_platt is slightly imprecise (the orchestrator's live path uses isotonic, not fit_platt, and fit_platt's outcomes only enter the optimizer's likelihood not bin edges) but the substantive claim — pairs feed a calibration fit that assumes binary outcomes — holds.

5. Documented contract violated (query.py:107-108): docstring says 'Only includes resolved binary predictions' but nothing enforces binary.

Refutation attempted and only partially succeeds: Metaculus continuous resolutions are typically API-normalized toward [0,1], which bounds the magnitude of corruption for that one platform. But the code neither relies on nor documents this; normalized continuous values are CDF positions, not event outcomes, so pooling them into binary bar_o and isotonic prob targets is still semantically wrong; and MULTIPLE_CHOICE / other platforms are unbounded. The refutation softens worst-case magnitude but does not negate the bug. The recommendation (filter question_type == 'binary' and outcome in {0,1} in all three builders) is correct.

### `SWEE-38` — Verification toggle mutates the shared global Config singleton, leaking across sessions and runs
- **confirmed ✓** · source: sweep · effort: small · file: `/Users/bjgilbert/Projects/Zoltar/sigma-predict/pages/2_Predict.py:234`
- **evidence:** get_config() is @st.cache_resource (app.py:33-35), so Config() is a single shared instance for the whole server. On Run Pipeline the code does `config.verification.enabled = verification_toggle`, permanently mutating the shared singleton. A user who toggles verification off for one run flips it off for every other session and every subsequent page load (the toggle default at 2_Predict.py:207 reads config.verification.enabled, so the UI default also drifts). There is no restoration after the run.
- **recommendation:** Do not mutate the cached Config. Pass the verification preference into the pipeline call (e.g. a per-run parameter or a shallow-copied Config) rather than writing to the shared singleton.
- **verifier:** Confirmed by reading the cited code. config = get_config() at 2_Predict.py:32 returns the @st.cache_resource singleton (app.py:33-35), which Streamlit shares across ALL sessions/users on the server (not per-session, unlike st.session_state, and not copied, unlike st.cache_data). Line 234 `config.verification.enabled = verification_toggle` mutates that shared singleton in place with no restoration. The toggle default at line 207 reads config.verification.enabled, so once mutated the UI default also drifts for every future session/page load. The same un-copied config object is then passed into the background thread (line 254) and read by the orchestrator (orchestrator.py:124-127 reads self.config.verification), so the mutation also propagates into running pipelines.

Refutation attempts all fail: (1) config is NOT per-session — it is cache_resource. (2) No defensive copy is made — the identical object is threaded in. (3) No restoration exists after the run.

Materiality: genuine but bounded. Leaked state is a single boolean UI preference, not data corruption or a prediction-correctness defect. This is a single-user/local Streamlit forecasting hub, so the multi-user 'leaks across other sessions' framing somewhat overstates impact. The sharper real concern is a concurrency race: background-thread pipeline runs read the shared config.verification mid-flight, so a second launch toggling the singleton can affect a concurrently running pipeline. The recommendation (per-run parameter or shallow-copied Config) is correct and fixes both drift and race.

### `SWEE-39` — UI reimplements 3 static progress lines and discards the orchestrator's real per-step progress
- **confirmed ✓** · source: sweep · effort: small · file: `/Users/bjgilbert/Projects/Zoltar/sigma-predict/pages/2_Predict.py:67-78`
- **evidence:** predict_until_review() emits granular progress via progress_queue ('Step 1/7: Decomposing...', per-run persona lines, 'Step 5/7: Calibrating...', final calibrated probability — orchestrator.py:109-377). But _run_pipeline calls orchestrator.predict(...) which never forwards a queue, and instead manually q.put three fixed strings ('Starting pipeline...', the title, and runs/personas). The user sees only those 3 lines for the entire multi-minute run; all real step progress is thrown away. The @st.fragment(run_every=1) polling infrastructure is in place but fed near-empty data.
- **recommendation:** Call predict_until_review(..., progress_queue=q) so the orchestrator's real progress strings flow to the fragment. Note predict_until_review already puts its own None sentinel (orchestrator.py:379-380); reconcile that with the UI's sentinel handling so completion is detected once.
- **verifier:** CONFIRMED against actual code. Cited orchestrator path in the finding (orchestrator.py:109-377) is technically a wrong path (the file lives at src/pipeline/orchestrator.py, not repo root), but every substantive claim holds at the real location.

1. predict_until_review() emits granular per-step progress via progress_queue: VERIFIED. src/pipeline/orchestrator.py:94-96 define _progress(); it fires at lines 109 (start), 140 (Step 1/7 Decompose), 161 (Step 2/7 base rate), 204 (Step 3/7 runs), 212 (per-run persona line), 249 (Step 4/7 aggregate), 334 (Step 5/7 calibrate), and 377 (final calibrated probability). These are exactly the strings the finding describes.

2. _run_pipeline calls orchestrator.predict(...) and never forwards a queue: VERIFIED. pages/2_Predict.py:72-78 calls orchestrator.predict(...) with no progress_queue arg. Stronger than the finding states: predict() (orchestrator.py:461) does not even accept a progress_queue parameter, and its internal predict_until_review() call (lines 485-490) omits it. So zero real step progress is forwarded.

3. UI manually puts 3 fixed strings: VERIFIED. pages/2_Predict.py:67-69 put 'Starting pipeline...', 'Question: ...', and 'Runs: N, Personas: [...]' before the multi-minute blocking predict() call. The only other queue items are the None sentinel (line 86) and an ERROR string on exception.

4. @st.fragment(run_every=1) polling is in place but fed near-empty data: VERIFIED. pages/2_Predict.py:264-294 implement the polling fragment; during the run it can only ever surface those 3 lines.

5. Recommendation/sentinel reconciliation note is accurate: predict_until_review puts its own None sentinel (orchestrator.py:379-380) and _run_pipeline's finally also puts None (line 86). Switching to predict_until_review(progress_queue=q) would enqueue two Nones, but the first triggers completion handling and st.rerun()+return (line 284), so the second is harmless; reconciliation advice is still sound.

The code is explicitly marked TODO (pages/2_Predict.py:62-63, 71) saying to switch to predict_until_review when the API is finalized — and that API now exists and accepts progress_queue, so the fix is trivial and the TODO is stale. The finding is genuine, material to a user-facing product (multi-minute blind wait), and the recommendation is correct.

### `SWEE-40` — Dashboard auto-rerun on 'running' state spins a tight loop with no throttle
- **confirmed ✓** · source: sweep · effort: medium · file: `/Users/bjgilbert/Projects/Zoltar/sigma-predict/pages/1_Dashboard.py:141-142`
- **evidence:** if st.session_state.get(KEY_PIPELINE_STATE) == 'running': st.rerun(). Unlike the Predict page which uses @st.fragment(run_every=1) to throttle polling, the Dashboard reruns immediately with no sleep. If a user starts a pipeline on Predict, then navigates to Dashboard, this reruns the entire page (cache.load(), full dataframe build) as fast as the server can cycle, burning CPU until the background thread flips state. But the state flip only happens in the Predict page's fragment (2_Predict.py:278) — which is not running while the user is on Dashboard — so KEY_PIPELINE_STATE may never leave 'running' from the Dashboard's perspective, producing an unbounded busy-rerun loop.
- **recommendation:** Remove the bare st.rerun() loop; use a throttled fragment (run_every) or st_autorefresh, and ensure the running→awaiting_review transition is driven by logic that executes regardless of which page is active (e.g. a shared fragment or checking the completed_slot on the Dashboard too).
- **verifier:** Confirmed against the code. /Users/bjgilbert/Projects/Zoltar/sigma-predict/pages/1_Dashboard.py:141-142 is exactly `if st.session_state.get(KEY_PIPELINE_STATE) == "running": st.rerun()` with no sleep, no fragment, no run_every throttle.

Mechanism verified:
1. KEY_PIPELINE_STATE lives in st.session_state (components/state.py:9), which persists across pages within a single session. So launching a pipeline on Predict (2_Predict.py:249 sets state="running") and then navigating to Dashboard means the Dashboard reads "running" — the finding's premise is correct and the user flow is realistic.
2. The running -> awaiting_review (and running -> idle) transitions exist ONLY inside pipeline_progress_fragment() on the Predict page (2_Predict.py:278, 283), plus the review-confirm handler (379). A grep across pages/, components/, app.py confirms the Dashboard's only reference to KEY_PIPELINE_STATE is the read at line 141. The Dashboard never reads get_completed_slot() or the pipeline queue, so it has no mechanism to flip the state itself.
3. The background thread (_run_pipeline) writes to completed_slot and queues a sentinel but never touches session_state. While the user is on the Dashboard, the Predict fragment is not running, so the queue is never drained and state never leaves "running" from the Dashboard's perspective.
4. Streamlit's st.rerun() re-queues the script immediately with no delay; an unconditional st.rerun() on every run is the canonical busy-loop anti-pattern. So line 141-142 spins as fast as the server can cycle the page, re-running the full script (row build + DataFrame construction + dataframe widget render) each iteration, and the loop does not self-terminate while the user remains on the page. The Refresh button at 145-147 is recreated every tight cycle and is not a reliable escape; only navigating back to Predict reliably breaks it.

The only thing that softens raw impact: cache.load() is dirty-flag memoized (components/cache.py:20-24), so each rerun returns in-memory records rather than re-reading disk. The per-iteration cost is CPU (script re-exec + pandas DataFrame rebuild + widget render), not disk thrash. Blast radius is one session's script runner, not a server-wide crash or data loss.

The finding's contrast with the Predict page's @st.fragment(run_every=1) throttle (2_Predict.py:264) and its recommendation (use a throttled fragment/st_autorefresh and drive the transition from logic that runs regardless of active page, e.g. have the Dashboard also check completed_slot) are both accurate and appropriate.

### `SWEE-47` — predict.py rejection-summary logic disagrees with orchestrator's rejection rule
- **confirmed ✓** · source: sweep · effort: trivial · file: `/Users/bjgilbert/Projects/Zoltar/sigma-predict/scripts/predict.py:145`
- **evidence:** predict.py decides the summary with `elif record.human_review.human_reasoning and "REJECTED" in (record.human_review.human_reasoning or ""):` (substring match). The orchestrator only treats a review as a rejection when `review.human_reasoning.startswith("REJECTED")` (orchestrator.py:416). A reasoning string that merely contains the word REJECTED somewhere mid-text (e.g. 'Earlier draft was REJECTED but now approved') is actually SUBMITTED by the orchestrator, yet predict.py prints 'Prediction rejected. Logged to registry.' Also, when the orchestrator genuinely rejects, `record.submitted` is None so the first branch (line 141) is skipped and this branch fires correctly — but the divergent matching rule is a latent inconsistency between the two modules.
- **recommendation:** Drive the summary off `record.submitted` state, not a re-parse of the reasoning string. e.g. submitted -> 'submitted'; else if reasoning.startswith('REJECTED') -> 'rejected'; else 'logged not submitted'. Match the orchestrator's `startswith` rule exactly.
- **verifier:** Confirmed at the code level. predict.py:145 uses `"REJECTED" in (record.human_review.human_reasoning or "")` (substring match), while the orchestrator at src/pipeline/orchestrator.py:416 uses `review.human_reasoning.startswith("REJECTED")`. The two modules genuinely apply different matching rules to the same concept, so the latent inconsistency the finding describes is real.

However, the finding's headline harm scenario is largely unreachable in practice, and the finding itself admits this. Trace: predict.py checks `record.submitted` first (line 141). The orchestrator sets `record.submitted` to a Submission object on any submit attempt that does not raise/return-False (lines 434 and 444). When the orchestrator does NOT reject (startswith false — exactly the mid-text case like "...was REJECTED but now approved"), it proceeds to submit, which sets `record.submitted`, so predict.py line 141 catches it and never reaches line 145. The false 'rejected' echo therefore only occurs under a narrow conjunction: submission returns False (line 440, leaving submitted=None) AND the human's free-text adjustment reasoning happens to contain the literal substring "REJECTED" mid-string. The interactive review path (human_review.py:225) only ever produces "REJECTED" or "REJECTED: ..." for actual rejections — both startswith-true — so normal operation is consistent between the two modules.

Impact is bounded to a single incorrect CLI summary line in that rare path. The registry record is written independently by the orchestrator, so there is no data-integrity consequence; only the user-facing echo can be wrong. The recommendation (drive the summary off record.submitted state and match the orchestrator's startswith rule) is correct and improves robustness.

Location note: the cited "orchestrator.py:416" is at src/pipeline/orchestrator.py:416, not under scripts/ — line number is correct, path is imprecise.

### `SWEE-49` — classify_error SEARCH_BLINDSPOT default makes the guard backwards
- **REFUTED ✗** · source: sweep · effort: small · file: `/Users/bjgilbert/Projects/Zoltar/sigma-predict/scripts/resolve.py:53`
- **evidence:** `if any(getattr(r, "search_quality_score", 5) < 2 for r in record.runs):` — but `search_quality_score` is a real RunResult field with default 0 (models.py:99), not a maybe-absent attribute. The getattr default of 5 is dead code (the attr always exists). More importantly, any run that never populated the score keeps the model default of 0, which is `< 2`, so SEARCH_BLINDSPOT fires on every unpopulated/default run. The defensive default of 5 signals the author expected 'missing = good (5)', but the actual missing value is 0 = blindspot. Same dead-getattr pattern at line 44 (`parse_failed`, default False) and line 38/53 generally — these attrs always exist on RunResult.
- **recommendation:** Drop the getattr fallbacks (fields always exist) and decide deliberately how to treat a default-0 score: either exclude runs where the score was never set, or document that 0 legitimately means blindspot. As written, error classification is biased toward emitting SEARCH_BLINDSPOT.
- **verifier:** The finding's load-bearing claim is refuted by the actual data path. It asserts that runs which never populate search_quality_score keep the model-field default of 0 (models.py:99), which is < 2, so SEARCH_BLINDSPOT "fires on every unpopulated/default run," biasing classification.

That is false. Every RunResult that ends up in record.runs is constructed by the forecaster at src/pipeline/forecaster.py:227-247, which sets `search_quality_score=int(data.get("search_quality_score", 5))`. The runtime default is 5, not 0 — and critically, even on parse failure (line 178: `data = {}`, parse_failed=True), the value resolves to 5, NOT 0. I traced every RunResult construction site: forecaster (always sets the field), aggregator.get_aggregate_run (omits the field, so defaults to 0 — but this synthetic "aggregate" run is local at orchestrator.py:299 and used only for challenge_forecast, never appended to record.runs), and deliberation.deliberate (returns the same runs list unchanged, orchestrator.py:326). record.runs is only ever populated via record.runs.append(run_result) at orchestrator.py:229 with forecaster-built runs.

Therefore the model-field default of 0 is never reached for any element classify_error iterates over. There is no bias toward SEARCH_BLINDSPOT; SEARCH_BLINDSPOT only fires when the model genuinely reported a score < 2. The getattr default of 5 actually MATCHES the real runtime default of 5 — the author's "missing = good (5)" mental model is consistent with production, not contradicted by it. The finding inverts the relationship by treating the unreachable model-field default (0) as operative.

What survives is only the secondary, cosmetic observation: the getattr wrappers (lines 39 confidence_self_score is a direct access, but 44 parse_failed and 53 search_quality_score use getattr) are dead because these are pydantic fields that always exist. That is true but a trivial LOW-severity cleanup nit, not a correctness or classification-bias bug. The finding as written — a medium-severity claim that error classification is "biased toward emitting SEARCH_BLINDSPOT" and the guard is "backwards" — does not hold.

Relevant locations: /Users/bjgilbert/Projects/Zoltar/sigma-predict/scripts/resolve.py:53; /Users/bjgilbert/Projects/Zoltar/sigma-predict/src/models.py:99; /Users/bjgilbert/Projects/Zoltar/sigma-predict/src/pipeline/forecaster.py:178,234; /Users/bjgilbert/Projects/Zoltar/sigma-predict/src/pipeline/orchestrator.py:229,299.

### `TOOL-272` — Config.primary_model dataclass field declared non-Optional but defaults to None

### `BIZ:-118` — _collect_platform_signals dead branch — membership check on a freshly-emptied dict is always false
- **confirmed ✓** · source: biz-logic · effort: trivial · file: `/Users/bjgilbert/Projects/Zoltar/sigma-predict/src/pipeline/orchestrator.py:546-551`
- **evidence:** signals = PlatformSignals() creates an empty prices_at_prediction_time dict (models.py:133 default_factory=dict). The code then does `if platform_key in signals.prices_at_prediction_time:` — but nothing has populated that dict yet, so the membership test is always False and the `if` branch (storing under the `_community` suffixed key) is dead. Execution always takes the else branch storing under the bare platform key. The `_community` key path is unreachable.
- **recommendation:** Remove the dead conditional and store under whichever key is actually intended; if the _community suffix matters, set it unconditionally. The current code cannot ever record the _community-suffixed signal.
- **verifier:** Confirmed at the cited location. orchestrator.py:542 instantiates `signals = PlatformSignals()`, and per models.py:133 `prices_at_prediction_time` uses `default_factory=dict`, so it starts empty. Between instantiation (542) and the membership test (548), nothing populates the dict — the only writes are inside the if/else itself (549/551). Therefore `if platform_key in signals.prices_at_prediction_time:` always tests against {} and is always False, making the `if` branch at line 549 (the `{platform}_community`-suffixed key) unreachable. Execution always takes the else at line 551, storing under the bare `question.platform.value` key. The intent-vs-implementation gap is real: the author evidently meant to record a community signal under a distinct `_community` key, and that path can never run.\n\nMateriality is genuinely low, not a refutation. The community prediction IS still recorded (under the bare platform key), so no data is lost — only the key name diverges from apparent intent. The sole consumers (human_review.py:145-151 and :339) just iterate `prices.items()` and print whatever keys are present; none of them rely on the `_community` suffix to disambiguate community vs. market signals. So the dead branch produces no observable behavioral defect today. The only latent risk is a future consumer that expects to distinguish a community price by suffix — which doesn't exist yet. The code claims (via the conditional structure) to differentiate two storage keys but only ever uses one.

### `BIZ:-119` — search_quality_score default diverges between parse-failure path and model default, weakening the search_quality_gate
- **confirmed ✓** · source: biz-logic · effort: trivial · file: `/Users/bjgilbert/Projects/Zoltar/sigma-predict/src/pipeline/forecaster.py:234`
- **evidence:** On JSON parse failure the forecaster sets data={} (forecaster.py:178-179), then builds RunResult with `search_quality_score=int(data.get("search_quality_score", 5))` — i.e. a parse-failed run is assigned a search quality of 5, well above the validator's warn threshold of 3 (validator.py:28,107-108). The model's own default is 0 (models.py:99). So a run that failed to produce any parseable output (and whose probability fell back to base_rate) is recorded as having mediocre-to-good search quality, masking it from the search_quality_gate. The parse_failure_gate still flags it, but the quality signal is fabricated rather than zeroed.
- **recommendation:** On parse failure, set search_quality_score=0 (or skip the run from the quality average) so the search_quality_gate reflects reality. Use a 0 default in the get() to match the model default.
- **verifier:** All code citations verified accurate. forecaster.py:178-179 sets data={} on JSON parse failure and flags parse_failed=True. forecaster.py:234 builds RunResult with search_quality_score=int(data.get("search_quality_score", 5)) — so a parse-failed run (empty dict) is assigned a score of 5. models.py:99 declares the model default as 0, and prompts/forecaster_base.md:111 anchors the 0-10 scale with "0 = no relevant results." validator.py:28,107-108 warns only when the average search_quality_score falls below 3. The divergence is genuine: a run that produced no parseable output and fell back to base_rate for its probability is recorded as having search quality 5 (mediocre-to-good), well above the warn threshold, which inflates the quality average and can mask a low-quality batch from the search_quality_gate. The inline get() default (5) contradicts both the model field default (0) and the prompt's semantic anchor (0 = nothing relevant). I could not refute it — the behavior does not match intent.

Materiality is correctly bounded at low, and I'd keep that severity. The parse_failure_gate (validator.py:97-104) still fires unconditionally on any parse_failed run, so a parse failure is never silently swallowed; the finding explicitly acknowledges this. The fabricated score only degrades a secondary, non-blocking signal (the quality average), and only at the margin where it could tip a sub-3 average above threshold. grep confirms search_quality_score feeds nothing but this one warning gate — it is not used in calibration or aggregation. So the gap is real and the description is fair, but the blast radius is genuinely small. The recommendation is sound; note that even with a 0 default the failed run is still averaged in, which the finding addresses via its "or skip the run" alternative.

### `BIZ:-120` — Non-scaled apply_hedging_correction is dead code — only the scaled variant is wired in
- **confirmed ✓** · source: biz-logic · effort: trivial · file: `/Users/bjgilbert/Projects/Zoltar/sigma-predict/src/pipeline/calibration.py:103-114`
- **evidence:** calibrate() calls apply_hedging_correction_scaled in both Phase 0 and the isotonic-fallback path (calibration.py:82,92). The flat-shift apply_hedging_correction (lines 103-114) is never called from the pipeline (grep shows no production caller). It coexists with the scaled version as an unused alternative implementation, a maintenance/confusion hazard since the two apply materially different corrections (flat shift vs distance-weighted).
- **recommendation:** Delete apply_hedging_correction or document it as test-only. Keeping two hedging functions invites a future edit to the wrong one.
- **verifier:** Confirmed against the code. In /Users/bjgilbert/Projects/Zoltar/sigma-predict/src/pipeline/calibration.py, calibrate() calls apply_hedging_correction_scaled at lines 82 (isotonic fallback) and 92 (Phase 0), and never calls the non-scaled apply_hedging_correction (defined lines 103-114). A repo-wide grep confirms the only references to apply_hedging_correction (non-scaled) are its definition plus tests/test_calibration.py (lines 7, 20, 24, 28, 32, 36) — zero production callers. The two functions do apply materially different corrections: apply_hedging_correction adds a flat `shift` independent of distance from 0.5, while apply_hedging_correction_scaled adds base_shift*2*distance (distance-weighted). So the divergence between the two implementations is real, and editing the wrong one would silently change behavior. The finding is accurate and does not overstate: it correctly identifies scaled as the wired-in variant and frames non-scaled as an unused alternative. The pipeline itself behaves as intended (module docstring and calibrate() docstring both describe Phase 0 as 'scaled hedging correction', matching what runs), so this is a maintenance/confusion hazard, not a behavioral bug — exactly the low severity assigned.

### `BIZ:-121` — estimate_base_rate warnings_out is accepted but never threaded by the orchestrator — base_rate_parse_failure warning is silently dropped
- **confirmed ✓** · source: biz-logic · effort: small · file: `/Users/bjgilbert/Projects/Zoltar/sigma-predict/src/pipeline/base_rate.py:84-86`
- **evidence:** On parse failure estimate_base_rate appends 'base_rate_parse_failure' to warnings_out (base_rate.py:84-85) and returns 0.5 with source 'parse_failure_default'. But the orchestrator calls estimate_base_rate WITHOUT passing warnings_out (orchestrator.py:162-168), so warnings_out is None and the warning is discarded. Same for decompose_question's warnings_out (decomposer.py:78-79) — the orchestrator call at orchestrator.py:141-143 omits it. A base-rate or decomposition parse failure that silently defaults to 0.5 / empty decomposition is therefore never surfaced in pipeline_warnings, and the validator only catches the base_rate range gate (0.5 passes it), so a parse-failed base rate is indistinguishable from a real 0.5 estimate downstream.
- **recommendation:** Pass record.pipeline_warnings (or a local list that gets merged) into estimate_base_rate and decompose_question, or have the orchestrator inspect base_rate_source == 'parse_failure_default' and append a warning. Right now a key failure mode is invisible.
- **verifier:** Confirmed via direct code reading. estimate_base_rate (base_rate.py:26, 84-86) and decompose_question (decomposer.py:28, 78-80) both accept a warnings_out parameter and append parse-failure markers ("base_rate_parse_failure" / "decomposition_parse_failure") on JSON parse failure, returning a 0.5/parse_failure_default base rate and an empty Decomposition() respectively. The orchestrator calls both functions WITHOUT passing warnings_out (orchestrator.py:141-143 for decompose, 162-168 for base rate), so the parameter defaults to None and the append branches are dead code. Grep across src/ confirms the two warning strings appear ONLY at their definition sites — never read or surfaced anywhere. No downstream rescue exists: base_rate_source=="parse_failure_default" is never inspected (only stored, shown in human review, and injected verbatim into the forecaster prompt); the validator's validate_base_rate (validator.py:45-57) only enforces the [0.01,0.99] range gate, which 0.5 passes, and validate_base_rate_instances returns early because instance_count is None on parse failure. So a parse-failed base rate is genuinely indistinguishable from a legitimate 0.5, and a parse-failed (empty) decomposition is never flagged in pipeline_warnings. The intent (warnings_out was deliberately plumbed into both functions to surface these failures) diverges from the implementation (orchestrator never wires it). Additional supporting evidence: even the recommendation's "merge a local list" path is undercut by a second pre-existing gap — the orchestrator's local pipeline_warnings list (line 112) is itself never merged into record.pipeline_warnings, so warning-surfacing wiring here is broadly incomplete. This is not a docstring/intent mismatch fabrication; the divergence is concrete and code-grounded.

### `BIZ:-122` — CI half-width uses aggregation stdev but Platt/isotonic calibration can shift the point estimate, producing a CI that may exclude the raw mean and is symmetric in probability space rather than logit space
- **confirmed ✓** · source: biz-logic · effort: medium · file: `/Users/bjgilbert/Projects/Zoltar/sigma-predict/src/pipeline/orchestrator.py:347-349`
- **evidence:** ci_low/ci_high are built as cal_prob +/- 1.96*stdev where stdev is the run-to-run dispersion from aggregate_runs, and cal_prob is the calibrated probability. The interval is centered on the calibrated value but its width derives from the uncalibrated run spread, so the two are on different scales after any nonlinear calibration transform (extremize/platt/isotonic all operate in logit space). For a single run stdev=0 so the code falls back to a flat +/-0.05 band (lines 348-349) regardless of the question — a fixed-width 'confidence interval' that carries no information about actual uncertainty. The CI is presented to the human reviewer as if it reflects model uncertainty.
- **recommendation:** Either propagate uncertainty through the calibration transform (compute CI in logit space then transform endpoints), or relabel the field as a heuristic band. The single-run flat +/-0.05 should be documented as a placeholder, not an estimated interval.
- **verifier:** Confirmed against the code at orchestrator.py:347-349 and the supporting modules.

WHAT THE CODE CLAIMS: The field is named `confidence_interval` and stored on `CalibrationData` (orchestrator.py:351-354), then surfaced to the human reviewer (consumed in src/review/human_review.py). The name implies a statistically meaningful interval reflecting model uncertainty.

WHAT THE CODE DOES:
1. Scale mismatch is real. `cal_prob` = calibrate(probability=aggregation.raw_aggregate, ...). The point estimate passes through nonlinear logit-space transforms: extremization is explicitly log-odds (aggregator.py:88-92, _extremize) for multi-provider, then Platt (calibration.py:210-214, operates on logit_p) and/or isotonic (calibration.py:175-203, interpolates on the logit scale). Confirmed by the module docstring 'isotonic regression on logit scale' (calibration.py:4).
2. The half-width is `1.96 * stdev` where `stdev = aggregation.stdev = np.std(probabilities, ddof=1)` over the raw run probabilities (aggregator.py:77) — run-to-run dispersion in raw probability space, computed BEFORE calibration. So the center (calibrated, logit-transformed) and the width (raw-space dispersion) are on different scales. The band is symmetric in probability space, not logit space, and can therefore exclude the raw mean. Confirmed.
3. Single-run flat band confirmed: n==1 returns stdev=0.0 (aggregator.py:64-72), and orchestrator falls back to cal_prob ± 0.05 (lines 348-349) — a fixed-width band carrying zero question-specific uncertainty, yet labeled confidence_interval.

The intent-vs-implementation gap is genuine: a field named/presented as a confidence interval reflecting uncertainty is, in fact, (a) a scale-inconsistent heuristic band post nonlinear calibration, and (b) a hardcoded placeholder for the common single-run case.

MATERIALITY: The self-assigned 'low' severity is correct. The CI is display-only — grep shows no control-flow branch keys off the CI bounds; it is not used as a gate, threshold, or submission filter, so the imprecision does not corrupt predictions or decisions in code. The impact is limited to potentially misleading the human reviewer who reads the band as a calibrated uncertainty estimate. The recommendation (propagate uncertainty through the calibration transform via logit-space endpoints, or relabel as a heuristic band and document the ±0.05 placeholder) is sound and proportionate.

### `BIZ:-123` — verify() / cross_verify() can verify against the primary model, defeating the 'external clients only' guarantee when only the primary provider is configured
- **confirmed ✓** · source: biz-logic · effort: small · file: `/Users/bjgilbert/Projects/Zoltar/sigma-predict/src/pipeline/llm_router.py:422-436`
- **evidence:** _external_clients() filters out the primary provider (lines 428-432) so you don't verify against the forecasting model — but the fallback at lines 433-435 says 'If the only available providers are the primary, include them anyway' and returns the primary client. cross_verify (lines 322-369) iterates _external_clients(), so in a config where the only verification provider equals the primary (e.g. verification_providers includes 'openai' and primary is openai), the system self-verifies despite docstrings at lines 5-6, 234-239, and 422-427 promising external-only verification. The class docstring (lines 7-9) claims Anthropic-as-primary is never self-verified, which holds for Anthropic specifically (init skips adding anthropic when it is primary, lines 130-140), but the same protection is NOT enforced for openai/google primaries — the fallback re-admits them.
- **recommendation:** Make the fallback explicit: if the only available verifier is the primary, return [] and emit a warning that cross-verification was skipped for independence, rather than silently self-verifying. Mirror the Anthropic exclusion logic for all primary providers.
- **verifier:** Confirmed end-to-end against the code. The intent-vs-implementation gap is real.

INTENT (docstrings): The module docstring (llm_router.py:7-9) states the local Anthropic primary is "Not used for self-verification." verify() (line 238) and cross_verify()/_external_clients() (lines 235, 423-426) promise "External clients only" / "Excludes the primary provider — you shouldn't verify against the same model that generated the forecast."

IMPLEMENTATION: The Anthropic exclusion is enforced once, structurally, in _init_external_providers (lines 129-140): when anthropic is primary it is never added to _external_providers, so it can never leak into verification. But for all OTHER providers (lines 142-153), the client is added whenever `name in allowed (verification_providers)` and `client.available` — with NO check that the provider is the primary. _external_clients() (lines 428-436) then filters the primary out of the list, but the fallback at lines 433-435 re-admits it: "If the only available providers are the primary, include them anyway." cross_verify (lines 332-335) iterates _external_clients() and calls .verify() on each.

CONCRETE TRIGGER (confirmed via config.py): Default verification_providers (config.py:102-105) includes "openai" and "google". Primary auto-detection (config.py:74-89) selects provider="openai" when only OPENAI_API_KEY is set (or "google" for only the Google key). In that config: _external_providers={"openai": client}; primary="openai"; _external_clients() filters openai → [] → fallback returns [openai_client]; cross_verify verifies the openai-generated forecast against openai. Self-verification, silently reported as cross-verification. verification.py:75/127/164 call cross_verify with no upstream guard, so nothing prevents this downstream.

ASYMMETRY is real and material for an analytical-integrity system: Anthropic-primary correctly yields zero self-verification (cross_verify returns []), but openai/google-primary single-provider configs self-verify while labeling results as external cross-verification — contaminating the independence guarantee the docstrings promise. I could not refute it: there is no primary-exclusion check on the non-anthropic branch, and no upstream guard in verification.py.

The recommendation (mirror the anthropic exclusion for all primaries, or return [] + warn when the only verifier is the primary) is sound.

### `BIZ:-124` — challenge_pre_mortem result is recorded but its vulnerability never feeds deliberation under default single-run config
- **confirmed ✓** · source: biz-logic · effort: small · file: `/Users/bjgilbert/Projects/Zoltar/sigma-predict/src/pipeline/orchestrator.py:296-324`
- **evidence:** challenge_entry_for_deliberation is produced (line 300) and passed into should_deliberate (line 322), and should_deliberate returns True on challenge vulnerability=='high' (deliberation.py:49). But the orchestrator ANDs the whole decision with `num_runs > 1` (line 324). With default num_runs=1, a 'high' vulnerability challenge — the exact signal deliberation exists to act on — cannot trigger deliberation. The challenge runs, costs tokens, is stored in verification.stages, but its actionable output is gated out in the common case.
- **recommendation:** Decouple the challenge-vulnerability deliberation trigger from the num_runs>1 guard, or allow a single-run deliberation mode that re-examines the forecast when challenge vulnerability is high. Otherwise the pre-mortem challenge is advisory-only in default runs.
- **verifier:** CONFIRMED. Every code claim verifies exactly as cited.

INTENT (what code claims): deliberation.py docstring (lines 3-9) and should_deliberate's docstring (lines 34-37) list three INDEPENDENT OR-conditions and explicitly name "Challenge vulnerability == 'high'" as a standalone deliberation trigger. should_deliberate() (lines 39-62) implements that OR-logic internally: line 49-51 returns True whenever challenge_entry.vulnerability == 'high', no num_runs check inside the function.

IMPLEMENTATION (what code does): orchestrator.py:319-324 calls should_deliberate(...) then ANDs the entire boolean with `num_runs > 1`. So the function's intended standalone challenge trigger is overridden by an external guard the function's contract knows nothing about.

DEFAULT-CONFIG claim verified across ALL entry points: orchestrator num_runs default=1 (lines 74, 465); CLI scripts/predict.py --runs default=1 (line 50, help text even says "default: 1 for Phase 0"); Streamlit pages/2_Predict.py number_input value=1 (line 195). Single-run is genuinely the common case, not an edge case.

CONSEQUENCE verified: challenge_forecast() runs whenever v_config.challenge_pre_mortem is enabled and record.runs is non-empty (lines 297-303) — a real LLM call independent of num_runs. Its result IS stored in record.verification.stages (line 313). But with default num_runs=1, a 'high' vulnerability challenge can never trigger deliberation. The signal is collected, costs tokens, persisted, but cannot drive action in the default case.

The gap is real and the divergence is genuine: the challenge-vulnerability condition was specified as a standalone OR-trigger but is in practice gated out by num_runs>1.

One nuance that supports the LOW severity (not a refutation): the deliberate() mechanism itself (deliberation.py:65-152) is built purely for cross-run DISAGREEMENT analysis — it summarizes multiple runs, prompts a supervisor for "WHY they disagree", computes min/max over probabilities. With a single run there is no disagreement to analyze, so the num_runs>1 guard is coherent for conditions 1 and 3. The challenge-vulnerability condition is the odd one out: it is a signal about aggregate-forecast fragility, not run disagreement, and could sensibly act on a single run — but the current deliberate() has no code path to do anything useful with it. So this is a design gap requiring a new single-run deliberation mode (exactly the recommendation), not a one-line fix.

### `BIZ:-131` — Kalshi cents-vs-probability heuristic `val > 1.0` misclassifies a 1-cent price as a probability
- **confirmed ✓** · source: biz-logic · effort: small · file: `src/platforms/kalshi.py:29-30, src/platforms/kalshi.py:173-174`
- **evidence:** `community_pred = val / 100.0 if val > 1.0 else val`. Kalshi quotes are integer cents 0-100. A market trading at 1 cent yields val=1.0, which is NOT > 1.0, so it is treated as a probability of 1.0 (certain YES) instead of 0.01. A 0-cent price likewise maps to 0.0 (coincidentally correct) but a 1-cent price inverts to near-certainty. The heuristic also silently mishandles any future API change to fractional-dollar pricing.
- **recommendation:** Key the unit conversion off the known API contract (Kalshi prices are always cents) rather than a value-range guess: always divide by 100 for Kalshi, or use `val >= 1.0` only as a defensive fallback with a logged warning.
- **verifier:** CONFIRMED. Code at src/platforms/kalshi.py:28-30 (_map_market) and 173-174 (get_community_prediction) both contain exactly `community_pred = val / 100.0 if val > 1.0 else val`. The stated intent is explicit and unambiguous in the comments: line 23 "0-100 cents -> 0.0-1.0" and line 29 "Kalshi prices are in cents (0-100)". So intent is "always divide by 100."

Tracing the heuristic against that intent confirms the divergence at val=1 (a 1-cent price): `1 > 1.0` is False, so the value is treated as an already-normalized probability and returned as 1.0 instead of 0.01. This is the maximal possible error — a near-zero-probability longshot reported as a near-certain YES. val=1 is a reachable, real Kalshi price (active binary markets trade in integer cents 1-99), not a hypothetical. val=0 also skips division but maps to 0.0 coincidentally correctly. Every other in-range value (2-100) converts correctly, so the bug triggers only at the single discrete price of 1 cent.

Downstream check: the value flows through orchestrator.py:545 into PlatformSignals.prices_at_prediction_time, the market-price comparison baseline, with no normalizing guard anywhere. So the inverted value would contaminate the recorded community-comparison signal for 1-cent markets.

The finding's secondary claim (the heuristic also breaks under a future fractional-dollar API change) is also valid in principle but speculative; the primary 1-cent claim stands on its own. The recommendation is correct: since the API contract guarantees cents, unconditionally dividing by 100 removes the range-guess entirely.

Refutation attempts failed: there is no upstream normalization, no clamping, and no documentation suggesting prices could already be fractional probabilities. The genuine ambiguity the heuristic tries to handle (cents vs. probability) does not exist for Kalshi — the contract is always cents — making the conditional both wrong at the boundary and unnecessary.

### `BIZ:-133` — Metaculus question-type detection: group-question branch is unreachable for the documented possibilities schema
- **confirmed ✓** · source: biz-logic · effort: small · file: `src/platforms/metaculus.py:19-30`
- **evidence:** _parse_question_type returns BINARY when possibilities.type=='binary', NUMERIC when 'continuous', then checks `if data.get('group') or data.get('type')=='group'` -> MULTIPLE_CHOICE. But any question with a `possibilities` block of type binary/continuous returns before reaching the group check, and a group/multiple-choice question on api2 typically lacks possibilities.type so it falls to the final `return QuestionType.BINARY` default unless the exact `group` key or `type=='group'` is present. For modern multiple-choice questions (which use options/sub-questions rather than a 'group' key), this branch never fires and they are misclassified as BINARY.
- **recommendation:** Detect multiple-choice from the actual api2 indicators (e.g. type in {'multiple_choice','group'} or presence of an options/sub_questions list) and order the checks so the MC detection runs before the BINARY default.
- **verifier:** Confirmed the code at src/platforms/metaculus.py:19-30 matches the finding verbatim. _parse_question_type returns BINARY when possibilities.type=='binary', NUMERIC when 'continuous', then checks `if data.get('group') or data.get('type')=='group'` for MULTIPLE_CHOICE, else falls through to a hardcoded `return QuestionType.BINARY`. A genuine intent-vs-implementation gap exists: any multiple-choice question that does NOT carry the literal `group` key or `type=='group'` marker is silently defaulted to BINARY. The repo's own tests (tests/test_platform_clients.py) only cover binary and continuous cases, so MC detection is genuinely untested.\n\nTwo corrections to the finding reduce its weight:\n1. FRAMING ERROR: The finding's headline claims the group branch is 'unreachable.' That is false. The branch IS reachable — it fires whenever the question is neither binary nor continuous AND has the group/type marker. The early returns for binary/continuous are correct (a binary question genuinely is binary). The actual defect is narrower: incomplete MC detection (missing the options/sub_questions indicators), not an unreachable branch. The recommendation (add options/sub_questions detection, order MC before the BINARY default) is sound, but the diagnosis is mislabeled.\n2. MATERIALITY IS LOW: question_type is consumed ONLY as display/prompt-context string interpolation (forecaster.py:136, decomposer.py:53, human_review.py:47, orchestrator.py:119). No code path branches on MULTIPLE_CHOICE — the pipeline has zero MC-specific handling. A misclassification therefore produces a slightly inaccurate label inside an LLM prompt, not an incorrect forecast computation. The empirical premise that 'modern api2 MC questions lack the group key' is an external assertion not verifiable from the code; the repo has no fixtures proving the current api2 MC schema.\n\nNet: the gap is real (hardcoded BINARY fallback can mislabel MC questions, and detection is incomplete/untested), but it is correctly rated 'low' given the cosmetic-only downstream impact, and the 'unreachable branch' characterization overstates/misdescribes the mechanism.

### `BIZ:-135` — Auth header schemes are assumed per platform with no validation; wrong scheme degrades silently to unauthenticated-style failures
- **confirmed ✓** · source: biz-logic · effort: medium · file: `src/platforms/kalshi.py:88-90, src/platforms/polymarket.py:67-69, src/platforms/metaculus.py:67-69`
- **evidence:** Kalshi sets `Authorization: Bearer {key}` and Polymarket sets `Authorization: Bearer {key}`. Kalshi's authenticated trade-api/v2 actually uses RSA-signed request headers (KALSHI-ACCESS-KEY / KALSHI-ACCESS-SIGNATURE / KALSHI-ACCESS-TIMESTAMP), not a static Bearer token; Polymarket CLOB uses L1/L2 API-key headers (POLY_ADDRESS / POLY_SIGNATURE / POLY_TIMESTAMP / POLY_API_KEY), not Bearer. So setting KALSHI_API_KEY or POLYMARKET_API_KEY produces a Bearer header the API ignores, and the read-only endpoints used here are public anyway — meaning the api_key config option is effectively inert for both platforms. The 'if api_key' branch runs but has no real auth effect.
- **recommendation:** Either implement the real signing schemes if authenticated endpoints are needed, or document that these clients are public-read-only and that the *_api_key config is currently unused, to avoid the false impression that setting the key enables authenticated access.
- **verifier:** Code confirmed at all three cited locations. Kalshi (kalshi.py:88-90) sets `Authorization: Bearer {api_key}` when kalshi_api_key is set; Polymarket (polymarket.py:66-69) sets `Authorization: Bearer {api_key}` when polymarket_api_key is set. Both config fields exist (config.py:63-64). The finding's auth-scheme claims are correct: Kalshi trade-api/v2 authenticated endpoints use RSA-signed KALSHI-ACCESS-* headers (not a static Bearer token), and Polymarket CLOB uses L1/L2 POLY_* header schemes (not Bearer), so a Bearer header is silently ignored by both APIs. The api_key branch runs but attaches a header the API disregards — the *_api_key config is effectively inert for both platforms. The intent-vs-implementation gap is genuine: the presence of the config option plus the `if api_key` branch implies setting the key enables authenticated access, which it does not.\n\nTwo refinements: (1) The Metaculus location citation (metaculus.py:67-69) is a red herring — that client correctly uses `Authorization: Token {key}`, the proper Django REST Framework TokenAuthentication scheme, and Metaculus actually exercises auth via submit_prediction. The finding's evidence body only argues Kalshi and Polymarket, so the Metaculus line number is boilerplate, not a real defect. (2) The title overstates runtime impact: it claims wrong scheme 'degrades silently to unauthenticated-style failures,' but both clients are explicitly read-only stubs (docstrings say so; submit_prediction raises NotImplementedError) and only hit public endpoints (/events, /markets) that need no auth. So no actual failures occur — the reads succeed regardless. The true defect is a dormant, misleading config option, not a functional failure path.

### `BIZ:-136` — Untrusted remote JSON is mapped without bounding/validation; price floats are not clamped to [0,1]
- **confirmed ✓** · source: biz-logic · effort: small · file: `src/platforms/polymarket.py:30-34, src/platforms/kalshi.py:27-30, src/platforms/metaculus.py:35-37`
- **evidence:** community_prediction is built directly from remote float(price)/float(q2) with no clamp to [0,1]. Polymarket uses float(price) as-is (polymarket.py:30, no range check). Metaculus uses float(q2) raw. A malformed or adversarial payload returning price=5.0 or q2=-2 flows straight into PlatformQuestion.community_prediction (a plain `float | None`, no pydantic bound at models.py:245) and onward into the orchestrator's signals/divergence logic. Kalshi's >1.0 heuristic partially masks this for cents but not for an out-of-range probability fed as <=1.0.
- **recommendation:** Clamp parsed community predictions to [0,1] (or reject + log out-of-range values) at the mapping boundary, since these values originate from untrusted remote APIs and feed downstream scoring.
- **verifier:** Confirmed at all cited locations. polymarket.py:31 does community_pred = float(price) with no range check. metaculus.py:36 does float(data["community_prediction"]["full"]["q2"]) raw. kalshi.py:30 applies a cents heuristic (val/100.0 if val>1.0 else val) that is normalization, not validation: a remote -2.0 passes through unchanged, and a 5.0 silently becomes 0.05. models.py:245 declares community_prediction: float | None = None with no pydantic ge/le bound. The intent-vs-implementation gap is genuine and grounded in a codebase-wide convention: the project clamps probabilities to [0.01,0.99] or [0.001,0.999] in ~10 other places (base_rate.py:73/230/242, forecaster.py:182/223, aggregator.py:108, calibration.py:187/206/216, orchestrator.py:348/423, scoring.py:15). The untrusted-remote community-price path is a conspicuous exception to that established clamping convention, so the divergence between expected handling and actual handling is real.

However, the finding overstates blast radius. Tracing actual consumption: the community value lands only in PlatformSignals.prices_at_prediction_time, a diagnostic dict. It is NOT fed back into the computed/calibrated probability — forecaster.py:221 pm_delta comes from LLM JSON output, not community price. The only real consumer is the human-review divergence flag at human_review.py:341 (abs(cal_p - community) > 0.15), a human-facing advisory. An out-of-range value yields a misleading flag string, not corrupted scoring, NaN, or a crash (float() already rejects non-numeric; out-of-range float arithmetic is well-defined). The finding's claim that bad values feed 'downstream scoring' is therefore not borne out for the prediction path. The recommendation (clamp to [0,1] or reject+log at the mapping boundary, and/or add a pydantic bound at models.py:245) is sound and cheap.

### `BIZ:-137` — Polymarket _map_market reads tags/description/created_at/resolution fields the CLOB market schema does not provide — these map to empty defaults
- **REFUTED ✗** · source: biz-logic · effort: small · file: `src/platforms/polymarket.py:18-54`
- **evidence:** _map_market does data.get('description',''), data.get('created_at',''), data.get('tags',[]), and hardcodes resolution_criteria='' and fine_print=''. The CLOB `/markets` schema (BASE_URL https://clob.polymarket.com, polymarket.py:15) returns condition_id, question, tokens, end_date_iso, market_slug — it does not return description, created_at, or tags at the CLOB layer (those live on the Gamma API). So description/created_at/tags will essentially always be empty for real CLOB responses, and _infer_domain (orchestrator.py:507-523) which keys off title+tags loses the tag signal entirely for Polymarket questions.
- **recommendation:** Either fetch descriptive metadata from the Polymarket Gamma API and merge it, or remove the fields that the CLOB endpoint never populates so the mapping does not imply data it cannot supply.
- **verifier:** REFUTED on its material claims. I read src/platforms/polymarket.py:18-54 (the _map_market code matches the evidence exactly) and src/pipeline/orchestrator.py:507-523 (the finding mis-cites the path as src/orchestrator.py, but _infer_domain matches — it keys off question.title + question.tags).

The finding's load-bearing assertion is that the CLOB /markets schema "does not return description, created_at, or tags at the CLOB layer (those live on the Gamma API)," concluding that description/tags "will essentially always be empty" and that _infer_domain "loses the tag signal entirely for Polymarket."

I verified the actual live CLOB response from https://clob.polymarket.com/markets. The market objects DO contain both `description` and `tags` (alongside condition_id, question, tokens, end_date_iso, market_slug, question_id, etc.). So:
- description=data.get('description','') -> populated. NOT empty. Finding wrong.
- tags=data.get('tags',[]) -> populated. The _infer_domain tag signal is NOT lost. Finding wrong (this was the only "material" downstream consequence claimed).

The repo's own test fixture (tests/test_platform_clients.py:54-77) also constructs Polymarket data with `description` and `tags` present, consistent with the real schema and contradicting the finding's premise.

The only accurate sub-claim is that `created_at` is not in the CLOB response, so created_at='' . But created_at is not consumed by _infer_domain, _compute_horizon (which uses resolve_date/end_date_iso), or any downstream logic — it's cosmetic. resolution_criteria/fine_print are hardcoded empty, but the CLOB schema has no equivalent fields, so empty correctly represents "not supplied" rather than an intent/impl gap.

Since the finding's two material claims are factually false against the real API and the surviving point (created_at) has no downstream consequence, the intent-vs-implementation gap is not real or material.

### `BIZ:-138` — Kalshi get_questions applies limit twice (server param and client slice), the client slice is redundant but the events-key fallback can hide pagination
- **confirmed ✓** · source: biz-logic · effort: trivial · file: `src/platforms/kalshi.py:124-130`
- **evidence:** `events = data.get('events', data) if isinstance(data, dict) else data` — if the response dict lacks an 'events' key, it falls back to the entire response dict, then `if not isinstance(events, list): events = []` discards it, returning []. This means any unexpected top-level envelope (e.g. {'markets': [...]} or a wrapper) yields an empty list silently rather than an error or a logged warning. The subsequent `events[:limit]` re-slices a list already limited by the server param, harmless but redundant.
- **recommendation:** Log a warning when the expected 'events' key is absent rather than silently returning [], so an API envelope change is observable.
- **verifier:** Verified at src/platforms/kalshi.py:124-130. Both code claims confirmed exactly.

Claim 1 (double limit): Line 117 sends `"limit": limit` to the server; line 130 re-slices with `events[:limit]`. Redundant but harmless — the finding itself correctly labels it so, so this is not the load-bearing claim.

Claim 2 (silent envelope masking — the material claim): Line 127 `events = data.get("events", data) if isinstance(data, dict) else data`. If the 200-OK response is a dict lacking an `events` key, `.get("events", data)` returns the whole dict; line 128-129 `if not isinstance(events, list): events = []` then discards it; line 130 returns `[]`. Confirmed: no log emitted on this path.

Why this is a genuine intent-vs-implementation gap and not refutable: The same function logs warnings on its OTHER failure paths — HTTP errors (line 132) and generic exceptions (line 135). The missing-key path is structurally a failure (got a successful response but couldn't extract the expected payload) yet is the ONLY failure path that returns [] silently. That asymmetry against the function's own established logging pattern is the real divergence. An API envelope rename/wrapper change (e.g. version bump) would silently yield zero questions with no diagnostic, masking a contract break.

Could not refute: the behavior does diverge from the function's own observability intent. The `data.get("events", data)` idiom recurs in get_question (147) and get_community_prediction (170), confirming it is an intentional "tolerate wrapped or unwrapped" pattern — but that intent silently fails for any third shape, which is precisely the gap claimed. Recommendation (log a warning when the key is absent) is sound and matches existing logging style.

### `BIZ:-146` — Unscaled apply_hedging_correction() is dead — only the scaled variant is ever used
- **confirmed ✓** · source: biz-logic · effort: trivial · file: `src/pipeline/calibration.py:103-114`
- **evidence:** Two hedging functions exist: apply_hedging_correction (flat +/- shift) and apply_hedging_correction_scaled (distance-weighted). The calibrate() function calls only apply_hedging_correction_scaled (lines 82, 92). grep shows apply_hedging_correction (the flat version) has no caller in src/ or scripts/. The module docstring describes only the scaled/distance-weighted behavior. The flat function is dead code that can mislead readers about the active correction.
- **recommendation:** Remove apply_hedging_correction or mark it clearly as deprecated/test-only. Keep one canonical hedging function.
- **verifier:** Confirmed against the code. apply_hedging_correction (flat +/- shift, calibration.py:103-114) is never called anywhere in src/ or scripts/ — grep shows production callers only for apply_hedging_correction_scaled (calibration.py:82 and :92, the Phase 0 and isotonic-fallback paths). The only callers of the flat function are in tests/test_calibration.py (5 assertions). The module docstring (line 3) and calibrate() docstring (line 50) reference only "scaled hedging correction," so the active path is the distance-weighted variant, matching the cited evidence exactly.

Two qualifications that bear on materiality, not existence: (1) it is not strictly orphaned dead code — it has 5 dedicated unit tests, so it functions as a tested-but-unwired utility rather than untouched text; (2) the docstrings do not falsely claim the flat function is active, so there is no active misdescription. The gap is narrower than "dead code that misleads readers about the active correction" — it is an unused public function sitting adjacent to the real one, which a reader could plausibly mistake for the active path. That is a genuine clarity/maintainability concern but has zero behavioral or correctness impact.

### `BIZ:-147` — search.search() max_results default parameter is shadowed and never honors caller intent vs config
- **confirmed ✓** · source: biz-logic · effort: trivial · file: `src/pipeline/search.py:83-92, 139-146`
- **evidence:** SearchModule.search(self, query, max_results=5) hardcodes a default of 5 in the signature, while the class also stores self._default_max_results = config.search.max_results (also defaulting to 5). The persona/base-rate callers pass max_results=self._default_max_results, but any direct call to search() that omits the arg silently uses the literal 5 rather than the configured value. The two sources of truth coincide only because both default to 5; if config.search.max_results is changed, direct search() calls ignore it.
- **recommendation:** Default the signature to None and fall back to self._default_max_results inside the method, so config.search.max_results is authoritative everywhere.
- **verifier:** Confirmed against the code. config.py:25 sets `max_results: int = 5` and search.py:75 stores `self._default_max_results = config.search.max_results`, while search.py:83 independently hardcodes `max_results: int = 5` in the signature — two sources of truth that coincide only because both default to 5. The intent (a configurable SearchConfig.max_results field) is not honored by every call path.

The finding's mechanism is correct, and there is a concrete in-tree counterexample that strengthens it: base_rate.py:127 calls `search_module.search(query)` with NO max_results argument, so it silently uses the literal 5 and ignores config.search.max_results entirely. If a user raised config.search.max_results to e.g. 10, search_with_persona (lines 139/146, which explicitly pass self._default_max_results) would fetch 10 per query, but the base-rate augmentation path would still fetch only 5. That is a real, demonstrable divergence between configured intent and implementation across two code paths.

Note one factual error in the finding's evidence: it states "the persona/base-rate callers pass max_results=self._default_max_results." That is true only for the persona caller; the base_rate caller does NOT pass it.
