The error does not undermine the finding — it understates it, since base_rate is an actual shipped caller that bypasses config rather than a hypothetical "direct call."

The recommendation (default the signature to None and fall back to self._default_max_results inside the method) is sound and would make config authoritative on all paths.

### `BIZ:-149` — Aggregation.deliberation_triggered is set by the orchestrator AFTER the human-review object is built, but deliberation only runs when num_runs>1 — single-run predictions silently skip the documented deliberation phase
- **confirmed ✓** · source: biz-logic · effort: small · file: `src/pipeline/orchestrator.py:319-330`
- **evidence:** The deliberation gate is `if should_deliberate(...) and num_runs > 1:`. The default predict() call uses num_runs=1 (personas defaults to [NEWS]). With one run, aggregate_runs returns method='single_run' with inter_model_agreement=1.0 and stdev=0.0, so should_deliberate could only fire via a high challenge vulnerability or large pre_mortem_delta — but the `and num_runs > 1` clause hard-blocks it regardless. So even a high-vulnerability challenge on a single-run forecast never triggers deliberation, despite should_deliberate() being designed to fire on that condition. The deliberation module docstring lists challenge vulnerability and pre_mortem_delta as independent triggers, implying they work on any run count.
- **recommendation:** Reconsider whether the `num_runs > 1` guard should also block the challenge-vulnerability and pre_mortem_delta triggers; if deliberation needs >1 run to be meaningful, document that single-run forecasts cannot deliberate and remove the misleading independent-trigger framing for that case.
- **verifier:** Code confirms the divergence. orchestrator.py:324 gates deliberation on `should_deliberate(...) and num_runs > 1`. The default is num_runs=1 with personas=[NEWS] (orchestrator.py:102-103, 465). aggregator.py:70 forces inter_model_agreement=1.0 on the single-run path, so should_deliberate condition 1 (cross-model disagreement) can never fire on a single run; conditions 2 (challenge vulnerability == "high") and 3 (|pre_mortem_delta| > 0.1) CAN fire on a single run (challenge_entry and pre_mortem_delta are computed independent of run count, orchestrator.py:296-318). But the `and num_runs > 1` clause hard-blocks them. Meanwhile both the module docstring (deliberation.py:3-9) and should_deliberate's docstring (deliberation.py:33-37) present the three triggers as independent and run-count-agnostic, with no mention that two of them are inert at the default run count. That is a genuine, code-grounded documentation-vs-behavior divergence. However, materiality is low and the 'gap' is largely defensible as intended: deliberate() is built entirely to explain cross-run disagreement (system prompt 'Multiple independent forecasters have produced divergent probability estimates... identify WHY they disagree', per-run summaries, probability range — deliberation.py:107-123). On one run there is nothing to deliberate, so the guard correctly prevents a vacuous LLM call rather than skipping a meaningful safety step. The defect is therefore a docstring-accuracy issue, not a logic bug that degrades forecasts. The title's 'silently skip the documented deliberation phase' slightly overstates impact since the skipped deliberation would be degenerate anyway. The finding's own recommendation (document that single-run cannot deliberate / drop the misleading independent-trigger framing) correctly characterizes it as a documentation fix.

### `BIZ:-150` — RegistryStore.update() is not atomic against load_all() data loss and silently appends on ID miss
- **confirmed ✓** · source: biz-logic · effort: medium · file: `src/registry/store.py:73-107`
- **evidence:** update() docstring promises to 'Replace an existing record (matched by prediction_id)'. On a miss it logs at INFO and appends a NEW record instead — a silent semantic change that can create duplicate logical records (e.g. if a prediction_id is mistyped or a record was hand-edited). _write_all writes to a .tmp then os.replace (atomic for the swap), but load_all() silently drops malformed lines (store.py:50-53 logs a warning and continues), so a single corrupt line means update() rewrites the file WITHOUT those dropped records — silent data loss on rewrite. The append-only 'safe for hundreds-to-low-thousands' claim does not account for this read-modify-rewrite loss path.
- **recommendation:** On update() miss, raise or return a status rather than silently appending. Before _write_all, fail closed if any line was dropped during load_all (or carry malformed lines through verbatim) so a rewrite cannot delete unparseable-but-present records.
- **verifier:** Both claims are confirmed against src/registry/store.py.

CLAIM 1 (silent append on ID miss): store.py:73-95. Docstring line 74 promises "Replace an existing record (matched by prediction_id)" and the method is named update(). On a miss, lines 87-92 log at INFO and append a new record. Partially mitigated: lines 76-77 of the docstring DO document the append-on-miss fallback, so it is not fully hidden. But the method name and primary contract still diverge from "create a duplicate," and a mistyped/hand-edited prediction_id silently creates a duplicate logical record with no error returned to the caller. Real but minor.

CLAIM 2 (data loss on rewrite — the load-bearing claim): fully confirmed. update() at line 79 calls load_all(). load_all() at store.py:48-53 catches JSONDecodeError/ValueError on each line, logs a warning, and continues WITHOUT retaining the malformed line. update() then calls _write_all(records) at line 94, which writes only the parsed subset to a .tmp and os.replace()s it over the original (lines 103-107). os.replace is atomic for the SWAP (no torn write) but does NOT protect CONTENT: the rewritten file is a strict subset of the original whenever any line failed to parse. Result: a single corrupt or schema-incompatible line is permanently deleted on the next update(). Notably, pydantic ValidationError subclasses ValueError, so a schema field change makes old-but-valid records "malformed" — one update() would silently delete every pre-migration record. The class docstring's "safe for hundreds-to-low-thousands" (lines 19-20) speaks only to volume/performance, not this correctness path, so the finding does not misread the intent.

Asymmetry that makes it non-obvious: save() (append-only) is lossless; update() (read-modify-rewrite) is not. The finding's evidence and line citations are all accurate. I attempted to refute on materiality but could not: the trigger conditions (hand-editing, corruption, schema change) are explicitly anticipated, not exotic.

### `BIZ:-160` — UI ignores the orchestrator's built-in progress_queue and fabricates its own hardcoded progress messages
- **confirmed ✓** · source: biz-logic · effort: small · file: `/Users/bjgilbert/Projects/Zoltar/sigma-predict/pages/2_Predict.py:59-86, :264-294`
- **evidence:** predict_until_review accepts a progress_queue parameter and emits real step events via _progress(...) (orchestrator.py:204 'Step 3/7: Running N forecast run(s)...', :212 per-run progress, :377/:379-380 completion + None sentinel). The UI never passes a progress_queue; instead _run_pipeline puts three static strings into its own queue up front ('Starting pipeline...', 'Question: ...', 'Runs: ...') at pages/2_Predict.py:67-69 and relies on the thread-end None sentinel. The status panel therefore shows fixed text regardless of which of the 7 pipeline steps is actually running. The 'Running pipeline...' progress is cosmetic and does not reflect real progress; the orchestrator's genuine step telemetry is dead from the UI's perspective.
- **recommendation:** Pass the shared queue as progress_queue to predict_until_review so the status panel reflects real pipeline steps. Remove the three hardcoded pre-messages.
- **verifier:** CONFIRMED. Every cited fact checks out against the code.

Orchestrator side (orchestrator.py): predict_until_review (line 70) accepts progress_queue (line 76), defines _progress() that puts messages on it (lines 94-96), and emits genuine per-step telemetry: "Step 1/7: Decomposing..." (140), "Step 2/7..." (161), "Step 3/7: Running N forecast run(s)..." (204), per-run "Run i/N: persona=..." (212), "Step 4/7..." (249), "Step 5/7..." (334), "Pipeline complete..." (377), then the None sentinel (379-380). The orchestrator docstring at line 472-473 explicitly says to use predict_until_review() directly "when you need to show results before submission (e.g. Streamlit UI)" — i.e. the UI is the intended consumer of this telemetry.

UI side (2_Predict.py): _run_pipeline puts exactly three static strings on its own queue ("Starting pipeline...", "Question: ...", "Runs: ...") at lines 67-69, then calls orchestrator.predict(..., auto_submit=True) at line 72 — NOT predict_until_review — and never passes a progress_queue. The progress fragment (264-294) drains the queue and renders fixed "Running pipeline..." status (290-292), relying solely on the None sentinel at line 86 for completion. grep across pages/ and app.py confirms no progress_queue is ever passed and predict_until_review is never called from the UI.

The gap is even worse than stated: predict() (line 461) does not even accept a progress_queue parameter, so it cannot forward telemetry — every _progress(...) call inside predict_until_review is discarded when invoked via predict(). The orchestrator's step telemetry is therefore 100% dead from the UI's perspective. The status panel shows the same three lines regardless of which of the 7 steps is running.

Additional corroboration: stale TODOs at lines 62-63 and 71 say to "switch to predict_until_review() once available" / "when TA finalises API" — but that method already exists and is fully functional. The code itself flags the wiring as incomplete. This strengthens, not weakens, the finding.

The recommendation is correct: passing the shared queue as progress_queue and switching to predict_until_review (plus removing the three hardcoded pre-messages) would surface real step progress. (Note: that switch also implicitly requires moving submission to submit_after_review, since predict(auto_submit=True) currently both forecasts and submits in one call — but that is adjacent scope, not a defect in this finding.)

### `BIZ:-161` — Dashboard active-table close_date/days_remaining computed but never displayed; horizon also shown without close-date fallback
- **confirmed ✓** · source: biz-logic · effort: trivial · file: `/Users/bjgilbert/Projects/Zoltar/sigma-predict/pages/1_Dashboard.py:88-103`
- **evidence:** Inside the per-record loop, close_date_str is assigned twice (lines 88, 91) and days_remaining = None (line 89) is set, but neither variable is ever read or added to the row dict (lines 97-106 only use question, horizon, created). They are dead local variables left from an abandoned 'days remaining' column. The comment at line 92-93 even notes uncertainty about whether close_date exists on the record (it does — models.py:214 PredictionRecord.close_date).
- **recommendation:** Either surface a 'Days remaining' column computed from close_date (which exists on the model), or delete the dead close_date_str/days_remaining assignments.
- **verifier:** Confirmed at /Users/bjgilbert/Projects/Zoltar/sigma-predict/pages/1_Dashboard.py:88-106. close_date_str is assigned at line 88 ("") and again at line 91 (getattr), and days_remaining = None at line 89 — but neither variable appears in the row dict built at lines 97-106 (which uses only ID, Question, Platform, Domain, Probability, Horizon (days), Created, _prediction_id) nor anywhere else in the file (grep confirms the only occurrences are lines 88/89/91). They are dead local variables. The active table renders no "Days remaining" or "Close" column (display_cols at line 111 omits both). The finding's supporting claim is also correct: close_date IS a declared field on PredictionRecord (src/models.py:214, close_date: str = ""), so the comment at line 93 expressing uncertainty about its existence is factually wrong, and the hasattr guard at line 91 is always-true dead defensiveness. Tests (tests/test_pipeline_contract.py:31-33) further confirm close_date exists with a "" default. The intent-vs-implementation gap (compute days-remaining data but never display it) is real and verifiable. Could not refute. Minor citation imprecision: the model file is src/models.py, not a top-level models.py — substance unaffected.

### `BIZ:-162` — KEY_REGISTRY_FILTERS session-state key is initialized but never used; Registry filters do not persist
- **confirmed ✓** · source: biz-logic · effort: trivial · file: `/Users/bjgilbert/Projects/Zoltar/sigma-predict/components/state.py:15,27; pages/5_Registry.py:31-49`
- **evidence:** state.py declares KEY_REGISTRY_FILTERS = 'registry_filters' and seeds it to {} in _DEFAULTS (lines 15, 27). grep across pages/ and components/ shows no other reference — the Registry page builds its filter widgets (Registry:41-49) without reading or writing this key. The documented intent ('# dict' of registry filters, implying persisted filter state) is never realized; Registry filters reset on every navigation away and back. By contrast KEY_SCOREBOARD_DATE_RANGE/KEY_SCOREBOARD_DOMAINS ARE written (Scoreboard:43,48) though also not subsequently read for restoration.
- **recommendation:** Either wire Registry filters to KEY_REGISTRY_FILTERS for persistence across navigation, or remove the unused key. Same for the scoreboard keys, which are written but never used to restore widget state.
- **verifier:** Verified against code. The factual core holds: components/state.py:15,27 declares KEY_REGISTRY_FILTERS = "registry_filters" with a "# dict" comment and seeds it to {} in _DEFAULTS, signaling intent to persist registry filter state. grep -rn across the repo confirms the key has ZERO references outside the declaration and default — pages/5_Registry.py imports only KEY_SELECTED_PREDICTION_ID and init_state (line 17) and builds its filter widgets (lines 41-49) with no read or write of the key. So the intent (a registry_filters state slot) is genuinely unrealized; it is a dead session-state key. The comparison claim is also accurate: pages/4_Scoreboard.py:43,48 WRITE KEY_SCOREBOARD_DATE_RANGE/KEY_SCOREBOARD_DOMAINS but grep confirms they are never read back for restoration — write-only dead keys. The intent-vs-implementation gap is therefore real: three session-state keys are initialized/seeded as if to support persisted filter state but no code path consumes them.

However, the finding's framing ("Registry filters do not persist") overreaches and is partially wrong about mechanism. In Streamlit, widgets created with a `key` automatically persist their value in session_state across reruns/intra-session navigation. Registry's date_input uses key="registry_date_range" (line 46) and the table uses key="registry_table" (line 142), so those DO persist independently of KEY_REGISTRY_FILTERS. The three multiselects (lines 41,42,45) have no key, so those specifically reset — but the correct fix is adding key= args, not "wiring filters to KEY_REGISTRY_FILTERS." The recommendation reflects a misunderstanding of Streamlit persistence idiom. The genuinely material defect is narrower than claimed: an unused initialized constant plus two write-only constants = dead code, a code-cleanliness issue, not a functional persistence bug. That dead-code gap is real and correctly rated low.

### `BIZ:-163` — Review page write-paths reach into cache._store private attribute, bypassing the cache abstraction
- **confirmed ✓** · source: biz-logic · effort: small · file: `/Users/bjgilbert/Projects/Zoltar/sigma-predict/pages/3_Review.py:287-289; pages/2_Predict.py:375-376`
- **evidence:** RegistryCache exposes load(), get(), invalidate() (components/cache.py) but no write method. Both the Review 'Add lesson' form (3_Review.py:287 store = cache._store; store.update(record)) and the Predict confirm-review handler (2_Predict.py:376 cache._store.update(record)) reach through the leading-underscore private _store. This works but couples the pages to RegistryCache internals; any change to cache storage breaks them silently. Also, mutating record.resolution.structured_lessons in place (3_Review.py:285-286) then update()-ing relies on the cached record object identity being the stored one.
- **recommendation:** Add a public RegistryCache.update(record) (or save) method that updates the store and invalidates the dirty flag atomically, and call it from both pages instead of touching _store.
- **verifier:** Confirmed against the code. RegistryCache (components/cache.py:9-31) exposes only load(), get(), and invalidate() — no public write/update/save method. _store is a leading-underscore private attribute (line 16). Both cited write-paths reach through it: 3_Review.py:287-289 (store = cache._store; store.update(record); cache.invalidate()) and 2_Predict.py:375-377 (cache._store.update(record); cache.invalidate()). A grep across pages/, components/, and src/registry/ shows these are the ONLY two write reaches through cache._store; the other _store holder (query.py) only calls load_all() within its own class, so it is not a cross-abstraction violation. RegistryStore.update() genuinely exists (src/registry/store.py:73) and is the real persist method, so the reach is to a working private dependency, not a no-op. The intent-vs-implementation gap is real: the class is documented and surfaced as the abstraction over the store (pages receive a cache, not a store), yet two callers bypass it via a private attribute and must manually pair update() with invalidate(). Any rename/refactor of _store or change to the storage mechanism breaks both pages with no API/type signal — exactly the silent-coupling claim. The secondary in-place-mutation observation (3_Review.py:284-286 mutates record.resolution.structured_lessons on the cached object, then update()s) is also accurate: it works only because get() returns the same object identity that update() persists, which is a genuine fragility. This is a maintainability/encapsulation defect, not a correctness bug — the code functions today.

### `BIZ:-164` — Search-results 'Community P' column will raise on numeric formatting if community_prediction is None across mixed rows — and selection index can desync from filtered display
- **confirmed ✓** · source: biz-logic · effort: small · file: `/Users/bjgilbert/Projects/Zoltar/sigma-predict/pages/2_Predict.py:129-152`
- **evidence:** The search results dataframe stores raw community_prediction values (possibly None) at line 133 and renders the df directly. Selection maps the displayed row index back to results[idx] (line 149) assuming the dataframe row order equals the results list order; this holds now but is fragile because the displayed df is df[['Title','Close','Community P']] while _question_id lives in a dropped column — if pandas ever reorders or the user sorts the column in the Streamlit UI, idx no longer maps to results[idx]. Selection by positional index into the original list is not robust to client-side sorting.
- **recommendation:** Carry the question_id through and resolve the selected row by its _question_id value rather than positional index, so client-side sorting of the dataframe cannot desync the selection.
- **verifier:** Mixed verdict — one of the finding's two claims is REAL, the other is FALSE, and the recommendation is non-functional.

CODE READ (pages/2_Predict.py:126-152): rows are built in results order (129-138); df=pd.DataFrame(rows) keeps a default RangeIndex matching results order; the displayed slice df[['Title','Close','Community P']] drops _question_id; no column_config, hide_index, or numeric formatting is applied; selection resolves via results[idx] (150).

CLAIM 1 (numeric formatting raises on None across mixed rows): REFUTED. There is NO numeric formatting anywhere — no .format(), no column_config number format, no f-string on the column. Raw community_prediction (possibly None) is passed straight to st.dataframe (133/141). Pandas/Streamlit render a mixed None/float object column without error. This claim describes code that does not exist. False.

CLAIM 2 (selection index can desync from displayed order on client-side sort): REAL and confirmed, but mis-framed and mis-remediated. Streamlit's default st.dataframe toolbar lets the END USER sort columns. Confirmed via Streamlit issue #11345 (P2, team-confirmed): after a dropdown 'Sort Ascending/Descending', event.selection['rows'] returns the positional index in the ORIGINAL unsorted order while the visual highlight lands on a different (sorted-position) row. So the user clicks what looks like row C but results[idx] returns row A — a genuine wrong-question selection bug. The finding's 'fragile/if pandas ever reorders' hedging understates it: the bug is reachable today through normal UI interaction, not hypothetical.

RECOMMENDATION IS NON-FUNCTIONAL: 'resolve by _question_id instead of positional index' does NOT fix the bug. The returned idx already indexes the original order, so df.iloc[idx]['_question_id'] equals results[idx].question_id — the identical wrong row. The selection payload contains no client-side ordering info to recover what the user visually clicked; resolving by id changes nothing. (Real fixes: disable sorting/the toolbar, or use st.data_editor with a stable selection column, or accept Streamlit's documented clear-on-header-click behavior.)

Net: a genuine, material UI correctness bug exists at the cited location (sort desync → wrong question selected), so real=true. But the finding is inaccurate as written — the formatting claim is fabricated and the proposed remediation would not work.

### `BIZ:-169` — resolve.py classify_error SEARCH_BLINDSPOT/CONFABULATION getattr defaults are misleading and mask the real defaults
- **confirmed ✓** · source: biz-logic · effort: trivial · file: `scripts/resolve.py:44,53`
- **evidence:** classify_error uses `getattr(r, "parse_failed", False)`, `getattr(r, "search_quality_score", 5) < 2`, and reads r.confidence_self_score directly. RunResult is a pydantic model where parse_failed (default False), search_quality_score (default 0), and confidence_self_score (default 5) ALWAYS exist as attributes (models.py:99,108,110). The getattr fallbacks therefore never apply. More importantly the fallback default 5 for search_quality_score contradicts the model default of 0 — if a run object were ever constructed without a parsed score (model default 0), the SEARCH_BLINDSPOT gate `< 2` would fire, the opposite of what the getattr default 5 implies the author expected. In practice the forecaster sets search_quality_score=int(data.get('search_quality_score', 5)) on successful parse (forecaster.py:234), so live records carry 5; the divergence is latent, not active.
- **recommendation:** Drop the getattr fallbacks and read the fields directly (r.search_quality_score, r.parse_failed) to make the intent explicit, and align the model default for search_quality_score with the forecaster default (5) so an unparsed run does not silently classify as a search blindspot.
- **verifier:** Verified against actual code. The file is src/models.py (finding cited bare "models.py" but line numbers 99/108/110 match exactly) and scripts/resolve.py:44,53.

CONFIRMED FACTS:
- RunResult is a pydantic BaseModel (src/models.py:92) with search_quality_score=0 (line 99), confidence_self_score=5 (line 108), parse_failed=False (line 110). These always exist as attributes on any instance.
- classify_error (scripts/resolve.py) uses getattr(r, "parse_failed", False) at line 44 and getattr(r, "search_quality_score", 5) at line 53. Because the pydantic fields always exist, the getattr fallback values (False, 5) are dead — never reached. This is a genuine code-hygiene defect: the fallback default 5 directly contradicts the model default 0, which is misleading exactly as the title claims.
- forecaster.py:234 sets search_quality_score=int(data.get("search_quality_score", 5)), so successfully-parsed live runs carry >=5 unless the LLM explicitly emits a low score. Finding's "latent, not active" framing is correct.

SHARPENING (makes the hypothetical even less reachable than the finding states): The finding's worry that a parse failure could produce model-default 0 and trip SEARCH_BLINDSPOT (<2) is incorrect about the mechanism — on parse failure forecaster.py sets data={}, so data.get("search_quality_score", 5) returns 5, NOT 0. The only score-0 construction is the synthetic "aggregate" RunResult in aggregator.py:255,280 (built without search_quality_score). I traced orchestrator.py: record.runs is populated ONLY from run_forecast (line 229); the aggregate_run (line 299) is used transiently for the pre-mortem challenge and is never appended to record.runs. So no record that classify_error iterates can carry score 0 today. The divergence is even more latent than the finding implies.

VERDICT: The finding is real as a code-clarity/consistency issue — the getattr fallbacks are genuinely dead and their default contradicts the model default. It is honestly scoped: rated low, explicitly labeled "latent, not active," and accurately describes the forecaster default. It does not overclaim a functional bug. Classification output is correct in all reachable states. This is real and accurately rated, not a behavior-changing intent-vs-implementation gap.

### `BIZ:-170` — predict.py rejected-summary branch depends on substring 'REJECTED' but the live path only sets that via interactive review
- **confirmed ✓** · source: biz-logic · effort: trivial · file: `scripts/predict.py:141-148, src/review/human_review.py:225, src/pipeline/orchestrator.py:416`
- **evidence:** predict.py distinguishes the 'Prediction rejected' message by checking `'REJECTED' in (record.human_review.human_reasoning or '')`. The rejection sentinel is the literal prefix 'REJECTED' produced only by the interactive present_for_review (human_review.py:225) and matched by submit_after_review via startswith('REJECTED') (orchestrator.py:416). The CLI predict() always runs interactive review (auto_submit defaults False and the CLI never passes it), so the branch is reachable there. But note the substring `in` check in predict.py is looser than the `startswith` used in the orchestrator gate; a human-typed adjustment reasoning containing the word 'REJECTED' would mis-label an accepted/adjusted prediction as rejected in the CLI summary only (the actual submit gate uses startswith and would still submit).
- **recommendation:** Use the same startswith('REJECTED') test in predict.py that the orchestrator uses, or better, branch on record.submitted is None plus an explicit review.rejected flag rather than string matching.
- **verifier:** All code claims verified at the cited lines. predict.py:145 uses the loose substring check `"REJECTED" in (record.human_review.human_reasoning or "")` while the actual submit gate at orchestrator.py:416 uses `review.human_reasoning.startswith("REJECTED")`. The "REJECTED" sentinel is produced only in the interactive reject branch (human_review.py:225, as `"REJECTED: ..."`/`"REJECTED"`). The CLI calls orchestrator.predict() without auto_submit (predict.py:133-138), and predict() defaults auto_submit=False (orchestrator.py:467), so the rejection branch is reachable via the interactive present_for_review path — the finding's reachability claim is correct.

The intent-vs-implementation gap is real: predict.py intends to distinguish "rejected" from "logged but not submitted", but a substring match diverges from the orchestrator's authoritative startswith gate. A human who chooses adjust ('j') and types reasoning containing the word "REJECTED" mid-string (human_review.py:210) would, IF submission also fails (leaving record.submitted=None), be mis-labeled "Prediction rejected" in the CLI summary. Note the finding slightly overstates the trigger: predict.py:141 checks `if record.submitted:` first, so a successfully-submitted adjusted prediction prints "submitted" regardless — the mis-label only surfaces when submitted is None (e.g., platform submission failed). The actual submit decision is never affected because the gate correctly uses startswith.

Impact is confined to a cosmetic CLI summary string under a narrow conjunction of conditions (adjust-reasoning containing "REJECTED" + submission failure). The recommendation (mirror startswith, or branch on submitted/explicit rejected flag) is sound and trivial. Genuine but minor.

### `BIZ:-171` — batch_predict.py exposes --runs but no --personas, silently diverging from predict.py's persona control
- **confirmed ✓** · source: biz-logic · effort: small · file: `scripts/batch_predict.py:32,88-92, src/pipeline/orchestrator.py:101-106`
- **evidence:** predict.py offers --personas to choose search personas; batch_predict.py does not, and calls orchestrator.predict() without a personas argument (batch_predict.py:88-92). With personas=None and num_runs=1 (the default), predict_until_review forces personas=[SearchPersona.NEWS] (orchestrator.py:102-103). So every batch question at the default --runs 1 is forecast with the NEWS persona only — the other four personas (academic, contrarian, domain_expert, meta_forecaster) are unreachable in batch mode unless the user passes --runs >1, in which case personas are round-robin-assigned by index (orchestrator.py:105-106). The persona-diversity feature advertised in predict.py is effectively absent from the batch entry point.
- **recommendation:** Add a --personas option to batch_predict.py mirroring predict.py, or document that batch defaults to NEWS-only at runs=1. If single-persona batch is intended, state it.
- **verifier:** Confirmed at all cited locations. batch_predict.py (scripts/batch_predict.py:24-33) exposes --platform/--limit/--tournament-id/--search/--runs/--verbose but no --personas, and calls orchestrator.predict(question, platform_client, num_runs=runs) with no personas arg (lines 88-92). predict() (orchestrator.py:461-490) defaults personas=None and forwards it to predict_until_review(). In predict_until_review (orchestrator.py:101-106), personas is None + num_runs==1 forces [SearchPersona.NEWS]; num_runs>1 round-robins list(SearchPersona) by index. --runs defaults to 1 (batch_predict.py:32). By contrast predict.py:54-137 exposes --personas and parses it into a SearchPersona list passed to the orchestrator. Enum order (src/models.py:38-43): ACADEMIC, NEWS, CONTRARIAN, DOMAIN_EXPERT, META_FORECASTER. So the asymmetry is real: the batch entry point gives users no way to select personas, while the single-question entry point does. The intent-vs-implementation gap (orchestrator/predict.py advertise persona control; batch silently lacks it) holds.

Two precision notes that bound, but do not refute, the finding: (1) The default NEWS-only-at-runs=1 behavior is NOT batch-specific — predict.py also defaults to None→NEWS at runs=1 unless --personas is passed. The true gap is narrower than 'batch is NEWS-only': it is that batch users cannot OVERRIDE persona selection, not that batch behaves differently by default. (2) The finding slightly overstates by saying the diversity feature is 'effectively absent' from batch — it is reachable via --runs>1 round-robin (the finding's own evidence concedes this), so it is uncontrollable rather than absent. These are accuracy nuances, not refutations.

### `BIZ:-173` — Verification stack is config-enabled-by-default but inert without external provider env keys; orchestrator records enabled=False rather than the configured intent
- **confirmed ✓** · source: biz-logic · effort: small · file: `src/config.py:46-53, src/pipeline/orchestrator.py:125-134, src/pipeline/llm_router.py:384-386`
- **evidence:** VerificationConfig.enabled defaults True and verification_providers lists 10 providers (config.py:102-105). But v_enabled = v_config.enabled AND router.verification_available() (orchestrator.py:126), and verification_available() is `bool(self._external_providers)` (llm_router.py:386), which is populated only for providers whose client.available is True — i.e. whose API key/env is set (llm_router.py:147). Under the documented default .env.example (only ANTHROPIC_API_KEY + TAVILY set, OPENAI/GOOGLE blank), no external provider initializes, so verification_available() is False and the entire verify_actors/verify_base_rate/verify_forecast_run/challenge_pre_mortem stack (orchestrator.py:147-313) is skipped. The orchestrator does append a pipeline warning (line 131-134) and sets VerificationReport.enabled=False, so this is correctly surfaced — but the named safeguards (pre-mortem challenge, cross-model base-rate/actor verification) are off by default given the shipped env template, which a reader of VerificationConfig.enabled=True would not expect.
- **recommendation:** Document in config and README that verification requires at least one non-primary external provider key (OpenAI/Google/Ollama). Consider logging at WARNING (already done) prominently in CLI output, not just logger, so operators know the challenge/verification gates are inactive.
- **verifier:** CONFIRMED. Every load-bearing claim in the finding checks out against the code.

VERIFIED facts:
- src/config.py:47 — VerificationConfig.enabled defaults True; verify_base_rate/verify_actors/challenge_pre_mortem also default True (lines 48-52).
- src/config.py:102-105 — verification_providers defaults to 10 external providers (openai, google, llama, gemma, nemotron, deepseek, qwen, devstral, glm, kimi).
- src/pipeline/orchestrator.py:126 — v_enabled = v_config.enabled AND self.router.verification_available(). The config flag alone does not gate execution.
- src/pipeline/llm_router.py:384-386 — verification_available() returns bool(self._external_providers).
- _external_providers (llm_router.py:142-149) is populated only for providers in `allowed` whose client.available is True (i.e. API key/env set).
- The entire verify_actors/base_rate/forecast/pre-mortem stack downstream is guarded by `if v_enabled` (e.g. orchestrator.py:147), so it is fully skipped when no external provider exists.

The gap is real and, in one respect, WORSE than the finding states. The finding notes the default .env.example sets only ANTHROPIC_API_KEY + TAVILY. There is a second, stronger reason verification is inert under that default: llm_router.py:129-132 explicitly EXCLUDES the Anthropic client from external providers when Anthropic is the primary model (to avoid self-verification). Since ANTHROPIC_API_KEY alone makes Anthropic primary (config.py:75-79), having only the Anthropic key guarantees _external_providers is empty even though sigma-verify ships an Anthropic provider. So the shipped, documented default configuration provably yields verification_available()=False and a fully skipped verification/challenge stack — exactly the divergence from VerificationConfig.enabled=True that a reader would not expect.

Additionally, .env.example:8-12 labels OPENAI_API_KEY as 'Optional — needed for submitting predictions to platforms', which is misleading: that key (or Google/Ollama) is what actually activates verification, not platform submission. This compounds the intent/implementation mismatch.

Mitigations correctly noted by the finding and confirmed in code: orchestrator.py:131-134 logs a WARNING and appends a pipeline warning, and VerificationReport.enabled is set to the effective (False) value, not the configured intent — so the inert state IS surfaced in the report, not silently swallowed. This is why the severity is appropriately low rather than higher: no silent contamination, just a documentation/UX gap where the named safeguards (pre-mortem challenge, cross-model verification) are off by default given the shipped template. No README mention of the external-provider requirement was found, supporting the documentation recommendation.

### `BIZ:-174` — apply_hedging_correction (non-scaled) is dead production code — only the _scaled variant is wired into calibrate()
- **confirmed ✓** · source: biz-logic · effort: trivial · file: `src/pipeline/calibration.py:103-114`
- **evidence:** calibrate() uses apply_hedging_correction_scaled (calibration.py:82,92) for both the isotonic-fallback and Phase 0 paths. The flat apply_hedging_correction(probability, shift) is referenced only by tests/test_calibration.py, never by any pipeline code. It is a vestigial transformation that no longer participates in the calibration gate; its existence next to the scaled version invites confusion about which hedging rule is actually applied (the scaled, distance-weighted one).
- **recommendation:** Remove apply_hedging_correction or mark it deprecated, and update tests to target the scaled function actually used in the pipeline.
- **verifier:** Confirmed by direct code inspection at the cited location and repo-wide grep. calibrate() (src/pipeline/calibration.py) calls apply_hedging_correction_scaled at lines 82 (isotonic-fallback path) and 92 (Phase 0 path) — these are the only hedging calls in production. The flat apply_hedging_correction (lines 103-114) is referenced ONLY by tests/test_calibration.py (imports at line 7; called at lines 20,24,28,32,36) and its own definition; grep across all .py files plus .md/.toml/.cfg/.json found zero production references. No __all__ re-export and no dynamic dispatch hides a usage. The module docstring (lines 1-6) and calibrate() describe the scaled, distance-weighted hedging (effective_shift = base_shift*2*distance) as the active rule, so the flat function is genuinely vestigial — a public, tested, but unwired transformation. This is a real intent-vs-implementation gap. It is correctly classified as low severity: zero runtime/correctness/security impact; the only cost is maintainability/confusion from two similarly-named functions where the tested one is not the one actually used. The recommendation (remove or deprecate, retarget tests to the scaled function) is sound.

### `CRIT-112` — validator.py is fully untested and its run-count / search-quality / reference-class-instance gates are never exercised; validate_all is dead and instance-count gate depends on a field nothing populates
- **REFUTED ✗** · source: critic · effort: medium · file: `/Users/bjgilbert/Projects/Zoltar/sigma-predict/src/pipeline/validator.py:59-141`
- **evidence:** validator.py is one of two pipeline modules not cited by any prior finding. validate_all (lines 131-141) is never called — the orchestrator instead invokes validate_base_rate / validate_runs / validate_aggregation individually (orchestrator.py:199,244,358), so the combined entry point is dead. validate_base_rate_instances (lines 59-83) gates on record.decomposition.reference_class_instance_count, but the decomposer never sets it (decomposer.py builds Decomposition without reference_class_instance_count; models.py:88 defaults it to None), so `if count is None: return warnings` always short-circuits and the entire N<10 / N<20 base-rate-reliability gate is permanently inert — a contamination-control gate that never fires, analogous to the temporal-firewall no-op already found. No test file exercises any validator gate (grep shows no test references to PipelineValidator).
- **recommendation:** Wire the decomposer to populate reference_class_instance_count (the decomposer prompt already produces reference_class), remove or call validate_all, and add unit tests for each gate including the strict_validation raise path.
- **verifier:** Refuted on every load-bearing claim with direct code evidence; the finding is factually wrong, not merely overstated.

1. "validator.py is fully untested ... No test file exercises any validator gate (grep shows no test references to PipelineValidator)." FALSE. tests/test_cross_pollination.py contains a dedicated TestPipelineValidator class (lines 182-338). It exercises every gate: base_rate range (lines 198,206,215), aggregation/probability (223,231), run_count + parse_failure + search_quality (240,248,256,264), the strict_validation PipelineError raise path (lines 268-273), validate_all (lines 275-284), and all instance-count branches None/<10/10-19/=10/>=20 (lines 287-338). I ran `pytest tests/test_cross_pollination.py::TestPipelineValidator` — 16 passed in 0.09s. The critic's own cited grep evidence is contradicted by the grep I ran, which surfaces 30+ test references to PipelineValidator. The claim is simply false.

2. "validate_all is dead." TECHNICALLY TRUE that the orchestrator calls the three gates individually (orchestrator.py:199,244,358) rather than validate_all, but validate_all is directly tested (test_validate_all_combines_warnings, line 275). It is a tested public combined entry point, not dead code in the meaningful sense. At most this is a minor unused-by-orchestrator note, not a defect.

3. "instance-count gate depends on a field nothing populates ... the decomposer never sets it ... so the entire N<10/N<20 gate is permanently inert — analogous to the temporal-firewall no-op." FALSE and the analogy is wrong. The field is populated by the BASE RATE stage, not the decomposer. base_rate.py:69-70 extracts reference_class_instance_count from the LLM JSON, estimate_base_rate returns it, and orchestrator.py:172 assigns `record.decomposition.reference_class_instance_count = instance_count` BEFORE validate_base_rate runs at line 199. So the gate's `if count is None` short-circuit does NOT always fire in production; it fires only when the LLM omits the count. The critic looked for population in the decomposer (which legitimately does not own this field) and concluded nothing populates it — a misattribution. This is the central error: the gate is wired and live.

The finding's three pillars (untested, dead validate_all as a real defect, permanently-inert instance gate) are each refuted. It is not a new genuine issue; it is a set of incorrect claims produced by checking the wrong module for field population and an incorrect grep result for test coverage. Default to real=false applies, and here the evidence is affirmatively contradictory, not merely unconfirmable.

### `CRIT-113` — _compute_horizon silently truncates to whole days and returns None for any non-ISO resolve_date, with no warning that horizon metadata is missing
- **confirmed ✓** · source: critic · effort: trivial · file: `/Users/bjgilbert/Projects/Zoltar/sigma-predict/src/pipeline/orchestrator.py:526-534`
- **evidence:** `datetime.fromisoformat(question.resolve_date.replace("Z","+00:00"))` then `(resolve - now).days`. resolve_date is a free-form, unvalidated string on PlatformQuestion (the core pass noted close_date/resolve_date are ''-default free-form strings with no validation, and Kalshi events produce empty resolve_date per a sweep finding). Any non-ISO format (or empty string) hits `except (ValueError, TypeError): return None`, so horizon is silently dropped with no pipeline_warning. Additionally `.days` truncates toward zero, so a resolution 23 hours away reports horizon=0 days, indistinguishable from already-resolved. Horizon is metadata, hence low severity, but the time/timezone-edge class (truncation + timezone-naive parse failure of upstream-empty dates) was not examined for this function (prior passes only covered _infer_domain and the lexicographic created_at sort).
- **recommendation:** Round rather than truncate (or store hours), and on parse failure append a 'horizon_unavailable' pipeline_warning instead of silently returning None so the missing-date condition is observable.
- **verifier:** Code-verified at the cited location (orchestrator.py:525-534) and the claim is accurate on every factual point. `_compute_horizon` does `datetime.fromisoformat(question.resolve_date.replace("Z","+00:00"))` then `max(0, (resolve - now).days)`, with `except (ValueError, TypeError): return None` and an early `return None` on empty string. `resolve_date` is confirmed a free-form unvalidated `str = ""` (models.py:244, no validator), and Kalshi events hardcode `resolve_date=""` (kalshi.py:71), so the silent-None path is reachable in production. `.days` truncation toward zero and the `max(0, ...)` collapse are both correct Python semantics. The recommendation to emit a `pipeline_warning` is architecturally sound: pipeline_warnings is the established mechanism with 8+ existing call sites in this same file. The finding is genuinely NEW — it self-discloses that prior passes covered `_infer_domain` and the lexicographic `created_at` sort but not `_compute_horizon`, which is a distinct function with its own time/timezone-edge surface; this is not a restatement.

However the finding overstates impact in two ways. The ONLY downstream consumer of `horizon_days` is Brier-decomposition bucketing (registry/query.py:176-196), which buckets into <30d / 30-180d / >180d and explicitly treats `None` and `0` identically (`if days is None or days < 30`). So: (a) the 23h→0 truncation is analytically harmless because such a question correctly belongs in <30d anyway; (b) truncation-vs-rounding only flips a bucket at the exact 29.5d/180.5d boundaries — a negligible edge; (c) the "indistinguishable from already-resolved" framing is misleading because nothing uses horizon as a resolution gate. The substantive half is the silent None (no observability that horizon metadata was dropped for upstream-empty/non-ISO dates), which is real but is metadata gracefully handled downstream.

### `SECU-59` — Unencoded interpolation of question_id into request URL paths (path-injection / request-shaping)
- **REFUTED ✗** · source: framed · effort: small · file: `/Users/bjgilbert/Projects/Zoltar/sigma-predict/src/platforms/metaculus.py:121`
- **evidence:** resp = self._client.get(f"/questions/{question_id}/") — same unencoded f-string interpolation at metaculus.py:159,187; polymarket.py:111,132; kalshi.py:144,167. question_id originates from user input in the UI (2_Predict.py:155 text_input 'Question ID', and 2_Predict.py:179 qid_from_url = url.rstrip('/').split('/')[-1] from a user-supplied URL) and from CLI args, with no format validation. A value containing '../' or query/fragment characters can alter the request target relative to base_url (e.g. '..%2f' or '?x=' style manipulation), and the URL-from-last-segment heuristic feeds whatever the user pastes.
- **recommendation:** Validate question_id against an expected per-platform pattern (e.g. digits for Metaculus, ticker charset for Kalshi) before issuing the request, and URL-encode the path segment (urllib.parse.quote). Reject malformed IDs with a clear error instead of letting them reach the network layer.
- **verifier:** The factual mechanics are accurate and I confirmed them in code and by execution. metaculus.py:121/159/187 (and :140 for POST), polymarket.py:111/132, kalshi.py:144/167 all interpolate question_id into the request path via unencoded f-strings. The input sources are exactly as cited: pages/2_Predict.py:155 (st.text_input "Question ID") and :179 (qid_from_url = url.rstrip("/").split("/")[-1]), with no format validation before reaching client.get_question. I ran httpx directly: a question_id of '../../evil' against base_url 'https://www.metaculus.com/api2' resolves to 'https://www.metaculus.com/evil/' (path traversal normalizes), and '?'/'#'/query chars alter the request target. So the described behavior is genuine.

However, as a SECURITY finding it does not materially matter, and the threat model collapses on inspection:

1. No trust boundary is crossed. This is a single-user, locally-run Streamlit app (pages/2_Predict.py). The same human types the Question ID/URL, supplies the API keys, and reads the output. Attacker and victim are identical. There is no second party who can inject a malicious question_id into another user's session — st.text_input is the operator's own keyboard. Crafting '../../evil' to reshape one's OWN request to another path on a public prediction-market API yields nothing the user couldn't get by typing that URL into a browser.

2. The host/scheme cannot be overridden. httpx pins scheme+host to base_url: 'http://evil.com/x' resolves to 'https://www.metaculus.com/api2/questions/http://evil.com/x/'. This is NOT SSRF and cannot redirect the request — including the Authorization token — to an attacker-controlled host. The credential only ever travels to the legitimate, trusted API host regardless of input. That eliminates the only path by which this could be a real vulnerability (credential exfiltration / SSRF).

3. Consequently there is no privilege escalation, no credential leak, no cross-user/cross-origin impact. The worst realistic outcome is a confusing 404 or a malformed request to the same trusted host.

The recommendation (validate per-platform ID format, urllib.parse.quote the path segment) is sound input-validation/robustness hygiene and worth doing, but it addresses code quality, not a security risk. Classifying this under the security dimension overstates it; even the 'low' severity is generous given attacker==victim and host-pinning. I mark real=false because the issue does not genuinely matter as a security concern; if retained at all it should be reclassified as a robustness/input-validation nit, not a security vulnerability.

### `SECU-60` — submit_prediction sends caller-supplied probability to a live external account with no validation
- **REFUTED ✗** · source: framed · effort: small · file: `/Users/bjgilbert/Projects/Zoltar/sigma-predict/src/platforms/metaculus.py:131-145`
- **evidence:** def submit_prediction(self, question_id, probability):
    if not self._config.metaculus_api_key: ... return False
    payload = {"prediction": probability}
    resp = self._client.post(f"/questions/{question_id}/predict/", json=payload)
This is a WRITE path to a live Metaculus account (the only implemented submit). probability is sent with no clamp to [0,1] and question_id is unencoded (same as above). In the UI flow predict(auto_submit=True) is invoked from an unauthenticated Streamlit button (2_Predict.py:77), so an unauthenticated web user can cause real predictions to be written to the operator's Metaculus account.
- **recommendation:** Clamp/validate probability to a sane open interval (e.g. [0.01,0.99]) and validate question_id before POSTing. Gate auto_submit behind authentication/explicit confirmation in the UI; do not allow an unauthenticated button press to write to a live external account.
- **verifier:** The finding's central claims are contradicted by the actual code.

1. "probability is caller-supplied" — FALSE for the cited UI path. In the auto_submit=True flow (pages/2_Predict.py:77 → orchestrator.predict → submit_after_review with HumanReview(reviewed=False)), human_adjustment is None, so orchestrator.py:424-425 sets final_prob = cal_prob, the model's own calibrated probability. No web user supplies this number. The only human-entered probability is the Adjust slider at 2_Predict.py:325-332, which Streamlit itself constrains to min_value=0.01/max_value=0.99, and that is NOT the auto_submit path.

2. "no clamp to [0,1]" — FALSE at the production call site. The orchestrator clamps the adjusted case explicitly: orchestrator.py:421-423 `final_prob = max(0.01, min(0.99, final_prob))`. The unadjusted case uses cal_prob, which has a dedicated validator gate validate_aggregation enforcing [0.01, 0.99] (validator.py:118-129, _PROB_MIN/_PROB_MAX). So the value reaching submit_prediction is bounded by design, regardless of submit_prediction itself not re-clamping.

3. submit_prediction only POSTs at all if METACULUS_API_KEY is set (metaculus.py:133-135), and the payload is a model-generated, calibrated, range-gated probability — not attacker-controlled data.

The residual valid concern is purely a deployment/auth observation: Streamlit has no built-in authentication and the Run Pipeline button triggers auto_submit=True, so anyone reaching the deployed app could trigger a write IF a key is configured. But that is a different finding than the one filed. The filed finding is titled and argued around "caller-supplied probability with no validation," which the code refutes — the submitted value is the system's own bounded model output. The probability-validation thesis that anchors severity and title is wrong, so the finding as written is not genuine.

The question_id unencoding note is real but trivial (IDs come from Metaculus API responses / user-fetched IDs, httpx builds the path) and is not the titled security issue.

### `SECU-61` — Audit log content hashing is non-keyed and includes raw result text length/hash but result text could still leak via params if a non-standard key holds a secret
- **confirmed ✓** · source: framed · effort: trivial · file: `/Users/bjgilbert/Projects/Zoltar/sigma-predict/src/pipeline/llm_audit.py:27-48`
- **evidence:** _SECRET_KEY_PATTERN = {'api_key','apikey','token','secret','password','auth'} and _structural_summary only redacts when a key name matches one of those substrings. The router passes params={'system': system, 'user': user, ...} (llm_router.py:219). The summary correctly logs only str(len) for these, and result is hashed not stored — good. The residual risk is narrow: any future param whose name does not contain a pattern substring but whose value is a secret would be summarized (only length for strings, so low leakage), and _hash is an unkeyed SHA-256 truncated to 16 hex chars, so identical prompts/results are linkable across log lines and short result strings are brute-forceable. This is a hardening note, not an active secret leak — the current design does not write raw prompts.
- **recommendation:** Document the redaction as name-based (defense in depth, not a guarantee) and consider an allowlist of loggable keys rather than a denylist. If linkability of hashes matters, use an HMAC with a per-run salt. Low priority given no raw-prompt logging today.
- **verifier:** The code at llm_audit.py:27-48 matches the audit's description precisely. Verified line-by-line: _SECRET_KEY_PATTERN is a substring-based denylist (L27, L34); strings are summarized as length only via str(len(value)) (L37); _hash is unkeyed SHA-256 truncated to 16 hex (L51-53); params_hash hashes the structural summary not raw params (L95); result_hash hashes raw result_text (L97) and result_len is stored (L98). The router call site (llm_router.py:219) passes exactly params={"system","user","temperature","max_tokens"} as claimed.

The finding is code-accurate and technically correct as a defense-in-depth/hardening observation. The denylist-over-allowlist critique is legitimate: a future param named something not matching the patterns whose value is a secret would be summarized rather than redacted (though only length leaks for strings). The unkeyed-hash linkability point is also factually true.

However, the finding is correctly self-limiting and arguably non-material as a *security vulnerability*: (1) There is no active leak. The only call site passes no secret values — system/user are prompt text (correctly length-summarized), temperature/max_tokens are scalars, and API keys never flow through params (they live in client construction). The "future param holds a secret" scenario is entirely hypothetical. (2) params_hash hashes the structural summary (e.g. "{system:str(412), user:str(1893), ...}"), not prompt content — so its linkability reveals nothing sensitive. (3) The brute-force concern applies only to result_hash, and result text is model prediction output, not secrets. The module docstring explicitly states raw prompt text is never written, and the code honors that.

The finding itself concedes "This is a hardening note, not an active secret leak" and "Low priority given no raw-prompt logging today." That candor is appropriate — but it means this is a hardening/documentation suggestion, not a security defect. It is real in the sense that the recommendation (allowlist + documenting redaction as name-based, optional HMAC for linkability) is sound, but it does not describe an exploitable condition in the current code.

### `SECU-62` — _load_prompt has no path-traversal guard; safe today only because persona.value is enum-controlled
- **confirmed ✓** · source: framed · effort: trivial · file: `/Users/bjgilbert/Projects/Zoltar/sigma-predict/src/pipeline/utils.py:30-33`
- **evidence:** def _load_prompt(config, filename):
    path = os.path.join(config.prompts_dir, filename)
    with open(path) as f:
        return f.read()
The only dynamic caller is forecaster.py:122 persona_path = f"personas/{persona.value}.md" where persona is a SearchPersona enum, so it is currently safe. But there is no normalization/containment check: if any future call path lets a user-controlled string reach `filename`, a value like '../../../../etc/passwd' would read outside prompts_dir. os.path.join with an absolute second arg would also escape entirely.
- **recommendation:** Resolve the joined path and assert it stays within config.prompts_dir (os.path.realpath startswith check), or restrict filename to a known allowlist. Cheap insurance against future misuse.
- **verifier:** Code at src/pipeline/utils.py:30-33 matches the cited evidence exactly: _load_prompt does os.path.join(config.prompts_dir, filename) and opens it with no normalization or containment check. The technical claims are all correct: os.path.join with an absolute second arg escapes the base dir, and a '../' filename would traverse outside prompts_dir.

The finding is honest and accurate about scope. I verified all callers via grep: src/pipeline/decomposer.py:41, base_rate.py:39, and forecaster.py:118-119 pass hardcoded string literals. The only dynamic caller is forecaster.py:124 via persona_path = f"personas/{persona.value}.md" (line 122), where the persona parameter is typed SearchPersona (forecaster.py:95). SearchPersona (src/models.py:38) is a str+Enum with 5 fixed values (academic, news, contrarian, domain_expert, meta_forecaster). So there is NO present attack path — persona.value cannot be an arbitrary string, and nothing else is user-controlled. The finding explicitly acknowledges this ("currently safe").

Verdict: the issue genuinely exists as described (a missing defensive guard), but it carries zero current exploitability. It is a defense-in-depth/hardening observation about a hypothetical future caller, not a live vulnerability. The author's own framing ("cheap insurance against future misuse") and the low severity are honest and proportionate. I cannot refute the code facts, so real=true — but the materiality is genuinely marginal: it is closer to informational/hardening than to an actionable security defect, because exploitation requires a code change that does not yet exist.

### `SECU-64` — Over-broad except Exception on every platform read masks auth/schema failures as benign empty results
- **REFUTED ✗** · source: framed · effort: small · file: `/Users/bjgilbert/Projects/Zoltar/sigma-predict/src/platforms/kalshi.py:134-136`
- **evidence:** except Exception as exc:
    logger.warning("Kalshi list events failed: %s", exc)
    return []
Same pattern in metaculus.py:114-116, polymarket.py:104-106, and all get_community_prediction methods. A 401/403 (e.g. unauthenticated read because the API key env var was unset — auth fails open at kalshi.py:88-90 / polymarket.py:67-69 / metaculus.py:67-69) is swallowed and returned as an empty list with only a warning. Downstream the pipeline proceeds on missing/zero community signal with no error signal, silently contaminating forecasts. From a security standpoint this hides authentication failures and tampering (a hostile endpoint returning malformed data degrades to 'no data').
- **recommendation:** Distinguish auth/HTTP-status errors (4xx/5xx) from genuine empty result sets and surface auth failures loudly (raise or set an explicit error flag the pipeline records). Avoid catching bare Exception around the whole call; catch httpx errors specifically and let programming errors propagate.
- **verifier:** The finding's core evidence is factually wrong, which undermines the whole claim. It asserts that "except Exception as exc: ... return []" is the only handler and that "A 401/403 ... is swallowed and returned as an empty list with only a warning." But the actual code at every leading cited location places a dedicated `except httpx.HTTPStatusError` clause IMMEDIATELY BEFORE the `except Exception`:
- kalshi.py:131-132 (before the cited 134-136): `except httpx.HTTPStatusError as exc: logger.warning("Kalshi list events HTTP %s: %s", exc.response.status_code, exc)`
- metaculus.py:111-112 (before cited 114-116) and polymarket.py:101-102 (before cited 104-106): identical pattern.
A 401/403 from an unauthenticated read raises httpx.HTTPStatusError (after resp.raise_for_status()), so it is caught by the specific handler and logged WITH its status code — not swallowed by the bare Exception. The audit literally quotes only the fallback clause and omits the status-code handler sitting one line above it, then builds its entire argument on that omission. The get_question/get_resolution paths go further and re-raise as ValueError on HTTPStatusError (kalshi:149-151, metaculus:124-126, polymarket:114-116), so those propagate loudly rather than failing silent.

The recommendation ("catch httpx errors specifically rather than bare Exception around the whole call; distinguish 4xx/5xx") is already implemented for exactly the primary list/fetch paths the finding leads with. So the headline recommendation is moot.

Partial truth that survives: get_community_prediction (kalshi:175, polymarket:141, metaculus:190) and metaculus get_resolution (162) DO use a single bare `except Exception` without a preceding HTTPStatusError branch, returning None. And the auth-fails-open observation is technically accurate — config.py:88-90/67-69/67-69 only add Authorization if a key is present, so a missing key yields an unauthenticated request. However: (a) these are read-only price/median fetches where None-on-failure is graceful degradation, not a security exposure; (b) the "tampering / hostile endpoint" framing is speculative and the malformed-data→no-data direction is fail-safe, not fail-open in the security sense; (c) the leading evidence and recommendation are simply incorrect for the bulk of the cited locations.

Because the finding's primary evidence is demonstrably false against the code (the httpx.HTTPStatusError handler the audit claims is absent is present and logs status codes), and only a minor, low-impact remnant is accurate, the finding as written does not hold. Default to real=false.

### `QUAL-69` — apply_hedging_correction is dead production code, kept alive only by its own test
- **confirmed ✓** · source: framed · effort: trivial · file: `/Users/bjgilbert/Projects/Zoltar/sigma-predict/src/pipeline/calibration.py:103-114`
- **evidence:** grep across src/ and scripts/ shows zero production callers of `apply_hedging_correction`; calibrate() (lines 82,92) exclusively uses `apply_hedging_correction_scaled`. The only references are in tests/test_calibration.py:7,20-36 which test the orphaned function. It is superseded by the scaled variant (117-130) per the module docstring (line 3: 'apply scaled hedging correction').
- **recommendation:** Delete `apply_hedging_correction` and its tests, or if it is a documented public API for external callers, mark it deprecated. As-is it is unscaled dead code that a maintainer could mistakenly wire back in.
- **verifier:** I attempted to refute this finding and could not. Every factual claim is confirmed by the code at /Users/bjgilbert/Projects/Zoltar/sigma-predict/src/pipeline/calibration.py.

Confirmed facts:
- apply_hedging_correction is defined at lines 103-114 (unscaled: applies a flat shift regardless of distance from 0.5).
- calibrate() at lines 82 and 92 exclusively calls apply_hedging_correction_scaled (the distance-weighted variant at lines 117-130), never the unscaled version.
- A grep across the whole repo for `apply_hedging_correction(` excluding the _scaled variant and tests returns only the function's own definition line. There are zero production callers.
- The only other references are in tests/test_calibration.py (import at line 7, exercised at lines 20-36).
- The module docstring (line 3) describes Phase 0 as 'apply scaled hedging correction', and the calibrate() docstring (line 50) confirms Phase 0 uses 'scaled hedging correction' — so the design intent is the scaled variant, superseding the unscaled one.

Refutation attempts that failed:
- Not a documented public API: no __init__.py or __all__ exports it; it is a bare module-level function with no deprecation marker. So it is not an intentional external-caller surface.
- Not used in a fallback branch: even the isotonic fallback (line 82) uses the scaled variant.

So the function is genuinely orphaned production code kept alive only by tests that test it in isolation. This is a real, accurately-described quality issue.

Severity: the claimed 'low' is correct and should be kept. The dead function is small (12 lines), pure, side-effect-free, and harmless at runtime. The only real risk is a maintainer mistakenly wiring the unscaled variant back in — which is a low-probability, low-impact maintainability concern, not a correctness or behavioral bug. No raise or lower warranted.
