### `QUAL-71` — router parameter is untyped across four pipeline modules
- **confirmed ✓** · source: framed · effort: small · file: `/Users/bjgilbert/Projects/Zoltar/sigma-predict/src/pipeline/forecaster.py:99`
- **evidence:** `router=None` appears as a bare untyped default in forecaster.py:99, base_rate.py:25, decomposer.py:27, and deliberation.py:69 — no annotation despite every other parameter in these signatures being typed and the modules using `from __future__ import annotations`. The actual type is LLMRouter, imported lazily inside the body (e.g. forecaster.py:114-115) specifically to dodge a circular import. The lazy import is fine, but the missing annotation defeats type checking on the most-passed object in the pipeline.
- **recommendation:** Add `router: "LLMRouter | None" = None` (string/forward-ref annotation, which `from __future__ import annotations` makes free of runtime import cost) and a `TYPE_CHECKING` import of LLMRouter. verification.py and the orchestrator already import LLMRouter at module top, confirming no true cycle for type-only use.
- **verifier:** All factual claims verified against the code. The `router=None` parameter is untyped at exactly the four cited locations: forecaster.py:99, base_rate.py:25, decomposer.py:27, deliberation.py:69. In each signature every other parameter is annotated (e.g. forecaster `model_id: str | None`, `provider: str | None`; base_rate/decomposer `warnings_out: list[str] | None`), making `router` the lone untyped param. All four modules declare `from __future__ import annotations`. The lazy in-body import of LLMRouter to dodge a circular import is confirmed (forecaster.py:114-115, base_rate.py:36, decomposer.py:38) and identical in pattern. The recommendation's premise checks out: verification.py:17 and orchestrator.py:42 import LLMRouter at module top, and verification.py already uses typed `router: LLMRouter` params — and crucially llm_router.py imports none of these four modules (only config and llm_audit), so a TYPE_CHECKING import creates no runtime cycle. Net effect: static type checkers see `router` as implicit `Any`, so all `router.cross_verify()/challenge()/predict()` calls in these stages go unchecked despite verification.py demonstrating the type is known. Real, but cosmetic/maintainability with zero runtime impact and a trivial fix. The 'most-passed object in the pipeline' phrasing is unverifiable rhetoric but does not affect the substance.

### `QUAL-72` — Defensive guards that are structurally impossible mask real intent
- **confirmed ✓** · source: framed · effort: trivial · file: `/Users/bjgilbert/Projects/Zoltar/sigma-predict/src/pipeline/orchestrator.py:274`
- **evidence:** `n_providers = len(set(r.provider for r in valid_runs if hasattr(r, "provider")))` — RunResult always sets `provider` (forecaster.py:230, and it is a declared model field), so `hasattr(r, "provider")` is always True. The guard is dead weight that implies provider might be absent. Compounding it: extremization is gated on `n_distinct_providers >= 2` (aggregator.py:91), but the default single-model multi-run configuration produces exactly one provider, so extremization is silently never applied in the common case — a behavioral surprise hidden behind a no-op guard.
- **recommendation:** Drop the `hasattr` guard (line 274). Separately, document or revisit the extremization gate: with a single primary model, extremization is dead by design — make that explicit in config/docs rather than letting it silently disable via a provider count.
- **verifier:** Both factual claims are confirmed against the code, so I cannot refute the finding.

1. hasattr guard (orchestrator.py:274): RunResult is a Pydantic BaseModel (models.py:92) with `provider: str = ""` as a declared field with a default (models.py:95). On a Pydantic instance, a declared field is always present, so `hasattr(r, "provider")` is invariantly True. The guard is genuinely dead weight. Confirmed.

2. Extremization gate (aggregator.py:91 requires `n_distinct_providers >= 2`): The orchestrator's run loop (lines 207-228) calls run_forecast WITHOUT a provider argument, and forecaster.py:111 defaults to `config.primary_model.provider`. Every run in a multi-run forecast therefore shares one provider, so `n_distinct_providers` computed at orchestrator.py:274-275 is always 1 in the standard pipeline. Verification runs append to record.verification.stages, not record.runs, so they don't add providers to valid_runs. Extremization is thus unreachable in the default single-model multi-run configuration. Confirmed.

Critical caveats that temper the finding:
- The title bundles two loosely-related issues. The hasattr half is purely cosmetic (zero behavioral impact, trivial cleanup). The substantive half — silently-dead extremization — is acknowledged by the audit itself to be only 'compounded by' the guard, not caused by it. The framing slightly oversells the connection.
- The extremization gate is arguably correct design, not a bug: extremizing correlated same-model runs would be statistically unsound (extremization assumes independent information sources). So the gate sensibly disables extremization when only one provider is present. The genuine gap is transparency/documentation — the default method is named 'extremized_trimmed_mean' yet never extremizes single-model — not a correctness defect.

Net: real and worth a cleanup + a doc note, but it is the lowest tier of finding. The recommendation (drop the guard; document the extremization gate) is sound.

### `QUAL-73` — estimate_cost silently defaults unknown models to Sonnet pricing
- **confirmed ✓** · source: framed · effort: small · file: `/Users/bjgilbert/Projects/Zoltar/sigma-predict/src/pipeline/llm_router.py:397-411`
- **evidence:** The pricing table is hardcoded with 'approximate, March 2026' rates (line 396) and `rates = pricing.get(model, (3.0, 15.0))  # default to Sonnet pricing` (409). Any model not in the dict — including the config-default `gpt-5.1`/`gemini-3.1` if renamed, or any Ollama model — is silently costed at Claude Sonnet rates with no warning. Cost tracking (record.cost.total_cost_usd) is reported to the user and CLI as authoritative; for unlisted models it is quietly wrong. The default Anthropic model 'claude-sonnet-4-6' is also hardcoded twice (llm_router.py:39 and 98).
- **recommendation:** Log a warning on the pricing fallback so unpriced models are visible, and source the pricing table and default model from Config (single source of truth) rather than two literals. Consider returning a (cost, is_estimate) signal so downstream display can mark approximate costs.
- **verifier:** Every factual claim in the finding is confirmed against the code. llm_router.py:409 is verbatim `rates = pricing.get(model, (3.0, 15.0))  # default to Sonnet pricing`, the pricing dict (397-408) carries the "approximate, March 2026" comment, and no warning is emitted on the fallback (the only logger.warning calls in the file are at lines 123/264/314/352, none in estimate_cost). The cost propagates as authoritative: forecaster.py:252 writes router.estimate_cost(...) into total_cost_usd, and human_review.py:177 prints it to the user as `Cost: ${record.cost.total_cost_usd:.4f}`. The double-hardcoded default model is confirmed (llm_router.py:39 and 98), and it is actually triple-sourced — config.py:77 and 93 also hardcode "claude-sonnet-4-6", reinforcing the single-source-of-truth recommendation.

The strongest concrete instance — which the finding understates — is the Ollama/local-model case. The module docstring references "10 Ollama-backed models" and config.py:102-105 enables verification providers (llama, gemma, nemotron, deepseek, qwen, devstral, glm, kimi) whose model IDs are absent from the pricing dict. estimate_cost is invoked for these via the audit path (llm_router.py:214). Free local inference would be silently billed at Sonnet's $3/$15-per-million rates — not merely imprecise but a fabricated nonzero cost for zero-cost calls. This is a current, reachable defect, not hypothetical.

Two caveats on the finding's framing, neither of which defeats it: (1) the gpt-5.1/gemini-3.1 "if renamed" scenario is speculative — those IDs are presently in the dict, so the default happy path prices correctly; (2) the cost is an explicitly approximate internal estimate that gates no decision logic. These bound materiality but do not refute the issue, because the Ollama instance is concrete and reachable today.

I could not refute the finding; it is code-grounded and the core mechanism (silent Sonnet-rate fallback with no warning, plus duplicated literals) is real.

### `QUAL-74` — _estimate_effective_n is a self-described heuristic with magic constants producing non-obvious output
- **confirmed ✓** · source: framed · effort: medium · file: `/Users/bjgilbert/Projects/Zoltar/sigma-predict/src/pipeline/aggregator.py:153-180`
- **evidence:** The docstring/comments label it 'Simple heuristic' / 'rough estimate' / 'rough heuristic'. `expected_var_single = mean_p * (1 - mean_p) * 0.1` uses an unexplained 0.1, and `int(round(n * min(1.0, ratio * n)))` multiplies a variance ratio by n inside another n — for moderate spread this can saturate effective_n to n or collapse it to 1 with no smooth middle. There is no test pinning the intended behavior. It feeds the reported effective_n but no decision logic, so impact is presentational.
- **recommendation:** Either replace with a defensible estimator (e.g. 1 + (n-1)*(1 - mean_pairwise_correlation_proxy)) and document the derivation, or rename to `_effective_n_indicator` and clearly mark it as a non-statistical display heuristic so no one builds a gate on it.
- **verifier:** I attempted to refute this finding by reading the code and exercising the function, and could not. Every technical claim is confirmed at /Users/bjgilbert/Projects/Zoltar/sigma-predict/src/pipeline/aggregator.py:153-180.

CONFIRMED claims:
1. Self-described heuristic with magic constant: the docstring/comments literally say "Simple heuristic" (line 163), "rough heuristic" (line 170), "rough estimate" (line 171), and expected_var_single = mean_p*(1-mean_p)*0.1 uses an unexplained 0.1 (line 171).
2. Odd math: line 176 computes effective_n = max(1, min(n, int(round(n * min(1.0, ratio*n))))), multiplying a variance ratio by n inside another n.
3. Saturate-or-collapse with no smooth middle: I ran the function. Tight-agreement inputs ([0.60,0.62,0.58,0.61,0.59]) collapse to effective_n=1; any moderate spread already saturates to n (moderate spread [0.5..0.7] -> 5, wide spread -> 5). A 200k-sample sweep for n=3..7 returned n ~99% of the time; intermediate values (2,3,...,n-1) are statistical noise. The behavior is effectively binary, even more degenerate than the finding states.
4. No test pins behavior: tests/test_aggregator.py only asserts the trivial n<=1 cases (effective_n <= 1, == 1). No test exercises the non-trivial estimator.

ONE INACCURACY, but it cuts AGAINST the finding (understatement, not overstatement): the finding says "no decision logic, so impact is presentational." That is false. src/review/human_review.py:348 gates a human-review flag: `if record.aggregation.effective_n < 3 and len(record.runs) >= 3:` -> "Low effective N — runs may be correlated". So effective_n DOES feed decision logic. Worse, because the estimator returns n ~99% of the time for any spread, this gate is effectively dead: with >=3 runs it almost never fires unless runs are near-identical. A correlation-warning flag that silently never fires for genuinely spread (and thus genuinely independent-looking, but possibly correlated) runs is a real, if low-consequence, quality defect.

The issue genuinely exists and matters: it is a non-statistical, untested heuristic with a near-binary output that feeds a human-review gate, and the audit even understated its reach.

### `QUAL-75` — Confidence interval mixes calibrated center with uncalibrated dispersion
- **confirmed ✓** · source: framed · effort: medium · file: `/Users/bjgilbert/Projects/Zoltar/sigma-predict/src/pipeline/orchestrator.py:347-349`
- **evidence:** `stdev = aggregation.stdev` is the RAW pre-calibration run stdev. The CI is then built as `cal_prob ± 1.96 * stdev` around the CALIBRATED probability (lines 348-349), with a hardcoded ±0.05 fallback for single runs (stdev==0). This is internally inconsistent: the center has been transformed by Platt/isotonic/hedging but the half-width has not, so after a strong calibration shift the interval can be asymmetric-in-meaning or extend past the calibrated point's plausible range. The 1.96 also implies a normal-approximation that does not hold near 0/1.
- **recommendation:** Either propagate stdev through the same calibration transform (compute CI endpoints by calibrating raw_aggregate ± 1.96*stdev), or compute the interval on the logit scale and transform back. At minimum, document that the CI is a heuristic display band, not a calibrated credible interval.
- **verifier:** I tried to refute this and could not — every technical claim is confirmed by the code at the cited location.

VERIFIED facts (source: orchestrator.py:339-354, aggregator.py:77, calibration.py:62-100, human_review.py:140):
1. stdev = aggregation.stdev (orchestrator.py:347) is the RAW pre-calibration dispersion: aggregator.py:77 computes it as np.std(probabilities, ddof=1) over the raw per-model probabilities. It is never passed through calibrate().
2. cal_prob is the CALIBRATED center: calibrate(probability=aggregation.raw_aggregate, ...) applies Platt scaling, isotonic-on-logit, or scaled hedging (calibration.py:62-100) — all genuinely nonlinear transforms that move the point.
3. The CI is literally cal_prob ± 1.96*stdev with a hardcoded ±0.05 fallback when stdev==0 (orchestrator.py:348-349). So the center is transformed and the half-width is not — the internal-inconsistency claim is exactly correct.
4. The normal-approximation-near-0/1 critique is also valid; the max(0.01,...)/min(0.99,...) clamps are a symptom of the interval running past plausible range, not a principled fix.

The fix recommendation is sound: calibrate the endpoints (calibrate(raw_aggregate ± 1.96*stdev)) or work on the logit scale. There is currently no comment/docstring at 347-349 and the display label (human_review.py:140) just says "Confidence interval" with no heuristic caveat, so the "at minimum, document" fallback is also unaddressed.

Materiality / why this is genuinely LOW (not higher): the confidence_interval is consumed in exactly one place — display in human_review.py:140. grep shows it is NOT used in any sizing, betting, submission, or gating decision. So the inconsistency misleads a human reader of the review output but does not corrupt any automated downstream computation. Low severity is correct.

This is a genuine issue, correctly scoped at low severity.

### `QUAL-76` — _infer_domain uses naive first-match substring keyword matching
- **confirmed ✓** · source: framed · effort: small · file: `/Users/bjgilbert/Projects/Zoltar/sigma-predict/src/pipeline/orchestrator.py:507-523`
- **evidence:** `if any(kw in text for kw in keywords)` does unbounded substring matching: the 'ai' keyword (line 511) matches 'air', 'campaign', 'maintain', 'detail', 'said', etc., and dict iteration is first-match-wins so 'geopolitics' (checked first) can claim a question that is really economics. Domain is metadata used by get_recent_in_domain reference-class lookup, so a misclassification routes calibration/recency context to the wrong domain bucket.
- **recommendation:** Match on word boundaries (`re.search(r"\b" + re.escape(kw) + r"\b", text)`) and either score all domains and take the max-hit-count, or have the decomposer LLM emit the domain directly (it already produces structured output). Low stakes but trivially wrong today.
- **verifier:** CONFIRMED at the code level, with a correction to the impact claim.

CORE TECHNICAL DEFECT — VERIFIED. orchestrator.py:519-522 builds `text = (question.title + " " + tags).lower()` and does `if any(kw in text for kw in keywords)` — unbounded substring matching. I executed the example: the "ai" keyword (line 511) matches "campaign", "maintain", "air", "detail", and "said". So an unrelated political/military question containing "campaign" or "maintain" gets classified "technology". First-match-wins over dict insertion order (line 520) is also real: geopolitics is iterated before economics, so a dual-topic question can be claimed by the earlier bucket. The recommendation (word-boundary regex `\b...\b`, score-and-max, or let the decomposer LLM emit the domain — which already returns structured output via decomposer.py) is correct and apt.

CLAIMED IMPACT — OVERSTATED / PARTIALLY REFUTED. The evidence asserts misclassification "routes calibration/recency context to the wrong domain bucket" through `get_recent_in_domain`. That wiring does not exist in the current code. I grep'd all of src/: `get_recent_in_domain` (query.py:94) has NO production caller — only its own definition and one unit test (tests/test_registry.py:140). `get_brier_decomposition_by_domain` (query.py:127) likewise has no caller in src/. Critically, the "reference class" that actually drives the live prediction pipeline (base_rate.py, forecaster.py:146, validator.py reference_class_instances_gate, verification.py) is `decomposition.reference_class` — an LLM-produced free-text string set at orchestrator.py:165-178 — entirely independent of the inferred `domain` field. The inferred domain (orchestrator.py:120) is currently persisted only as record metadata; nothing in the active prediction path reads it to route calibration or recency context. So the real present-day consequence is mislabeled metadata, not contaminated calibration/reference-class routing. It becomes a real functional bug only when get_by_domain/get_recent_in_domain are actually consumed — a latent risk, not a live one.

NET: The matching logic is genuinely and trivially wrong (confirmed by execution), so the finding is real. But the severity rationale leans on a downstream consequence that isn't wired up yet.

### `QUAL-77` — Repeated ad-hoc question-text assembly instead of a single helper
- **REFUTED ✗** · source: framed · effort: small · file: `/Users/bjgilbert/Projects/Zoltar/sigma-predict/src/pipeline/orchestrator.py:136,215`
- **evidence:** The orchestrator builds question text three different ways inline: `question.title + "\n" + question.description` (136), `question.title + " " + question.description[:200]` (215), and `(question.title + " " + " ".join(question.tags)).lower()` (519). forecaster.py:132-138 and decomposer.py:43-59 each build their own full prompt block from the same PlatformQuestion fields. There is no canonical 'question as text' accessor, so the truncation length (200), separator, and field set drift per call site.
- **recommendation:** Add a method/property on PlatformQuestion (e.g. `as_prompt_context()` and `as_search_seed(max_desc=200)`) so the field selection and truncation are defined once. Reduces drift and is the natural place to later add the prompt-injection delimiting the platforms audit flagged.
- **verifier:** All four cited code sites exist exactly as claimed (orchestrator.py:136, 215, 519; forecaster.py:132-138; decomposer.py:43-59). However, the finding's central premise — that these are "the same question-text assembly" drifting across call sites — does not hold up under inspection. They are four DIFFERENT operations on DIFFERENT field sets for DIFFERENT consumers:

1. Line 136: title + description (newline) → a verification/base-rate context string.
2. Line 215: title + description[:200] (space) → a SEARCH QUERY SEED, deliberately truncated because it feeds a search engine, not a prompt.
3. Line 519: title + tags (lowercased) → a keyword bag for domain CLASSIFICATION; uses tags, not description at all.
4. forecaster.py / decomposer.py: full structured markdown prompt blocks with many labeled fields (**Title:**, **Description:**, **Resolution Criteria:**, etc.). These two are not even identical to each other — decomposer includes fine_print and tags; forecaster includes resolution_criteria, close/resolve dates, and omits fine_print/tags.

So the recommended single accessor (`as_prompt_context()`) could not serve these consumers without parameterization that would reintroduce the very divergence it claims to eliminate. The "truncation length, separator, and field set drift per call site" framing misreads intentional differences as accidental drift: the 200-char truncation is correct and necessary for a search query but would be wrong for an LLM prompt.

Additionally, the "repeated ad-hoc assembly" claim is weakened by the fact that line 136's result is assigned to `question_text_full` and reused across five verification call sites (154, 163, 193, 239, 302) — it is computed once, not re-assembled. A repo-wide grep found no other proliferation beyond the cited sites.

There is a small genuine kernel: sites 1 and 2 both combine title+description with inline separators/magic-200, so a tiny `as_search_seed()` helper would de-duplicate one real magic value. But that is a marginal nit, not the multi-site drift hazard the finding describes. The finding overstates by conflating four distinct, purpose-appropriate representations into one "drifting pattern."

### `CORR-83` — Confidence interval mixes calibrated center with uncalibrated dispersion
- **confirmed ✓** · source: framed · effort: medium · file: `src/pipeline/orchestrator.py:347-349`
- **evidence:** `stdev = aggregation.stdev` is the standard deviation of the RAW pre-calibration run probabilities (aggregator.py:77). The CI is then `cal_prob +/- 1.96*stdev` (lines 348-349) where cal_prob is the post-calibration probability. Calibration (Platt/isotonic/hedging) is a nonlinear logit-space transform that compresses or expands dispersion, so applying the raw-scale stdev around the transformed center produces an interval that is neither the raw nor the calibrated uncertainty. For a single run, stdev is 0 and the CI falls back to an arbitrary +/-0.05. The interval is stored as confidence_interval and shown to the human reviewer, implying more rigor than it has.
- **recommendation:** Either propagate the raw runs through the same calibration transform to derive a calibrated-scale CI, or relabel/document the interval explicitly as a heuristic raw-dispersion band. At minimum note that single-run +/-0.05 is a placeholder, not a computed interval.
- **verifier:** Confirmed against the code; all factual claims hold. orchestrator.py:347-349 computes `stdev = aggregation.stdev` then `ci = cal_prob +/- 1.96*stdev`. aggregator.py:61,77 confirm stdev is np.std(ddof=1) of the RAW run probabilities (r.probability), computed before any calibration. calibrate() (calibration.py:38-100) is invoked on `aggregation.raw_aggregate` and returns cal_prob via one of three NONLINEAR transforms: Platt (1/(1+exp(-(a*logit(p)+b))), line 215), isotonic interpolation on the logit scale (lines 188-204), or scaled hedging where the shift is distance-weighted from 0.5 (lines 124-129). None of these preserve raw-scale dispersion, so a symmetric raw-stdev band around the transformed center is statistically inconsistent — it represents neither the raw nor the calibrated uncertainty. The single-run case is also confirmed: aggregator.py:64-72 sets stdev=0.0 for n==1, triggering the hardcoded +/-0.05 fallback at orchestrator.py:348-349, which is an arbitrary placeholder, not a computed interval. The result is stored as CalibrationData.confidence_interval (orchestrator.py:354) and surfaced to the reviewer, implying more rigor than it has. The critique is correct and well-grounded. I attempted to refute it but could not: the only mitigations are the hard [0.01,0.99] clamp and the fact that the interval is display-only — it does not feed threshold/submission/aggregation decision logic. Those limit blast radius but do not invalidate the finding.

### `CORR-85` — Platform community_prediction has no [0,1] validation; scale heuristic can silently mislabel
- **confirmed ✓** · source: framed · effort: small · file: `src/platforms/kalshi.py:25-31,171-174; src/platforms/metaculus.py:33-37`
- **evidence:** Kalshi coerces price then applies `val/100.0 if val>1.0 else val` (lines 30,174). A legitimately-valued price of exactly 1.0 cent is treated as probability 1.0 (not 0.01), and any value already in [0,1] from an unexpected field bypasses the /100 scaling. Metaculus _extract_community_prediction returns `float(data['community_prediction']['full']['q2'])` (line 36) with no [0,1] clamp. These values are written into platform_signals and compared against the model's probability in human review, and PlatformQuestion.community_prediction is a plain float|None with no pydantic constraint (models.py). A scale mismatch produces a wrong-but-plausible community probability with no error.
- **recommendation:** Clamp community predictions to [0,1] after extraction and log a warning when a value falls outside expected bounds before scaling, rather than relying on the >1.0 heuristic. Add a pydantic ge=0/le=1 constraint or validator on community_prediction.
- **verifier:** Verified against source. All cited code claims are accurate:

- Kalshi src/platforms/kalshi.py:30 and :174 both apply `val / 100.0 if val > 1.0 else val`. Because the condition is strictly `> 1.0`, a price of exactly 1.0 (1 cent) stays 1.0 and is interpreted as probability 1.0 (100%) instead of 0.01 — a genuine off-by-boundary inversion. Real, though narrow (a 1-cent market is a near-resolved "No" contract).
- Metaculus src/platforms/metaculus.py:36 returns `float(data['community_prediction']['full']['q2'])` with no [0,1] clamp, and _extract_community_prediction is called unconditionally for all question types at line 54 (including QuestionType.NUMERIC). For continuous questions q2 is a normalized location in the value range, not a probability, yet it is stored as community_prediction and later compared against a probability. Legitimate semantic concern.
- src/models.py:245 confirms community_prediction is a plain `float | None = None` with no pydantic ge/le constraint or validator.
- The value is material to downstream behavior: src/review/human_review.py:149-151 displays each platform price to the reviewer, and line 341 computes `abs(cal_p - community) > 0.15` to raise a "Diverges from community" flag. A scale-mismatched community value silently corrupts both the displayed comparison and the auto-flag, with no error raised anywhere (the try/except blocks only catch parse/HTTP errors, not out-of-range values).

Weaknesses in the finding: (1) The "any value already in [0,1] from an unexpected field bypasses /100" scenario is partly speculative for Kalshi — its API returns integer cents, so in practice only the exact 1.0 boundary is misread; 0 correctly stays 0. (2) The most dangerous case (1.0 boundary) is rare. (3) There is also a latent key-mismatch the finding doesn't mention: the divergence check keys on "metaculus_community" (line 340) but the orchestrator writes the key as question.platform.value = "metaculus" by default (orchestrator.py:551), so the auto-flag may not even fire — but the display path still surfaces the wrong value, so the core "wrong-but-plausible value reaches human review with no error" claim still holds.

Net: a genuine silent correctness/mislabeling risk in a human-in-the-loop comparison value, on edge cases. The recommendation (clamp to [0,1], warn on out-of-bounds before scaling, add a pydantic validator) is sound and would catch both the 1-cent boundary and the continuous-q2 case.

### `CORR-86` — Mutable Aggregation reused after deliberation; raw_aggregate clamped but stdev/agreement may be stale post-deliberation
- **confirmed ✓** · source: framed · effort: medium · file: `src/pipeline/orchestrator.py:319-330; src/pipeline/deliberation.py:151-152`
- **evidence:** deliberate() returns the SAME aggregation object with only deliberation_result mutated in place (deliberation.py:151 `aggregation.deliberation_result = deliberation_result; return runs, aggregation`); it explicitly does not recompute probabilities or stdev ('runs unchanged'). The orchestrator then reassigns `record.aggregation = ...; aggregation = record.aggregation` (lines 326-330) and proceeds to calibrate on `aggregation.raw_aggregate` and build the CI from `aggregation.stdev`. Because deliberation can be triggered precisely by high disagreement (low inter_model_agreement / high stdev, deliberation.py:40), the system flags high disagreement, runs a supervisor call, then calibrates on the unchanged aggregate and dispersion anyway — the deliberation has no effect on the numeric forecast, only on a stored note. Not a crash, but the deliberation stage does not do what its trigger implies.
- **recommendation:** Document explicitly that deliberation is advisory-only and does not alter the aggregate, or have it return a possibly-revised aggregate (e.g. widen CI or moderate toward base rate when recommended_action=='investigate_further'). As written the stage spends an LLM call with no forecast impact.
- **verifier:** Verified against source; every load-bearing claim holds. deliberation.py:151 mutates only `aggregation.deliberation_result` in place and returns the same Aggregation object with runs explicitly unchanged (docstring line 84; no probability/stdev recomputation in the function body). orchestrator.py:326-330 reassigns record.aggregation/aggregation then calibrates on the untouched `aggregation.raw_aggregate` (line 340) and builds the CI from the untouched `aggregation.stdev` (lines 347-349). Deliberation is triggered precisely by high disagreement (inter_model_agreement < 0.6, deliberation.py:40), so the system flags high disagreement, spends a supervisor LLM call, and then calibrates on the unchanged aggregate and dispersion — no numeric forecast impact. I tried to refute via "it is intentionally advisory and surfaced to a human": that defense fails because grep shows the only consumer of the deliberation output (src/review/human_review.py:124) prints only the boolean `deliberation_triggered` flag, never the deliberation_result content. Furthermore `recommended_action` ("accept_aggregate"|"investigate_further") is parsed but has no consumer anywhere in src/ — even an explicit "investigate_further" recommendation does nothing. So the substantive LLM output is computed and discarded. The issue is genuine and material as a wasted-call / dead-output / trigger-semantics-mismatch defect.

### `CORR-87` — Dead code: unscaled apply_hedging_correction is never used
- **REFUTED ✗** · source: framed · effort: trivial · file: `src/pipeline/calibration.py:103-114`
- **evidence:** grep for `apply_hedging_correction\b` across src/ and scripts/ returns only the definition at calibration.py:103; all call sites (lines 82, 92) use apply_hedging_correction_scaled. The unscaled version with a flat additive shift is fully superseded and unreachable.
- **recommendation:** Delete apply_hedging_correction to avoid future confusion about which hedging policy is active.
- **verifier:** REFUTED. The finding's central claims — "never used," "fully superseded and unreachable," "dead code" — are false. The evidence was gathered with an incomplete grep (src/ and scripts/ only, explicitly excluding tests/). A full search shows apply_hedging_correction is imported at tests/test_calibration.py:7 and exercised by 5 active unit tests (TestHedgingCorrection, lines 18-37: test_pushes_high_higher, test_pushes_low_lower, test_no_change_at_center, test_clamps_high, test_clamps_low). I ran them: 5 passed in 0.37s. So the function is reached on every test run — it is not unreachable and not dead.\n\nThe only true sub-claim is narrow: the production calibrate() path never calls the unscaled version — both call sites (calibration.py:82 and :92) use apply_hedging_correction_scaled. But that is "not used in production," which is materially weaker than the filed claim of dead/unreachable code.\n\nMost importantly, the recommendation ('Delete apply_hedging_correction') is actively harmful as written: deleting the function would break the import at tests/test_calibration.py:7 and cause 5 test failures, breaking the test suite. The finding presents a build-breaking action as a safe cleanup, justified by an incomplete search whose conclusion does not hold once tests/ is included.

### `CORR-88` — estimate_cost silently defaults unknown models to Sonnet pricing
- **confirmed ✓** · source: framed · effort: small · file: `src/pipeline/llm_router.py:396-409`
- **evidence:** The pricing table is hardcoded with approximate rates and estimate_cost falls back to Sonnet pricing for any model not in the table (line 409 per repo map). For non-listed models (e.g. external verification providers, future model IDs), CostTracking.total_cost_usd is computed with wrong rates and no error or warning, so reported per-prediction cost (surfaced in CLI summaries and the UI) is silently inaccurate.
- **recommendation:** Log a warning when a model is not found in the pricing table and/or mark the cost as estimated/unknown rather than silently substituting Sonnet rates.
- **verifier:** Confirmed from code at src/pipeline/llm_router.py:409: `rates = pricing.get(model, (3.0, 15.0))  # default to Sonnet pricing`. The fallback is silent — no log, no warning, no estimated/unknown flag. The pricing table (lines 397-408) is hardcoded with exact-model-ID keys and a "approximate, March 2026" comment.

The cost does surface to users: estimate_cost feeds CostTracking.total_cost_usd (forecaster.py:252), which is printed in CLI summaries (orchestrator.py:375, :456) and the human-review console (human_review.py:177). So an inaccurate rate would be visibly reported. The audit-log path (llm_router.py:214) also calls estimate_cost for every dispatched call.

The finding's mechanism is real. Two refinements on materiality, both of which I checked rather than assumed:

1. The actual cost-bearing forecaster path uses config.primary_model.model_id, which auto-detects to claude-sonnet-4-6, gpt-5.1, or gemini-3.1 (config.py:74-97) — all three ARE present in the pricing table. So the default, most-traveled path reports correct rates today. The silent-fallback only bites on config drift, future model IDs, or alternate model IDs.

2. The audit's specific example of "external verification providers" is weaker than implied: cross_verify (llm_router.py:340-349) does NOT route through estimate_cost — it logs with tokens_in=0, tokens_out=0 and no cost_usd (defaults to 0.0). So verification calls produce $0 cost, not wrong-rate cost. The verification_providers config list (llama, gemma, nemotron, deepseek, qwen, etc., config.py:102-105) would only hit the bad fallback if routed through the generic call() dispatch, not the verify() path.

Net: the issue genuinely exists (silent substitution with no signal, plus an unmaintainable exact-match pricing table that will drift), but its concrete impact is latent rather than active — the common path is correct, and the cited verification-provider example doesn't actually trigger the bug. The recommendation (log a warning / mark cost as estimated) is sound and cheap. Costs here are informational, not used for billing or gating.

### `DEPS-94` — No SBOM/vulnerability gating in the dependency workflow
- **confirmed ✓** · source: framed · effort: small · file: `/Users/bjgilbert/Projects/Zoltar/sigma-predict/pyproject.toml:6`
- **evidence:** 73 packages are installed transitively (pip freeze count) with no lockfile, no `pip-audit`/`safety`/Dependabot configuration in the repo, and no hash pinning. Network-facing transitive deps present in the tree (requests 2.32.5, urllib3 2.6.3, tornado 6.5.5 via streamlit, protobuf 6.33.6 via google-genai) are exactly the classes of package that accrue CVEs; with `>=`-only and no audit step there is no mechanism to detect a vulnerable transitive version landing in an install.
- **recommendation:** Add `pip-audit` (or `uv pip audit`) to CI against the committed lockfile and enable Dependabot/Renovate for the GitHub repo so transitive CVEs surface as PRs. Pair with hash-pinned installs (`--require-hashes`) for production deploys.
- **verifier:** Could not refute. Every factual claim verified directly against the repo and installed environment:

- pyproject.toml (lines 6-27) uses >=-only lower-bound constraints with no hash pinning. Confirmed.
- No lockfile: git ls-files shows no requirements.txt, uv.lock, or poetry.lock. Confirmed.
- No Dependabot/Renovate/CI: no .github directory exists; no tracked YAML workflow files at all. The only "audit" hit (src/pipeline/llm_audit.py) is unrelated application code. Confirmed.
- No pip-audit/safety anywhere in deps, dev deps, or repo. Confirmed.
- 73 packages via `pip freeze` — exact match.
- Named network-facing transitive deps all installed at the stated versions: requests 2.32.5, urllib3 2.6.3, tornado 6.5.5 (via streamlit), protobuf 6.33.6 (via google-genai). Exact match.

The reasoning is sound: floating lower bounds + no committed lockfile + no audit step means a vulnerable transitive version can land on a fresh install with no detection mechanism. The recommendation (pip-audit in CI, Dependabot, hash-pinned prod installs) is standard and appropriate.

This is a genuine dependency-hygiene gap, not a fabrication.

### `DEPS-95` — License posture is clean for direct deps — no copyleft exposure found
- **confirmed ✓** · source: framed · effort: trivial · file: `/Users/bjgilbert/Projects/Zoltar/sigma-predict/pyproject.toml:7-27`
- **evidence:** Spot-checked the heavier direct deps: streamlit Apache-2.0, plotly MIT, scipy BSD, numpy BSD-3-Clause AND MIT AND Zlib AND CC0/0BSD, sigma-verify MIT (pip show). All permissive; no GPL/AGPL copyleft surfaced among direct dependencies. Note this is NOT a full transitive audit (73 packages) — no license scan exists in the repo, so a copyleft transitive could slip in unnoticed under the unpinned `>=` ranges.
- **recommendation:** Low priority given direct deps are permissive, but add a transitive license scan (e.g. `pip-licenses` in CI against the lockfile) so the clean posture is verified continuously rather than assumed.
- **verifier:** Every factual claim in the finding is verified against the actual installed package metadata in /Users/bjgilbert/Projects/Zoltar/sigma-predict/.venv and pyproject.toml (lines 6-20, the cited 7-27 range).

Direct-dep licenses confirmed from each package's dist-info METADATA: streamlit License-Expression Apache-2.0; plotly MIT; scipy declares BSD (Classifier: OSI Approved :: BSD License); numpy License-Expression "BSD-3-Clause AND 0BSD AND MIT AND Zlib AND CC0-1.0". All permissive. No strong copyleft (GPL/AGPL) among direct deps. Confirmed.

No license scan / continuous verification exists: there is NO .github directory, no CI workflows, no lockfile (no uv.lock/poetry.lock/requirements.txt), and no pip-licenses/licensecheck reference anywhere in the repo (excluding .venv). All 18 dependency constraints use unpinned >= ranges. The finding's premise — clean posture is assumed, not continuously verified — is accurate.

I went further than the audit and ran the full 74-package transitive scan it admitted it did not do. The only copyleft-adjacent items found are: scipy's bundled binary dylibs libgfortran/libgcc (GPL-3.0-or-later WITH GCC-exception-3.1) and libquadmath (LGPL-2.1-or-later), plus certifi and tqdm under MPL-2.0. These are weak/file-level copyleft: the GCC Runtime Library Exception explicitly permits redistribution without copyleft obligations, LGPL/MPL impose no obligations on an application via dynamic linking, and MPL is file-level. So the transitive posture is in fact also clean — strengthening, not contradicting, the finding's title.

This is a genuine, accurately-scoped, honestly-hedged observation. It is correctly a "clean bill of health plus a hardening recommendation" rather than a defect. The recommendation (add pip-licenses CI) is valid. I could not refute the facts.

### `TEST-106` — Orchestrator metadata helpers (_compute_horizon, _infer_domain) untested including the error path
- **confirmed ✓** · source: framed · effort: trivial · file: `/Users/bjgilbert/Projects/Zoltar/sigma-predict/src/pipeline/orchestrator.py:507-534`
- **evidence:** _compute_horizon parses question.resolve_date with fromisoformat after a Z->+00:00 replace and returns None on ValueError/TypeError (lines 529-534); horizon_days feeds the Brier-by-horizon bucketing in query.py. _infer_domain does first-match-wins substring matching (lines 519-522) and the resulting domain keys get_recent_in_domain and the by-domain decomposition. Neither is tested — a malformed resolve_date or an ambiguous title silently produces wrong horizon/domain metadata that skews the scoreboard groupings.
- **recommendation:** Add tests for _compute_horizon with a valid future ISO date (positive days), a past date (clamped to 0 via max), a malformed string (None), and resolve_date absent (None); and _infer_domain for a clearly-geopolitics title, a no-keyword title -> 'general', and first-match ordering when two keyword sets could match.
- **verifier:** Confirmed against the code. orchestrator.py:507-534 matches the finding verbatim: _infer_domain (507-523) does first-match-wins substring matching over an ordered dict and falls back to "general"; _compute_horizon (525-534) does the Z->+00:00 replace, fromisoformat parse, max(0, ...) clamp, and returns None on ValueError/TypeError. grep confirms neither helper is referenced anywhere in tests/ — they appear only at their definition site and the single call site (orchestrator.py:120-121). The downstream coupling claims also hold: horizon_days drives the Brier-by-horizon bucketing in query.py:189-196 (<30d / 30-180d / >180d) and domain drives get_recent_in_domain (query.py:87-98) and get_brier_decomposition_by_domain (query.py:127-157). So untested metadata genuinely flows into scoreboard groupings. The issue is real and the recommended test cases are appropriate (valid future date -> positive, past date -> 0 via max, malformed -> None, absent -> None; domain match, no-match -> general, first-match ordering). Severity LOW is correct and should be kept, not raised: these are pure, low-complexity functions, the error path is non-crashing (None and missing domain are both handled defensively downstream — None horizon buckets into "<30d", missing domain coalesces to "unknown"), and the blast radius is limited to scoreboard grouping accuracy, not core prediction or calibration output. The wrong-bucket-on-malformed-date behavior is a quiet correctness skew rather than a failure, which fits LOW.

### `IMPR-178` — Prompt template files are re-read from disk on every single forecast run
- **REFUTED ✗** · source: improvement · effort: trivial · file: `/Users/bjgilbert/Projects/Zoltar/sigma-predict/src/pipeline/forecaster.py:118-124`
- **evidence:** Each run_forecast call does `_load_prompt(config, 'forecaster_base.md')`, `_load_prompt(config, 'pre_mortem.md')`, and `_load_prompt(config, persona_path)` — and _load_prompt (utils.py:30-33) opens and reads the file fresh every time with no memoization. For a 10-run prediction that is 30 redundant disk reads of static template files. The files never change within a process lifetime.
- **recommendation:** Memoize _load_prompt with functools.lru_cache keyed on (prompts_dir, filename). One-line change in utils.py. Minor latency win but removes filesystem I/O from the hot per-run path entirely; the bigger value is making the parallelization above purely network-bound rather than contending on synchronous file reads.
- **verifier:** The factual current-state claims are accurate: _load_prompt (src/pipeline/utils.py:30-33) opens and reads the file fresh on every call with no memoization, run_forecast calls it 3x per run (forecaster.py:118, 119, 124), and runs execute in a sequential for-loop (orchestrator.py:207), so a 10-run forecast does ~30 reads. But the opportunity is not worthwhile and the proposed fix is broken.

REFUTE on three grounds:

1. The proposed "one-line" fix is technically incorrect and would BREAK the pipeline. The signature is _load_prompt(config, filename), and Config is a plain @dataclass (config.py, no frozen=True) — unhashable by default (eq=True sets __hash__=None). A literal @functools.lru_cache decorator as proposed would raise TypeError: unhashable type 'Config' on the first call, breaking every caller (decomposer.py:41, base_rate.py:39, forecaster.py). The claim that it can be keyed on (prompts_dir, filename) is false without refactoring the signature first. So it is not a one-line change.

2. The "bigger value" justification is built on code that does not exist. The claim cites making "the parallelization above purely network-bound." There is no parallelization — orchestrator.py:207 runs forecasts in a sequential for-loop with two blocking LLM API calls per run (router.call at forecaster.py:161 and 204). The parallelization premise is speculative/hypothetical.

3. The payoff is negligible regardless. The templates are 2-8 KB markdown files (verified: forecaster_base.md 6KB, pre_mortem.md 7KB, personas ~2.5KB), read sub-millisecond and served from the OS page cache after first access. Against two LLM round-trips per run (seconds to tens of seconds each), the file I/O is invisible — 4-6 orders of magnitude smaller. This is micro-optimization of a non-hot path, justified by a non-existent parallelization. Churn for its own sake.

The current code is fine. If anything were done, the correct fix would be an lru_cache on a helper keyed on (prompts_dir, filename) extracted before the call — but the motivating payoff still doesn't exist.

### `IMPR-180` — SearchModule is instantiated twice per prediction instead of reused
- **REFUTED ✗** · source: improvement · effort: trivial · file: `/Users/bjgilbert/Projects/Zoltar/sigma-predict/src/pipeline/orchestrator.py:175,205`
- **evidence:** Line 175 `search_module_for_base = SearchModule(self.config)` for the base-rate augmentation, then line 205 `search_module = SearchModule(self.config)` for the forecast runs — two separate instances built from the same config in the same predict_until_review call. Combined with the per-query client creation above, neither instance can share a connection pool across the base-rate search and the forecast searches.
- **recommendation:** Construct a single SearchModule once (e.g. in Orchestrator.__init__ or once at the top of predict_until_review) and pass it to both base_rate augmentation and the run loop. Trivial dedup; becomes meaningful once SearchModule owns a persistent connection pool, since base-rate and forecast searches would then share warm connections.
- **verifier:** Verified against orchestrator.py:175,205 and search.py. The factual current-state is accurate: two SearchModule instances are constructed in one predict_until_review call (line 175 for base-rate augmentation, line 205 for forecast runs). But the PERFORMANCE payoff is unfounded.

SearchModule.__init__ (search.py:72-77) only reads three scalar config attributes (api_key, search_depth, max_results). It owns NO connection pool and NO httpx.Client. Instantiating it twice costs nothing measurable. The actual connection churn lives inside search() (search.py:98), which opens and closes a fresh `with httpx.Client(...)` per query — that happens regardless of how many SearchModule instances exist, so deduping the instances yields zero connection reuse.

The proposal itself concedes the present payoff is zero: it says the dedup "becomes meaningful once SearchModule owns a persistent connection pool." That is a bet on a feature that does not exist. As filed (performance), the improvement delivers no measurable gain today; it is speculative/conditional churn.

The genuinely impactful change — if connection warmth is the goal — is the opposite shape: give SearchModule a persistent httpx.Client so connection reuse spans the ~5 queries per persona and across base-rate + forecast searches. The constructor dedup is a cosmetic byproduct of that refactor, not the lever. Recommending the dedup as the performance fix misdirects effort.

Not reductive in a dangerous way: grep confirms the two SearchModule( call sites are the only references; the locals aren't imported/tested elsewhere, so the dedup would be SAFE if done. But SAFE does not make it WORTHWHILE under a performance justification with zero current payoff.

### `IMPR-182` — Inert temporal-firewall code in base-rate augmentation can be deleted (reductive cleanup)
- **confirmed ✓** · source: improvement · effort: trivial · file: `/Users/bjgilbert/Projects/Zoltar/sigma-predict/src/pipeline/base_rate.py:121-165`
- **evidence:** search_augmented_base_rate imports date/timedelta, computes `cutoff = date.today() - timedelta(days=180)`, then loops over results doing `getattr(r, 'published_date', None) or getattr(r, 'date', None)` to filter recent ones. But SearchResult (search.py:59-66) defines only title/url/content/score — neither published_date nor date exists — so getattr always returns None and the filter NEVER excludes anything. The docstring (lines 100-101) even admits 'temporal firewall is not currently active.' The entire filtered_results loop and the `if not filtered_results` early-return (lines 160-165) are dead computation executed on every base-rate search, plus a misleading 'temporal firewall applied' string injected into the LLM prompt (line 193).
- **recommendation:** Delete the cutoff computation and the filtering loop; use `results[:3]` directly (the code already truncates to 3). Remove the false 'temporal firewall applied' claim from the prompt. This is a net deletion (~30 lines) that removes per-search dead work AND fixes a docstring/prompt that misrepresents behavior — a stronger outcome than 'adding' a real firewall the system was never designed to support. If recency filtering is genuinely wanted later, it must start by adding a date field to SearchResult, which is a separate decision.
- **verifier:** VERIFIED against the code. All load-bearing claims hold:

1. SearchResult (search.py:60-66) is a dataclass with ONLY title/url/content/score — no published_date or date field. It is instantiated in exactly one place (search.py:116) from those four keys; grep confirms no other SearchResult construction and no date population anywhere in src/. So getattr(r, 'published_date', None) or getattr(r, 'date', None) at base_rate.py:140 returns None for every result, unconditionally.

2. Consequence: the filter loop (138-158) always appends every result; filtered_results == results on every call. The temporal firewall NEVER excludes anything. The 'if not filtered_results' early-return (160-165) is logically redundant with the 'if not results' check already at line 132. The cutoff computation (137) and timedelta/date import (121) feed only this dead path.

3. The prompt string at line 193 ('historical only — temporal firewall applied') is a factually false claim injected into the LLM context. This is the substantive defect: it asserts contamination filtering that did not happen, which directly undermines the module's stated contamination-control design (docstring lines 1-7, 98-107). Replacing the loop with results[:3] and removing the false claim is correct.

SAFETY (reductive): confirmed safe. search_augmented_base_rate is called only from orchestrator.py:176, which consumes the (augmented_rate, citation_note) tuple — the proposed change preserves that return contract exactly. grep of tests/ (6 test files) shows ZERO references to the function, 'temporal', 'firewall', 'published_date', or filtered_results. No test keeps the code alive; nothing breaks on deletion.

TWO CORRECTIONS to the proposal's framing:
(a) The docstring (lines 98-103) is already HONEST — it explicitly states 'temporal firewall is not currently active.' Only the PROMPT (line 193) misrepresents behavior. The proposal's 'docstring/prompt that misrepresents' is half-wrong; the docstring just needs its now-obsolete note trimmed, not a correctness fix.
(b) The 'performance' area tag and 'per-search dead work' framing are inflated. The dead work is a loop over <=5 items with 2 getattr calls each — negligible against the surrounding 30s-timeout network search and two LLM calls. Performance payoff is effectively zero. The genuine value is correctness (removing a false prompt claim) and clarity (deleting ~30 lines of inert code), not speed.

Net: real, grounded, and safe — but it is a correctness/clarity cleanup miscategorized as performance.

### `IMPR-184` — get_community_prediction re-fetches a question already retrieved earlier in the pipeline
- **confirmed ✓** · source: improvement · effort: small · file: `/Users/bjgilbert/Projects/Zoltar/sigma-predict/src/pipeline/orchestrator.py:545`
- **evidence:** _collect_platform_signals calls `platform_client.get_community_prediction(question.question_id)`, which issues a fresh single GET to the platform API (per platforms repo notes, no caching). The PlatformQuestion object was already fetched upstream (scripts/predict.py / UI fetch) and in several platforms the community prediction is derivable from the same question payload that was already downloaded. This is a redundant network round-trip per prediction for data the pipeline likely already held.
- **recommendation:** Where the platform's question payload already carries the community price, populate community_prediction onto PlatformQuestion at fetch time and have _collect_platform_signals read from the in-memory question first, falling back to a network call only when absent. Removes one network round-trip per prediction. Low individual cost but it is on the critical path of every prediction and is pure redundant I/O.
- **verifier:** Confirmed against the code. PlatformQuestion (src/models.py:245) carries community_prediction, and all three platform clients populate it at map time from the same payload field that get_community_prediction later re-fetches:
- Metaculus: _map_question -> _extract_community_prediction(data) (metaculus.py:54); get_community_prediction re-GETs /questions/{id}/ and calls the identical extractor (metaculus.py:187-189).
- Polymarket: _map_market extracts yes-token price (polymarket.py:24-34); get_community_prediction re-GETs /markets/{id} with identical logic (polymarket.py:132-140).
- Kalshi: _map_market extracts yes-price (kalshi.py:24-32); get_community_prediction re-GETs /markets/{id} with identical logic (kalshi.py:167-174).
The orchestrator (orchestrator.py:362) calls _collect_platform_signals(question, ...) where `question` is the same PlatformQuestion passed into predict_until_review (orchestrator.py:70-72), already fetched upstream by scripts/predict.py get_question/get_questions. _collect_platform_signals (orchestrator.py:536-555) ignores question.community_prediction and fires a fresh GET via get_community_prediction(question.question_id) (line 545). So the redundant round-trip is genuine for every platform and every prediction. This is NOT reductive (no deletion); proposed fix adds a fallback read of the in-memory field, which is safe. Note get_community_prediction stays in the public PlatformClient interface (base.py:87) and is still needed as the fallback, so nothing is removed/broken.

Caveats lowering payoff: (1) The call runs once per prediction, not per estimation run. The pipeline does multiple LLM research/aggregation/calibration runs between the upstream fetch and signal collection — seconds of latency and real dollar cost. One extra GET is negligible against that. The proposal itself concedes "low individual cost." (2) Semantic difference: the re-fetch gets a fresher price than the upstream snapshot. The dict key is literally named prices_at_prediction_time, suggesting intentional freshness capture; reading the stale upstream snapshot for fast-moving markets (Polymarket/Kalshi) slightly changes behavior, though the pipeline's own multi-second latency already makes "prediction time" fuzzy. This is a correct, safe micro-optimization, not a meaningful perf win.

### `IMPR-191` — Verification/challenge failures are logged with exception class but the failing finding/context is not captured, and cross_verify swallows ImportError silently
- **REFUTED ✗** · source: improvement · effort: trivial · file: `/Users/bjgilbert/Projects/Zoltar/sigma-predict/src/pipeline/llm_router.py:264`
- **evidence:** verify() catches Exception and logs `logger.warning("Verification failed (%s): %s", type(e).__name__, e)` (llm_router.py:264) — good that it has exception class, but it then constructs a VerificationError only if sigma_verify importable; on ImportError it returns None (276-277) with no log, silently dropping the verification with zero signal. challenge() on failure returns a dict but does not log the provider/model context beyond the exception (314). cross_verify's ImportError branch (367-368) does `pass` — a verification result is silently lost. When external providers degrade (rate limits, auth expiry), the pipeline continues with verification.stages quietly shorter than expected and nothing surfaces the systematic drop.
- **recommendation:** Replace the bare ImportError `pass`/`return None` with a logger.warning, and increment/record a per-pipeline 'verification_failures' count onto record.verification (a single int field) so the registry shows that N expected verifications failed. Cheap, and turns a silent gap into a queryable one. No new layer — one field plus log lines on the existing except branches.
- **verifier:** Code citations are exact (llm_router.py:264, 276-277, 314, 367-368), but the opportunity's core justification is factually wrong about where signal is lost.

DEGRADATION PATH IS ALREADY OBSERVABLE. The headline scenario — "external providers degrade (rate limits, auth expiry), pipeline continues with verification.stages quietly shorter than expected and nothing surfaces the systematic drop" — does not match the code. Rate-limit/auth/API errors raise inside client.verify(), get caught by the broad `except Exception`, which (a) LOGS at WARN (lines 264, 314, 352) and (b) constructs a VerificationError that is appended to results. In verification.py, _result_to_entry() converts that into a VerificationEntry with status="failed" and error_class populated, which flows into StageVerification.verifications. _compute_agreement() filters on status=="success", so failures are excluded from agreement but STILL RECORDED as entries. So failures are already logged AND already queryable in the registry — the stage list is not "quietly shorter," it contains failed entries. The proposed verification_failures counter would largely duplicate `[e for e in entries if e.status != 'success']`.

THE TRULY-SILENT BRANCH IS EFFECTIVELY DEAD. The only genuinely silent path is cross_verify's `except ImportError: pass` (368) and verify()'s `except ImportError: return None` (276-277). But self._external_providers is populated only inside _init_external_providers (line 118), AFTER a successful `from sigma_verify import PROVIDERS` (line 121, itself guarded with a warning). _get_external_client/_external_clients source exclusively from _external_providers, so if no client exists, verify()/cross_verify() short-circuit (line 242-243) before the try block. Therefore the inline `from sigma_verify import classify_error, VerificationError` at 266/357 can only raise ImportError under a version skew where PROVIDERS imports but classify_error/VerificationError are absent. I confirmed all three symbols are present in the installed sigma_verify (which is a declared required dependency, sigma-verify>=0.1.0, not optional). In practice these branches never fire.

ALSO MISSTATED: verify() is not "zero signal" — it logs at 264 before the ImportError return. Only cross_verify's bare `pass` is fully silent.

NET: The real defect is one bare `pass` on a near-dead branch. Adding a logger.warning there is a fine 1-line hardening (harmless), but the bulk of the proposal (the verification_failures counter, the "systematic silent drop" narrative) rests on a mischaracterization of the code — operational verification failures are already logged and recorded. Not reductive, so removal-safety is N/A; no tests touch these paths.

### `IMPR-193` — Each script independently calls logging.basicConfig with divergent levels/formats and there is no shared logging setup
- **confirmed ✓** · source: improvement · effort: small · file: `/Users/bjgilbert/Projects/Zoltar/sigma-predict/scripts/resolve.py:74`
- **evidence:** resolve.py:74 (level WARNING default), batch_predict.py:44 (WARNING), predict.py:74 (INFO) each call logging.basicConfig with their own format. The Streamlit app and the library code (src/) configure nothing, so when run under Streamlit the rich INFO-level pipeline logs (orchestrator step logs, calibration phase, forecaster results) go to whatever the default root handler is — effectively nowhere captured. There is no single place that decides where logs go or at what level, so the same library code emits debug-critical info (raw parse failures, calibration phase) at levels that are dropped in most run contexts. This is the root cause amplifying findings #3 and #4.
- **recommendation:** Consolidate into one `setup_logging(level, audit_path)` helper in src/ (or config) that all entry points (3 scripts + app.py) call, removing the three duplicated basicConfig blocks. This is net-reductive (collapses 3 divergent setups into 1) and gives a single knob for log destination/level — the prerequisite for the other observability fixes to actually reach an operator.
- **verifier:** Every factual claim in the proposal checks out against the code.

CURRENT STATE — VERIFIED:
- scripts/resolve.py:74 — logging.basicConfig, level=WARNING default (INFO if -v), format "%(asctime)s %(levelname)s: %(message)s". Confirmed.
- scripts/batch_predict.py:44 — identical basicConfig, same WARNING default, same format. Confirmed.
- scripts/predict.py:74 — basicConfig at level DEBUG/INFO/WARNING with a DIFFERENT format ("%(asctime)s [%(name)s] %(levelname)s: %(message)s", datefmt="%H:%M:%S"). Confirmed the formats genuinely diverge — predict.py includes logger name and a custom time format; the other two do not.
- Library code (src/) uses logging.getLogger(__name__) in 16 modules and emits 123 logging calls. It configures NO handlers/levels itself. Confirmed.
- app.py (Streamlit entry) and the components/ and pages/ dirs contain ZERO logging configuration. Confirmed (grep returned nothing).
- The Streamlit app DOES drive the pipeline: pages/2_Predict.py:65 instantiates Orchestrator(config) in a background thread. So the orchestrator's INFO step logs (orchestrator.py:108,139,160,203,248,333,428... "Step N/7...") and calibration/forecaster INFO logs genuinely fire under Streamlit — and with no root config, Python's root logger defaults to WARNING, so all those INFO lines are dropped/uncaptured. Claim confirmed exactly.
- The "debug-critical at dropped levels" claim holds: forecaster.py:176 logs the parse failure at WARNING but the raw response at DEBUG (177), and calibration.py:65 logs at INFO — both invisible in the default WARNING contexts the proposal describes.

PAYOFF CREDIBLE: A single setup_logging(level, audit_path) helper called by the 3 scripts + app.py is a real consolidation (collapses 3 divergent basicConfig blocks into 1) and is the genuine prerequisite for routing pipeline/audit logs to an operator under Streamlit, where today they vanish. The "root cause amplifying findings #3 and #4" framing is reasonable: those findings depend on logs actually reaching a destination, which currently they don't in the Streamlit path.

REDUCTIVE SAFETY: This is net-additive-then-reductive (adds one helper, removes 3 basicConfig blocks). It deletes nothing that is imported, called, or tested elsewhere — basicConfig calls are local to each main(). No callers or tests reference these inline blocks. There is no setup_logging symbol today, so nothing collides. Safe.

One scope note (not a refutation): the three scripts already partially "work" for CLI use today — basicConfig succeeds because each script is the first to touch the root logger in its own process. The real gap is specifically the Streamlit/library path and the format/level divergence, not total breakage. The proposal states this accurately.

### `IMPR-195` — AnthropicClient default model arg at llm_router.py:39 is dead — the only caller always passes model explicitly
- **confirmed ✓** · source: improvement · effort: trivial · file: `src/pipeline/llm_router.py:38-40,97-100,168-172`
- **evidence:** AnthropicClient.__init__ defaults self.model = model or 'claude-sonnet-4-6', but every construction site passes model explicitly: LLMRouter.__init__ passes model='claude-sonnet-4-6' (line 98) and _get_or_create_client passes model=model (line 169). The call() method also takes a model param and resolves model if model is not None else self.model (line 57), and router.call always forwards a model (forecaster.py:162, base_rate.py:54-56, decomposer.py:62-63). So self.model is effectively never the value actually used for a request — it is a vestige of the pre-cache, model-mutation design the class comments say was removed.
- **recommendation:** Drop the default literal from AnthropicClient.__init__ (or drop self.model entirely and require model on call()). Removing the fallback eliminates one of the duplicated 'claude-sonnet-4-6' literals and makes the per-(provider,model) cache contract explicit: model always comes from the caller. Net deletion.
- **verifier:** Verified against the code. The claimed current state is accurate:

- AnthropicClient.__init__ (llm_router.py:39) sets self.model = model or "claude-sonnet-4-6".
- call() resolves resolved_model = model if model is not None else self.model (line 57).
- LLMRouter.call ALWAYS forwards model: self._anthropic.call(system, user, temperature, max_tokens, model=model) (line 206).
- All three orchestrator call sites pass an explicit model: forecaster.py:161 (model var), base_rate.py:54-55 (config.primary_model.model_id), decomposer.py:61 (config.primary_model.model_id). I read each; none pass None.
- Both construction sites pass model explicitly: LLMRouter.__init__ line 98 (model="claude-sonnet-4-6") and _get_or_create_client line 169 (model=model).
- self._anthropic.model is never read anywhere (grepped all *.py). _provider_name returns "anthropic" without reading .model; audit logging uses the model PARAMETER of router.call, not self._anthropic.model. So self.model is genuinely vestigial for request routing — confirmed dead, matching the class/router comments that say the model-mutation design was removed.

SAFETY (reductive proposal): Dropping the default literal is safe.
- The only test touching AnthropicClient construction (test_cross_pollination.py:159, test_available_providers_includes_anthropic) passes model="claude-sonnet-4-6" explicitly and only exercises .available, not .model. Dropping the default does not break it. The mock-based test at line 75 patches the class entirely.
- No code is kept alive solely by tests reading the default.

Two caveats on scope, which is why I would not rubber-stamp the broader variant:
1. Variant "drop just the literal" (self.model = model): this leaves self.model possibly None and keeps the line-57 fallback. That is safe today but creates a latent footgun — a future model=None call would pass None to the Anthropic API instead of failing loudly. Net code reduction is ~zero (one literal removed, one duplicate at line 98/398 remains).
2. Variant "drop self.model entirely + require model on call()": cleaner and makes the per-(provider,model) cache contract explicit, but it is a signature change to call() (model: str | None -> required), and the proposal frames it as a trivial "net deletion." It is slightly more than that. It is still safe given current callers, but the description undersells the blast radius of removing the call()-level fallback.

The "duplicated literal" payoff is overstated: "claude-sonnet-4-6" also appears at line 98 (router construction) and line 398 (pricing table). Removing the line-39 default eliminates one of three, not "the" duplication.

VERDICT: Real and grounded — self.model default is genuinely dead for request routing and removal is safe (no caller/test depends on it). But it is a tiny, low-impact cleanup, not an architecture-level win. The honest framing is: prefer the stronger variant (remove self.model and make model a required param on call() with an explicit error if None) so the dead state cannot silently regress; the bare "drop the literal" variant trades dead code for a latent None-passing footgun.

CAVEAT worth noting: the proposal frames this around external backward-compat ("confirm no out-of-repo importer reaches into router._openai"). ### `IMPR-200` — SearchModule is instantiated twice per prediction for no reason
- **confirmed ✓** · source: improvement · effort: trivial · file: `src/pipeline/orchestrator.py:175,205`
- **evidence:** orchestrator.py:175 creates search_module_for_base = SearchModule(self.config) for the search-augmented base rate, then orchestrator.py:205 creates a second search_module = SearchModule(self.config) for the forecast loop. SearchModule is constructed from the same config and is stateless w.r.t. the request (it holds config + the Tavily backend). Two instances per run is harmless but signals the orchestrator is managing collaborator lifecycles ad hoc rather than holding them as instance state like router/store/query/validator do (set up once in __init__, orchestrator.py:63-68).
- **recommendation:** Construct SearchModule once in Orchestrator.__init__ (self.search = SearchModule(config)) alongside router/store/validator, and reuse it in both places. One-line-net reduction and consistent with how every other collaborator is already managed.
- **verifier:** Verified against the code. Both instantiations exist exactly as claimed: src/pipeline/orchestrator.py:175 (search_module_for_base = SearchModule(self.config)) and :205 (search_module = SearchModule(self.config)).

SearchModule is genuinely stateless w.r.t. the request. Its __init__ (src/pipeline/search.py:72-77) only reads three config values (tavily_api_key, search_depth, max_results). Both public methods (search, search_with_persona) create their own short-lived httpx.Client per call and hold no per-request state. The two instances are therefore functionally identical, and a single instance reused in both places produces identical behavior. The "twice per prediction for no reason" framing is accurate.

The inconsistency claim also holds: router/store/query/validator are all set up once in __init__ (orchestrator.py:65-68), while SearchModule alone is constructed ad hoc inside the method body. Moving it to self.search alongside the others is consistent.

Safety (this is partly reductive — collapsing two constructions into one shared instance): grep shows SearchModule is referenced only in those two orchestrator lines plus the import and a docstring mention in base_rate.py:112. No test patches SearchModule or asserts on its construction count, so the refactor breaks nothing. No existing self.search attribute on Orchestrator collides (config.search is an unrelated config sub-object). Orchestrator is constructed plainly as Orchestrator(config) in all call sites (scripts/predict.py, batch_predict.py, pages/2_Predict.py), none of which introspect collaborator attributes. The refactor is safe.

One caveat the proposal slightly oversells: pages/2_Predict.py:65 deliberately creates a new Orchestrator per thread (comment: "NEW per-thread instance"). Hoisting SearchModule to __init__ means each per-thread Orchestrator gets its own SearchModule — which is correct and actually cleaner, since SearchModule is stateless and httpx.Client is created per-call anyway, so there is no shared-mutable-state thread-safety concern introduced. So the move is safe under the threaded usage too.

Net effect is real but tiny: ~1 line saved, modest consistency gain, zero behavior change, zero performance impact (SearchModule construction is trivial — three attribute reads). This is a legitimate micro-cleanup, not churn, but it is cosmetic, not a correctness or performance issue.

- **confirmed ✓** · source: improvement · effort: trivial · file: `src/pipeline/calibration.py:103-114`
- **evidence:** Confirmed via grep: the only call sites (calibration.py:82,92) use apply_hedging_correction_scaled. apply_hedging_correction (line 103) is defined but has zero callers in src/. It is superseded by the scaled variant.

PRODUCTION CALLERS: Confirmed. apply_hedging_correction (src/pipeline/calibration.py:103-114) has zero callers in src/. The only production call sites (calibration.py:82, 92) use apply_hedging_correction_scaled, the distance-weighted variant. ### `IMPR-202` — verification_providers list in config.py must stay manually in sync with sigma-verify PROVIDERS registry, with no validation
- **confirmed ✓** · source: improvement · effort: small · file: `src/config.py:102-105; src/pipeline/llm_router.py:126-153`
- **evidence:** Config hardcodes a 10-name allowlist ['openai','google','llama','gemma','nemotron','deepseek','qwen','devstral','glm','kimi']. The actual sigma_verify.PROVIDERS registry currently exposes 13 names (includes anthropic, nemotron-nano, qwen-local which are intentionally excluded). Nothing validates that the config names still exist in PROVIDERS — a renamed/removed provider in sigma-verify silently drops from the active set with only a debug-level 'init failed' log (llm_router.py:139,153). The default is effectively 'all external providers except the 4B-local ones and anthropic'.
- **recommendation:** Reductive option (preferred): drop the explicit allowlist and derive it as PROVIDERS.keys() minus a small EXCLUDED set ({'anthropic','nemotron-nano','qwen-local'}) computed in _init_external_providers. That removes the hand-maintained 10-element list entirely and makes new sigma-verify providers opt-in-by-default rather than silently ignored. If an explicit allowlist must stay, add a one-line validation in LLMRouter.__init__ that logs a WARNING for any configured name absent from PROVIDERS.
- **verifier:** VERIFIED, source: src/config.py:102-105, src/pipeline/llm_router.py:118-153, live PROVIDERS introspection.

Current state is accurate. config.py:102-105 hardcodes exactly the 10-name allowlist ['openai','google','llama','gemma','nemotron','deepseek','qwen','devstral','glm','kimi']. The live sigma_verify.PROVIDERS registry (/Users/bjgilbert/Projects/sigma-verify/src/sigma_verify) has 13 keys; the 3 not in the allowlist are exactly {'anthropic','nemotron-nano','qwen-local'}, matching the claim. There is no validation anywhere that configured names still exist in PROVIDERS.

One mechanism correction: the claim attributes the silent drop to the debug-level 'init failed' log at llm_router.py:139/153. That is wrong — those debug lines fire on client *instantiation* exceptions. The actual silent-drift path is the loop at line 128 iterating PROVIDERS.items() and skipping via 'if name not in allowed: continue' (line 142). A renamed/removed sigma-verify provider whose old name is still in the config never matches any PROVIDERS key, so it is dropped with NO log line at all — silent and worse than the claim states. The conclusion (silent, unvalidated drift) holds and is reinforced.

MEDIUM severity is appropriate — keep. external_providers feed the verification/challenge pool (consumed at llm_router.py:381), so silent drift directly degrades cross-model verification, but it is a latent maintenance hazard contingent on sigma-verify renaming a provider, not an active bug today.

On the two options:

(1) Reductive option (derive PROVIDERS.keys() minus EXCLUDED) — REFUTE as 'preferred'. It changes a documented-intentional semantic from opt-out-by-default to opt-in-by-default. config.py:99-101 explicitly documents the allowlist as the activation control and excludes 4B local models for 'unreliable JSON'. Auto-activating any future sigma-verify provider in a verification pipeline is a behavioral risk, not a clear win. It would also break tests: test_verification_providers_default and test_verification_providers_configurable (test_cross_pollination.py:31-56) assert on the config field, and removing the field breaks the four verification_providers=[] router setups. Not clearly safe; trades a deliberate safety property for marginal LOC reduction.

(2) Validation option (WARN in LLMRouter.__init__/_init_external_providers for any configured name absent from PROVIDERS) — CONFIRM. Small (~2 lines), additive, non-breaking, preserves the intentional opt-out design, and directly fixes the real defect (silent drift surfaces as a startup WARNING). This is the worthwhile improvement.
