### `IMPR-211` — Duplicated platform-client factory across CLI scripts — drift risk between predict.py and batch_predict.py
- **confirmed ✓** · source: improvement · effort: small · file: `/Users/bjgilbert/Projects/Zoltar/sigma-predict/scripts/batch_predict.py:53-58`
- **evidence:** predict.py:24-34 defines _get_platform_client(platform, config); batch_predict.py:53-58 inlines an identical clients dict; pages/2_Predict.py:38-52 has a third copy with try/except. Three implementations of the same platform->client mapping. The Streamlit copy returns None on failure while the CLI copies raise — already a behavioral divergence.
- **recommendation:** Reductive: extract one factory (e.g. src/platforms/__init__.py: get_client(platform, config)) and have all three call sites use it. Deletes two copies, removes drift risk. The Streamlit wrapper can keep its try/except locally around the shared call.
- **verifier:** VERIFIED against code. All three copies exist exactly as claimed: scripts/predict.py:24-34 (_get_platform_client with raise-on-unknown), scripts/batch_predict.py:53-58 (inlined identical dict, KeyError on bad platform via clients[platform]), pages/2_Predict.py:38-52 (third copy with try/except returning None). The platform->client mapping {metaculus,polymarket,kalshi} is genuinely identical across all three. The claimed behavioral divergence is real: Streamlit returns None on failure, CLI scripts raise/KeyError. Three platforms today, so adding a 4th requires editing all three sites — a concrete (if bounded) drift surface.

SAFETY (reductive): This is consolidate-and-rewire, not blind deletion. grep for _get_platform_client found only definitions+call sites in predict.py and 2_Predict.py; batch_predict inlines anonymously. Zero test references to the factory or get_client — tests/test_platform_clients.py imports client-module internals (_map_question, _map_market, etc.) directly, untouched by this change. Removing the two duplicate bodies in favor of one shared get_client breaks no callers and no tests. The proposal correctly preserves the Streamlit None-on-failure semantics by keeping try/except local.

Caveats lowering payoff: (1) Proposed location src/platforms/__init__.py already eager-imports all three clients with no side effects or key requirements at import time, so the Streamlit page's 'lazy import to avoid mandatory API keys at boot' rationale was never actually load-bearing — consolidation won't regress it but the original concern was cargo-cult. (2) Magnitude is small: ~12 lines across 3 sites, 3 stable platforms. (3) The factory is not the only per-platform edit point — Click's type=click.Choice([...]) lists in both CLIs also enumerate platforms, so a new platform still requires multi-site edits regardless. The improvement is real, safe, and worthwhile but minor and non-urgent.

### `IMPR-212` — Dead unscaled apply_hedging_correction superseded by the scaled variant but still exported and tested
- **confirmed ✓** · source: improvement · effort: trivial · file: `/Users/bjgilbert/Projects/Zoltar/sigma-predict/src/pipeline/calibration.py:103-114`
- **evidence:** calibrate() exclusively uses apply_hedging_correction_scaled (lines 82, 92). The unscaled apply_hedging_correction is called only by tests/test_calibration.py:20-36 — never by production code. It is a maintenance liability: two hedging functions with near-identical names, one of which silently does nothing in the live pipeline.
- **recommendation:** Reductive: delete apply_hedging_correction and its tests, OR if kept intentionally as a reference implementation, add a one-line docstring note that it is unused by the pipeline. Removing it eliminates the name-collision footgun. Net lines removed.
- **verifier:** Claim verified against code. calibrate() calls only apply_hedging_correction_scaled at calibration.py:82 and :92 (all three branches — Phase 0, Phase 1 isotonic-fallback — route to the scaled variant; Platt and isotonic handle the others). The unscaled apply_hedging_correction (calibration.py:103-114) has zero production callers. Grep across the whole repo confirms its only references are in tests/test_calibration.py: the import (line 7-8) and the 5 TestHedgingCorrection tests (lines 18-37). No __all__ export of it, no wildcard imports of the calibration module, no getattr/dynamic dispatch. So the function is truly dead in the live pipeline.\n\nSAFETY (reductive proposal): Deleting the function alone WOULD break the test suite — tests/test_calibration.py:7 imports apply_hedging_correction at module load, so removing just the function causes an ImportError that fails the entire test file (not just the 5 hedging tests). This is exactly the failure mode the instructions warn about. The safe removal is to delete BOTH the function (lines 103-114) AND the TestHedgingCorrection class (lines 18-37) AND the import on line 7. The proposal text does say 'delete apply_hedging_correction and its tests' so it acknowledges this, but it is essential and must not be done piecemeal. This is the classic 'dead only because a test keeps it alive' case — the test is testing an otherwise-unused function, so the test provides no real coverage value; removing both is correct.\n\nWORTHWHILE: The DX concern is legitimate. Two functions with near-identical names (apply_hedging_correction vs apply_hedging_correction_scaled) where the shorter/more-obvious name is the dead one is a genuine footgun — a future edit could easily wire the wrong function into calibrate(). The unscaled and scaled variants also produce materially different outputs (e.g. at p=0.7, shift=0.04: unscaled gives 0.74, scaled gives ~0.716), so silently calling the wrong one would corrupt calibration without an obvious error. That said, the payoff is modest: ~12 lines of function + ~20 lines of test removed, no behavioral change to the pipeline. It is real cleanup, not churn, but it is low-stakes.\n\nThe docstring-note alternative in the proposal is weaker — it leaves the name-collision footgun in place. Deletion (of both function and tests) is the better resolution and is safe as long as it is done as a pair.","

- **confirmed ✓** · source: improvement · effort: small · file: `/Users/bjgilbert/Projects/Zoltar/sigma-predict/src/config.py:77-97`

The router repeats claude-sonnet-4-6 in two places: AnthropicClient.__init__ default (src/pipeline/llm_router.py:39) and LLMRouter.__init__ (line 98). Inaccuracies in the proposal text, all minor: the router path is src/pipeline/llm_router.py, not llm_router.py; line refs 39/98 are exact, but pricing is at 397-409 (the proposal cited it only generically). Substance is fully accurate.

SAFETY of the reductive part: Centralizing the literals into a shared constant and removing the redundant router constructor default (line 98) is SAFE. The router default self.model is dead in normal flow — every dispatch passes model= explicitly (line 206), and forecaster/decomposer/base_rate/deliberation/orchestrator all pass config.primary_model.model_id. No test depends on the hardcoded defaults: test_cross_pollination.py constructs AnthropicClient(model=...) and ModelConfig(model_id=...) explicitly, and test_pipeline_contract.py sets mock_config.primary_model.model_id directly. The opportunity is real and grounded, not inflated. The reductive duplication fix is genuine but small (a few string literals). The additive feature (--model flag / UI selectbox + an optional validation/known-IDs check) is legitimately useful for operability when a provider renames a model, but for a single-user forecasting tool this is a low-frequency failure mode. The payoff is real but modest; this is a DX/operability nicety, not a correctness or reliability defect.

### `IMPR-218` — validate_all() is a vestigial method kept alive only by its own test
- **confirmed ✓** · source: improvement · effort: trivial · file: `/Users/bjgilbert/Projects/Zoltar/sigma-predict/src/pipeline/validator.py:131-141`
- **evidence:** grep shows the only production reference is the definition; the orchestrator calls the individual gates (validate_base_rate at orchestrator.py:199, validate_runs at 244, validate_aggregation at 358) directly and interleaves them with the pipeline stages they validate. validate_all is referenced only at tests/test_cross_pollination.py:275,284. The orchestrator never calls it because the gates must run at different pipeline points, not all at once.
- **recommendation:** Delete validate_all (lines 131-141) and its test (test_cross_pollination.py:275-284). The real call sites run gates individually at the correct pipeline stages; a batch convenience wrapper that no production path can use is dead surface that only exists to give the test something to assert on.
- **verifier:** Verified against the actual code. Every factual claim holds:

1. grep confirms `validate_all` has exactly two references: the definition at src/pipeline/validator.py:131 and a single test call at tests/test_cross_pollination.py:284 (test method at 275-285). No production caller, no import elsewhere.

2. The orchestrator calls the three gates individually and interleaved at genuinely distinct pipeline stages, not as decoration:
   - validate_base_rate at orchestrator.py:199 — BEFORE Step 3 forecasting (so a bad base_rate is caught before spending forecast API calls).
   - validate_runs at orchestrator.py:244 — AFTER forecast runs complete, BEFORE Step 4 aggregation.
   - validate_aggregation at orchestrator.py:358 — AFTER calibration produces calibrated_probability.
   Each gate reads a different part of PredictionRecord that only becomes populated at that stage. validate_all runs all three at once and therefore can only operate on a fully-populated record. The orchestrator structurally cannot use it without abandoning the early-stage gating it depends on. The 'gates must run at different points' justification is correct, not inflated.

3. validate_all (validator.py:131-141) just sequentially calls the three real gates and concatenates output — pure convenience wrapper with no unique logic. Nothing is lost by removing it.

SAFETY (reductive proposal): Confirmed safe. The only thing keeping validate_all alive is its own test. The proposal correctly recommends deleting BOTH the method (131-141) and the test (test_cross_pollination.py:275-284/285) — it explicitly avoids the prior-pass failure mode of deleting code while leaving a dangling test. No other test, import, or call site references it. The test asserts `len(warnings) >= 3`; removing it removes the only consumer. No breakage.

This is legitimate dead-surface removal, not churn. The wrapper exists only to give a test something to assert on, and that test exercises behavior already covered by the individual-gate tests (base_rate, parse_failure, run_count, probability gates each have their own tests in the same file).

### `IMPR-219` — Legacy _openai / _gemini router attributes — set but never read
- **confirmed ✓** · source: improvement · effort: trivial · file: `/Users/bjgilbert/Projects/Zoltar/sigma-predict/src/pipeline/llm_router.py:155-157`
- **evidence:** Lines 156-157 assign self._openai and self._gemini 'for backward compatibility.' grep across the whole codebase finds zero reads — every provider access goes through self._external_providers (e.g. _get_external_client line 416, _external_clients line 430). The comment claims backward compatibility but nothing depends on them.
- **recommendation:** Delete lines 155-157. The _external_providers dict is the single source of truth; these two aliases are write-only dead state that imply a second access path which does not exist.
- **verifier:** CONFIRMED real and safe, with one factual correction to the proposal's claim.

WHAT I VERIFIED (source: /Users/bjgilbert/Projects/Zoltar/sigma-predict/src/pipeline/llm_router.py:155-157 and grep across repo):

1. Write-only state confirmed. Lines 156-157 assign self._openai = self._external_providers.get("openai") and self._gemini = self._external_providers.get("google"). I grepped the entire codebase for any READ of these attributes. There are ZERO reads. Every provider access goes through self._external_providers (verified _get_external_client/_external_clients usage). The "backward compatibility" comment is unsupported — nothing reads them. The proposal's core claim is correct.

2. CORRECTION to the proposal's claim. The proposal states "grep across the whole codebase finds zero reads" and implies zero references. That is imprecise: tests/test_cross_pollination.py references _openai/_gemini at lines 85-86, 109-110, 161-162. However, all six references are WRITES (router._openai = None), not reads. The tests build a router via LLMRouter.__new__(LLMRouter) — bypassing __init__/_init_external_providers entirely — and manually stub the attribute surface. So the claim "zero reads" is technically true (no reads exist), but "zero references" would be false. This distinction matters for the safety analysis below.

3. SAFETY (reductive) verified empirically. I temporarily deleted lines 155-157 and ran the full suite: 143 passed (test_cross_pollination.py alone: 44 passed). Then restored the file (confirmed lines 155-157 back in place). The deletion does not break any test because: (a) tests never call _init_external_providers, so the deleted source lines are not exercised by tests; (b) the test writes router._openai = None remain valid Python (arbitrary attribute assignment) and become harmless dangling assignments that nothing reads.

4. Note on the prior-deletion warning. This is NOT the dead-code-kept-alive-by-tests trap. The attributes are not kept alive by a test reading them; the tests only set them defensively as part of manual router construction. The source-side aliases (155-157) and the test-side stubs (85-86 etc.) are independent. Deleting 155-157 alone is safe and self-contained.

WORTH IT: Yes, low-effort, low-risk cleanup. The dead aliases actively mislead — the comment asserts a backward-compat access path that does not exist, and a single source of truth (_external_providers) is the documented design. Removing them removes a false signal. Recommend also deleting the now-orphaned test writes (lines 85-86, 109-110, 161-162) in the same change so the test fixtures don't continue stubbing a removed attribute; leaving them is harmless but perpetuates the same misleading implication the reduction is trying to kill.

### `IMPR-220` — aggregate_runs method dispatch: the trailing else duplicates the extremized branch — collapse the dead method strings
- **REFUTED ✗** · source: improvement · effort: small · file: `/Users/bjgilbert/Projects/Zoltar/sigma-predict/src/pipeline/aggregator.py:90-105`
- **evidence:** The config only ever supplies method='extremized_trimmed_mean' (config.py:31, the sole AggregationConfig.method default; no other assignment exists). The function branches on five method values plus a catch-all else (101-105) that is byte-identical to the 'extremized_trimmed_mean' branch (90-94). raw_median (line 76) is computed solely to serve the 'median' branch (98), which is unreachable in practice. The 'trimmed_mean'/'median'/'mean' branches are flexibility for a configurability that never materialized.
- **recommendation:** Either (a) collapse to the single real path: compute the trimmed mean, extremize when n_distinct_providers>=2, and drop the method dispatch, raw_median, and the duplicated else — or (b) at minimum delete the redundant else (101-105) since unknown methods should fall through to the documented default explicitly, not via a copy-paste of one branch. Removing the unused branches also lets you drop the raw_median computation. Net: simpler function, one code path to reason about.
- **verifier:** REFUTED. The proposal's core premise — that the 'mean'/'median'/'trimmed_mean' branches are unreachable flexibility "that never materialized" — is false. tests/test_aggregator.py:66-74 directly calls aggregate_runs(runs, method="mean") and method="median" with assertions on result.raw_aggregate (test_method_mean, test_method_median). These are passing tests exercising those exact branches and the raw_median computation (line 76). Option (a) ("drop the method dispatch, raw_median, and the duplicated else") would break two tests. aggregate_runs is the module's public, orchestrator-imported function (orchestrator.py:276) and `method` is a documented parameter with a default; the alternate values are a real public-API contract, not dead config.

Option (b) ("delete the redundant else, let unknown methods fall through to the documented default explicitly") is worse than stated: the dispatch is an if/elif/elif/elif/else chain that ASSIGNS raw_aggregate. Removing the else leaves raw_aggregate UNBOUND for any unknown method string, producing an UnboundLocalError at line 108. There is no "fall through to the default" mechanism in this structure — the else IS the fallback. So (b) as literally written introduces a latent crash, not a simplification.

Accurate parts of the claim: the trailing else (101-105) is byte-identical to the extremized_trimmed_mean branch (90-94); raw_median serves only the median branch. But the duplication is intentional fallback behavior, and the only honest, safe micro-refactor would be merging the two identical branches into one condition — marginal churn on a 5-line block with negligible payoff.

Location inaccuracy: AggregationConfig.method is at src/config.py:30-31 (the file is src/config.py, not the path-less "config.py:31" implied; default is line 31 inside the class starting at 30). The claim that "no other assignment exists" is true for production code, but irrelevant given the tests.

This is precisely the prior-pass failure mode the mandate flags: a deletion recommendation that breaks the test suite. The branches are not dead-only-because-a-test-keeps-them-alive in a remove-both sense — they back a public parameter contract.

### `IMPR-221` — monitor.py is a pure aspirational stub — remove it from the CLI surface
- **confirmed ✓** · source: improvement · effort: trivial · file: `/Users/bjgilbert/Projects/Zoltar/sigma-predict/scripts/monitor.py:1-25`
- **evidence:** The entire command body is three click.echo calls stating 'Phase 3 feature and not yet implemented.' It constructs no Config, hits no network/fs, and does nothing. It is a 25-line placeholder advertising functionality that does not exist.
- **recommendation:** Delete scripts/monitor.py until Phase 3 is actually built. A CLI entry point that only prints 'not implemented' is worse than absent — it implies a capability the system lacks. The design intent (poll platforms for divergence) belongs in a roadmap doc or issue, not as executable dead surface in the shipped CLI.
- **verifier:** Verified against the actual code. scripts/monitor.py is exactly as claimed: a 25-line click command whose body is three click.echo calls stating it is a Phase 3 feature not yet implemented. It constructs no Config, hits no network/fs, and does nothing functional.

Reductive safety check passed. The file is NOT imported, called, or tested anywhere: (1) pyproject.toml [project.scripts] registers only sigma-predict = scripts.predict:main — monitor is not a console entry point, only reachable via python -m scripts.monitor; (2) grep of tests/ for 'monitor' returns nothing — no test keeps it alive; (3) no markdown/doc references it (the only Phase 3 mention is an unrelated prompts/deliberation.md placeholder). Deletion breaks no callers and no tests. Safe removal.

It is genuine dead surface with zero current value, so removal is justified and worthwhile. Two qualifications keep this from being high-impact: (a) because it is not a registered console script, a normal CLI user would never stumble onto it — the 'advertises a capability that doesn't exist' harm is real but minor, not a user-facing footgun; (b) the file's only content of value is the design-intent comment (poll Metaculus/Polymarket/Kalshi for divergence). The proposal correctly says that intent belongs in a roadmap/issue, so the safe sequence is capture-then-delete to avoid losing the only record. Net: real, safe, low blast radius either way.

### `IMPR-222` — Defensive hasattr(r,'provider') guard in provider counting is dead weight — RunResult always has provider
- **confirmed ✓** · source: improvement · effort: trivial · file: `/Users/bjgilbert/Projects/Zoltar/sigma-predict/src/pipeline/orchestrator.py:274`
- **evidence:** `n_providers = len(set(r.provider for r in valid_runs if hasattr(r, "provider")))`. RunResult is a Pydantic model and the forecaster always sets provider (per repo map, forecaster.py:230), so hasattr is always True. The guard defends a state that cannot occur for a typed model field; it silently masks a real bug if provider were ever genuinely missing (it would drop the run from the count rather than error).
- **recommendation:** Replace with `n_providers = len({r.provider for r in valid_runs})`. The field is always present on the model; the guard adds nothing but the false impression that provider is optional.
- **verifier:** Confirmed against the actual code.

CURRENT STATE (verified): orchestrator.py:274 reads exactly `n_providers = len(set(r.provider for r in valid_runs if hasattr(r, "provider")))`. The claim is accurate.

GUARD IS DEAD (verified): `valid_runs` is always a `list[RunResult]`. Trace: `record.runs: list[RunResult]` (models.py:222); `valid_runs = [r for r in record.runs if not r.parse_failed]` (orchestrator.py:252); the only reassignment is `valid_runs = record.runs` (line 263). RunResult is a Pydantic BaseModel (models.py:92) with `provider: str = ""` as a declared field with default (models.py:95). Pydantic instances expose every declared field as an attribute regardless of whether it was explicitly set, so `hasattr(r, "provider")` is unconditionally True. The forecaster does set it explicitly (forecaster.py:230, provider=prov), but even if it didn't, the default "" guarantees the attribute exists. The guard filters on a condition that can never be False for this collection. Dead weight, confirmed.

MASKS-A-BUG ARGUMENT (partially valid, but weaker than the proposal frames it): The proposal claims the guard "silently masks a real bug" by dropping a run from the count. In practice, because the field always exists, the guard never drops anything today, so there is no current masking. The stronger and accurate framing is the opposite: the guard creates a false impression that provider is optional on a typed model where it is not. Note also that the downstream value is floored: `n_distinct_providers = max(1, n_providers)` (line 275), so even a hypothetical drop would be clamped — reinforcing that the guard adds no protective value.

SAFETY (reductive proposal — verified safe): The change is one line, internal to _process_record, touches no public interface. grep of tests/ shows no reference to the `hasattr` guard or the `n_providers` local; tests reference only `n_distinct_providers` as a parameter to aggregate_runs (the consumer), and the replacement `len({r.provider for r in valid_runs})` yields identical output for all RunResult inputs. No caller or test breaks. This is NOT a case of code kept alive only by a test.

WORTH IT: This is a genuine but micro improvement — one line, zero behavior change, modest clarity gain (set comprehension + removes a misleading guard). It is real and safe but low-stakes; it is the kind of cleanup that is correct to make opportunistically rather than to prioritize on its own.

### `IMPR-223` — Duplicated platform-client factory logic across predict.py and batch_predict.py
- **confirmed ✓** · source: improvement · effort: small · file: `/Users/bjgilbert/Projects/Zoltar/sigma-predict/scripts/predict.py:24-34`
- **evidence:** predict.py defines _get_platform_client with a {metaculus/polymarket/kalshi: lambda} dict (24-34). Per the repo map, batch_predict.py:53-58 inlines an identical clients dict. Two copies of the same platform->client routing, which will drift (e.g. a fourth platform added to one and not the other).
- **recommendation:** Promote one factory to a shared location (e.g. src/platforms/__init__.py as `get_client(platform, config)`) and have both scripts import it. The click.Choice already constrains the valid platforms, so the function can be a thin dict lookup. Collapses two sources of truth into one; the platforms package is the natural home.
- **verifier:** CONFIRMED and grounded in code. predict.py:24-34 defines _get_platform_client with the exact {metaculus/polymarket/kalshi: lambda} dict claimed; batch_predict.py:53-57 inlines a byte-for-byte identical dict (client = clients[platform]() at :58). The proposal actually UNDERSTATES the problem: there is a THIRD copy in pages/2_Predict.py:38-52 (an if/elif chain with lazy imports doing the same platform->client routing). So it is three sources of truth, not two.

The proposed home is correct and ready: src/platforms/__init__.py already exists and already imports MetaculusClient, PolymarketClient, KalshiClient, and the PlatformClient base. Adding get_client(platform, config) there is a natural fit with zero new import wiring.

Drift risk is real and already partially materialized: the two CLI copies use eager top-of-module imports and let exceptions propagate; the Streamlit copy uses lazy imports inside a try/except that returns None ("avoid mandatory API keys at boot"). That divergence is exactly what a single factory eliminates.

SAFE as a reduction: _get_platform_client is module-private in each file. grep across src/, scripts/, pages/, and app entry points shows only local definitions and local call sites (predict.py:94, batch_predict.py:58); nothing imports these factories cross-module, and no test references them by name. Consolidation = add get_client to the package + replace inline dicts with a call; callers keep working. No dead-code-kept-alive-by-test situation.

Important caveat the proposal glosses over: the three copies are NOT behaviorally identical. The Streamlit version intentionally wraps construction in try/except returning None and warns via st.warning; the CLI versions raise. A naive 'thin dict lookup' that collapses all three into identical behavior would change the page's no-mandatory-keys-at-boot semantics. Correct fix: the shared factory does the dict lookup, and the page retains its own try/except around the call rather than being flattened. With that, the consolidation is sound.

### `READ-244` — Brier decomposition reliability/resolution use equal-width bins and will not reconcile with the actual Brier score
- **confirmed ✓** · source: readiness:methodology · effort: small · file: `src/registry/query.py:26-64`
- **evidence:** Murphy's 2-term (reliability - resolution + uncertainty) decomposition equals the mean Brier score ONLY under the standard calibration-refinement partition with the within-bin term handled correctly. The code computes brier_check = reliability - resolution + uncertainty (line 63) and rounds, but never compares it to the directly-computed mean Brier; with equal-width binning and few points the identity holds only approximately, and 'brier_check' is presented as if validating the decomposition when it is just the algebraic sum of the three computed terms (it will always 'check' against itself, not against the true Brier). Adaptive n_bins=min(10, N//3) means for N=15 you get 5 bins of ~3 points each — high-variance reliability estimates.
- **recommendation:** Compute the actual mean Brier independently and report the residual (mean_brier - brier_check) as the real consistency check; rename brier_check to reflect it is the reconstructed sum. Consider quantile (equal-count) bins for stable per-bin estimates and document that the decomposition is unreliable below ~50 resolved points.
- **verifier:** Code-confirmed. In src/registry/query.py:59-63, compute_brier_decomposition returns brier_check = round(reliability - resolution + uncertainty, 6). This is literally the algebraic sum of the three terms computed on lines 44-57; the actual mean Brier (sum((p-o)^2)/N) is never computed in this function. So the finding's central technical claim is correct: brier_check is a reconstruction that trivially equals the sum of its own components, not an independent consistency check against the true Brier score. The finding also correctly reasons the math: the code implements the 2-component calibration-refinement decomposition using per-bin mean predictions (bar_p_k) and outcomes (bar_o_k), and BS = reliability - resolution + uncertainty holds exactly only when each bin contains identical predictions (or with a within-bin variance correction). With equal-width bins it holds only approximately — the finding states this accurately. The adaptive-binning variance concern (n_bins = min(10, N//3), so N=15 -> 5 bins of ~3 points) is also real, and the recommendation (compute true Brier independently and report the residual; consider quantile bins; document unreliability below ~50 points) is methodologically valid.

HOWEVER, the harm premise is overstated. I searched the entire repo: compute_brier_decomposition, get_brier_decomposition_by_domain, get_brier_decomposition_by_horizon, and the brier_check key are NEVER consumed, displayed, returned to a user, or exercised by any test outside query.py itself. The only UI surface (pages/4_Scoreboard.py:89-132) re-implements the decomposition inline and displays only reliability/resolution/uncertainty — it never calls compute_brier_decomposition and never surfaces brier_check. So the finding's claim that brier_check is 'presented as if validating the decomposition' is not supported anywhere in the codebase; nothing presents it at all. It is effectively dead code. This is a genuine methodology/code-quality issue worth fixing (a self-referential value labeled like a check is misleading to a future reader of the module), but it does not affect any production output today.

### `READ-245` — Dead unscaled hedging function retained alongside the scaled version invites accidental misuse
- **confirmed ✓** · source: readiness:methodology · effort: trivial · file: `src/pipeline/calibration.py:103-114`
- **evidence:** apply_hedging_correction (flat shift, min(0.99, p+shift)) is never called in src/ (superseded by apply_hedging_correction_scaled at line 117, which is the one calibrate() invokes). The two have materially different statistical behavior — the flat version shifts a p=0.51 estimate by the full 0.04 while the scaled version shifts it by ~0.0008 — so a future caller picking the wrong one silently changes the correction profile. Given the hedging correction is already a questionable step (see separate finding), a latent second variant compounds the risk.
- **recommendation:** Delete apply_hedging_correction, or if kept for tests, mark it clearly internal/deprecated so it cannot be wired into the calibrate path by mistake.
- **verifier:** Confirmed against the code. apply_hedging_correction (flat shift, min(0.99, p+shift)) is defined at src/pipeline/calibration.py:103-114 and is never invoked anywhere in src/. A repo-wide grep shows the only call sites for the wired-in path use apply_hedging_correction_scaled (lines 82 and 92 inside calibrate()); the unscaled function is referenced only by tests/test_calibration.py (import + 5 assertions). No dynamic dispatch (getattr/globals) routes to it. So the "dead in src, alive only in tests" characterization is accurate.

The behavioral-divergence claim is also mathematically correct. At p=0.51: flat version returns min(0.99, 0.51+0.04)=0.55; scaled version computes distance=0.01, effective_shift=0.04*2*0.01=0.0008, returns ~0.5108. The two genuinely produce materially different corrections, and they sit in the same module with near-identical names (apply_hedging_correction vs apply_hedging_correction_scaled), which is a plausible mis-import/typo trap for a future caller.

That said, this is a latent/hypothetical hygiene issue, not a present defect — the calibrate() path is correct today, and the function is not orphaned cruft (it backs a real, passing test suite). The recommendation (delete, or mark internal/deprecated) is reasonable. The finding does not overclaim: it self-rates as low and frames the risk as latent.

### `READ-266` — .env.example and config.validate() disagree on which keys are required — operator gets contradictory signals
- **confirmed ✓** · source: readiness:ops-cost · effort: trivial · file: `/Users/bjgilbert/Projects/Zoltar/sigma-predict/.env.example:4-6`
- **evidence:** .env.example labels ANTHROPIC_API_KEY and TAVILY_API_KEY under '# Required' (lines 4-6) and lists OPENAI/GOOGLE under '# Optional'. But config.__post_init__ treats OpenAI or Google as a valid primary if Anthropic is absent (config.py:80-89), and validate() (config.py:151) accepts ANY one of the three LLM keys — so ANTHROPIC is not actually required. Meanwhile TAVILY is marked Required in the example but is never validated and search degrades silently if absent. The documented contract does not match enforced behavior, which will mislead operators during setup.
- **recommendation:** Reconcile: either make validate() enforce what .env.example claims, or rewrite .env.example to state 'at least one of ANTHROPIC/OPENAI/GOOGLE' and mark TAVILY optional-but-recommended. Keep the two in sync via the README.
- **verifier:** A genuine but narrower-than-described doc/enforcement mismatch exists. Confirmed against actual code (config is at /Users/bjgilbert/Projects/Zoltar/sigma-predict/src/config.py, not the cited config.py; line numbers coincidentally match).

CONFIRMED TRUE: (1) .env.example lines 4-6 mark ANTHROPIC_API_KEY and TAVILY_API_KEY under '# Required'. (2) validate() at src/config.py:147-155 only requires ANY ONE of ANTHROPIC/OPENAI/GOOGLE, so ANTHROPIC is not individually required — the 'Required' label overstates it. (3) validate() never checks tavily_api_key, so TAVILY's 'Required' label is unenforced. (4) __post_init__ (lines 74-89) accepts OpenAI or Google as primary when Anthropic is absent. So the documented contract genuinely diverges from enforced behavior, and an operator copying .env.example would reasonably believe both ANTHROPIC and TAVILY are mandatory when neither is individually enforced.

FALSE/FABRICATED in the evidence: (a) The finding claims .env.example 'lists OPENAI/GOOGLE under # Optional.' GOOGLE_AI_API_KEY appears NOWHERE in .env.example — fabricated. OPENAI is listed, but under 'Optional — needed for submitting predictions to platforms,' a mislabel the finding did not catch. (b) The finding claims 'search degrades silently if absent.' FALSE — src/pipeline/search.py:77 emits logger.warning('No TAVILY_API_KEY — search will return empty results (LLM knowledge only)'). Degradation is logged, not silent. (c) The recommendation to 'keep the two in sync via the README' references a README that does not exist in the repo (only .pytest_cache/README.md).

Operationally this is a setup-time, fully recoverable annoyance: validate() prints a clear error if zero LLM keys exist (predict.py:83-87 exits with a readable message), and missing Tavily produces an explicit runtime warning. The underlying defect — .env.example labels not matching validate(), plus GOOGLE_AI_API_KEY being undocumented — is real and worth a 5-minute fix, so I do not refute it outright. But the evidence is contaminated by one fabricated detail, one false claim, and a nonexistent remediation target.

### `READ-267` — Audit log is opt-in and off by default — no spend/usage trail unless an operator sets audit_path
- **confirmed ✓** · source: readiness:ops-cost · effort: trivial · file: `/Users/bjgilbert/Projects/Zoltar/sigma-predict/src/config.py:109`
- **evidence:** audit_path defaults to None (config.py:109) and the router only constructs an LLMAuditLogger when it is set (llm_router.py:90-92). With the default config — which is what every CLI and the Streamlit app use — there is NO persisted per-call record of provider, model, tokens, or cost. The only cost signal is the aggregate total_cost_usd on the saved PredictionRecord, which (per the accounting finding above) omits verification/decomposition spend. For a tool that incurs real per-call API spend across up to 10 providers, the default posture is zero operational observability into where money went.
- **recommendation:** Default audit_path to a sane location (e.g. data/audit.jsonl) or document prominently that operators should enable it. Given the cost-accounting gap, the audit log is currently the only place that could reconstruct true spend per provider/model.
- **verifier:** Confirmed against the actual code. The router path in the finding was wrong (it is src/pipeline/llm_router.py, not src/llm_router.py), but all substantive claims hold:

1. audit_path defaults to None — verified at config.py:109. Unlike the seven API keys (config.py:58-64) which read from os.environ, audit_path has NO env/.env wiring. A grep across the whole repo (src/, app.py) shows audit_path is never assigned anywhere except the dataclass default. So it cannot even be enabled without editing source or constructing Config programmatically.

2. Router only builds the logger when set — verified at llm_router.py:90-92: `LLMAuditLogger(config.audit_path) if config.audit_path else None`. All log_call sites (llm_router.py:212, 248, 299, 336) and flush (371-374, orchestrator.py:369) are guarded by `if self._audit is not None`. With the default, _audit is None → zero per-call records written.

3. Every entrypoint uses default Config — app.py:35 returns Config() with no audit_path. There is no separate CLI module in the repo (only tests/test_platform_clients.py matched). So the Streamlit app, the only user-facing entrypoint, runs with audit off.

4. The dependent accounting gap is real and material to this finding. total_cost in orchestrator.py only accumulates run_cost from forecast runs (line 230 inside the persona loop). The verification stages (Step 3b, lines 232-241), decomposition (Step ~2), and challenge steps issue LLM calls through the same router (verify/challenge log_call sites at llm_router.py:248-340) but their cost is never added to total_cost. So record.cost.total_cost_usd genuinely understates true spend. The audit log captures provider/model/tokens/cost_usd PER CALL across all methods (including verify/challenge), making it the only artifact that could reconstruct true per-provider spend — and it is off by default.

So: with the default config that every entrypoint uses, there is zero persisted per-call usage/cost trail, and the one aggregate cost number that does persist is incomplete. The finding is accurate and code-grounded.

On severity: the lens is ops-cost and severity is 'low'. This is a personal/single-operator tool (the audit module docstring explicitly says 'single-threaded, personal-tool context'). The data is not lost permanently in the sense that provider dashboards (Anthropic/OpenAI/etc.) still bill and show usage out-of-band, so an operator is not flying completely blind on total spend — just on per-call/per-provider attribution within this tool. That, plus the personal-tool framing, keeps it genuinely low rather than higher. The recommendation (default to data/audit.jsonl or add AUDIT_PATH env wiring + docs) is sound and cheap. Severity 'low' is appropriately calibrated; keep.

### `READ-253` — Cost/token figures are estimates from a hardcoded price table, not recorded actuals, and unknown models silently mis-price
- **confirmed ✓** · source: readiness:provenance · effort: small · file: `src/pipeline/llm_router.py:396-411; src/pipeline/forecaster.py:252`
- **evidence:** estimate_cost uses a hardcoded approximate March-2026 price table and `pricing.get(model, (3.0, 15.0))` defaults any unlisted model to Sonnet pricing (llm_router.py:409). CostTracking.total_cost_usd persisted on the record (models.py:201) is therefore a derived estimate, not the provider-reported cost, and is silently wrong for any model not in the table. Tokens_in/out for verify/challenge/cross_verify are logged as 0 (llm_router.py:258-259,308-309,346-347), so verification cost is uncounted in the audit log entirely. An auditor reading cost.total_cost_usd cannot trust it as an actual spend figure.
- **recommendation:** Record provider-reported usage/cost where available; tag stored costs as estimates with the price-table version used; raise or warn (not silently default) when a model is missing from the pricing table; capture verification token usage.
- **verifier:** All four code claims verified against the actual source. (1) estimate_cost (llm_router.py:396-409) uses a hardcoded "approximate, March 2026" price table and pricing.get(model, (3.0, 15.0)) silently defaults any unlisted model to Sonnet pricing — confirmed. (2) total_cost_usd persisted on CostTracking (models.py:201, default_factory in PredictionRecord at models.py:230) is set in forecaster.py:252 via router.estimate_cost(...), i.e. a derived estimate, never a provider-reported figure. The Anthropic SDK call (llm_router.py:59-70) returns only response.usage.input_tokens/output_tokens — no dollar cost — so cost is always computed locally. (3) verify/challenge/cross_verify log tokens_in=0 and tokens_out=0 (lines 258-259, 308-309, 346-347), and in these paths cost_usd is not passed to log_call at all (defaults to 0.0 per llm_audit.py:86,102), so verification spend is entirely uncounted in the audit log. (4) The estimate is surfaced to humans verbatim as a dollar figure (human_review.py:177) and summed in orchestrator (_add_costs, lines 558-563). The absent controls genuinely do not exist anywhere: no estimate tagging, no price-table versioning beyond a comment, no warn/raise on unknown models, no verification token capture. The finding is accurate and material as a provenance/auditability gap: an auditor reading cost.total_cost_usd cannot distinguish an estimate from an actual, an unknown model is silently mispriced as Sonnet, and verification cost is invisible. One caveat in the finding's favor and against over-rating: the recommendation to "record provider-reported cost" is only partially achievable since the host Anthropic SDK exposes usage but not cost; the genuinely actionable fixes are tagging estimates, warning on missing models, and capturing verification tokens.

### `READ-254` — Audit log buffers up to 10 entries in memory and can lose them on crash; appends are not atomic
- **confirmed ✓** · source: readiness:provenance · effort: trivial · file: `src/pipeline/llm_audit.py:69,104-119`
- **evidence:** LLMAuditLogger buffers entries (BUFFER_LIMIT=10, llm_audit.py:69,104-106) and only writes on flush. flush is called at pipeline end (orchestrator.py:369) but if the pipeline crashes or the process is killed mid-run, up to 10 logged calls are lost — exactly the calls relevant to a failure investigation. flush() also does a plain append with no fsync/atomic-write (llm_audit.py:113-115), unlike the registry which uses temp+os.replace; a crash mid-write can leave a torn final line.
- **recommendation:** Flush per-call (or lower the buffer) for an audit log, and fsync after write. The audit trail's value is highest precisely in the crash case the buffer drops.
- **verifier:** All five load-bearing claims verified against the actual code. (1) BUFFER_LIMIT=10 at llm_audit.py:69. (2) Entries buffered in self._buffer, flushed only on buffer-full or explicit flush() (lines 104-106, 108-119). (3) flush_audit() called at orchestrator.py:369 inside the linear body of predict_until_review (method spans lines 70-382) with NO try/finally protecting it — so any exception/crash/SIGKILL between lines 70 and 369 drops up to 10 buffered entries, including the very call that failed. (4) flush() does a plain open(\"a\") + f.write loop with no os.fsync and no temp+os.replace (lines 112-119). (5) The registry IS durable: store.py:101-107 writes to a .tmp file then os.replace() — confirming the cited durability double-standard. The torn-line claim is technically correct for OS-level crashes (no fsync); a clean process kill after the with-block exits would not tear a line because data is in the page cache, but the un-flushed buffered entries are lost in either case, so the core 'data loss on crash' claim holds independent of the torn-line subtlety. Severity LOW is well-calibrated: this is an opt-in (audit_path=None disables), single-threaded personal tool per the module docstring; lost data is metadata only (hashes, token counts, durations, costs) — never raw prompts — and the prediction record itself is persisted separately and atomically via the registry. Blast radius is limited to post-hoc cost/latency forensics for the crashed run, which is genuinely the case where an audit log is most valuable, as the finding notes. The recommendation (flush per-call + fsync) is sound and low-cost in this context.

### `READ-233` — Deliberation is the only LLM call site outside base_rate-search that degrades on failure — confirming the safe pattern was selectively applied
- **confirmed ✓** · source: readiness:resilience · effort: trivial · file: `/Users/bjgilbert/Projects/Zoltar/sigma-predict/src/pipeline/deliberation.py:126-152`
- **evidence:** deliberate() wraps router.call in try/except and on failure records `recommended_action: accept_aggregate` (lines 143-149) rather than crashing. This (with search_augmented_base_rate) is the second of only two pipeline LLM sites with degradation, while the load-bearing decompose/base_rate/forecast/pre-mortem sites have none. Deliberation is also conditional (only fires on disagreement), so the degradation is in a rarely-hit path while the always-hit forecasting path is unprotected. There is still no retry/backoff here — only catch-and-degrade.
- **recommendation:** Promote the catch-and-degrade pattern from deliberation/base_rate-search to the mandatory forecasting call sites, and layer retry/backoff beneath it so transient failures are retried before degradation is accepted.
- **verifier:** VERIFIED. Every factual claim in the finding checks out against the code.

1. deliberation.py:126-152 — deliberate() wraps router.call in try/except and on failure records recommended_action: accept_aggregate (lines 143-149) rather than propagating. Confirmed verbatim.

2. "Second of only two pipeline LLM call sites with degradation" — Confirmed. I enumerated all router.call sites: deliberation.py:127, forecaster.py:161 (main forecast), forecaster.py:204 (pre-mortem), base_rate.py:54 (estimate_base_rate), base_rate.py:199 (search_augmented_base_rate), decomposer.py:61. Only deliberation.py:127 and base_rate.py:199 (search_augmented) wrap the LLM call in catch-and-degrade. The other four (decompose, isolated base_rate, forecast, pre-mortem) have only JSON-parse error handling — if router.call itself raises, the exception propagates uncaught.

3. "Load-bearing forecast/base_rate/decompose/pre-mortem sites have no degradation" — Confirmed. Their try/except blocks only cover json.loads (degrading to base_rate / defaults on parse failure), NOT the network call. A transient API failure raises out of router.call before any parsing.

4. "No try/except wraps these in the orchestrator" — Confirmed. predict_until_review() (orchestrator.py:70-228+) calls decompose_question, estimate_base_rate, run_forecast with no surrounding try/except. The only try/except blocks in orchestrator.py (lines 429, 529, 544) are outside this main pipeline flow. An LLM-call exception crashes the prediction run.

5. "Deliberation is conditional, only fires on disagreement" — Confirmed. should_deliberate() (lines 26-62) gates it on inter_model_agreement < 0.6, high challenge vulnerability, or |pre_mortem_delta| > 0.1. So the degradation lives in a rarely-hit path while the always-hit forecast path is unprotected.

6. "No retry/backoff here — only catch-and-degrade" — Confirmed. grep for retry/backoff/tenacity/sleep/max_retries across src/pipeline found nothing. LLMRouter.call() (lines 188-227) has no retry. Note: the verify/challenge/cross_verify methods DO catch-and-degrade (returning VerificationError/None), but call() — the forecasting path — does not. (The anthropic SDK does perform 2 default transient retries internally, but that is SDK-default behavior, not application-level resilience for the broader provider surface, and does not refute the finding.)

The finding's framing — "the safe pattern was selectively applied to the rarely-hit deliberation/search paths while the mandatory forecasting path crashes on transient failure" — is accurate and material for production readiness. The recommendation (promote catch-and-degrade to mandatory call sites + layer retry/backoff beneath) is sound.

Materiality: This is a real resilience gap. A single transient LLM API error (rate limit overflow past SDK retries, 5xx, network blip) during decompose/base_rate/forecast aborts the entire prediction with no graceful degradation. For a batch forecasting pipeline this means lost work on the in-flight question.

### `SWEE-5` — challenge() assumes provider returns a mutable dict; non-dict/None return raises and is silently swallowed
- **REFUTED ✗** · source: sweep · effort: trivial · file: `/Users/bjgilbert/Projects/Zoltar/sigma-predict/src/pipeline/llm_router.py:296-320`
- **evidence:** `result = client.challenge(claim, evidence)` then immediately `result["status"] = "success"` (line 298). If a provider's challenge() returns None or a non-subscriptable object, this raises TypeError, which is caught by the broad `except Exception` (line 313) and reported as a 'failed' challenge with the error string — masking a contract/programming error as a normal challenge failure. verify() handles None explicitly; challenge() does not.
- **recommendation:** Guard: `if result is None: return None`, and verify result is a dict before mutating; otherwise log a distinct contract-violation warning rather than reporting it as a model challenge failure.
- **verifier:** Refuted on both the mechanism and the asymmetry claim.

CODE GROUNDING (verified):
- Router challenge() at /Users/bjgilbert/Projects/Zoltar/sigma-predict/src/pipeline/llm_router.py:296-320 does `result = client.challenge(claim, evidence)` then `result["status"] = "success"` (line 298), wrapped in a broad `except Exception` (line 313). That part of the description is accurate.
- The router only routes to clients in `_external_providers` (line 290 -> _get_external_client at 413-420), which are the sigma_verify client classes.
- All four challenge() implementations in /Users/bjgilbert/Projects/sigma-verify/src/sigma_verify/clients.py (lines 318, 451, 600, 937) are declared `-> dict[str, Any]` and follow an identical pattern: call `_parse_json_response(...)`, then immediately perform 4 subscript writes on the result (`parsed["model"]=`, `parsed["provider"]=`, `parsed["tier"]=`, `parsed["provenance_tag"]=`) BEFORE returning.

WHY THE TYPEERROR-MASKING SCENARIO IS UNREACHABLE:
If a client's parsed object were ever a non-dict or None, the client's own `parsed["model"] = ...` assignment would raise TypeError INSIDE the client, before it ever returns to the router. By the time the router executes `result["status"] = "success"`, the object is already a proven-subscriptable dict (the client just wrote four keys to it). So the router can never receive a non-dict/None from these clients without an exception already having been raised. The "raises TypeError at line 298, silently swallowed" path the finding describes cannot occur with any actual client. `_parse_json_response` (clients.py:162) also always returns a dict — worst case it returns `{"raw_response":..., "parse_error":True}`. (Edge note: valid JSON like a bare list/null would make _parse_json_response return a non-dict, but that surfaces as a raise inside the client at `parsed["model"]`, handled identically to any other client failure — not at router line 298.)

ASYMMETRY CLAIM IS FALSE:
The finding asserts "verify() handles None explicitly; challenge() does not." Reading verify() at lines 246-262, it does NOT handle None explicitly — the `if result is not None` on line 251 only guards the audit log's result_text string; the actual return on line 262 passes `result` straight through unchanged. The real difference is simply that challenge() MUTATES its result (adds status) while verify() does not. There is no None-guard in verify() to contrast against.

NET: The cited masking behavior depends on a client returning a non-dict/None without raising, which no current client can do given the dict-subscript-on-return contract. The supporting asymmetry argument misreads verify(). The recommendation (guard for None / verify dict) would be harmless defensive hardening against a hypothetical third-party client that violates the contract, but describes a bug that does not exist in the current codebase and masks nothing today.

### `SWEE-6` — Community-prediction signal is always stored under the bare platform key; the '_community' branch is dead
- **confirmed ✓** · source: sweep · effort: trivial · file: `/Users/bjgilbert/Projects/Zoltar/sigma-predict/src/pipeline/orchestrator.py:545-551`
- **evidence:** `signals = PlatformSignals()` starts with an empty `prices_at_prediction_time` dict (models.py:133). The code then does `if platform_key in signals.prices_at_prediction_time:` (line 548) where `platform_key = f"{platform.value}_community"`. Since the dict is always empty here, the condition is always False, so the value is always stored under `question.platform.value` and never under the `_community` key. The if-branch (line 549) is unreachable dead code; the suffix scheme it implements never takes effect.
- **recommendation:** Decide the intended key scheme and store directly (e.g. always under the '_community' key, or under the bare platform key) — remove the dead conditional.
- **verifier:** Confirmed at /Users/bjgilbert/Projects/Zoltar/sigma-predict/src/pipeline/orchestrator.py:542-551. `signals = PlatformSignals()` constructs the object with `prices_at_prediction_time: dict[...] = Field(default_factory=dict)` (real path src/models.py:133, not src/pipeline/models.py — minor path typo in the evidence, but the field and behavior are exactly as described). The dict is therefore empty immediately after construction. The only code between construction and the check (lines 545-547) calls `get_community_prediction` and computes `platform_key`; nothing writes to `prices_at_prediction_time`. So `if platform_key in signals.prices_at_prediction_time:` (line 548) is necessarily False, and execution always falls to the else branch (line 551), storing under the bare `question.platform.value`. The if-branch (line 549) implementing the `_community` suffix key is unreachable dead code. The suffix scheme never takes effect. This is confirmed, not refuted.

Materiality: low is correct. The only consumers (src/review/human_review.py:145 and :339) iterate over whatever keys exist and print/read values generically, so the value is still surfaced — just labeled with the bare platform key instead of the intended `_community` label. No crash, no data loss, no incorrect prediction. It is genuinely dead code with a cosmetic labeling effect, plus latent confusion risk if future code expects the `_community` key. The recommendation (pick one key scheme and store directly, drop the dead conditional) is sound.

### `SWEE-7` — effective_n heuristic uses dimensionally incoherent math and can collapse to a meaningless value
- **confirmed ✓** · source: sweep · effort: medium · file: `/Users/bjgilbert/Projects/Zoltar/sigma-predict/src/pipeline/aggregator.py:171-176`
- **evidence:** `expected_var_single = mean_p*(1-mean_p)*0.1` (an arbitrary 0.1 factor), then `ratio = actual_var / expected_var_single`, then `effective_n = max(1, min(n, int(round(n * min(1.0, ratio * n)))))`. The term `ratio * n` multiplies a variance ratio by n with no statistical justification; with tightly-clustered runs (actual_var near 0) effective_n collapses to 1 regardless of n, and the 0.1 fudge factor means the 'expected variance' is not grounded in any reference-class assumption. The docstring calls it 'a rough heuristic' but the output feeds nothing downstream that I can find — it is computed and stored but appears unused in calibration or CI.
- **recommendation:** Either replace with a defensible correlation-aware effective-N estimate, or if effective_n is not consumed anywhere, drop it to avoid presenting a spurious statistic.
- **verifier:** Code at aggregator.py:153-180 matches the cited snippet exactly. Assessing the three sub-claims against the code:

1. AD-HOC MATH (CONFIRMED): `expected_var_single = mean_p*(1-mean_p)*0.1` uses an undocumented 0.1 factor with no reference-class derivation, and `effective_n = ...round(n * min(1.0, ratio*n))` multiplies a variance ratio by n. I simulated the function: it behaves as a near-binary step — clustered runs (e.g. [0.50,0.501,0.499,0.50,0.502]) give 1, and once spread exceeds a narrow band (d~0.05 at mean 0.5, n=5) it saturates to full n. The transition threshold is governed entirely by the arbitrary 0.1 constant. The author honestly labels it 'a rough heuristic,' but the critique that the math lacks statistical justification is fair.

2. 'COLLAPSES TO 1 REGARDLESS OF N' (MISLEADING FRAMING): For tightly-clustered runs effective_n does go to 1, but this is the EXPLICITLY DOCUMENTED INTENDED behavior (docstring: 'if all estimates are the same, effective_n = 1'). Treating intended correlation-detection behavior as a defect overstates the problem.

3. 'OUTPUT FEEDS NOTHING DOWNSTREAM / UNUSED' (REFUTED — load-bearing error): grep shows effective_n is consumed at src/review/human_review.py:348: `if record.aggregation.effective_n < 3 and len(record.runs) >= 3:` which raises a human-review flag 'Low effective N — runs may be correlated', and displayed at :122. It is NOT unused. The finding's recommendation explicitly hinges on this false premise ('if effective_n is not consumed anywhere, drop it').

Net: the underlying observation (ad-hoc, undocumented heuristic math) is genuine and the location/evidence on the formula is accurate. But the finding contains a material factual error (claims unused; it is consumed in a human-review gate) and mischaracterizes intended behavior as collapse. Since it IS consumed only in a soft human-review warning flag — not in calibration, CI, or the published probability — the practical impact is small: a poorly-calibrated heuristic could spuriously raise or suppress a 'may be correlated' nudge to a human reviewer. Real, but low severity, and the recommendation needs correcting (the 'drop it' branch is invalid).

### `SWEE-8` — Confidence interval mixes calibrated/extremized center with raw-scale stdev and a fixed fallback
- **confirmed ✓** · source: sweep · effort: small · file: `/Users/bjgilbert/Projects/Zoltar/sigma-predict/src/pipeline/orchestrator.py:347-349`
- **evidence:** `stdev = aggregation.stdev` is the standard deviation of the RAW per-run probabilities. The CI is built as `cal_prob ± 1.96*stdev` where `cal_prob` is the post-calibration (and potentially extremized) center. The calibration transform (Platt/isotonic/hedging) is nonlinear in probability space, so adding a raw-space stdev around the transformed center is inconsistent. When stdev==0 (single run, or identical runs) it falls back to a hardcoded ±0.05 band that has no basis in model uncertainty.
- **recommendation:** Propagate uncertainty through the same transform applied to the center (transform cal of center±stdev on the raw scale, then take the calibrated endpoints), or document the CI as a rough heuristic band.
- **verifier:** Confirmed at orchestrator.py:347-349. All factual claims verified against the code:

1. `stdev = aggregation.stdev` is the std dev of RAW per-run probabilities — aggregator.py:77 computes `stdev = float(np.std(probabilities, ddof=1))` over the raw `probabilities` array, stored unchanged on the Aggregation.

2. The CI is built as `cal_prob ± 1.96*stdev` (orchestrator.py:348-349) where `cal_prob` comes from `calibrate(probability=aggregation.raw_aggregate, ...)` (orchestrator.py:339-340) — i.e. the post-calibration center.

3. The center is potentially extremized BEFORE calibration: aggregator.py:90-105 applies `_extremize(..)` via log-odds when n_distinct_providers>=2, then `calibrate()` applies a nonlinear transform — Platt (sigmoid), isotonic on logit scale, or distance-weighted hedging (apply_hedging_correction_scaled, calibration.py:117-130, shift scales with distance from 0.5). All are nonlinear in probability space. So placing a symmetric raw-scale ±1.96*stdev band around the transformed center is genuinely inconsistent; the band does not represent the uncertainty mapped through the transform, and the asymmetry grows near 0/1.

4. The stdev==0 fallback is a hardcoded ±0.05 band (orchestrator.py:348-349) with no model-uncertainty basis. It fires for single runs (aggregator.py:64-72 sets stdev=0.0) and identical runs.

Materiality is genuinely LOW and that severity should be kept. The confidence_interval is consumed only for display in the human review summary (human_review.py:140); grep found no use in submission, scoring, calibration, or any automated decision path. It is a communicative heuristic band, not a load-bearing computation — so the inconsistency mis-describes uncertainty to a human reader but does not corrupt any forecast or decision. The recommendation (propagate through the same transform, or document as a rough heuristic band) is sound and proportionate.

This is a real, correctly-scoped low-severity finding, not a false positive.

- **confirmed ✓** · source: sweep · effort: trivial · file: `/Users/bjgilbert/Projects/Zoltar/sigma-predict/src/pipeline/calibration.py:103-114`
- **evidence:** `apply_hedging_correction(probability, shift)` applies a flat shift. The calibrate() path only ever calls `apply_hedging_correction_scaled(...)` (lines 82, 92). CONFIRMED parts:

The calibrate() path uses only apply_hedging_correction_scaled at lines 82 and 92 (both the isotonic-fallback and Phase 0 branches). VERIFIED by grep: the only src/ call sites are the two scaled calls.

REFUTED part (material): The finding's central evidence claim — "A repo-wide grep shows no caller of the unscaled apply_hedging_correction other than its own definition" — is FALSE. tests/test_calibration.py imports it (line 7) and exercises it in a dedicated TestHedgingCorrection class with 5 assertions (lines 19, 24, 28, 32, 36). That is legitimate but modest — keeping a documented flat-shift baseline alongside its scaled variant is a defensible design choice, and the function carries test coverage.
