### `SWEE-10` — Temporal firewall in search-augmented base rate is silently a no-op when results carry no date
- **confirmed ✓** · source: sweep · effort: medium · file: `/Users/bjgilbert/Projects/Zoltar/sigma-predict/src/pipeline/base_rate.py:136-158`
- **evidence:** The function filters results published within 180 days via `getattr(r, "published_date", None) or getattr(r, "date", None)` (line 140). But `SearchResult` (search.py:59-66) has only title/url/content/score — no published_date or date field. So `pub_date_str` is always None, the date check is skipped, and EVERY result is included. The docstring even contradicts itself: lines 100-103 say 'temporal firewall is not currently active — SearchResult has no published_date field. All search results pass through regardless of recency', while lines 136-137 and 193 present the firewall as applied ('historical only — temporal firewall applied'). The contamination-control claim fed to the extractor LLM is false in practice.
- **recommendation:** Either add a published_date to SearchResult (populate from Tavily's published_date field) and make the firewall real, or remove the firewall code and the 'temporal firewall applied' label so the prompt does not assert a control that is not enforced.
- **verifier:** Confirmed at code level. SearchResult (search.py:59-66) is a dataclass with only title/url/content/score; its sole constructor (search.py:114-122) never sets published_date or date. Therefore in base_rate.py:140, `getattr(r, "published_date", None) or getattr(r, "date", None)` always evaluates to None, the `if pub_date_str:` block is never entered, and every result reaches `filtered_results.append(r)` at line 158. The temporal firewall (lines 136-158) is a genuine no-op.

The self-contradiction is also real and verbatim: the docstring at lines 100-103 states "temporal firewall is not currently active — SearchResult has no published_date field. All search results pass through regardless of recency," while line 193 injects "Search results (historical only — temporal firewall applied)" into the user prompt sent to the extractor LLM. So the system asserts to the model a contamination control it does not enforce. In a forecasting pipeline whose stated design centers on contamination controls (lines 98-103), a false control claim fed to an LLM is a legitimate integrity defect, not a cosmetic one.

Materiality is bounded, which keeps severity low rather than higher: (1) the no-op is already documented honestly in the docstring, so it is a known state, not a hidden trap; (2) the augmented rate is only a statistical anchor that narrows, not replaces, the LLM estimate (lines 105-107); (3) the query is already restricted to "historical frequency statistics," and the extractor receives only short excerpts. The harm is a second-order recency-contamination risk on an anchor plus a false label to the LLM. The recommendation (populate published_date from Tavily's published_date field and make the firewall real, or remove the firewall and the label) is sound — note Tavily's basic search payload here sets include_raw_content=False and does not request published_date, so making it real requires both a field addition and a payload/parse change.

### `SWEE-11` — Aggregate-run reasoning summary includes parse-failed runs that were excluded from the probability
- **confirmed ✓** · source: sweep · effort: trivial · file: `/Users/bjgilbert/Projects/Zoltar/sigma-predict/src/pipeline/orchestrator.py:299`
- **evidence:** For aggregation, parse-failed runs are excluded into `valid_runs` (lines 252-282). But the challenge step builds the synthetic aggregate via `get_aggregate_run(record.runs, record.aggregation)` (line 299) passing ALL runs, including parse-failed ones whose probability defaulted to base_rate and whose reasoning_chain is empty. get_aggregate_run averages base_rate_used and confidence over all runs (aggregator.py:271-291), so parse-failed runs dilute the summarized base rate / confidence that the challenge model sees, even though the actual aggregate probability excluded them.
- **recommendation:** Pass `valid_runs` to get_aggregate_run for the challenge step so the synthetic aggregate's metadata matches the runs that actually produced the aggregate probability.
- **verifier:** Confirmed at the code level. Three load-bearing claims all verify:

1. orchestrator.py:252 builds `valid_runs` as a new list comprehension excluding parse_failed runs; it does NOT mutate `record.runs`. So at line 299, `get_aggregate_run(record.runs, record.aggregation)` is passed ALL runs, including parse-failed ones. Confirmed.

2. aggregator.py:271 and :291 — `get_aggregate_run` computes `mean_base_rate = np.mean([r.base_rate_used for r in runs])` and `confidence_self_score = mean([r.confidence_self_score for r in runs])` over every run passed in, and builds `combined_reasoning` (lines 262-268) from every run. But `probability = aggregation.raw_aggregate` (line 289), which was computed from `valid_runs` only (aggregate_runs call at orchestrator.py:276-282). Confirmed mismatch: probability excludes parse-failed runs, metadata does not.

3. Parse-failed run contents (forecaster.py:178-245): on JSONDecodeError, `data={}`, so `probability` defaults to `base_rate` (line 181), `reasoning_chain` defaults to "" (line 244), `confidence_self_score` defaults to 5 (line 243), `base_rate_used=base_rate` (line 235). So a parse-failed run contributes a probability==base_rate, empty reasoning, and confidence 5 to the synthetic aggregate's averaged metadata. Confirmed.

Materiality is real: challenge_forecast (verification.py:223-234) embeds the synthetic run's `base_rate_used`, `reasoning_chain`, and `inside_view_adjustment` into the claim/evidence strings the challenge model reads. So the challenge model sees a base rate / confidence / reasoning summary diluted by parse-failed runs while the displayed probability was computed without them — an internal inconsistency, and one that directly contradicts the orchestrator's own stated intent at line 251 ("Exclude parse_failed runs — they default to base_rate and anchor the aggregate"). The recommendation (pass valid_runs) is correct and matches the intent.

Refutation attempts that fail: (a) record.runs is not mutated, so the bug is not a non-issue. (b) The divergence is genuine, not cosmetic — averaged base_rate and confidence shift. The recommendation is sound.

Bounding factors that keep severity LOW (not higher): the divergence only occurs on the parse-failure error path (when n_valid == n_total the run sets are identical); it affects only the challenge step's INPUT metadata, not the final aggregate probability that is the actual prediction output; and the challenge is a qualitative LLM stress-test, so soft metadata drift in an evidence string has limited and non-deterministic downstream effect. No numeric correctness of the prediction is harmed.

### `SWEE-12` — forecaster always overwrites parse-failure probability with base_rate but still records empty fields as a real forecast
- **confirmed ✓** · source: sweep · effort: small · file: `/Users/bjgilbert/Projects/Zoltar/sigma-predict/src/pipeline/forecaster.py:181-247`
- **evidence:** On JSON parse failure, `data = {}` and `parse_failed=True` (lines 178-179), then `probability = float(data.get("probability", base_rate))` yields base_rate, and search_quality_score defaults to 5 (line 234), confidence_self_score defaults to 5 (line 243). A parse-failed run is therefore stored with a plausible-looking search_quality_score=5 and confidence=5, which then feed the validator's search_quality average (validator.py:107) and any confidence aggregation. The defaults make a failed run look like a mediocre-but-valid one in downstream stats, partially undermining the parse-failure exclusion done at aggregation time.
- **recommendation:** On parse failure, set search_quality_score and confidence_self_score to 0 (or sentinel) so failed runs do not inflate quality/confidence averages, and exclude parse_failed runs from validator.validate_runs search-quality averaging.
- **verifier:** CONFIRMED. The finding's core mechanism and both downstream-impact claims are accurate against the actual code.

Mechanism (forecaster.py:170-247): On JSON parse failure, `data = {}` and `parse_failed = True` (lines 178-179). Then `probability = float(data.get("probability", base_rate))` yields base_rate (line 181). Critically, line 234 builds the RunResult with `search_quality_score=int(data.get("search_quality_score", 5))` and line 243 with `confidence_self_score=int(data.get("confidence_self_score", 5))`. With `data={}`, both resolve to the literal default 5. So a parse-failed run is persisted carrying a plausible search_quality_score=5 and confidence=5 — confirmed exactly as described. (Note: the RunResult model default for search_quality_score is actually 0 at models.py:99, but the forecaster's explicit `.get(..., 5)` overrides that to 5, so the finding's stated value of 5 is correct, not a misread.)

Downstream impact 1 — validator search-quality average (validator.py:106-114): CONFIRMED. `avg_sq = sum(r.search_quality_score for r in record.runs) / len(record.runs)` iterates over `record.runs` (ALL runs), not over the parse-failure-filtered set. A failed run injects 5, which pulls the average up and can suppress a legitimate search_quality_gate warning (threshold = 3). This is a genuine diagnostic-fidelity defect: the parse-failure exclusion that orchestrator.py:251-252 (`valid_runs = [r for r in record.runs if not r.parse_failed]`) applies to the probability aggregate is NOT applied to the validator's search-quality averaging — exactly the inconsistency the finding describes.

Downstream impact 2 — confidence aggregation: PARTIALLY CONFIRMED and actually slightly understated. The main probability aggregate (`aggregate_runs`, called with `valid_runs` at orchestrator.py:276-282) correctly excludes failed runs. BUT `get_aggregate_run` is called with `record.runs` (ALL runs) at orchestrator.py:299, and at aggregator.py:290-291 it computes `confidence_self_score = round(mean([r.confidence_self_score for r in runs]))` over all runs — so a failed run's default 5 does contaminate the aggregate-run confidence used in the challenge/pre-mortem step. The finding's hedged phrase "any confidence aggregation" is borne out.

Materiality: Real but bounded. It does NOT corrupt the actual forecast probability (correctly excluded at aggregation). It degrades two diagnostic signals — the search_quality warning gate and the challenge-step confidence display — by making a failed run look like a mediocre-but-valid one. The recommendation (set the two scores to 0/sentinel on parse failure, and average over non-failed runs in validate_runs) is sound and directly addresses both code paths. The validator is also warning-only/non-blocking by default, which further limits blast radius.

### `SWEE-16` — _map_event splits a string category into tags but real Kalshi categories aren't comma-delimited
- **confirmed ✓** · source: sweep · effort: trivial · file: `src/platforms/kalshi.py:74`
- **evidence:** tags=data.get("category", "").split(",") if data.get("category") else []. If `category` is ever returned as a non-string (e.g. a list or null object) `.split` raises AttributeError; the call is inside the list comprehension in get_questions with no per-item guard, so one malformed event aborts the entire page (caught by the broad except -> returns []). Even in the happy path, splitting a single category label like "Politics & Economics" on comma produces a one-element list, so the 'multiple tags' behavior the name implies essentially never fires.
- **recommendation:** Guard the type (isinstance check like the markets path at line 50 already does) and only split if comma actually present, or wrap the value in a single-element list.
- **verifier:** Confirmed at src/platforms/kalshi.py:74. The cited line is exact: `tags=data.get("category", "").split(",") if data.get("category") else []`.

All three sub-claims hold against the code:

1. AttributeError on non-string category: The guard is `if data.get("category")` (truthiness), not a type check. A truthy non-string value (non-empty list, dict) passes the guard and then `.split(",")` is called on it, which raises AttributeError. Verified — the truthy guard does not exclude non-strings. (Note: a literal `null`/None category is falsy and safely yields `[]`, so the "null object" example in the evidence is wrong, but the list/dict case is valid.)

2. One malformed event aborts the whole page: `_map_event` is called inside the list comprehension at line 130 (`return [_map_event(e) for e in events[:limit]]`), which sits inside the try block. A raised AttributeError propagates out of the comprehension, is caught by the broad `except Exception` at line 134, and `get_questions` returns `[]`. Confirmed — no per-item guard; one bad event discards the entire page.

3. Single-label comma split: `"Politics & Economics".split(",")` returns a one-element list, so the implied multi-tag behavior rarely fires. Confirmed, but cosmetic.

The recommendation is also grounded: the markets path at line 50 already uses an isinstance guard (`if isinstance(data.get("tags"), list) else []`), so the events path is genuinely inconsistent and less defensive.

Materiality is modest. Tags are non-load-bearing — used only for keyword text (orchestrator.py:519) and prompt display (decomposer.py:58), not correctness or resolution. The AttributeError path requires Kalshi to return `category` as a truthy non-string, contrary to its documented string field, so it is a defensive-robustness gap rather than a likely real-world break. The file is an acknowledged read-only stub (line 1 docstring).

### `SWEE-21` — Kalshi event mapping produces empty close_date/resolve_date/community_prediction, so events are non-actionable as questions
- **confirmed ✓** · source: sweep · effort: medium · file: `src/platforms/kalshi.py:61`
- **evidence:** _map_event hardcodes resolution_criteria="", close_date="", resolve_date="", community_prediction=None, url="". get_questions maps the /events endpoint through _map_event, so every browseable Kalshi 'question' has no close date, no price, and an empty URL — yet get_question(ticker) expects a market ticker, not an event_ticker. The IDs returned by get_questions (event_ticker) are not valid inputs to get_question (which hits /markets/{ticker}). The list and detail endpoints return non-interoperable IDs.
- **recommendation:** Either fetch markets within each event and emit those (with real tickers, prices, dates), or document clearly that get_questions returns browse-only event stubs whose IDs cannot be passed to get_question. The current shape silently breaks the list-then-fetch workflow.
- **verifier:** CONFIRMED via code at src/platforms/kalshi.py. Every factual claim holds:

1. _map_event (lines 61-75) hardcodes resolution_criteria="", fine_print="", created_at="", close_date="", resolve_date="", community_prediction=None, url="". Only question_id (event_ticker), title, description, BINARY type, and category-derived tags are populated. VERIFIED.

2. get_questions (line 124-130) hits /events and maps each through _map_event, so every browseable Kalshi "question" is an empty stub. VERIFIED.

3. get_question (line 144) hits /markets/{question_id}; get_community_prediction (line 167) also hits /markets/{question_id}. VERIFIED.

4. ID non-interoperability is real. get_questions emits event_ticker (e.g. "ELECTION-2026"); get_question requires a market ticker (e.g. "AAPL-26MAR-100"). These are distinct Kalshi namespaces (events contain markets), confirmed by tests/test_platform_clients.py:80-107 which use exactly these two ID shapes. Passing an event_ticker to /markets/{ticker} 404s, and get_question raises ValueError. The abstract base.py contract (lines 46-59) explicitly documents that get_question accepts "the platform's identifier for the question" — i.e. the IDs returned by get_questions — so this violates the declared interface contract, not just an undocumented assumption.

Additional finding (impact is broader than the report states): in the actual consumer (pages/2_Predict.py:140-151), the Search workflow does NOT re-fetch via get_question — it pushes the selected event stub straight into KEY_PIPELINE_QUESTION. The pipeline then runs on a question where _compute_horizon returns None (orchestrator.py:527, empty resolve_date) and get_community_prediction(event_ticker) silently returns None (orchestrator.py:545; the 404 is swallowed at kalshi.py:175-177). So browsing Kalshi yields predictions with no horizon, no community-price calibration anchor, no URL, and IDs that cannot round-trip to the detail endpoint. The community_prediction column (2_Predict.py:133) and Close column (line 132) render blank.

The recommendation is sound: either fetch nested markets per event (Kalshi supports nested markets) and emit real tickers/prices/dates, or document that get_questions returns browse-only event stubs.

Genuine and material.

### `SWEE-22` — Auth scheme for Kalshi and Polymarket is almost certainly wrong (both use plain Bearer)
- **confirmed ✓** · source: sweep · effort: medium · file: `src/platforms/kalshi.py:90`
- **evidence:** headers["Authorization"] = f"Bearer {api_key}" in both kalshi.py:90 and polymarket.py:69. Kalshi's trade-api/v2 uses RSA request signing (KALSHI-ACCESS-KEY + KALSHI-ACCESS-SIGNATURE + timestamp headers), not a static Bearer token. Polymarket CLOB authenticated endpoints use an L2 HMAC header scheme (POLY_ADDRESS / POLY_SIGNATURE / POLY_TIMESTAMP / POLY_API_KEY), not Bearer. As stubs these clients only call public read endpoints so it's latent, but the moment an authenticated call is attempted (or a key is set), the Bearer header is silently ignored or rejected. The 'if api_key' branch creates a false sense that auth is wired up.
- **recommendation:** Remove the misleading Bearer header for these two platforms until the real signing schemes are implemented, or implement the documented schemes. Leaving a wrong-but-present Authorization header invites a future submit_prediction to look authenticated when it isn't.
- **verifier:** Confirmed against source. kalshi.py:90 and polymarket.py:69 both set headers["Authorization"] = f"Bearer {api_key}" inside an `if api_key:` branch. config.py:63-64 wires KALSHI_API_KEY / POLYMARKET_API_KEY from env, and the ONLY consumers of those keys are these two Bearer-header lines.

The factual auth-scheme claims are accurate:
- Kalshi trade-api/v2 (exactly the BASE_URL used, api.elections.kalshi.com/trade-api/v2) authenticates with RSA-PSS request signing via KALSHI-ACCESS-KEY / KALSHI-ACCESS-SIGNATURE / KALSHI-ACCESS-TIMESTAMP headers, not a static Bearer token.
- Polymarket CLOB authenticated endpoints use L1 EIP-712 or L2 HMAC headers (POLY_ADDRESS / POLY_SIGNATURE / POLY_TIMESTAMP / POLY_API_KEY / POLY_PASSPHRASE), not Bearer.

Materiality: I tried to refute on the grounds that nothing is broken. That refutation partially holds — there is NO live defect. Both clients are explicit read-only stubs; all HTTP calls (/events, /markets) hit public endpoints that ignore an unexpected Authorization header; submit_prediction raises NotImplementedError before any authenticated call is ever made. So setting an API key today neither helps nor breaks anything; the header is inert.

But the finding does not claim a live bug — it explicitly scopes itself as latent and classifies severity as low. On those terms it survives: the Bearer scheme is genuinely wrong for both platforms, the `if api_key` branch creates a real false impression that auth is partially wired, and the recommendation (remove the misleading header until real signing is implemented) is proportionate and correct. This is a legitimate latent-correctness / code-clarity issue, accurately self-rated low.

One minor overstatement: the title says "almost certainly wrong" — it is definitively wrong versus the documented schemes, which strengthens rather than weakens the finding.

### `SWEE-23` — No response size / content-type guard before .json() on fully untrusted remote bodies
- **REFUTED ✗** · source: sweep · effort: small · file: `src/platforms/metaculus.py:108`
- **evidence:** resp.json() is called directly on remote responses across all three clients (metaculus.py:108/123/161/189, kalshi.py:126/146/169, polymarket.py:98/113/134) with timeout=30.0 but no max response size and no content-type check. httpx will buffer the entire body into memory before parsing; a hostile or malfunctioning endpoint can return an arbitrarily large body or a deeply nested JSON document, consuming memory in the synchronous worker. raise_for_status only gates HTTP status, not body size.
- **recommendation:** Set httpx limits and/or check Content-Length / content-type before json(), and consider a max-bytes read. Low severity given these are first-party APIs, but the clients treat the bodies as fully trusted.
- **verifier:** CODE-ACCURATE BUT NOT MATERIAL. The factual claims are all verified against the source:

1. resp.json() is called directly on remote bodies at exactly the cited lines: metaculus.py:108, 123, 161, 189; kalshi.py:126, 146, 169; polymarket.py:98, 113, 134. CONFIRMED.
2. All three clients use httpx.Client with timeout=30.0 and no httpx.Limits/max_bytes config (metaculus.py:70-74, kalshi.py:91-95, polymarket.py:70-74). CONFIRMED.
3. raise_for_status() gates only HTTP status, not body size. CONFIRMED — httpx does buffer the full body before .json().

So the mechanism described is real. The problem is the THREAT MODEL, which makes this immaterial:

- The endpoints are NOT untrusted in any attacker-controllable sense. BASE_URL is a hardcoded module constant pointing to the three legitimate first-party prediction-market APIs (www.metaculus.com/api2, api.elections.kalshi.com, clob.polymarket.com). The finding's own title says "fully untrusted remote bodies" and the evidence says "treat the bodies as fully trusted" — these are contradictory, and reality is the latter: trusted first-party APIs.
- src/config.py exposes only API KEYS as env-configurable (lines 62-64); the base URLs are NOT overridable via config or env. There is no injection path. An attacker would need to either MITM TLS (much bigger problem than memory) or compromise Metaculus/Kalshi/Polymarket themselves.
- For the "hostile or malfunctioning endpoint returns an arbitrarily large body" scenario to bite, one of three major prediction-market operators would have to turn hostile or malfunction badly — at which point an OOM in a synchronous Streamlit worker is a self-inflicted availability blip on a single-user analytics app, not a security boundary breach. No data integrity or confidentiality impact.
- This is a Streamlit prediction-hub app (app.py), not a multi-tenant service. A worker exhausting memory affects only the operator running it. There is no privilege boundary crossed.

The finding is a generic "best-practice hardening" observation (set httpx.Limits, check Content-Length) dressed up with a "fully untrusted" framing that the code does not support. It is the kind of finding that is technically true of nearly any httpx-based client against any API and provides essentially no risk reduction here. The exception handlers also already catch parse/transport failures and degrade gracefully (return [] or raise ValueError), so a malformed body is handled; only the unbounded-size case is unguarded, and that case requires a hostile first-party API.

Real bug? No — it is a defensible coding choice for a first-party-API client, not a defect. Even as a hardening nit, "low" overstates it given the absence of any attacker-controllable endpoint. I would lower it below actionable (informational at most). Default-to-false applies: the genuine-AND-material bar is not met.

### `SWEE-24` — Cross-module: _collect_platform_signals '_community' suffix branch is dead because the dict starts empty
- **confirmed ✓** · source: sweep · effort: trivial · file: `src/pipeline/orchestrator.py:548`
- **evidence:** PlatformSignals.prices_at_prediction_time defaults to an empty dict (models.py:133). In _collect_platform_signals: `if platform_key in signals.prices_at_prediction_time:` where platform_key = f"{platform}_community" — but the dict is freshly empty (PlatformSignals() at line 542), so this is always False and the `_community`-keyed branch never executes. The stored key is always the bare platform name, never the intended `<platform>_community` key. This is consumer-side of the platform clients but is a direct footgun in how the clients' community_prediction output is recorded.
- **recommendation:** Decide the intended key unconditionally (likely always `<platform>_community`) and drop the membership check, which can never be true for a newly constructed PlatformSignals.
- **verifier:** Confirmed against the code. At src/pipeline/orchestrator.py:542 `signals = PlatformSignals()` is freshly constructed, and PlatformSignals.prices_at_prediction_time defaults to an empty dict (src/models.py:133, `Field(default_factory=dict)`). At lines 547-551, `platform_key = f"{question.platform.value}_community"` and the guard `if platform_key in signals.prices_at_prediction_time:` is therefore always False on the empty dict, so the `_community`-keyed branch (549) is dead and the value is always stored under the bare platform name (551). The finding's core structural claim is exactly correct.

The finding actually UNDERSTATES the impact. There is a real producer/consumer key mismatch: the human-review divergence check at src/review/human_review.py:340 reads `ps.get("metaculus_community")` — precisely the `<platform>_community` key the dead branch was meant to produce. Since the producer can never write that key (it writes bare `"metaculus"`, confirmed via Platform.METACULUS.value == "metaculus" at models.py:14), `community` is always None and the "Diverges from community by ..." review flag at lines 341-345 can never fire. This is a silently disabled safety/quality check, not a cosmetic dead branch.

Minor evidence imprecision: the finding cites the default as `src/pipeline/models.py:133`, but the actual path is `src/models.py:133` (line number exact, directory qualifier wrong). Does not affect the conclusion. The recommendation (store the `_community` key unconditionally and drop the membership check) is correct and would also restore the divergence flag.

### `SWEE-28` — RunResult.search_persona default is a literal string that bypasses the SearchPersona enum
- **confirmed ✓** · source: sweep · effort: trivial · file: `/Users/bjgilbert/Projects/Zoltar/sigma-predict/src/models.py:96`
- **evidence:** search_persona: str = SearchPersona.NEWS.value — the field is typed plain str, so the enum gives no validation. Any arbitrary string is accepted (e.g. a typo'd persona), and there is no constraint that values come from SearchPersona. Same pattern for PeripheralActor.influence_estimate: str = 'low' (models.py:72) which carries a '# low | medium | high' comment but, unlike Actor.influence_estimate (line 59, a Literal), accepts anything.
- **recommendation:** Type search_persona and PeripheralActor.influence_estimate as the SearchPersona enum / Literal['low','medium','high'] to get validation, matching Actor.influence_estimate.
- **verifier:** Code-grounded verification confirms the factual claims. At src/models.py:96, search_persona: str = SearchPersona.NEWS.value is indeed a plain str field, so the enum provides no validation. At models.py:72 PeripheralActor.influence_estimate: str = "low" (with a "# low | medium | high" comment) is a plain str, whereas Actor.influence_estimate at models.py:59 is Literal["low","medium","high"]. I empirically confirmed the asymmetry: PeripheralActor(influence_estimate="banana") is accepted; the same value on Actor raises ValidationError. PeripheralActor.influence_estimate is populated from raw LLM JSON (decomposer.py:121, pa.get("influence_estimate","low")) with no normalization, so an off-vocabulary/typo value would silently persist — this is the only genuine validation gap.

However, two qualifiers matter. (1) The recommendation to type search_persona as the SearchPersona enum is partly WRONG: aggregator.py:282 deliberately sets search_persona="aggregate", a sentinel that is NOT a valid enum value (confirmed: enum values are academic/news/contrarian/domain_expert/meta_forecaster). Applying the recommendation naively would break the aggregate-run path with a ValidationError. So the loose str typing on search_persona is partly load-bearing, not pure oversight. (2) Materiality is genuinely low: the affected fields only feed verification prompt strings (verification.py:112,154,175,233) and human-review console display (human_review.py:72) — cosmetic text, never a numeric/probability pathway. search_persona itself is only ever set internally from persona.value (forecaster.py:231) or the "aggregate" sentinel, never from free external input, so the "typo'd persona" risk is largely theoretical.

Net: the finding is real and code-accurate (a true typing-validation inconsistency, demonstrable), but its search_persona recommendation would introduce a regression, and the practical impact is cosmetic. The PeripheralActor Literal fix is the only valid actionable part.

### `SWEE-31` — Calibration phase has a coverage gap when Platt threshold is reached but params are missing
- **REFUTED ✗** · source: sweep · effort: small · file: `/Users/bjgilbert/Projects/Zoltar/sigma-predict/src/pipeline/calibration.py:62-100`
- **evidence:** Phase 2 branch is `if n_resolved >= min_resolved_for_platt and platt_params:`. min_resolved_for_platt and min_resolved_for_isotonic are both 50 by default (config.py:41-42). When n_resolved >= 50 but platt_params is None (refit not yet run, or load_platt_params returned None), the first branch is skipped and the elif `n_resolved >= min_resolved_for_isotonic` (also 50) catches it — so a mature dataset silently falls back to isotonic/hedging instead of the intended Platt scaling, with only an info log. With the defaults equal, the isotonic phase window (20-49 per the docstring) does not exist at all.
- **recommendation:** Lower min_resolved_for_isotonic below min_resolved_for_platt to make the documented 20-49 isotonic window real, and explicitly log/handle the 'Platt-eligible but params missing' case rather than silently degrading.
- **verifier:** REFUTED. The finding's central factual premise is false. It claims "min_resolved_for_platt and min_resolved_for_isotonic are both 50 by default (config.py:41-42)" and concludes "With the defaults equal, the isotonic phase window (20-49 per the docstring) does not exist at all." The actual config (src/config.py:41-42) sets min_resolved_for_platt=50 and min_resolved_for_isotonic=20 — they are NOT equal. The 20-49 isotonic window is real and is even covered by a passing test (tests/test_calibration.py:161-170, with the comment "n_resolved=25: 20<=25<50 -> Phase 1").

The recommendation ("Lower min_resolved_for_isotonic below min_resolved_for_platt to make the documented 20-49 isotonic window real") prescribes a change to a condition that already holds: 20 is already below 50.

The secondary claim is also mischaracterized. When n_resolved>=50 but platt_params is None, the code does NOT fall back to "isotonic/hedging" ambiguously — because 50>=20, the elif at calibration.py:70 catches it and runs isotonic regression (the next-best calibration method), with an info log. Falling back from Platt to isotonic on a mature dataset is reasonable graceful degradation, not a "silent" coverage gap. There is no n_resolved value that hits no branch; the else at line 90 covers everything below 20. There is no actual coverage gap.

Tangential observation (not the finding as stated, and not material to scoring it): save_platt_params and fit_platt are never called anywhere in src/, so load_platt_params (orchestrator.py:336) will essentially always return None, meaning Phase 2 Platt scaling never activates in practice and everything >=20 resolves to isotonic. That is a different, arguably more interesting issue — but it is not what this finding describes, and the finding's stated reasoning (equal thresholds, missing window) is verifiably wrong. Default to real=false stands.

### `SWEE-32` — Config exposes seven API-key secrets as plain dataclass fields with no redaction
- **confirmed ✓** · source: sweep · effort: trivial · file: `/Users/bjgilbert/Projects/Zoltar/sigma-predict/src/config.py:58-64`
- **evidence:** anthropic_api_key, openai_api_key, ... kalshi_api_key are stored as plain str fields on a standard @dataclass. dataclass auto-generates __repr__, so repr(config) / logging the config object / an exception that includes it will print every secret in cleartext. There is no field(repr=False) and no __repr__ override.
- **recommendation:** Mark secret fields with field(repr=False) (or wrap in a SecretStr-like type) so they don't leak via repr/logs/tracebacks.
- **verifier:** CONFIRMED (with scope correction). |source: src/config.py:58-64, src/pipeline/llm_audit.py| Severity: LOW (keep).

Structural facts verified exactly as claimed:
- src/config.py:58-64 declares seven secret fields (anthropic_api_key, openai_api_key, google_ai_api_key, tavily_api_key, metaculus_api_key, polymarket_api_key, kalshi_api_key) as plain `str` on a standard `@dataclass`.
- The class uses default `@dataclass` (line 56), so Python auto-generates `__repr__` that includes all fields. There is no `field(repr=False)` on any secret field and no `__repr__` override. The recommendation (mark with field(repr=False) or a SecretStr wrapper) is valid and standard.

Scope correction to the evidence: the finding asserts that "repr(config) / logging the config object / an exception that includes it will print every secret." That is a *latent* capability, not an *active* leak. I searched the codebase and found NO call path that actually exercises it: no repr(config)/str(config)/print(config), no logger call on the config object, no dataclasses.asdict(config), no vars()/__dict__ dump, no Streamlit st.write/st.json of config, and no serialization (json/pickle/yaml) of the Config object. So today nothing prints the secrets via repr.

Notably, the one place that does touch secret-adjacent data — src/pipeline/llm_audit.py — already redacts: _SECRET_KEY_PATTERN (line 27) forces any key matching api_key/token/secret/password/auth to "[REDACTED]" (line 35), and raw prompt/result text is hashed not stored. The module docstring explicitly states secrets are never written. This shows the codebase is already secret-aware, which both (a) lowers the practical risk and (b) confirms the team would accept the hardening recommendation.

Verdict: genuine and material as a defensive-hardening finding. The auto-generated __repr__ on a config that holds seven live API keys is a real foot-gun — the next developer who logs `config` for debugging, or any future traceback that captures the Config frame's locals, would leak all keys in cleartext. LOW severity is correct given no current exploit path and the personal-tool context. The fix is a one-line-per-field, zero-risk change.

### `SWEE-33` — apply_hedging_correction is dead code superseded by the scaled variant
- **confirmed ✓** · source: sweep · effort: trivial · file: `/Users/bjgilbert/Projects/Zoltar/sigma-predict/src/pipeline/calibration.py:103-114`
- **evidence:** calibrate() only ever calls apply_hedging_correction_scaled (lines 82,92). The non-scaled apply_hedging_correction (flat shift) is never invoked in the pipeline. Two near-identical functions with different math (flat shift vs distance-weighted) invite a future caller picking the wrong one.
- **recommendation:** Remove apply_hedging_correction, or document it as exported-for-tests-only if tests depend on it.
- **verifier:** Confirmed against the cited code. In /Users/bjgilbert/Projects/Zoltar/sigma-predict/src/pipeline/calibration.py, calibrate() invokes only apply_hedging_correction_scaled (lines 82 and 92). The flat-shift apply_hedging_correction (lines 103-114) is never called by any production code. A repo-wide grep shows its only references are its own definition and tests/test_calibration.py (import at line 7, plus 5 assertions lines 20-36). It is not in any __all__ (calibration.py has none), so it is not an intentional public export. The two functions share signature and naming stem (differ only by a _scaled suffix) but implement different math (flat shift vs distance-weighted), which is a legitimate maintainability smell.

Two caveats that temper but do not negate the finding: (1) The function is not strictly orphaned — 5 tests pin its behavior, so a naive deletion that leaves the tests breaks the build; the cleaner remediation is to delete the function AND its tests, since they validate logic production no longer exercises. The finding's recommendation to document it as exported-for-tests-only is weaker, because the tests test the function itself, not a production-relied-upon behavior. (2) The 'future caller picks the wrong one' risk is real but bounded: no production code imports individual functions from this module — calibrate() is the sole entry point — so the wrong-caller hazard is confined to future test/dev code, not the live pipeline.

The finding is factually accurate and materially correct as a code-hygiene issue. Low severity is appropriate.

### `SWEE-34` — Human adjustment is stored as a delta against calibrated_probability, making it ambiguous after the fact
- **REFUTED ✗** · source: sweep · effort: small · file: `/Users/bjgilbert/Projects/Zoltar/sigma-predict/src/review/human_review.py:211-216`
- **evidence:** adjustment = new_p - cal.calibrated_probability is stored as human_adjustment, and orchestrator.py:421-422 reconstructs final_prob = cal_prob + review.human_adjustment then clamps. The record persists only the delta, not the human's chosen absolute probability. If calibrated_probability is later recomputed/migrated, the stored delta no longer reproduces the human's intended value, and the clamp at orchestrator.py:423 can silently move the submitted prob away from what the reviewer typed (e.g. reviewer enters 0.99 but cal_prob+delta clamps differently).
- **recommendation:** Store the human's absolute chosen probability (the reviewer typed an absolute value 0.01-0.99) rather than a delta, and derive the delta for display only.
- **verifier:** The code at human_review.py:211-216 and orchestrator.py:421-423 matches the finding's literal description: `adjustment = new_p - cal.calibrated_probability` is stored as `human_adjustment`, and the orchestrator reconstructs `final_prob = cal_prob + review.human_adjustment` then clamps. But the claimed HARM does not occur, on two counts.

CLAIM 1 (clamp silently moves the submitted prob away from what the reviewer typed) is mathematically false. The reviewer's input `new_p` is already constrained to [0.01, 0.99] at human_review.py:204 before the delta is computed. Reconstruction is exact: `cal_prob + (new_p - cal_prob) == new_p`. The clamp at orchestrator.py:423 is therefore a no-op on a value already in range. The parenthetical example ("reviewer enters 0.99 but cal_prob+delta clamps differently") cannot happen — 0.99 reconstructs to exactly 0.99 and the clamp leaves it unchanged. There is no path where the submitted value diverges from the typed value in the same-session flow, which is the only flow that exists.

CLAIM 2 (delta becomes ambiguous after the fact if calibrated_probability is recomputed/migrated) is neutralized by persistence. The human's absolute chosen value is durably stored as `record.submitted.probability` (orchestrator.py:434-436), and ALL downstream consumers read the submitted absolute value, not the reconstructed delta: query.py:116-119 (resolved pairs), 145-148 (brier-by-domain), 184-187 (brier-by-horizon) all prefer `record.submitted.probability` and only fall back to `calibrated_probability` when no submission exists. The delta is reconstructed exactly once, in-session, immediately before submission — before any migration could occur. No code path ever re-derives the final probability from `cal_prob + human_adjustment` at a later time. There is no calibration-recompute-in-place path that mutates existing records' calibrated_probability either. So the migration-ambiguity scenario has no actual consumer that would be harmed.

What remains true is a code-style observation: storing a delta against a derived quantity, when the reviewer actually typed an absolute value, is awkward and the recommendation (store the absolute, derive the delta for display) is reasonable hygiene. But this is cosmetic, not a material correctness or data-integrity defect, because the authoritative submitted value is already persisted as an absolute. Default-to-false applies: the asserted harm is refuted by the persisted Submission.probability and by the algebra of the clamp.

### `SWEE-41` — URL question fetch uses naive last-path-segment heuristic that breaks on real platform URLs
- **confirmed ✓** · source: sweep · effort: small · file: `/Users/bjgilbert/Projects/Zoltar/sigma-predict/pages/2_Predict.py:178-179`
- **evidence:** qid_from_url = url.rstrip('/').split('/')[-1]. Real Metaculus URLs are like https://www.metaculus.com/questions/12345/some-slug/ where the last segment is the slug, not the numeric id; trailing query strings (?...) or fragments (#...) are also passed verbatim to client.get_question(). The 'platform-specific heuristic' comment (line 172) is aspirational — the code is a single platform-agnostic guess. The mode is labeled 'URL' but does not reliably do what the name claims.
- **recommendation:** Add per-platform URL parsing (regex for the numeric id segment, strip query/fragment), or delegate to a client.parse_url() method. Validate the extracted id before calling get_question and surface a clear error when extraction fails.
- **verifier:** Confirmed at the code level and empirically. /Users/bjgilbert/Projects/Zoltar/sigma-predict/pages/2_Predict.py:178-179 contains exactly `qid_from_url = url.rstrip("/").split("/")[-1]`, passed directly to client.get_question() on line 182.

Behavioral claims verified by tracing real URLs:
- Canonical Metaculus URL `https://www.metaculus.com/questions/12345/some-slug/` extracts `'some-slug'`, NOT the numeric id `12345`. Metaculus client (src/platforms/metaculus.py:121) hits `/questions/{question_id}/`, so this raises ValueError "Question some-slug not found". Confirmed metaculus.py:55 that canonical URLs carry the numeric id mid-path with a trailing slug + slash.
- Query strings yield `'?foo=bar'`, fragments yield `'#section'` — both garbage forwarded verbatim. Confirmed.
- Polymarket URLs are `/event/{slug}` but the client (polymarket.py:111) expects `/markets/{condition_id}` — last-segment slug is also wrong.
- Only Kalshi (last segment IS the ticker) and bare numeric-final Metaculus URLs happen to work.

No client.parse_url() exists anywhere in base.py or the platform modules — the "platform-specific heuristic" comment (line 172) is aspirational; the inline comment on line 178 itself admits "Naive ID extraction". The URL mode does not reliably do what its name claims.

Could not refute: the bug is genuine and material to the URL input feature.

Severity: keep at low. It is confined to a Streamlit convenience UI; failures surface a visible "Fetch failed" error rather than corrupting data or producing silent wrong results, and the ID input mode is a working fallback. No security or data-integrity impact.

### `SWEE-42` — Search personas multiselect has no session key — selection is lost on rerun and reverts to default
- **REFUTED ✗** · source: sweep · effort: trivial · file: `/Users/bjgilbert/Projects/Zoltar/sigma-predict/pages/2_Predict.py:198-203`
- **evidence:** sel_persona_vals = st.multiselect('Search personas', options=all_personas, default=[SearchPersona.NEWS.value], help=...) has no key=. The Predict page reruns frequently (search selection on_select='rerun', the run_every=1 fragment, and the explicit st.rerun() at line 258). Without a key persisting the widget into session_state, Streamlit re-applies the default on widget recreation in some rerun paths, so a user's persona customization can silently snap back to ['news'] right before/at launch. num_runs (line 195) and verification_toggle (line 205) have the same no-key issue.
- **recommendation:** Give the persona multiselect (and num_runs / verification toggle) explicit key= values so selections persist across the page's frequent reruns, consistent with how KEY_SELECTED_PLATFORM is keyed.
- **verifier:** REFUTED. The finding's load-bearing technical claim — that a Streamlit multiselect without an explicit key= "re-applies the default on widget recreation in some rerun paths" so the selection "snaps back to ['news']" — misrepresents Streamlit's widget-state model.

Code reviewed: /Users/bjgilbert/Projects/Zoltar/sigma-predict/pages/2_Predict.py:195-258.

How Streamlit actually works: widget value persistence across reruns does NOT require an explicit key. Streamlit assigns each widget an auto-generated ID from its type + identity parameters (label, options, default). On a rerun, the prior user-set value is restored via that ID; the `default`/`value` argument is honored only on first instantiation, not re-applied each rerun. A no-key widget reverts to default only when (a) its identity params (e.g. options/label) change between runs, or (b) it is conditionally removed from the rendered page and later re-added (state is dropped when a widget is not rendered).

Neither condition holds here:
- Line 197-203: options=all_personas is a static list ([p.value for p in SearchPersona]); default and label are static. The multiselect, num_input (195), and toggle (205) are rendered unconditionally inside `with right_col:` on every run — never behind a conditional, so they are never removed and re-added.
- The cited rerun triggers are all plain reruns that re-render these widgets with identical identity params: on_select="rerun" (143), the explicit st.rerun() (258), and the run_every=1 fragment (264). The fragment moreover re-executes only the fragment scope, not the main script, so it does not re-instantiate these widgets at all.
- The launch path reads sel_persona_vals (237), num_runs (254), and verification_toggle (234) WITHIN the same script run, BEFORE st.rerun() at 258. There is no rerun between read and use, so no "snap back right before launch" path exists.

The only true property of no-key here is that the values are not readable from st.session_state elsewhere — but the code never reads them from session_state; it uses the direct return values. So there is no functional bug. The KEY_SELECTED_PLATFORM comparison (line 104) is not analogous: that widget is keyed because its value IS read from session_state to drive KEY_SEARCH_RESULTS logic — a genuinely different requirement.

Conclusion: no selection loss occurs on any rerun path described. The asserted user-facing symptom does not exist. Adding keys would be a harmless minor cleanup, but the finding describes a bug that does not happen.

### `SWEE-43` — Adjustment-vs-Brier scatter recomputes pre/post Brier from clamped calibrated prob, not the actual submitted probability
- **REFUTED ✗** · source: sweep · effort: small · file: `/Users/bjgilbert/Projects/Zoltar/sigma-predict/components/charts.py:269-272`
- **evidence:** pre_brier = (cal_prob - outcome) ** 2; post_prob = max(0.01, min(0.99, cal_prob + adj)); post_brier = (post_prob - outcome) ** 2; delta = post_brier - pre_brier. This reconstructs the 'before' Brier from calibrated_probability, but the actually-scored Brier (record.resolution.brier_score) is computed by the pipeline from the submitted probability, which already incorporates the human adjustment. So the chart's 'pre_brier' is a re-derived hypothetical that does not match the stored brier_score, and the human_adjustment may double-count (it was applied at submission AND is re-applied here). The chart claims to show 'post-review improvement' (line 247) but compares two synthetic quantities rather than pre- vs post-adjustment reality.
- **recommendation:** Define the baseline explicitly: compute pre_brier from the pre-adjustment calibrated prob and post_brier from the actually-submitted prob (or the stored resolution.brier_score), and confirm whether human_adjustment is the delta-from-calibrated or delta-from-submitted. Document which quantity the axis represents.
- **verifier:** REFUTED. I traced the full data flow across charts.py, orchestrator.py, resolve.py, and human_review.py.

Data flow confirmed:
- human_review.py:211 — human_adjustment = new_p - calibrated_probability (delta-from-calibrated, applied once).
- orchestrator.py:421-435 — final_prob = clamp(cal_prob + human_adjustment); record.submitted.probability = final_prob.
- resolve.py:105-108 — resolution.brier_score = brier_score(record.submitted.probability, outcome) = (clamp(cal_prob+adj) - outcome)**2.

In charts.py:269-272:
- post_prob = max(0.01, min(0.99, cal_prob + adj)) = clamp(cal_prob+adj) = exactly the submitted probability.
- post_brier = (post_prob - outcome)**2 = EXACTLY the stored resolution.brier_score (modulo 6-decimal rounding).
- pre_brier = (cal_prob - outcome)**2 = the pre-adjustment counterfactual.
- delta = post_brier - pre_brier = the genuine before/after effect of the human adjustment.

The finding's three core claims fail:
1. "pre_brier does not match stored brier_score" — true but not a defect. pre_brier is intentionally the pre-adjustment counterfactual; brier_score is the post value. A before/after delta REQUIRES them to differ. The chart never claims pre_brier equals the stored score.
2. "human_adjustment may double-count (applied at submission AND re-applied here)" — FALSE. The delta computation does not consume the stored brier_score at all (it is only used as a skip-gate). It reconstructs post from cal_prob + adj once. cal_prob + adj = cal_prob + (new_p - cal_prob) = new_p = the submitted prob. Single application, no double-count.
3. "compares two synthetic quantities rather than pre- vs post-adjustment reality" — FALSE. post_brier IS post-adjustment reality (equals the stored score); pre_brier IS the legitimate pre-adjustment counterfactual. Axis label ("Brier delta, negative = improved") and docstring ("post-review improvement") accurately describe exactly this delta. The chart is correct.

Only marginally-defensible nit (not what the finding alleges): the chart re-derives post_brier rather than reusing the stored resolution.brier_score, so a future divergence in the submission formula or 6-decimal rounding could create a tiny mismatch — a cosmetic robustness/maintainability nit, not an analytical error. The finding's substantive thesis is wrong.

### `SWEE-44` — Adjustment slider can raise if calibrated_probability falls outside [0.01, 0.99]
- **REFUTED ✗** · source: sweep · effort: trivial · file: `/Users/bjgilbert/Projects/Zoltar/sigma-predict/pages/2_Predict.py:325-332`
- **evidence:** st.slider('Adjusted probability', min_value=0.01, max_value=0.99, value=float(cal_prob), ...). cal_prob comes from getattr(cal, 'calibrated_probability', 0.5). Most calibration paths clamp to [0.01,0.99] (src/pipeline/calibration.py:206,216), but CalibrationResult.calibrated_probability has a raw default of 0.5 and not every code path is guaranteed clamped; any value <0.01 or >0.99 (or an exact 0.0/1.0 from an unclamped path) makes Streamlit raise 'value must be between min and max'. There is no clamp at the UI boundary.
- **recommendation:** Clamp the slider value: value=float(max(0.01, min(0.99, cal_prob))) before passing to st.slider.
- **verifier:** The mechanical UI claim is accurate: pages/2_Predict.py:325-332 passes value=float(cal_prob) to st.slider with min=0.01/max=0.99 and no boundary clamp, and Streamlit does raise if value is outside [min,max]. However, the finding's load-bearing premise — that an out-of-range calibrated_probability can actually reach this slider — does not survive code tracing.

Source verification:
- Only ONE construction site for the calibration object exists in the entire codebase (orchestrator.py:351-353; note the class is CalibrationData, not "CalibrationResult" as the finding states). It always sets calibrated_probability=cal_prob returned from calibrate().
- Every one of calibrate()'s four return paths is clamped to [0.01,0.99]: Phase 2 platt_transform returns max(0.01,min(0.99,...)) (calibration.py:216); Phase 1 isotonic_transform returns max(0.01,min(0.99,result)) (line 206); Phase 1 fallback and Phase 0 both call apply_hedging_correction_scaled, whose >0.5 branch returns min(0.99,...) and <0.5 branch returns max(0.01,...). The only passthrough that skips a clamp is the exact probability==0.5 case (line 130), which is in range.
- The getattr default is 0.5 (models.py:128) — in range.
- The record feeding the slider is the fresh in-memory output of orchestrator.predict() (pages/2_Predict.py:72,277), not a disk-deserialized or externally-tampered record, so there is no path to inject an unclamped/legacy value.

The finding's own hedge ("not every code path is guaranteed clamped") is factually wrong — I traced all paths and they are all clamped. The "exact 0.0/1.0 from an unclamped path" scenario has no actual source. The validator gate at validator.py:119-128 checks for out-of-range but is a NON-BLOCKING warning (appended to pipeline_warnings); its existence is belt-and-suspenders defensiveness, not evidence the condition occurs. This is a latent defense-in-depth gap, not a material reachable bug.

### `SWEE-48` — monitor.py --interval option is a complete no-op
- **confirmed ✓** · source: sweep · effort: trivial · file: `/Users/bjgilbert/Projects/Zoltar/sigma-predict/scripts/monitor.py:13`
- **evidence:** `@click.option("--interval", type=int, default=3600, ...)` then `def main(interval: int):` whose entire body prints three 'Phase 3 / not yet implemented' lines and never references `interval`. The flag is typed and documented as a poll interval but has zero effect — a footgun for anyone scripting against it expecting polling behavior.
- **recommendation:** Either drop the option until implemented, or have main exit non-zero / print that the interval is ignored, so callers don't assume a daemon is running.
- **verifier:** Confirmed against the actual code at scripts/monitor.py. The cited evidence is accurate verbatim: line 13 declares `@click.option("--interval", type=int, default=3600, help="Poll interval in seconds")`, line 14 is `def main(interval: int):`, and the function body (lines 19-21) only calls three `click.echo` statements with "Phase 3 / not yet implemented" text. The parameter `interval` is never referenced anywhere in the body — verified by reading the entire 26-line file. So the factual core of the finding is correct: the flag is fully wired into the CLI signature but has zero behavioral effect.

The "complete no-op" characterization is literally true for the interval value. The recommendation (drop the option or signal it is ignored) is reasonable.

However, the finding's severity framing slightly overstates the footgun. The module is self-evidently a stub: the docstring (line 17) says "Phase 3 feature — not yet implemented," and critically the FIRST line of runtime output (line 19) prints "Platform monitor is a Phase 3 feature and not yet implemented." Any caller who actually runs the command is told unambiguously that nothing is polling — they cannot reasonably assume a daemon is running after seeing that output. The whole command does nothing regardless of any flag, so a no-op flag on a no-op command is benign. The risk is confined to someone reading only the module-level usage example (line 4) and scripting against it without running it. That is a narrow path, and the tool exits 0 with explanatory text rather than silently appearing to work.

### `SWEE-50` — dashboard.py imports sys but never uses it
- **confirmed ✓** · source: sweep · effort: trivial · file: `/Users/bjgilbert/Projects/Zoltar/sigma-predict/scripts/dashboard.py:9`
- **evidence:** `import sys` at line 9; grep for `sys.` in the file returns nothing. Dead import (the early `return` at line 44 is used instead of sys.exit). Minor, but it suggests an exit path was refactored away and the import left behind.
- **recommendation:** Remove the unused import.
- **verifier:** Confirmed by reading the file and grepping. `import sys` appears at line 9 (scripts/dashboard.py). A grep for "sys" returns only that line — there is no `sys.exit`, `sys.argv`, `sys.path`, `sys.stdout`, or any other reference anywhere in the 112-line file. The `if not resolved:` branch at line 42-44 uses a bare `return` to exit early rather than `sys.exit()`, consistent with the finding's hypothesis that an exit path was refactored to a return and the import left behind. The import is genuinely unused (dead). The finding is accurate, code-grounded, and does not overstate impact. Severity is correctly classified as low — it is a cosmetic lint-level issue with no functional, security, or correctness impact (linters like ruff/flake8 F401 would flag it). The recommendation (remove the unused import) is correct and trivial.

### `SWEE-51` — predict.py --url overrides explicit --platform without warning, and ignores --question-id
- **confirmed ✓** · source: sweep · effort: small · file: `/Users/bjgilbert/Projects/Zoltar/sigma-predict/scripts/predict.py:90`
- **evidence:** When `--url` is supplied, line 91 does `platform, question_id = _parse_url(url)`, silently clobbering any explicitly-passed `--platform`/`--question-id`. A user running `--platform kalshi --url <metaculus url>` gets metaculus with no notice. Polymarket/Kalshi URL parsing (lines 161-167) just takes the last path segment with no validation, so a trailing-slash or query-string URL yields a garbage 'question id' that later fails deep in get_question.
- **recommendation:** Make --url mutually exclusive with --platform/--question-id (or warn on conflict), and validate the extracted id for non-metaculus platforms instead of blindly taking parts[-1].
- **verifier:** Both claims are confirmed against the code at /Users/bjgilbert/Projects/Zoltar/sigma-predict/scripts/predict.py.

Claim 1 (silent --url override): Lines 90-91 unconditionally do `platform, question_id = _parse_url(url)` when --url is supplied. --platform has `default="metaculus"` (line 41), so a user passing `--platform kalshi --url <metaculus-url>` silently gets metaculus, and `--question-id` is likewise clobbered. CONFIRMED. Caveat: because --platform defaults to metaculus, Click cannot distinguish an explicit --platform from the default without extra wiring, so the explicit-conflict scenario is somewhat narrow — but the silent clobber behavior is real.

Claim 2 (no validation on polymarket/kalshi id): Lines 161-167 do `url.rstrip("/").split("/")` then `parts[-1]` with zero validation. rstrip handles trailing slashes, but a query string (e.g. .../foo?bar=baz) yields a garbage id `foo?bar=baz` that fails later in get_question (confirmed get_question exists at polymarket.py:108 / kalshi.py:138 and takes question_id directly). The metaculus path (lines 153-159) by contrast parses properly and raises BadParameter on failure, so the gap is specifically non-metaculus. CONFIRMED.

Neither issue silently corrupts results in the common documented path (metaculus, properly validated): one is silent precedence when the user supplied conflicting flags, the other is a downstream error on malformed input. Both are genuine robustness/usability defects in a developer CLI.

### `SWEE-52` — batch_predict counts cost/submitted but a per-question crash leaves partial state with no summary of failures
- **confirmed ✓** · source: sweep · effort: trivial · file: `/Users/bjgilbert/Projects/Zoltar/sigma-predict/scripts/batch_predict.py:96`
- **evidence:** The per-question loop catches `Exception` (line 96), echoes the error, and `continue`s, but never tracks a failure count. The final summary (lines 100-102) reports `{submitted}/{len(questions)} submitted` and total cost, with no indication of how many questions errored out. A run where 4/5 questions threw still prints a clean-looking '1/5 submitted'. Cost from failed runs is also not accumulated since the exception skips line 93.
- **recommendation:** Track and report a failure count in the summary (e.g. 'submitted X, errored Y, logged Z') so partial batch failures are visible.
- **verifier:** Confirmed against the actual code in /Users/bjgilbert/Projects/Zoltar/sigma-predict/scripts/batch_predict.py.

Verified claims:
- Lines 96-98: the per-question loop catches `except Exception as e`, echoes `ERROR: {e}` to stderr, and `continue`s. No failure counter is incremented.
- Lines 79-80: only `total_cost` and `submitted` are tracked across iterations. There is no `errored`/`failed` counter anywhere.
- Lines 100-102: the final summary prints only `{submitted}/{len(questions)} submitted` and `Total cost`. It contains no count of errored questions.
- Line 93 (`total_cost += record.cost.total_cost_usd`) is skipped when `predict()` raises, so cost from failed runs is not accumulated — accurate.

The finding is actually slightly understated in one way that supports it: the summary conflates two distinct non-submission outcomes. Line 94 (`if record.submitted`) means a question can run successfully yet not be counted as submitted. So `len(questions) - submitted` does NOT equal the error count — it mixes errored questions with successfully-predicted-but-not-submitted ones. This means the operator genuinely cannot recover the failure count from the summary, even by subtraction. The finding's example ('4/5 threw still prints 1/5 submitted') is correct.

Materiality: this is a real observability gap. Errors ARE echoed per-question to stderr (line 97), so failures are not fully silent in an interactive run — the finding correctly acknowledges this. The defect is confined to the aggregate summary, which masks partial failures when the stream is logged/scraped or scrolled past. The recommendation to report errored/logged counts is sound and directly closes the gap.

### `SWEE-53` — predict.py search picker can loop forever on EOF / non-interactive stdin
- **REFUTED ✗** · source: sweep · effort: small · file: `/Users/bjgilbert/Projects/Zoltar/sigma-predict/scripts/predict.py:114`
- **evidence:** `while True: choice = click.prompt("Select question number", type=int)`. Under a piped/closed stdin (CI, `< /dev/null`), click.prompt raises Abort on EOF which exits, but on repeated invalid integer input there is no attempt limit — and the loop only breaks on a valid 1..len choice. There is no escape/cancel option offered to the user other than Ctrl-C, and the prompt is unreachable in any non-interactive batch context even though predict.py is the documented single-question entry point.
- **recommendation:** Offer a '0 to cancel' option and/or bound retries; detect non-interactive stdin and fail with a clear message rather than relying on Abort.
- **verifier:** REFUTED. The central claim — that the loop "can loop forever on EOF / non-interactive stdin" — is false. I tested the exact construct against click 8.3.1 (the installed/declared version, click>=8.1.0 in pyproject.toml).

Empirical results (/tmp test reproducing predict.py:114-119):
- Piped/closed stdin (`< /dev/null`): click.prompt immediately raises click.Abort on EOF, which propagates out and terminates the program. No loop.
- Repeated invalid integer ("abc\nxyz\n"): each is re-prompted, then EOF -> Abort. Loop terminates.
- Repeated out-of-range integers ("9\n99\n"): each prints "Enter 1-3", then EOF -> Abort. Loop terminates.

The loop cannot spin forever from a piped or closed stdin because any finite input stream ends in EOF, and click.prompt converts EOF to Abort, which is NOT caught by the loop and therefore breaks out. The only way to loop indefinitely is an infinite stream of non-EOF invalid bytes — which is not the "CI / < /dev/null" scenario the finding describes. /dev/null yields immediate EOF and immediate Abort.

The finding even concedes "click.prompt raises Abort on EOF which exits" in its own evidence, then contradicts itself by titling the issue "loop forever on EOF." The title is the refuted part; the body partially knows better.

Secondary framing is also misleading: the prompt at line 114 is reached ONLY on the `--search` path (line 100). The documented batch/single-question entry paths are `--question-id` (line 97) and `--url` (line 90-91), neither of which touches the loop. So "unreachable in any non-interactive batch context even though predict.py is the documented single-question entry point" is wrong — the canonical non-interactive invocation (`--question-id`) bypasses the picker entirely. Search-by-term is inherently an interactive disambiguation step.

What remains true is minor and cosmetic, not the claimed defect: there is no explicit "0 to cancel" affordance and no retry bound. But Ctrl-C and EOF both cleanly abort, so there is no hang, no resource leak, no infinite loop. This is a UX nicety at most, not a material correctness or robustness bug.

### `TOOL-273` — llm_router response.content[0].text union-attr errors (Anthropic SDK block union)
- **confirmed ✓** · source: tool · effort: small · file: `/Users/bjgilbert/Projects/Zoltar/sigma-predict/src/pipeline/llm_router.py:67`
- **evidence:** 11 mypy [union-attr] errors all on line 67: e.g. "Item 'ThinkingBlock' of 'TextBlock | ThinkingBlock | ... <7 more items>' has no attribute 'text' [union-attr]". Code: `return (response.content[0].text, ...)` — content[0] is a union of ~12 block types and only TextBlock has .text. If the API ever returns a non-text first block (thinking/tool-use enabled), this raises AttributeError.
- **recommendation:** Narrow the block type before access, e.g. iterate content and pick the first TextBlock, or assert/cast: `block = response.content[0]; text = block.text if isinstance(block, anthropic.types.TextBlock) else ...`. Low risk today (plain messages return a text block first) but brittle if thinking/tools are enabled.
- **verifier:** reported by an executed analysis tool (ground truth)
